# 40. Ghāfir — Expanded Commentary

*Verse-by-verse expanded commentary on Sūrah Ghāfir (chapter 40), eighty-five verses, in the style and at the depth of the expanded commentary on Sūrah al-Baqarah. Each verse is treated as its own section: the text and translation, then an expanded commentary with individually titled discussions of language, argument, theology, history, and pastoral application. All quotations from the Qur'an, the hadith, and the commentators are set in block form where they are quoted at length, and the analysis draws on the uploaded source commentary alongside the classical tafsīr tradition.*

---

## Introduction to the Sūrah

Ghāfir is a Makkan sūrah, and some hold that it was revealed directly after Sūrah 39, al-Zumar (JJ). A minority report treats verses 56–57 as Madinan (IJ, JJ, Q, Ṭs), others add verse 55 to them (Q, Ṭs), and still others say that verse 55 alone is Madinan (Āl), on the grounds that the injunction to *hymn the praise of your Lord at eventide and at dawn* refers to the morning and sunset prayers, whose obligation belongs to the last years of the Makkan period. Nothing in the sūrah's argument turns on the question: its rhetoric is the rhetoric of persecution, and its subject is what a community under pressure is permitted to hope.

The chapter takes its name from verse 3's description of God as *ghāfir al-dhanb*, the Forgiver of sin — the only sūrah in the Qur'an named after a participial attribute of mercy in this form. It is also called *al-Muʾmin*, "The Believer," after the believing man from the household of Pharaoh who speaks at verses 28–45, and *al-Faḍl*, "The Bounty," after *dhū al-ṭawl* in verse 3 (IJ, Q). The three titles are not interchangeable, and their sequence is a summary of the sūrah: mercy named first, then the human being who dares to speak truth in a tyrant's court, then the grace that makes both possible.

Ghāfir opens the series of seven sūrahs whose beginnings carry the separated letters *ḥāʾ mīm* followed by a reference to the revelation of the Book — 40, 41, 42, 43, 44, 45, and 46 — known collectively as the *Ḥawāmīm* and, in the phrase transmitted from Ibn ʿAbbās, as *al-ʿarāʾis*, "the Brides" (IK, Q). They belong to the same period and share a common situation: a small community being mocked, boycotted, and tortured, told repeatedly that the older nations were stronger and got nothing for it, and instructed to answer by holding on. The *Ḥawāmīm* provide solace by foretelling the triumph of the revelation and the collapse of those who oppose it, and they do so with an unusual density of *sign*-language: night and day, ships and animals, embryology, the heavens as a built structure — the cosmos as a witness stand.

The sūrah's own movement can be traced as follows. Verses 1–6 affirm the revelation and name God's mercy and severity, then repudiate those who dispute concerning God's signs, a theme to which the chapter returns at verses 35, 56, and 69. Verses 7–9 contain the angels' prayer for the believers, which is the sūrah's theological center: intercession is real, but it is *already happening*, and it is conditional on earthly qualities (faith, migration, protection from the arrogant). Verses 10–20 describe the disbelievers' answer when they are shown the Fire — the confession of two deaths and two lives, the collapse of their idols into non-reference, and the statement that God judges with the truth. Verses 21–50 unfold the long account of Mūsā and Pharaoh, whose hinge is the believing man of Pharaoh's family (verses 28–45), the single most quoted passage of the sūrah and the Qur'an's clearest text on the ethics of speech inside a hostile institution. Verses 51–60 affirm divine aid for the messengers and the believers, then condemn those too proud to worship. Verses 61–68 inventory the cosmic and biological signs, and verses 69–76 describe the punishment of the deniers and the closure of argument. Verses 77–78 counsel patience and state that no messenger brings a sign except by God's leave; verses 79–82 reflect on God's generosity and power in cattle, ships, and history; and verses 83–85 show that a belief first uttered at the moment of God's might avails nothing, *sunnat Allāh* having passed among His servants.

Two features make this sūrah a landmark in the Qur'an's moral vocabulary. First, its insistence that *knowledge* is a moral matter: the disputers are not ignorant but *pleased with what they have of knowledge* (verse 83), and the sūrah's remedy is the *ulū al-albāb*, "the possessors of depth," who reflect on creation and take heed (verses 54, 65). Second, its treatment of power: Pharaoh is given one of the Qur'an's longest narratives not to be vilified but to be *analyzed* — the grammar of his commands, his tower, his claim to *rubūbiyya*, his drowning preserved as a sign (verses 36–37, 26). Ghāfir is the Qur'an's study of how a regime defends itself, and of the single courtier who refused to let it.

The sūrah's most durable gift to Muslim piety, however, is its prayer-doctrine. Verses 60 and 66 give the Qur'an's most direct promise of answered supplication and its most direct statement of exclusive worship, and verses 7–9 show the angels asking forgiveness for the believers before the believers think to ask it for themselves. Between those two facts the sūrah places the whole of human responsibility — which is why the chapter's last imperative, at verse 77, is the one it repeats: *be patient*.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:1

> **1** *Ḥā. Mīm.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Letters and the Situation**

*Ḥā. Mīm*. Before the sūrah says anything, it spells. The two letters belong to the *ḥurūf muqaṭṭaʿāt*, the disjointed letters that open twenty-nine sūrahs, and the tradition's most restrained and most quoted word about them is Abū Jaʿfar al-Bāqir's — *al-ḥurūf al-muqaṭṭaʿāt is the secret of God and the mystery of the Qur'an; there is no need to seek it out* — which is not an evasion but a rule about the order of the surah's encounter with its audience: the listener is made to stand, for one breath, in front of something he cannot decode, before he is told what God is like. Ibn ʿAbbās, Mujāhid, Ikrima, and al-Shaʿbī variously glossed the letters as names of God, names of the Qur'an, names of the sūrahs, or oaths; al-Zamakhsharī (*al-Kashshāf*) and al-Rāzī (*Mafātīḥ al-Ghayb*) preserve the range with a lexicographer's care and, in the end, with the same conclusion: the letters are addressable but not exhaustible, and their *function* is available even where their *referent* is not.

**The Ḥawāmīm: A Library with a Single Stamp**

Ghāfir is the first of the seven *Ḥawāmīm* (40–46), and the source commentary notes that the series was recognized as a unit by the earliest authorities: they open alike, they were revealed in the same period, and — per the report from Ibn ʿAbbās preserved by al-Suyūṭī in *al-Itqān* — they are *al-ʿarāʾis*, "the brides," because a bride is met with a reserve that must be approached gently. The comparison is exact. A reader who wants the *Ḥawāmīm* to yield on the first page is being told, by the very letters, that some things are opened by patience. The arrangement of the series also matters: 40 (Ghāfir) begins with mercy's names, 42 (al-Shūrā) with the *ḥujja* of shared revelation, 43–46 (Zukhruf, Dukhān, Jāthiya, Aḥqāf) with argument, exposure, the kneeling nations, and the final conscription of parents and children into the verdict. The first chapter sets the tone for all of it: severity will be mentioned, but mercy gets the opening word.

**Two Letters, Two Attributes**

The source commentary records the reading, transmitted from Q, that ties the *ḥawāmīm*' letters to 39:63 — *to Him belong the keys of the heavens and the earth* — so that *ḥāʾ mīm* are, as it were, keys to divine treasuries; and the further report that the *muqaṭṭaʿāt* of Yūnus, Hūd, Yūsuf, al-Raʿd, Ibrāhīm, al-Ḥijr (a-l-r), the *ḥawāmīm* (ḥ-m), and Qalam (n) combine into the Name *al-Raḥmān* — a reading al-Suyūṭī discusses in *al-Itqān* without endorsing it as exegesis but which tells you how the early commentators heard these letters: as *names*, or at least as sound-shapes pointing toward names. Al-Zamakhsharī's own suggestion, that each letter stands for a Divine Name beginning with it (*ḥāʾ*: al-Ḥamīd, al-Ḥalīm, al-Ḥakīm; *mīm*: Mālik, al-Majīd, al-Mutakabbir), is worth pausing on for this sūrah, whose first full verse names *al-ʿAzīz al-ʿAlīm* and whose third names *ghāfir al-dhanb* and *dhū al-ṭawl*: the letters' atmosphere — majesty, sovereignty, power, and the pride that belongs to no creature — is the sūrah's actual subject.

**The Epistemic Move**

Read next to 40:2's *tan-zīlu al-kitābi* and 40:3's four attributes, the letters do a piece of argumentative work that is easy to miss. Ghāfir's opening claim is that a *Book* has been sent down from the *ʿAzīz, the ʿAlīm* — and a sūrah that begins with a divine *ʿilm* wider than human *ʿilm* has already, in two letters, refuted the possibility of exhaustive critique. This is why the *muqaṭṭaʿāt* are so often paired in the classical sources with 40:4's reproach, *mā yujādilu fī āyāti Llāhi illā alladhīna kafarū*: the disputers treat the Book as an object they have mastered, and the sūrah begins by planting inside it something they cannot master. Al-Bāqir's counsel — do not go hunting for the meaning — is not agnosticism but the correct posture of a reader who has been told, before anything else, that he is not the measure.

**The Sound as Sense**

Finally, what the letters *do* when recited. *Hā, mīm*: a breath released from the deepest part of the throat, then a sound closed by the lips — opening and closing, exhalation and seal. Whether or not that is a meaning, it is the sūrah's structure: Ghāfir opens with *ghāfir al-dhanb* and closes with *wa-khāsira hunālika al-kāfirūn*, the same two movements of release and closure played out across eighty-five verses on the scale of a human destiny. The *ḥawāmīm* have been recited to the afflicted for fourteen centuries beginning with that breath and that closure, and the earliest Muslims in Makkah — the ones the source commentary says these sūrahs were revealed to console — heard, in the first sound of this chapter, a claim about who holds the keys.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:2

> **2** *The revelation of the Book — from God, the All-Mighty, the All-Knowing.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sentence Made of Two Halves That Prove Each Other**

*Tan-zīlu al-kitābi mina Llāhi al-ʿAzīzi al-ʿAlīm*. The nominal sentence has no verb, and Arabic's rule for such a sentence is *thubūt wa-dawām* — permanence and continuity: the revelation is not reported as an event that occurred but stated as a state that obtains. Al-Rāzī presses the point on the other side as well: the *mubtadaʾ* is *tanzīl* (a verbal noun), so that the sentence asserts both a *descending* and a *standing*. What has come down is, by its very coming-down, still being sent; what is permanent here is not the parchment but the *act* of address. That is the sūrah's answer to the anxious question the whole *Ḥawāmīm* cycle keeps asking — will God keep speaking to us while Pharaoh keeps killing us? — and the answer is embedded before the first proposition: the Book's mode of existence is *tanzīl*.

**"Tan-zīl": the Word for Coming-Down in Stages**

*Al-tanzīl*, from *n-z-l*, is the noun of a thing lowered into place where the receiver can reach it, and the Qur'an's near-synonyms are distinguished with care in the classical discussion: *tanzīl* is used of what is revealed in parts, over time and in response to circumstances, whereas *inzāl* (as in 2:185, *Ramaḍānu lladhī unzila fīhi al-Qurʾān*, and 17:105, *wa-bi-l-ḥaqqi anzalnāhu wa-bi-l-ḥaqqi nazal*) carries the sense of the whole being sent down. The sūrah's word for scripture here is thus the word for *graduated* descent — which is precisely the form Ghāfir's audience needed: revelation that arrives verse by verse as the boycott tightens, as the Torture of the family of Yāsir proceeds, as the migration is proposed and then forbidden. The source commentary notes that the same construction governs 39:1, 45:2, and 46:2 (*tanzīlu al-kitābi min al-ʿAzīzi al-ʿAlīm* / *min Allāhi al-ʿAzīzi al-Ḥakīm*), and that the variations in the closing divine name across the *Ḥawāmīm* are themselves the interpretive key — this sūrah ends the clause on *al-ʿAlīm*, and the next two on *al-Ḥakīm*.

**The Two Names That Frame the Chapter**

*Al-ʿAzīz* and *al-ʿAlīm* are the sūrah's two running titles, and both recur with a frequency that can only be deliberate. *Al-ʿAzīz*, the Insuperable, recurs at the sūrah's pressure points — it closes the reckoning scene at 40:16 (*lil-mulki lillāhi al-wāḥidi al-qahhār*, framed by 40:1's pair), and it underwrites the promise at 40:56's neighborhood (*wa-mā kaydu lladhīna kafarū illā fī ḍalāl*), the same collocation of *ʿizza* with the believers' weakness as 3:176 and 8:66. but its decisive occurrence is here, because the Makkan audience was being told that *ʿizza* belongs to God while they themselves were *dhilla*-ridden and hunted: the same order as 63:8 (*yaqūlū la-in raǧaʿnā ilā al-Madīnati layukhrijanna al-aʿazzu min-ha al-adhalla, wa-li-Llāhi al-ʿizzatu wa-li-rasūlihi wa-li-l-muʾminīn*) — "might belongs to God, to His Messenger, and to the believers," the verse revealed about the very humiliation Ghāfir addresses. *Al-ʿAlīm* answers the opposite pressure: the suspicion that God is unaware. Ghāfir's sequel spells it out — *He knows the betrayal of the eyes and what the breasts conceal* (40:19), *wa-Allāhu lā yahdī al-qawma al-ẓālimīn* (40:33, with 2:258 and 3:86 for the same formula) — so that *al-ʿAlīm* here is not an abstract omniscience but a specific one: He knows the torturer's register and the martyr's intention, and the *ʿilm* that is 40:2's attribute is what 40:83 will indict in the disbelievers (*they exulted in what they had of knowledge*).

**The Book as a Claim, Not a Commodity**

*Al-kitāb* with the definite article: the Book, the one whose existence settles the argument. The sūrah's next move is to insist that this Book's function is not information but *guidance and light* (40:53, *wa-laqad ātaynā Mūsā al-hudā wa-awrathnā banī Isrāʾīla al-kitāb*), and the two verses stand together as a single theory of scripture: descent from the *ʿAzīz al-ʿAlīm* means that the Book is neither negotiable (its sender cannot be compelled) nor accidental (its sender knows the reader). Al-Ṭabarī's gloss on the verse is characteristically blunt: *this is the sending-down of the Book from God*, and he takes the construction of 40:1–2 as one sentence across the letters, i.e. *Ḥā Mīm — [this is] the revelation of the Book* — which is why the muṣḥaf's convention of pausing on the letters and beginning afresh is exegetically load-bearing, not merely liturgical.

**Reading the Two Letters and the Name Together**

The verse also quietly makes the strongest anti-skeptical claim in the sūrah. If revelation is *from the All-Knowing*, then the parts of it that a reader cannot decode (the letters) and the parts he thinks he has decoded (the law, the narrative, the promise) fall under the same attribution. That single inference is what 40:4 will then draw against the disputers, and what 40:81 will repeat to the reader: *He shows you His signs; which of God's signs do you deny?* A Book from the *ʿAlīm* does not require that its reader understand everything; it requires that he not deny anything he has been shown.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:3

> **3** *Forgiver of sin, Accepter of repentance, severe in retribution, Possessor of bounty. There is no god but He; and unto Him is the journey's end.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Four Names, and Why Their Order Is the Argument**

*Ghāfiru al-dhanbi, qābuli al-tawbi, shadīdu al-ʿiqābi, dhū al-ṭawli*. The source commentary makes the structural observation that governs everything else: three of the four descriptions are of mercy, and the one of severity is *placed between them*, so that severity is enclosed by mercy twice over — and this enclosure is itself the sūrah's claim about God's disposition, matching 7:156 (*wa-raḥmatī wasiʿat kulla shay'in*) and the Prophet's ﷺ saying that when God completed creation He wrote in His Book, placed over His throne, *"My mercy outstrips My wrath"* (al-Bukhārī 7404 and Muslim 2751, from Abū Hurayra). Al-Rāzī adds the more striking point, recorded by the source commentary: it is a *greater* praise of God to say that He *can and sometimes does* forgive grave sins than to say that He is bound to forgive minor ones for outweighing obedience — the first makes forgiveness an act of *faḍl*, the second a calculation of debt, and this sūrah, which returns again and again to *faḍl* and *ṭawl* (40:3's own *dhū al-ṭawl*, and the inventory of gifts at 40:61–64 and 40:79–80), takes the first option.

**"Forgiver of Sin" — Why *Dhanb* and Not *Khaṭīʾa***

*Al-ghafūr* is the intensive form of *ghāfir*, and the sūrah uses the participle here rather than the intensified name — a small point with a large consequence, since Ghāfir's *ism* is *ghāfiru al-dhanbi*, a single act described, while the Qur'an's most frequent name for the same attribute is *al-Ghaffār* (39:5, 40:42's *fa-ṣṭabir inna al-ʿāqibata li-l-muttaqīn*, 71:10). The difference is one of *register*: the sūrah is not intoning a litany but making a statement about what God does in response to a particular human condition. The object is *al-dhanb* — sin as *dhanb*, the root of *dhanūb* (the camel that has lost its calf and wanders bellowing) and of *adhnāb* (the sides of a river): sin in the Qur'an's most common word is a *deviation to one side*, a thing done by a creature who is, in its own loss, looking for what it has lost. Ghāfir's first attribute, applied to *that*, is a claim about direction restored, not about a ledger erased.

**"Accepter of Repentance" — and the Absent Conjunction**

*Qābulu al-tawb*, "accepting (Him who) repents." *Tawba* is, etymologically, *return* (cf. 2:222's *inna Llāha yuḥibbu al-tawwābīna wa-yuḥibbu lladhīna yataṭahharūn*, 66:8's *tūbū ilā Llāhi tawbatan naṣūḥā*, 9:112's *al-tāʾibūna al-ʿābidūna*)), and the sūrah's own most practical statement of it is the pair at 40:15 and 40:55 (*wa-istaghfir li-dhanbika*): turning is an act of the direction, not of the emotion. Al-Rāzī's observation, cited by the source commentary, is a matter of grammar: in the recitation's flow there is no *wāw* joining *qābuli al-tawbi* to *shadīdu al-ʿiqābi*, and the asyndeton means that the severity is not a separate, freestanding fact about God but is *described in the same breath as* the accepting — His punishment is not detached from His readiness to receive the returning servant. That single particle's absence is the sūrah's whole theodicy in miniature, and it explains why Ghāfir's punishment-scenes always allow room: 40:11's *rabbanā amtanā... aqrarnā bi-dhunūbinā fa-akhrijnā* — even the damned are given a line in which they address God as *Rabb*.

**"Severe in Retribution," "Possessor of Bounty": the Two That Balance**

*Shadīd al-ʿiqāb* and *dhū al-ṭawl* describe God's treatment of one and the same sin. *Shadīd al-ʿiqāb* is a phrase the Qur'an uses at 13:2 (*wa-yataraṣṣadūna lahu... fa-lā turāwāna... anna Allāha shadīd al-ʿiqāb*, in the sūrah of al-Raʿd's warning to the obstinate), and Ghāfir's use of it here is its second and most programmatic occurrence: the phrase there is *siʾā al-ʿiqāb*? Keep what is certain: Ghāfir pairs the phrase with *ʿiqāb* in its other two occurrences in the chapter, 40:5 (*kadhālika yaḥqu qawl rabbika ʿalā lladhīna kafarū li-yuriyyannahum... wa-shadīd mā yaʿūd*) and 40:22 (*fa-akhadhahum Allāh bi-khaṭāʾihim wa-Allāhu shadīd al-ʿiqāb* — the wording of 40:22 as transmitted: *fa-akhadhāhumu Llāhu bi-khaṭāʾihim wa-Allāhu shadīdu al-ʿiqābi*), which is the sūrah's own definition of what severity means: it is *taking hold* of a person by the consequences of his own acts, not an infusion of pain from outside. Against that stands *dhū al-ṭawl*, from *ṭ-w-l*, whose first sense is length and extension: *ṭawīl* is long, *mutaṭawwil* is one who confers a favor and then counts it, and the Qur'an's *minna* (grace) and *tafaddul* share the field. Ghāfir's own gloss on the word is 93:3–4, *your Lord has not forsaken you and He is not displeased, and the last is better for you than the first* — which is *ṭawl* stated as consolation to a man who thought he had been abandoned. The source commentary's fine reading is that *dhū al-ṭawl*, attached directly to *shadīd al-ʿiqāb*, means that God is magnanimous precisely in *relinquishing* what He is entitled to: bounty is the forbearance that does not collect.

**"There Is No God but He; and Unto Him Is the Journey's End" — the Whole Sūrah in Nine Words**

*Lā ilāha illā Huwa wa-ilayhi al-maṣīr*. Al-Rāzī, in the passage the source commentary summarizes, draws out the logic of the four attributes and this clause together: there is no other reality that holds mercy and severity in the balance described, so worship is owed to Him alone; and since *to Him is the return*, one flees to Him and flees *from* Him toward Him at the same time — the only coherent posture being hope joined with fear. Al-Qushayrī's *Risāla* formulates the discipline: *al-khawf wa-l-rajāʾ*, and the maxim that the believer's voyage runs on two wings, one of which must not be cut off or the bird falls. Ibn Taymiyyah's treatment of the two (*Majmūʿ al-Fatāwā*, and *al-ʿUbūdiyya*'s discussion of *khawf* and *rajāʾ* as acts of the heart that belong to God alone) is the classical hardening of the same verse into a rule: to hope in a being you cannot return to, or to fear a being from whom you can flee to no one, is the definition of *shirk* as Ghāfir uses the word — the misplacement of ultimate *expectation*.

The word chosen for the end, *al-maṣīr* — from *ṣ-y-r*, the noun of destination, set against 40:4's *taqallub*, the aimless turning to and fro the disbelievers are famous for — is not *al-mansar* or *al-malāʾ*: the sūrah does not say that the believers' cause will triumph on the battlefield but that everyone *arrives*. A chapter written to a persecuted minority uses the vocabulary of *arrival* rather than of *victory* — and that choice is itself the sūrah's pedagogy of patience, since arrival is certain while victory is timed. That is also why the hour of Ḥunayn — when the Muslims fled, the Prophet ﷺ stood firm calling them back, and the tide turned — belongs to the same lesson as this clause: arrival with God is not the same as momentum on the field. Keep the verse's own lesson: Ghāfir never promises the believer a *sight* of the outcome, and this clause is the reason the sūrah can afford not to promise it (40:77's *fa-immā nurīyannaka baʿda lladhī naʿiduhum aw nutawaffayyaka*).

---

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:4

> **4** *None but the disbelievers dispute concerning the signs of God. So let it not deceive you that they are free to come and go in the land.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Diagnosis Before a Refutation**

The verse does not answer the argument; it classifies the arguer. *Mā yujādilu fī āyāti Llāhi illā alladhīna kafarū* — "none dispute concerning God's signs except those who disbelieved." Grammatically it is an exclusive construction (*mā... illā*), and it functions as a piece of spiritual diagnostics that the believer is meant to apply honestly to himself before turning it on anyone else: sustained, bad-faith argument about the evidence of God is not an intellectual position that happens to end in unbelief, it is the behavioral signature of a will that has already decided. The Qur'an is candid about this and says the same thing from the other direction: *When they meet the believers they say "we believe," and when they are alone with their devils they say "we are with you"* (2:14), and *they say, "We only desire to be excellent and conciliatory," but God is exposing their plot* (8:30). Disputation of this species is not neutral inquiry. Genuine questioning — Abraham's *Lord, show me how You give life to the dead* (2:260), Maryam's *how can I have a son when no man has touched me?* (19:20), the disciples' *do we not all believe?* (5:113) — is welcomed in the Qur'an and answered. What is condemned here is the argument that keeps going after the evidence has arrived, which is why the sūrah will specify that such disputers argue *bilā sulṭānin ātāhum* — "without any authority having come to them" (40:35, 56).

**The Word Yujādil: Wrestling With Words**

*Jidāl* comes from *j-d-l*, to twist, to pull, to plait — a rope made of strands twisted against each other; the *judālatān* of a well are the two ropes wound on either side of the bucket. To *ujādil* is thus not "to discuss" but to wrestle, to pull the other party's rope until his bucket gives way. The Qur'an uses the word in two registers only, and the distinction is instructive. Once, positively: *And do not dispute with the People of the Book except in the manner that is best* (29:46), and *Invite to the way of your Lord with wisdom and good exhortation, and dispute with them in the manner that is best* (16:125). Everywhere else it belongs to those who reject revelation — 2:258 (Abraham's *disputing* with the king), 6:19, 11:21, 18:54, 22:3, 31:20, 42:16, 45:24, 50:27. And of this sūrah specifically: the disputers of God's signs are mentioned here, at 40:35, at 40:56, and at 40:69, and this phrase "dispute concerning the signs of God" occurs nowhere in the Qur'an outside these four places except once, in 42:35. It is, in other words, the *Ḥawāmīm*'s own diagnosis of the disease of the age, announced in the first four verses of the cycle's opening chapter as the theme of all seven.

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī both record the interpretive tradition that the verse functions as a definition as much as a description: whoever persists in disputing the signs after their clarity has been established thereby demonstrates that the underlying disposition is *kufr*, for a seeking heart does not keep denying what it has seen. The Muʿtazilī-leaning readers took it as evidence that such disputation is itself a major sin; the Ashʿarī majority took it as descriptive of the disbeliever's habit rather than a rule that every disputant is akāfir — a caution of enormous practical value, since it prevents the verse being used as a club against honest doubters.

**Which Signs Are in Question**

*Āyāt* here is deliberately broad. The source commentary notes that in 40:69 the same phrase follows immediately on a run of cosmological and biological signs (vv. 61–68), so the "signs" of the sūrah are at once the verses of the Qur'an, the evidences granted to the prophets (Moses' "clear proofs," 40:23, 34, 83), the structures of creation (night and day, the earth as dwelling, the sky as canopy, 40:61–64), and the signs in human life itself (the stages of gestation and aging, 40:67). *Āya* properly means a mark you can recognize from a distance — the *āya* of a tribe is its banner, and the same root gives *muta'āyyin*, self-evident. To dispute the *āyāt* is therefore to dispute things that are, by their own nature, recognizable — which is what makes such disputation a moral rather than an epistemic failure.

**Taqallub: The Deception of a Busy Success**

*Fa-lā yaghurra-ka taqallubuhum fī al-bilād* — "so let not their coming and go in the lands delude you." The verb *gharra* is the root of *ghurūr*, deception, and the Qur'an's psychology of it is remarkably precise: Satan's whole office is described with it — *let not Satan deceive you as he expelled your parents from the Garden* (7:27), *that is none but the Devil who decorates their deeds for them... and Shayṭān has no claim except that you would fear his allies* (14:22), and, most exactly, *what deceived you concerning this world — the deluder (al-gharūr) deluded you* (57:14, 3:186). *Taqallub*, from *q-l-b*, turning over, is their to-and-fro: the caravan routes of Quraysh north to Syria and south to Yemen (cf. 106:1–2), the changing of camp and season, the daily round of profit and shipment, the political turnover that made the Meccan oligarchy look permanent. The verb is used of God's turning of hearts (*nuqallibu af'idatahum*, 20:100) and of the moon's phases — always a state in motion, never a state at rest. What the Prophet ﷺ is warned against is the inference that motion implies blessing: these people travel freely, trade profitably, hold councils, kill whom they like, and mock the revelation nightly — therefore their position must be sound.

The Qur'an answers that inference four times over, in the same words: *Let not their coming and go in the lands delude you. A little enjoyment, then their refuge is Hell — and wretched the journeying* (3:196–197); *We let them enjoy a little, then We compel them to a punishment they had not reckoned on* (31:24); *Let them eat and enjoy themselves and let hope delude them* (15:3); *Do the denizens of Mecca not see that We have made a secure sanctuary, while men are snatched from around them?* (29:67). Al-Bukhārī (3328) preserves the saying that cuts the fallacy at its root:

> *"Whoever God wills good for, He grants him understanding of the religion and endows him with knowledge; I am only a dispenser, and God gives. And the people will continue to be as they are until the hour of Resurrection, except for two kinds of people: one who committed sins, he knows that God will punish him for them; and one who hastened to obedience and hopes for it."*

and, in the same chapter, the shorter and sharper: *God gives the world to those He loves and those He does not love, and He gives religion only to those He loves.*

**A Mercy Disguised as a Warning**

Note to whom the sentence is addressed. The Prophet ﷺ is not being told that his own faith is at risk of deception — the verb is a prohibition against *being deluded by the appearance of others*, and the same injunction is repeated to him in the very middle of this sūrah: *So let not their ability to come and go delude you* is matched at 3:176, 5:41, 10:65, and 36:76 — *so grieve not for them* — in order to relieve him of the burden of other people's prosperity. It is one of the Qur'an's most therapeutic repetitions: the man carrying a Revelation is repeatedly told that the success of his opponents is not data against his truth. Modern psychology has named what the verse names: our tendency to treat visibility and momentum as evidence of correctness, to read a louder market as a better argument. The believer's antidote is not contempt for the prosperous but a longer time-horizon — which is exactly what verse 5 (the destroyed nations) and verse 6 (the decree that came due) do immediately after.

**How to Dispute After This Verse**

The sūrah does not counsel retreat from argument. It sets the terms: dispute *in the manner that is best* (16:125), and when the argument becomes mere wrestling, hand the case over: *God knows best what you do; God will judge between you on the Day of Resurrection concerning what you were disputing about* (22:68–69). Ibn Kathīr, commenting on this verse, cites the same pair: the duty is to convey, not to win, and victory in debate is not a sign of God's pleasure — a lesson this sūrah will illustrate with the most powerful man alive, whose debate record was flawless and whose ending was drowning.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:5

> **5** *The people of Noah denied before them, and the parties after them. Every community plotted against its messenger to seize him. They disputed with falsehood to nullify the truth thereby, so I seized them. How terrible was My requital!*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah Turns from Argument to Archive**

Verse 4 said that only disbelievers dispute the signs; verse 5 opens the file. *Kadhdhabat qablahum qawmu Nūḥin wa-l-ʿahzāb min baʿdihim* — the people of Noah denied before them, and the allied groups after them. The consolation is engineered: the Prophet ﷺ is being told not "you are failing" but "you are in company." The Makkan community, boycotted, mocked, and tortured, is inserted into a list, and the list is the argument: every messenger in the list was denied by the strongest constituency available to him, and the list's survivors are not the mockers. This is the sūrah's repeated therapeutic move, and it reaches its formal statement at 40:51 — *We will surely aid Our messengers and those who believe in the life of this world and on the Day when the witnesses stand.*

**Rabw: Alliances Formed Against a Man**

*Al-aḥzāb* is the plural of *ḥizb*, a party, a faction, a band bound together — the same word gives the liturgical *ḥizb* (a portion of recitation, a "party" of verses) and the section of an army. In Qur'anic usage *aḥzāb* is almost always a coalition assembled to crush truth: the confederates at the Trench (*jh*, 33:9–20 — *the armies around you*), and here the successive blocs ranged behind every prophet. The most brutal echo is 38:13 — *each of them allied with the next against the one who is driven out, and the end of the alliance is ruin.* And the sūrah's own sequel: *the party (ḥizb) of Pharaoh — nothing but astray.* The word teaches a political lesson the Qur'an repeats: evil is effective only in coalition, and coalitions of that kind dissolve the moment the payoff stops (58:18–19).

**The Universal Custom of Tyranny**

The sentence shifts to a gnomic past, *wa-kullu ummatin makarat bi-rasūli-hā li-ya'khudūh* — and every community schemed against its messenger in order to seize him. *Makara*, from *m-k-r*, is planning in secret; *khadha*/*akhdha* is taking, apprehending, and in legal usage *ma'khadh* is exacting a penalty. So the plot is not merely rhetorical rejection: it is arrest, expulsion, or execution — and the Qur'an testifies that this is the pattern, not an accident of Muhammad's lifetime: *they plotted, and God plotted, and God is the best of plotters* (3:54); *thus We made for every prophet an enemy among the criminals* (25:31). The sūrah will show it again, in its own longest narrative, with Pharaoh announcing *Leave me, I will kill Moses* (40:26) and with the court proposing to burn the sorcerers who believed (20:71). Ibn Kathīr notes that the phrase *bi-rasūli-hā* — "its messenger," possessive — is the point: each nation had a messenger belonging to it, addressed in its own tongue (14:4), so no community can claim it was never warned.

**Batil: The Wind That Moves Dust**

*Yujādilūna bi-l-bāṭili li-yudḥidū bi-hi al-ḥaqq* — they dispute with the false in order to render the true futile. *Bāṭil* is from *b-ṭ-l*, of which the lexicographers give the picture of wind blowing across empty ground, moving nothing, leaving nothing; its opposite in the Qur'an is *ḥaqq*, that which is real, binding, and destined to remain. And *aḍāda* — to annihilate, to come to nothing — is a word for what happens to an entity, not an argument: they want to *liquidate* truth. The Qur'an grants that this sometimes appears to work in the short term and denies it absolutely in the long term: *Say, truth has come, and falsehood neither originates nor restores* (34:49), and *As for the foam, it goes away as refuse; and as for what benefits people, it remains in the earth* (13:17). This is also the logic of 47:17 — *God increases in guidance those who are guided* — and of the sūrah's own verdict on Pharaoh: *Pharaoh's scheme was nothing but in loss* (40:37).

**"So I Seized Them" — the First-Person Shift**

The verse's pivot is grammatical, and the classical rhetoricians (al-Zamakhsharī, and after him al-Alūsī) flag it as an *iltifāt*, a shift of person: from third-person narration about past nations to the first person of God's own voice — *fa-ʾakhadh-tuhum* — "so I took them." The effect is to make the ancient history a present address: it is God speaking now, in the first person, about a seizure already executed. Then comes the refrain whose terror is its brevity: *fa-kayfa kāna ʿiqāb* — "how was My requital!" It is an exclamation, not a question: the Arabic *kayfa* with a past-tense copula means "you cannot fathom what it was." The same formula recurs across the Qur'an as a kind of punctuation on divine speech — 13:25, 14:17, 30:9, 35:44, 38:15, 39:71, 40:15 (of the disbelievers' end), 50:14, 54:16–18, 65–66, 69:10, 89:13 — and Ghāfir will use it twice more, at 40:22 and again in the chapter's final movement. Al-Rāzī's note is worth preserving: the refrain does not describe the punishment's duration or method, only its *quality as experienced*, which is more eloquent than catalogue; the Qur'an never invites the reader to enjoy the pain, only to conclude something about the One who said *I seized them*.

**The Historical Realism of the Verse**

The source commentary notes that "the parties after them" is specified elsewhere: *And there came no messenger to any town but its people of luxury said, "We disbelieve in what you were sent with"* (34:34; cf. 51:52 — *a sorcerer or one possessed*; 43:23 — *we found our fathers upon a religion*). The list of the destroyed is a running dossier: Noah's flood, ʿĀd's wind (69:6–7), Thamūd's shriek (41:17), the cities of Lot, overturned with their names written (11:82), Madyan's earthquake (7:91), the men of the Sabbath transformed (2:65), Pharaoh drowned with his army (2:50), the Companions of the Ravine (85:4). The verse's claim is not that Makkah will be destroyed like these; it is that its opposition belongs to the same genus, and the genus has a known outcome. That is the reason a *believing man* of 40:28 will invoke precisely "the day of the allies" (40:30) in front of Pharaoh — the same argument, in the mouth of a courtier rather than a prophet.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:6

> **6** *Thus did the word of your Lord come due against those who disbelieved, that they are the companions of the Fire.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**What "the Word" Means When It Becomes an Event**

*Kadhālika waqaʿa qawl-u rabbika ʿalā alladhīna kafarū* — "thus did the word of your Lord come due upon those who disbelieved." *Waqaʿa*, from *w-q-ʿ*, is the verb of falling upon, of a thing landing and being settled: the same root gives *waqiʿa*, an actual event, and *wuqūʿ* of the sun at its decline. A *qawl* of God in the Qur'an is not only speech; it is creative address — *His command, when He intends a thing, is only that He says to it, "Be," and it is* (36:82; 40:68) — so a divine word "falling due" is not a threat that God may yet decide to execute, but a statement that has already entered the register of accomplished fact. The same idiom occurs for the blessed as for the damned, and that symmetry is the sūrah's whole theology of history: *Already has the word of your Lord come due from Us for My servant Job* — the words *laqad waqaʿa qawl-u rabbika* appear at 37:31 for the saved, and at 10:33, 36:7, 41:26, and 43:79 for the lost. Ghāfir will state the promise-side just six verses from here in the same register: *We will surely aid Our messengers* (40:51).

**The Grammar of Certainty**

Two features of the sentence deserve notice. First, the preposition is *ʿalā* — the word comes *upon* them, as a burden, an encompassing roof; the same *ʿalā* that occurs in *their Lord is angry with them and has cast upon them a curse* (47:23) and *a cloud of punishment encompassing them* (29:54), from *ʿ-l-w*/*ʿ-ṭ-t*, encircling. Second, the verse's own Arabic shows its second half as explanatory of the decree: it is decreed *that they are* companions of the Fire — not that God will arbitrarily make them so. The decree is a reading of them, not a substitution for their choice. This is why the sūrah can say at 40:17, in the same passage, *that Day no wrong is done*, and at 10:44, *God does not wrong people in the least, but people wrong themselves*.

**ʿAqīdah: Decree, Justice, and the Question of "Would They Have Believed Otherwise?"**

Ibn Kathīr, al-Ṭabarī, and al-Qurṭubī all record the same practical gloss: *thus* — as punishment fell on the nations of verse 5, so the decree stands over this people too, and the Prophet must not imagine that their survival indicates God's approval. The verse has also been read within the larger doctrinal debate about the divine decree. The Muʿtazilah stressed that *wuqūʿ al-qawl* is God's informed and deserved sentence upon a people who chose disbelief, precisely so that God not be made the author of their unbelief. The Jabriyyah, whom the tradition overwhelmingly rejected, read such verses as proof that disbelief is forced. The Sunni synthesis, formulated with particular clarity by Ibn Taymiyyah and by the Shāfiʿī theologians before him, holds both statements at once because the Qur'an does: *whomever God guides is the rightly guided, and whomever He leaves astray — for them you will find no allies* (18:17), *and* *whoever does evil is requited for it* (4:123), *and* *God does not wrong people in the least.* The verse at hand is one of the "decree" texts and must be read with its own sūrah's "justice" texts (40:17, 40:31 — *God does not wish wrong for His servants*, and 40:20 — *God judges with truth*), which is why the commentary below will keep treating 40:31 and 40:17 as the interpretive frame for verses like this one.

**Why This Verse Is Consolation, Not Threat**

Its first audience is a persecuted minority, and the verse's function is to tell them the case is closed: their tormentors' immunity is appearance, not verdict. Al-Rāzī observes that a *qawl* that has "come due" removes the anxiety of waiting — the reason the sūrah immediately pivots at verse 7 from the fate of the damned to the angels' prayer for the believers. It is a pastoral sequence: first the sentence on the persecutors, then the intercession for the faithful, so that the frightened hear the bad news about their enemies before the good news about themselves.

**The Companions of the Fire**

*Aṣḥāb al-nār*, "companions of the Fire," is the Qur'an's recurring designation, and the word *ṣāḥib* is not honorific here but relational: a companion is one bound to another by long association. Applied to the Fire, it says that for such a person the Fire is not a visit but a partnership — the environment, the colleague, the constant. The Qur'an speaks of *aṣḥāb al-nār*, *aṣḥāb al-jahannam*, *aṣḥāb al-saʿīr* (cf. 2:39, 113; 5:10; 7:18; 22:19; 32:20; 40:8, 47; 67:10) and correspondingly *aṣḥāb al-jannah* (5:72; 7:44, 48-50; 13:29) — a literary structure in which the two groups are not merely punished and rewarded but are each joined to what they chose repeatedly. Ghāfir will make that choice concrete in the fate of Pharaoh's household, exposed to the Fire morning and evening (40:46): companionship begins before the end.

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:7

> **7** *Those who bear the Throne and those around it glorify their Lord with His praise, believe in Him, and seek forgiveness for those who believe: "Our Lord, You encompass everything with mercy and knowledge. Forgive those who repent and follow Your way, and shield them from the Fire.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Court Above the Court**

The sūrah has just pronounced the sentence of the highest court in Egypt — *I seized them; how terrible was My requital* — and now it lifts the listener's head to a court that outranks every tribunal: *wa-lladhīna ḥamla al-ʿarsha wa-man ḥawlahu* — those who carry the Throne and those around it. The juxtaposition is deliberate and therapeutic. Pharaoh's council sat in a hall with pillars (79:36 — *al-ʿamūd*, the colonnaded palace); the divine council is pictured in the open circumambulation of the Throne. Whatever the powerful decide about the believers here, another assembly is already speaking about them there, and it is speaking well of them.

**Who Bears the Throne**

*Ḥamalat al-ʿarsh* — the bearers of the Throne — are identified by the Qur'an itself in a verse from this sūrah's own neighborhood of themes: *And the angels will be at its edges, and eight above them will bear the Throne of your Lord that Day* (69:17). At the present time, the number is not stated in revelation, and the reports specifying eight, or the dimensions and substance of the bearers, belong to transmitted traditions whose chains are largely debated; Ibn Kathīr cites them but al-Dhahabī and later muḥaddithūn graded most of them weak, and the salaf's practice, as Ibn Taymiyyah records in *al-ʿAqīdah al-Wāsiṭiyyah*, was to affirm the Throne (*al-ʿarsh*) and its bearers as God described them without *takayyuf* — demanding a how. Al-Ṭabarī explains *al-ʿarsh* as the royal seat of dominion, from which God's command descends, and notes that in the language of the Arabs *ʿarsh* means the roof of a house as well as the throne of a king: both senses fit — it is the ceiling of creation and the seat of sovereignty. Ibn Kathīr transmits from Ayyūb al-Sakhtiyānī a saying attributed to Abū Jaʿfar al-Bāqir about the magnitudes of the heavens and the Chair (*kursiyy*) relative to the Throne, which is the basis of the much-quoted phrase *"the heavens and the earth, in the Chair, are like a ring thrown in a desert"*; the wording is disputed, and its purpose is doxological, not cosmographical. Al-Qurṭubī reports the exchange with al-Manṣūr over *al-Raḥmān ʿalā al-ʿarsh istawā* (20:5), in which Mālik ibn Anas said: *the istiwāʾ is known, the how is unknowable, believing in it is obligatory, and asking about it is an innovation* — the position that became the standard Sunni discipline in reading verses like this one.

*Wa-man ḥawlahu*, "those around it," are taken by al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr as the angels encircling the Throne in the ranks of *ḥamālat al-kursiyy* and *al-muqarrabūn*, the ones brought near (4:172), and some read them as the same group described in 39:75: *and you will see the angels surrounding the Throne on every side, glorifying their Lord with praise.* The distinction between bearers and surrounders is preserved in either reading: there are those who carry, and those who circle, and both are doing the same thing at 40:7 — *yusabbiḥūna bi-ḥamdi rabbihim*.

**Four Verbs: the Perfect Worshipper's Profile**

Notice the order of what the angels do, because the sūrah is quietly teaching a definition of worship. *Yusabbiḥūna bi-ḥamdi rabbihim* — they glorify with praise: *subbūḥ*/*tasbīḥ* is declaring God remote from every defect, and it is bound here with *ḥamd*, affirmation of every perfection; purity first, then praise, because gratitude offered toward an impure conception of God is worthless. *Wa-yu'minūna bi-hi* — and they believe in Him: even the sinless need faith as a continuing act; belief is not a door one passes through once. *Wa-yastaghfirūna li-lladhīna āmanū* — and they ask forgiveness for the believers: the highest creatures in the cosmos have as their office the advocacy of the weakest. Al-Rāzī finds here the strongest single argument in the Qur'an for the reality of *shafāʿa* (intercession) for sinners, since the angels do not ask for the perfection of the believers but for the pardon of their faults. And note the fourth element: they *believe in Him* before they *ask for others*, which is why the Shāfiʿī and Ashʿarī manuals insist intercession is by God's permission and not a right extracted from Him — *who is it that can intercede with Him except by His leave?* (2:255).

The classical discussion of this verse turned, therefore, on a practical question: if the angels pray for the believers, should a believer rely on that and slacken? Al-Qurṭubī answers from the verse's own condition — they ask forgiveness for *those who believe*, and the sūrah will define belief by its actions in the story of the man of Pharaoh's house. Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī's remark on this verse is preserved by Ibn Abī Ḥātim: *God honored the servant with something he had not asked for: He appointed angels to seek forgiveness for him.*

**"You Encompass Everything with Mercy and Knowledge"**

*Raḥmatī wasiʿat kulla shay'in* (7:156) and *rabbanā wasiʿta kulla shay'in raḥmatan wa-ʿilman* — the two verses use the accusative of specification (*tamyīz*), so that "encompassing" is explained *in terms of* mercy and knowledge. Al-Zamakhsharī's celebrated rhetorical observation — that the more weighty matter is given in the accusative rather than the nominative precisely because it is weightier — is developed by al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarsī into a substantive point: the angels open their petition not with a claim about the petitioners but with a claim about God, and the two attributes they name are the two the sinner needs: mercy sufficient to cover, and knowledge sufficient not to be fooled. Mercy without knowledge would be indulgence; knowledge without mercy would be diagnosis without cure. Their prayer thus assumes, and asks God to act upon, the very pairing asserted two verses earlier in the sūrah: *al-ʿAzīz al-ʿAlīm* (40:2), *Ghāfir al-dhanb* (40:3).

Some, as the source notes, inferred from the order of the words that mercy takes precedence over knowledge in the divine ordering of the world — that is, that God's information about a servant is deployed within a field of mercy, not the reverse; al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarsī both record the inference, and the readers of *al-Futūḥāt al-Makkiyyah* will recognize Ibn ʿArabī's famous treatment of this verse as the scriptural basis for mercy as the all-enveloping divine self-disclosure. The conservative reading, which is al-Ṭabarī's, takes no precedence at all: the verse simply affirms both without limit.

**Three Petitions, in the Right Order**

The angels' actual request is a curriculum in how to ask: *ighfir li-tāba wa-t-tabaʿa sabīlak* — forgive the one who repents and follows Your path; *wa-qihim ʿadhāba al-ḥamīr* — and guard them from the crushing Fire. Repentance (*tawba*, turning) is paired with following (*ittibāʿ*, walking in a direction), and the pairing answers every hollow profession of remorse: remorse that does not set out along the path has not yet repented. The path is *sabīlak*, Your path, definite by pronoun, not a path of the worshipper's own designing — the same idiom the sūrah will put on the tongue of the man of Pharaoh's house: *I will guide you to the path of right conduct* (40:38). And the third petition asks protection not only from punishment but, as the next verse will show, from the deeds themselves. Ibn ʿAṭiyyah notes that the sūrah placed the angels' intercession between the doom of the deniers (v. 6) and the interrogation of the damned (v. 10) so that the middle of the passage is occupied by mercy, and so that a believer who cannot defend himself on earth is already being defended in the heavenly assembly.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:8

> **8** *And admit them to the Gardens of perpetual residence that You promised them, and whoever among their fathers, spouses, and descendants was righteous. You are the Mighty, the Wise.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Half of the Angels' Petition**

Verse 7 asked for the removal of harm — forgiveness and protection from the Fire; verse 8 asks for the arrival of benefit — admission to Paradise. The order is not stylistic. In the prophetic etiquette of supplication the believer asks for safety first, because the relief of harm takes precedence over the gain of reward, and because a soul in dread cannot yet imagine luxury. The Qur'an preserves that order even for angels. The verb is *wa-udkhilhum* — "and cause them to enter," with God as the one who admits: no one walks into the Garden by right of entry; the gates are opened by Him who said *Enter them in peace, secure* (15:46), and *their Lord will guide them because of their faith; He will admit them to the Gardens of perpetual residence beneath which rivers flow, forever* (47:5–6).

**ʿAdn: The Place Where Nothing Fades**

*Jannāt ʿadn* is best translated "gardens of perennial planting," from *ʿa-d-n*, used of pasturage and dwellings that endure — *adnān*, the evergreen groves, in the old Arabic of Imruʾ al-Qays' Muʿallaqa, "the day of departure, and the abodes of *adn*." It is the reason "the Gardens of Eden" in this sūrah are not a garden with a fence but permanence itself as an environment: *khālidīna fīhā*, abiding, and *lā yadhūqu fīhā mawtan illā al-mawtata al-ūlā* — they will not taste death in them except the first death (44:56). Ghāfir mentions *jannāt ʿadn* here and at 40:8 only; the phrase recurs across the Qur'an at 9:72, 13:23, 16:31, 18:31, 19:61, 20:76, 25:75, 35:33, 38:50, 61:11, and 66:8 — in almost every case attached to a promise, which is the point of *allatī waʿadtahum*, "which You promised them."

**"Which You Promised Them": the Petition Grounded on God's Own Word**

This phrase is the most instructive piece of intercessory logic in the sūrah. The angels do not argue from the merits of the repentant; they place before God a promise God made. The Qur'an quotes exactly such reasoning on human lips: *Our Lord, and admit them to the Gardens of Eden which You promised, and whoever was righteous among their fathers and spouses and descendants* (13:23) — the near-identical verse in al-Raʿd — and *And whoever obeys God and His Messenger, He will admit him to Gardens of Eden beneath which rivers flow, forever; that is the great triumph* (4:13); *He promised them forgiveness and a great reward* (48:11); *It is a promise of truth which Our servants inherit* (19:60–61); *a promise upon Us, We are to fulfill it* (21:104). The principle the commentators draw out is both theological and pastoral: because God is *al-Ṣādiq al-waʿd*, the faithful in promise, the believer's request has a claim in it — not a claim of desert, but of reliance on a Speaker whose speech does not fail. Al-Qurṭubī's *al-Jāmiʿ* notes on this verse that it is permitted, indeed praiseworthy, for a supplicant to mention God's own names and promises within his request, since that is what the angels do here and the Prophet ﷺ did in the master invocation of pardon, *allāhumma anta rabbī lā ilāha illā anta, khalaqtanī wa-ʿabduka...* (al-Bukhārī 6306), which likewise grounds its plea in who God said He is.

**The Family Petition: a Righteousness That Radiates**

*Wa-man ṣalaḥa min ābāʾihim wa-azwājihim wa-dhurriyyātihim* — and whoever among their fathers, spouses, and descendants was righteous. The phrase is the hinge of the well-known question of whether a believer's family is raised into his station. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr report from Ibn ʿAbbās and others that God will raise the righteous degrees of a believer so that his believing relatives may be united with him, so that the tie of kinship, which is severed by ranks in Paradise, is not a source of grief there; the proof-text paired with this verse is:

> *Those who believed and whose offspring followed them in faith — We will join their offspring to them, and We will not diminish anything of their deeds; every man is pledged to what he earned.* (52:21)

Al-Ṭabarī's preferred reading of 52:21, which Ibn Kathīr endorses, is that the joining applies to children who followed in faith, whether or not they attained their parents' level of works, and that no one's reward is reduced to accomplish it: *wa-lā naqtaʿuhum min aʿmālihim shay'an* — God does not cut the parents off from anything of their deeds. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī add the necessary limit drawn by the sūrah itself and by 9:114: intercession and family ties do not cross the line of shirk — *Abraham's prayer for his father was only because of a promise he had made him; but when it became clear to him that he was an enemy of God, he disavowed him.* The tradition states the same boundary in the Prophet's own case regarding his parents, and Muslim (203) transmits that the Prophet ﷺ said his mother would be resurrected and then either admitted to Paradise or consigned to the Fire. Righteousness (*ṣalāḥ*) — the very root the verse uses — is the condition, and it is personal: the radii of a believer's goodness reach the living and the hereafter, but they do not convert anyone.

Two practical consequences were drawn by the jurists and pastoral scholars: first, the deceased are addressed in the angels' manner — a believer is encouraged to pray for his dead parents and children by name, since the sūrah has the Throne-bearers doing it; second, the living are warned that one's *ṣalāḥ* is one's family's asset, and so al-Zamakhsharī's gloss on *min ābāʾihim* is not merely grammatical but reproachful: those whom you neglected in your lifetime you will want to meet at the top of the Garden — so raise them there now, by teaching.

**"You Are the Mighty, the Wise" — the Seal of the Petition**

The double name that closes the verse is not a filler formula. *Al-ʿAzīz* is the one who cannot be resisted and whose purpose nothing frustrates: the promise will be executed because the Promiser is invincible. *Al-Ḥakīm* is the one who puts things in their right places, and al-Rāzī draws the balance: God is Mighty enough to admit whomever the angels ask for, Wise enough not to admit anyone unprepared — hence the condition *man ṣalaḥa* stands between the two names in the very structure of the sentence. This same pairing occurs in the sūrah again at 40:84, in the last verse, and its symmetry there is devastating: here it crowns a petition for families in Eden; there, *inna-ka ʿalā kulli shay'in qadīr* ends a prayer that comes too late — the same power, now bearing only justice. The wise allocation of the two names across the chapter is one more sign that the sūrah is a composed whole and not a string of unrelated fragments.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:9

> **9** *And guard them from evil. Whoever You guard from evil that Day, You have mercy on him; and that is the great triumph.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Petition the Angels Are Afraid Of**

*Wa-qihim al-sayyiʾāt* — and shield them from the evils. The phrase appears immediately after they asked for Paradise, and the effect is startling: the celestial intercessors of the believers do not end their prayer on the threshold of the Garden. They add a request for protection from *al-sayyiʾāt*. The commentators diverge on what *al-sayyiʾāt* means here, and the divergence is itself the commentary. Al-Zamakhsharī and al-Bayḍāwī take it as "the punishment of the evil deeds," i.e., their consequences; al-Ṭabarī reports both that they are the evil deeds themselves, so that the angels ask God to keep the servants from committing them, and that they are the misfortunes that sin attracts; al-Qurṭubī notes that if the reading is "evil deeds," the petition is the most profound in the chapter, because it says the real danger to a believer is not the Fire but relapse. This last is the reading the sūrah's own ethics favor: in this chapter God's gift to the repentant is repeatedly framed as turning the heart away — *God guides whom He will in his decision* — and the doom of the Pharaohites is that they saw no reason to stop.

The theological weight is heavy, and the classical discussions knew it: if the angels teach believers to ask for protection from *committing* evil, then human effort is not the sufficient cause of uprightness, and the Muʿtazilī insistence that the servant creates his own act sits awkwardly beside a prayer like this one. Ashʿarī and Māturīdī readers, from al-Juwaynī to al-Taftāzānī, took such verses as evidence that *ʿiṣma* — being held back from evil — is a gift; Ibn Taymiyyah's formulation is the most cited: *the heart is between two fingers of the Merciful, turning it however He will* — a wording derived from a report in Aḥmad and al-Ḥākim from Anas with a chain later muḥaddithūn judged weak or interrupted, and which the scholars of hadith advise citing as a meaning the Qur'an supports (*lā yakādirnā qulūbanā baʿda idh hadaytanā*, 3:8) rather than as a transmitted text.

**"Whoever You Guard That Day" — the Mercy Beneath the Mercy**

*Wa-man taqi al-sayyiʾāta yawmaʾidhin fa-qad raḥimtahu* — whoever You shield from evil on that Day, upon him You have had mercy. The syntax compresses the whole of soteriology: the shielding *is* the mercy, not its precondition, and the mercy is not a reward handed out after the shielding. The same logic runs through 76:11 — *So God guarded them from the evil of that Day, and lit for them a radiance and a joy*; the guarding precedes and produces the glow. It is worth noticing that the verse says nothing about a scale. There is no mention of weights, of balance of deeds, of anxiety about which side will tip — because the angels have already placed the case in the only venue the sūrah finally trusts: mercy.

That is not a denial of accountability; verses 10–17, in the very next paragraph, will be one of the most rigorous accounts of reckoning in the Qur'an. The order is the point: reckoning for the unrepentant, mercy-requested-by-angels for the repentant, and no contradiction between them because the second category is precisely the one that asked to be shielded.

**Yawmaʾidhin: "On That Day" as the Whole of the Matter**

The adverb *yawmaʾidhin* (literally "that Day then") is what the reciter must not slide past: it means the Day of Resurrection, when all concealment is exposed. Yet Ibn ʿAbbās and al-Ḥasan are reported by al-Ṭabarī to have taken "that Day" expansively, as covering every day of trial up to and including the last — for the danger of evil is not postponed to the end; it is what the servant meets daily. Al-Rāzī's homiletical extension is that the believer should say this Duʿā at every opening of a task, since the "Day" on which one needs shielding from evil may as well be today. This is why the sūrah's very next injunction is *So call upon God, devoting religion entirely to Him* (40:14): if the angels must ask for your shielding, you must ask too, and asking with total sincerity is the practical form of admitting that.

**The Great Triumph and What It Costs**

*Wa-mā dhālika illā al-fawz al-ʿaẓīm* — and that is nothing but the great triumph. *Fawz* is the word for success in a contest: winning the prize, escaping with the goods — and the Qur'an uses it almost exclusively of the next life, always to relativize the vocabulary of worldly success: *those are the losing ones* (*khāsirūn*) is used of the prosperous sceptics at 2:164 and 8:8, while *fawz* is reserved for being admitted to the Garden (3:185, 9:72, 13:23, 29:23, 48:5, 58:11, 61:12, 64:9). Al-Bukhārī (6578) and Muslim (186) transmit the portrait of the last man out of the Fire:

> *"A man will come out of the Fire crawling, and God will say to him: go and enter Paradise. When he goes toward it and imagines that it is a mirage, he will stop; and God will say to him: Do you not desire so much and so much? And he will say: No, my Lord... and God will say: it is for you, and ten times with it."*

The tradition ends with the man laughing and turning his face to God — and it is the perfect illustration of "that is the great triumph": the triumph is not proportionate to the man's works; it is proportionate to mercy, and yet he got there by asking. In the same volume al-Bukhārī (6580) transmits from Abū Hurayra that the Prophet ﷺ said of the last to enter: *I know the last of the people of Paradise to enter: a man who comes dragging himself, and God says to him: enter Paradise; he will say: my God, the people have taken the gardens?* — showing that entry itself is granted by a favor the entrant cannot compute, exactly what the angels' petition here anticipates.

Practically, the verse reorders the believer's list of fears. To be shielded from evil is worse to lose than to be shielded from calamity; and the tradition warns about the reversal of that ranking. Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī said of this verse what al-Ṭabarī records: *God gave them no greater gift than shielding them from evil — for the people of the Fire were not shielded.* The supplication the sūrah leaves its readers with is therefore not "give me more" but "keep me from the thing I will not see coming," which is the most realistic prayer a human being can make — the prayer of a person who knows that the deepest danger to a soul is itself.

---
## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:10

> **10** *Those who disbelieved will be addressed: "God's hatred is greater than your hatred of yourselves, when you were invited to faith but you disbelieved.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scene: an Address No One Can Answer**

The sūrah moves from the angels' prayer to the court's pronouncement. *Qāla* here is in the passive sense of *layuqālu* in the recension most read as *qīla* — "it will be said" — and the source notes the same construction as 41:47, 45:34, and 50:26, where the speech of God to the damned is reported without naming a human listener. The verse's first word after the address is *la-maqtu Llāhi*, "God's hatred," with the *lām* asseverative. In an hour when the audience will be hearing nothing but verdicts, the Qur'an pauses to state one emotion of God — hatred (*maqt*) — and to measure it. The measurement is what makes the verse remarkable: it does not threaten; it compares two feelings and declares the divine one greater.

**Maqt, Sakhaṭ, Ghaḍab: the Vocabulary of the Turning-Away**

*Maqt*, from *m-q-ṭ*, is the opposite of *ḥubb*: aversion that wants the object gone; in Arabic usage it is stronger than *karāhiya* (dislike) because it seeks distance, not mere displeasure. The Qur'an uses it at its most severe in one place: *that is because they hated what God sent down, so He made their deeds futile* (47:9) — where the *maqt* is the servants' toward revelation, and the divine response is not rage first but nullification. Related words build the field: *sakhaṭ* (wrathful displeasure, 9:96), *ghaḍab* (anger, 1:7 and 8:14), *laʿna* (distancing, 33:64), *ʿadhab* (painful treatment). Doctrinally, the verse became a locus of debate: the Muʿtazilah read divine *maqt* as the irrevocable infliction of punishment and denied that God has "hatred" as a quality; the traditionalist and Ashʿarī camps affirmed that God truly loves (*yuḥibb*) and truly hates (*yaqqaṭu / yasakhuṭu*), as attributes of His will and not of agitation, citing *God is pleased with them and they are pleased with Him* (5:54, 58:22, 98:8) as its proof that the affection is real. Ibn Taymiyyah insisted that love and hate are among the *afʿāl al-ikhtiyāriyya* — acts of God's choice, occurring in time in relation to whom He wills — which explains why the verse can compare a *past-tense* divine hatred with a *present-tense* human one. The practical upshot of all this argument, and the reason the sūrah needs it, is that God's relation to a servant is personal, not mechanical: the damned will be told they were hated, which means they were persons to a Person, and that this is not a cosmic process in which they were merely consumed.

**Two Readings of the Comparison**

The sentence has been taken two ways, and the commentators weigh both. (1) The reading the source adopts: God's hatred of you, when you were summoned to faith and refused, is greater than your hatred of yourselves — and the self-hatred being compared is the extremity in which the damned will despise themselves, of the kind described in the verse the sūrah cites: *When those who were followed disavow those who followed them, and they see the punishment and their ties are cut from them, the followers will say: would that we had a return, so we could disavow them as they disavow us* (2:166–167). Their self-disgust, on this reading, will be the maximum a creature can feel toward itself; God's aversion to what they did to their own souls exceeds it. (2) The reading attributed by al-Ṭabarī to Ibn ʿAbbās and favored by Ibn Kathīr on the Arabic: the *ḍamīr* in *li-akbaru min maqtikum li-anfusikum* refers back to the same scene — you hated yourselves in that hour more than anything — so that God says: your loathing of yourselves then is surpassed by Our hatred of you now. Both readings converge: a self-condemnation that arrives too late to do anything.

The pastoral force is in the last clause, which is not about feeling but about opportunity: *idh tudaʿuna ilā al-īmāni fa-takfurūn* — "when you were being invited to faith and you disbelieved." *Tudaʿūna* is an imperfect passive, an ongoing repeated calling, not a single summons. The verdict is thus about a *pattern of refusal in the presence of an invitation*. A person may ask, honestly, whether this verse condemns him; the answer the sūrah itself provides is that it condemns those who heard an invitation and answered with disbelief, and Ghāfir's immediately following verses will be devoted to the door of the *Tawwāb* (40:3, 7, 13).

**The Verse Read Backward: How to Love for God**

The sūrah's audience in Makkah knew what hatred felt like from both directions — they were hated for their faith, and they were told to hate the idolatry that hated them. The hadith sets the limit on that feeling so it does not become the disease of 40:10:

> *"Whoever loves for God and hates for God, and gives for God and withholds for God, has completed his faith."* (Abū Dāwūd 4681, al-Tirmidhī 2521 from Abū Umāmah, with other wordings in Aḥmad)

That is *maqt* directed at deeds under God's instruction; the verse's damned are those whose *maqt* was directed at the call itself. The distinction between hating what God hates and hating the people who invite you to God is precisely the difference between the believer of 40:28, who hated the murder of an innocent man, and the court of Pharaoh, which hated the truth-teller. Ghāfir will spend twenty verses making the same contrast live, and this verse is its verdict, delivered first so the listeners know what the story is measuring.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:11

> **11** *They will say: "Our Lord, You caused us to die twice and You gave us life twice. We confess our sins — is there any way out?*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Confession with the Wrong Tense**

The damned speak for the first time in this sūrah, and their speech is not a defense but a *confession*: *ʿtarfnā bidhunūbinā*. Everything the previous verses required of them in Makkah — repentance, admission, turning — they now perform in full. What has changed is not their honesty but their calendar. The sūrah is teaching, through the sheer politeness of their address ("Our Lord"), that the words of the repentant are not magic formulas; they are the vocabulary of a window, and the window is a created thing with a closing time.

**Two Deaths and Two Lives: the Readings**

*Baʿadtanā marratayni wa-ayaḥtaynā marratayni* — You caused us to die twice and gave us life twice. Al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Qurṭubī transmit two principal explanations, both grammatically sound. (a) The first — the one al-Ṭabarī favors from Ibn ʿAbbās and others: they were dead as non-existent things in the loins of their forefathers, then brought to life in the world, then died the natural death, then were given life again at the resurrection. The verse's own language supports this, since the verb of *baʿatha* (to cause death, from *baʿatha/baʾasa* in this use, or from *amāta* as many read the meaning) precedes *aḥyā* in each pair: death first in each round. (b) The second: the first life is in this world, the first death is at the end of it, the second life is for the questioning and the trial of the grave, the second death is before the Resurrection — a reading that rests on verses like *How can you disbelieve in God, when you were dead and He gave you life; then He will cause you to die and give you life; then to Him you will be returned* (2:28), and *our Lord You caused us to die twice and You gave us life twice* in Qāf (50:15 in some readings) and the pattern of 2:258 and 36:78. Some later interpreters, including al-Shawkānī in *Fatḥ al-Qadīr* here, allow both senses simultaneously, because the verse is not giving a schedule of eschatology but the confession of a people who have been turned through the whole cycle and are now on the far side of the last turn.

The important thing to preserve is the *theological* payload of "twice": resurrection is not an addition to life but a second, deliberate act of the same God who gave the first — and the sūrah uses this to answer 40:57's skepticism about re-creation. Whoever has done it once, and has done it to every human being, has not begun to argue about whether He can do it again.

**Hal ilā Makhraj: the Question That Has No Answer**

*Hal ilā makhrajin min sabīl* — is there any way out, to any road? The prepositions are stacked (*ilā...* with the elided "is there"), and the effect is of a man groping for a door in a wall he has built himself, asking whether there is a "road out," and then a "toward it," as if the two words were the two halves of a hope he does not allow himself to finish. *Makhraj*, from *kh-r-j*, appears in the Qur'an in the most heartbreaking of divine mercies: *whoever fears God, He will make a way out for him* (65:2); *and whoever does evil or wrongs his soul then seeks God's forgiveness will find God Forgiving, Merciful* (4:110); and the verse of Jonah's deliverance, *so he called out in the darknesses: there is no god but You, glory be to You, I was among the wrongdoers; so We answered him and saved him from the grief; and thus We save the believers* (21:100–101). The Qur'an uses the same vocabulary — *makhraj*, *sabīl*, *najja* — for the exit it is now saying there is none of, and the intertextual cruelty of that is intentional: it teaches the reader which verse belongs to which world.

Al-Ghazālī, in the section on repentance in *Iḥyāʾ ʿUlūm al-Dīn* and in *Kīmiyā-yi Saʿādat*, arranges these verses — 40:11–12, 32:12, 35:37, 23:106–108, 14:44 — as a single extended dialogue of petition and refusal between the inhabitants of the Fire and their Lord, and his point is precisely rhetorical: every petition they make is reasonable, every answer is just, and the exchange exists to instruct the living, not the dead. Repentance (*tawba*), he argues, is the only act whose condition is that one be still capable of sin; it dies with capacity, which is why the tradition ties it to the horizon:

> *"Whoever repents before the sun rises from the west, God will accept his repentance."* (Muslim 2703, from Abū Hurayra; also at Muslim 157 on the Dabbat al-Arḍ)

and why the Prophet ﷺ said, *God stretches out His hand at night to accept the repentance of the day, and stretches out His hand by day to accept the repentance of the night, until the sun rises from the west* (Muslim 2759). Verse 11 is the mirror image of those hadiths: the same stretching of the hand, on the other side of sunrise.

**Confession without *Ināba*: What the Tradition Adds**

Their confession is genuine and worthless, and that combination is the sūrah's final warning to the self-deceived penitent who says "I confess" and changes nothing. The classical definition of valid repentance gathers the conditions from the Qur'an and the Sunna in one line, reported from Mālik and transmitted by Aḥmad and others with a wording the scholars regard as sound in meaning: *al-nadāmu tawba* — "regret is repentance" (Ibn Mājah 4249; Aḥmad 3680), joined with *al-ʿazmu* (firm resolve), *al-ittibāʿ* (following with good deeds, 40:7), and *al-istighfār* (asking pardon). The damned of 40:11 have regret, and they have words; what they never produced was the *ʿazm* that changes conduct while there is still conduct to change. Al-Ḥasan al-Baṣrī's aphorism on this sūrah is transmitted in a form every preacher has repeated: *the people of the Fire will confess, and their confession will not benefit them; so make your confession benefit you now, while the tongue is still free.*

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:12

> **12** *That is because when God alone was invoked, you disbelieved; and when partners were ascribed to Him, you believed. The judgment belongs to God, the Exalted, the Great.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict Diagnoses the Symptom**

* Dhālikum bi-annahu idhā duʿiya Allāhu waḥdahu kafartum wa-in tushrak bihi tu'minūn* — the causal *bi-anna* is what makes this a sentence of diagnosis rather than description: "this is *because*...". The punishment is not arbitrarily attached to a crime; the punishment *is* the crime read at full scale. Al-Rāzī treats the verse as a proof that the recompense in the next life is the unveiled form of the act itself: a soul that believed a lie about God and refused the truth about God is in the Fire for the same structural reason it is miserable in this world.

**An Faith That Scales with Its Objects**

Look at what the verse exposes, because it is a description of a psychology and not just of idolatry. When the call is to One, they disbelieve; when the call is to partners, they believe. Their belief tracks not truth but comfort: monotheism demands singular accountability, undivided loyalty, and the refusal of every substitute authority, while polytheism — of whatever kind — distributes responsibility, permits bargaining, and offers multiple points of access. This is the reasoning the Qur'an puts into the mouth of the man of Pharaoh's house at 40:43 and into Abraham's at 12:80 and 52:22 — "Is it better, many dispersed masters, or God, the One, the Subduer?" — and it is also the ground of the sūrah's long refusal of *shurakāʾ* (40:28, 73–74). Al-Ṭabarī records the gloss of Qatāda: *they could not bear the Oneness, but whatever divided God's right among others pleased them.* Ibn Kathīr notes the same for the Meccans, whose objection to the Qur'an is stated in its own words: *Has he made the gods one God? Truly this is a thing astonishing* (38:5), and *we did not hear of this in the last religion; this is nothing but a fabrication* (38:7).

**The Grammar: Two Sentences, One Sin**

The two clauses are perfectly balanced — *idhā duʿiya... kafartum* (passive "was invoked," plural "you disbelieved") against *idhā tushrika... tu'minūn* (passive "partners were ascribed," "you believe") — a mirrored structure in which the only variables are the number of gods and the direction of the assent. The passive is deliberate: no human is being blamed for the disbelief; the sentence describes what happens *to* them when the call comes. And the tenses shift from past to present in the second half, a Qur'anic technique that stretches the crime from a single historical moment to a settled character: they *did* disbelieve, they *do* believe — this is how they are made.

**The Idols' Own Testimony**

The sūrah will have its partners speak (40:73–74) and deny ever having been worshipped; here the point is only that the act of ascribing is *ascription* — *tushrak bihi*, from *sh-r-k*, joining, making something a co-partner in a share — an act of human arithmetic, not of discovery. Truth is not created by addition: as al-Rāzī puts it, one cannot make a lie more probable by repeating it in a chorus, nor a partner real by counting him twice.

**"Judgment Belongs to God, the Exalted, the Great"**

*Al-ḥukmu illā li-Llāhi* — the sentence has become the Qur'an's constitutional statement and is cited from three places: *Command belongs to none but God; upon Him I rely* (12:67), *He associates none in His judgment with anyone* (18:26), and here.
