Jurists read it as the foundation of the sovereignty of the law; the mystics read it as the dissolution of the creature's claim to assess; the here-and-now reading of this verse is simpler and more severe: on the Day when all appeals are exhausted, the only bench still open is God's, and His *ḥukm* is not a vote. The two names that close it are chosen with care. *Al-Aʿlā* — the Most High — is the name the angels themselves glorify in the sūrah named after it: *Glorify the name of your Lord, the Most High* (87:1), and its use here means that the Judge stands so far above the case that no negotiation, brokerage, or kinship reaches Him — exactly the point the sūrah's Day of Mutual Calling will drive home (40:32) when fathers cannot help sons. *Al-Kabīr* is the Great, from *k-b-r*, whose first sense is bigness that swallows smaller measures: the greatness which makes human scales of comparison inapplicable. It is the name to invoke when one is about to claim that God has been unfair — and it occurs at a moment in the Qur'an when the claim about to be made is precisely that: 40:12's *al-Aʿlā al-Kabīr* closes a sentence about *why* they are being punished, as if to say: the reason is stated, and the greatness of the One who stated it does not require your assent to be right.

The practical moral of the verse is not about polytheists elsewhere. It is about the human tendency to find God easy in proportion as He is diluted — to accept a deity who shares the throne with a board of other considerations, and to resist the One who does not. Ibn ʿAṭiyyah notes in this verse the sharpest reproach in the sūrah: they were not invited to something difficult and refused the impossible; they were invited to oneness and refused because it was singular.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:13

> **13** *It is He who displays His signs to you and sends down provision for you from the sky. And no one reflects except one who turns to Him.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Streams of Evidence, One Audience**

*Wa-huwa alladhī yurīkum āyātihī wa-yunazzilu lakum mina al-samāʾi rizqan* — the conjunction of *āyāt* and *rizq* in one sentence is the sūrah's epistemology in miniature: God shows, and God feeds. The signs address the mind; the provision addresses the body, and both are called revelations of the same Giver. *Yurīkum* is an imperfect of active causation — He keeps showing you, repeatedly, continuously — and *yunazzilu*, the same intensive verb as in 40:2, *tanzīl al-kitāb*, is used of rain. In other words: the Book descends, and the rain descends, from the same sender, in the same manner, in portions fitted to what receives them. The sūrah's readers had no difficulty picturing this: they had just heard of a God who sends down the Book (40:2), and they lived where rain is the difference between life and death.

The pairing occurs elsewhere in identical form: *It is He who sends down for you from the sky water; from it is drink and from it is vegetation* (16:10); *Have you seen the water you drink — is it you who send it down from the clouds, or are We the senders?* (56:68–69); *And in their livelihood and what they tread is a sign for them* (26:8, 33); *and We sent down from the sky blessed water* (50:9). Al-Ṭabarī quotes Mujāhid on this verse: *the signs are what God made plain in creation; the provision is what comes down from the sky* — the two categories are not merely examples but the whole of evidence, since nothing a rational creature can know about God comes except through what He shows or what He gives, and what He gives is itself a showing.

**Rizq: Nourishment as a Sign, Not a Reward**

*Rizq* in the Qur'an is systematically neutralized as a marker of merit: God gives sustenance *without reckoning* to whom He will (2:212, 3:37), and the disbelievers' prosperity is explicitly excluded as evidence of divine favor in this very sūrah's 40:4. The same point is made in the sūrah of the Cave: *We let them eat and enjoy and follow their own play; and how many a town We destroyed that was insolent in its affair* (18:44–45 in effect). Ghāfir will return to this at 40:26 and 40:39: the man of Pharaoh's house will call the world *matāʿ* — goods carried briefly — precisely against the people who read abundance as endorsement. So verse 13 asks its audience to reverse the habit: provision is not a verdict on your worth, it is a *sign* about His character. If God sends down *rizq* on those who dispute His signs (40:4), then the daily loaf is a call, not a certificate — and the reflection (*tafakkur*) the verse demands is precisely the act of reading provision as address.

**"No One Reflects Except the One Who Turns"**

*Wa-mā yatafakkaru illā man yunīb* — and no one reflects except he who *unību*. *Tafakkur* is the exercise of *fikr*, which the Arab lexicographers derive from *f-k-r*, the tracking of footprints: to reflect is to follow traces. That etymology is preserved in the classical discussion (al-Aṣmaʿī's report, cited by al-Rāzī and repeated by al-Zamakhsharī in *Asās al-Balāgha*): *the fikr is the following of the traces*, and *fakr* is poverty, i.e., the state of a man who must hunt for traces because he owns nothing. Reflection is thus tracking: reading the sign as a footprint leading beyond itself, and it fails when the reader mistakes a footprint for a decoration. *Munīb*, from *n-w-b*, is the one who repeatedly turns back, the penitent — and the sūrah's claim, which the verse makes logically airtight in the sūrah's own terms, is that moral repair is a precondition of intellectual clarity. This is not fideism; it is a repeated observation the Qur'an makes in other registers too: *they know an outward part of the life of this world, but of the Hereafter they are heedless* (30:7), and *they say: there is only our worldly life; we die and live, and nothing destroys us except time; they have no knowledge of that; they only conjecture* (45:24). Sin clouds, and repentance wipes: al-Rāzī says *al-ṣadā* over the intellect is removed by *ināba*; Ibn al-Qayyim, who devotes a chapter of *al-Fawāʾid* to the point, writes that a sin is a darkness in the heart and that the light of *tafakkur* cannot enter a room already occupied.

**A Prophetic Report on the Believer's Sight**

The source commentary cites the tradition, transmitted at Tirmidhī (and in Aḥmad and Abū Yaʿlā from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī):

> *"Beware the firāsa of the believer, for he sees by the light of God."* (al-Tirmidhī 3093, grading it disputed but noting its currency; the wording appears with other chains in al-Ḥākim's *Mustadrak* and Abū Nuʿaym's *Ḥilya*)

The grading is contested — al-Tirmidhī calls it *gharīb* and Aḥmad ibn Ḥanbal criticized a supporting narration — and it should be cited with that note. But its meaning belongs exactly to this verse: *firāsa*, from *f-r-s*, is the quick, penetrating judgment of a man on horseback who sees the ground and the prey at once; the "insight of the believer" is *tafakkur* turned into habit, and the sūrah says the habit grows on one soil only: turning back to God. The verse thus links 40:13 to the sūrah's central psychological thesis: perception is moral. Pharaoh's people are not said to have lacked evidence — they lacked *ināba*, and so their seeing was intact and their reflection was dead: *they rejected them, while their own souls were convinced, out of wrongdoing and haughtiness* (27:14).

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:14

> **14** *So call upon God, devoting the religion entirely to Him, even though the disbelievers dislike it.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's First Command to the Prophet**

After the letters, the Book, the four names, the diagnosis of disputation, the archive of the destroyed, the angels' prayer, and the verdict on the damned, the chapter arrives at an imperative addressed to the Messenger himself: *fa-udʿū Llāha mukhliṣīna lahu al-dīn*. The particle *fa* ties the command to everything preceding it, and the connection is worth spelling out: because the angels ask forgiveness for the repentant (v. 7), because judgment belongs to God alone (v. 12), and because only the one who turns reflects (v. 13) — therefore call. Worship here is not a separate obligation inserted for balance; it is the consequence of the creed just established. Al-Rāzī makes the point explicitly: if the religion is to be sincere, it must be directed to a single addressable reality, and the verse before said that God alone judges.

**Duʿāʾ: the Act that Names the Relation**

*Duʿāʾ*, from *d-ʿ-w*, is calling out to another so that he comes — the word used for inviting guests to a meal (cf. 33:53, the *walīma* invitation) as well as for prayer, and the pairing is not accidental: in duʿāʾ the servant invites nothing and is invited to everything. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr report that ʿĀʾisha and Ibn ʿAbbās preferred the reading *fa-uʿbudū* — "so worship God" — for this verse, and al-Ṭabarī confirms the standard reading while noting that the two are congruent, because the sūrah's own 40:60 says *Call upon Me and I will respond to you* and then labels that calling *ʿibāda* ("those too proud for My worship"). The Prophet ﷺ fixed the equation in the report cited by the source: *al-duʿāʾ huwa al-ʿibāda* — "supplication is the worship," and then he recited this very verse and 40:60 (al-Tirmidhī 2969, Abū Dāwūd 1479, Ibn Mājah 3828, from al-Nuʿmān ibn Bashīr; graded *ḥasan* by al-Tirmidhī and *ṣaḥīḥ* by al-Ḥākim). The implication the commentators draw is subtle but strong: an act whose essence is asking is the essence of servitude, because asking acknowledges the only two facts that matter — one's need and God's power to meet it.

**Ikhlaṣ: What the Word Was Made From**

*Mukhliṣīna* is the participle of *akhlaṣa*, from *kh-l-ṣ*, whose concrete sense is to clarify a liquid by drawing off what clouds it — the same root gives *khāliṣ*, unmixed milk, and *iḫlīṣ*, straining. To devote religion to God is therefore not to add sincerity as an extra quality to an act, but to *refine the act* until nothing else remains in it: hope of the audience's approval, dread of their blame, the wish to be seen as pious, the calculation of worldly advantage. Al-Junayd defined *ikhlaṣ* as "that God not desire in your act of obedience anyone other than Him," and Ibn al-Qayyim, in *al-Fawāʾid*, gives the practical test: "the sincerity of the act is known by whether it continues when no one sees it and when it costs." The Qur'an's own warning against its opposite is vivid — *Woe to those who pray, who are heedless of their prayer, who do their deed to be seen* (107:4–6) — and its promise for the sincere is the astonishing exception in the scene of divine intimacy: Iblīs cries *I will come upon them from before and behind, from their right and their left* (7:17) and does not mention above or below; and God answers *My servants — you have no authority over them, except those who follow you among the deviants* (15:42), while al-Ḥijr adds the specific exemption: *except Your servants among them, purified — mukhlaṣīn* (15:40). Al-Ṭabarī stresses that the exception is not the result of human effort alone but of God's own choosing for whom He wills — which is the exact meaning of the passive reading (*mukhlaṣ* as "one whom God has purified"), a reading he preserves for 38:41 and which the sūrah's own theology of *taqwā* supports.

The foundational hadith governs here as it does throughout:

> *"Actions are only by intentions, and every person has only what he intended."* (al-Bukhārī 1; Muslim 1907, from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb)

and the same collection preserves the warning attached to it: the one who fights for spoils and the one who recites for a reputation are the first for whom the Fire is kindled (Muslim 1905). Sūrah Ghāfir's *mukhliṣīn* is the chapter's answer to both.

**"Even Though the Disbelievers Dislike It"**

*Wa-law kariha al-kāfirūn* is a clause of emotional protection, not a threat. The Makkan community had understood from the beginning that sincerity was cheap to intend and expensive in social consequences: praying facing an unseen house in a city whose entire economy ran on the sanctuary's idols was a public act, and purity of intention could not make the hostility stop. So the sūrah says, in effect: the objection is not your fault and not your problem; you cannot make the disliking disappear, and you are not being asked to. The hadith of Islam's strangeness fits here exactly:

> *"Islam began as something strange, and it will return as something strange, so glad tidings to the strangers."* (Muslim 145, from Abū Hurayra)

The verse's second half also quietly redefines the field of battle: the opponent is *dislike*, not argument. Verse 4 said disputation is the disbelievers' medium; verse 14 tells the believer that his medium is *duʿāʾ*, and that the two will not be adjudicated by who persuades whom. Al-Qurṭubī notes the practical instruction that the *duʿāʾ* be accompanied by patience toward the dislike; hence the sūrah's next two appearances of the imperative of patience (40:55, 40:77), and the final word of the whole chapter on the *khāsirūn*.

**The Verse at the End of the Prayer**

The source commentary records that the Prophet ﷺ used to recite this verse and its companion at the conclusion of the canonical prayer, saying: *lā ilāha illā Allāh, lahu al-mulku wa-lahu al-ḥamd, wa-huwa ʿalā kulli shay'in qadīr; lā ḥawla wa-lā quwwata illā bi-Llāhi...* — a wording transmitted by Ibn ʿAbbās and collected by al-Nasāʾī in *ʿAmal al-Yawm wa-al-Layla* and by Aḥmad, with chains the muḥaddithūn rate variously and the wording "lā ḥawla wa-lā quwwata illā bi-Llāh" firmly established in the two Ṣaḥīḥs (al-Bukhārī 6409, 7439: *lā ḥawla wa-lā quwwata illā bi-Llāh* is described by the Prophet ﷺ as "a treasure of the Garden"). The *ḥawla* formula is the practical embodiment of *ikhlaṣ*: "there is no moving and no power except with God" is a statement that the agent has no independent motor at all — the perfect antidote to the hidden wish to be the author of one's own religion. The verse thus closes its own circle: to call upon God sincerely is to ask for the strength to do it from Him.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:15

> **15** *The Raiser of degrees, the Lord of the Throne. He casts the Spirit of His command upon whomever He wills of His servants, to warn of the Day of Meeting.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Sentence That Continues the Previous One**

*Rafiʿu al-darajāti dhu al-ʿarsh* is in the genitive, attached to what precedes it: the God to whom the religion is to be devoted is described here by two attributes of elevation and sovereignty, and then by one of the most consequential acts in the sūrah's universe — the sending of revelation. So the verse sets up an asymmetry the Makkan audience felt bodily: He *raises* degrees, and He *owns* the Throne; you, meanwhile, are asked only to *call*. The two halves of the sūrah's teaching — divine generosity in elevation, and human work in supplication — meet in a single sentence.

**Darajāt: the Rank that Is Earned and Given**

*Daraja*, from *d-r-j*, is a step in a stair — hence degree, level, rank — and the Qur'an uses it of both the Garden's levels and, here, of a raising that God performs. Al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Qurṭubī transmit two explanations, both preserved: (a) He raises the seven heavens without pillars, as a structure lifted in place — supported by *He raised the heavens without pillars that you see* (13:2) and *the heaven, He raised it and set the balance* (55:7); (b) He raises the degrees of the believers in Paradise, supported by *they have degrees with their Lord* (2:217, 3:163, 57:21, 58:11) and by the famous hadith:

> *"When you ask God, ask Him al-Firdaws, for it is the middle of Paradise and the roof of Paradise, and above it is the Throne of the Merciful."* (al-Bukhārī 7423, from Abū Hurayra)

That hadith is decisive for the verse's ordering: degrees are raised *and* there is a Throne above them, which is exactly the sequence in 40:15. It also makes *rafaʿ al-darajāt* an act of nearness, not merely of honor: in the sūrah that has just shown the angels bearing that same Throne praying for the believers (40:7), elevation is not competitive but chained — the lowest believer's rank is bound to the highest creature's intercession.

Ibn Kathīr adds the reading of *al-darajāt* as including prophetic ranks and the degrees of reward for patience: *"They are given their reward twice over for what they were patient"* (28:54), which is the same verse's logic for those who "buy not vanity with it" (25:72). And al-Zamakhsharī's comment deserves quoting because it welds the verse to the previous:

> *"He who raises the degrees of the obedient and the friends of God above the Throne, in the Garden, does not leave those who call upon Him with sincerity without their share — He is the Lord of the Throne."*

**Dhū al-ʿArsh: Ownership as Relationship**

The Throne is *dhu*, "possessor of," and this construction — the same as *dhū al-ṭawl* in 40:3 — signifies that the attribute belongs to Him by way of generous relationship, not by way of need: He does not sit because He requires support, nor does He need the Throne as a house, but He is its Owner. The sūrah's theology of divine attributes, contested in detail by the Muʿtazilah (who read *ʿarsh* as dominion and *istiwāʾ* as power) and affirmed by the traditionalists (who read both as real and unlikened, following the salaf's *bilā kayf*, without asking how — Ibn Baṭṭah, Ibn Khuzayma, and Ibn Taymiyyah in *al-ʿArshiyya* and *Bayān Talbīs al-Jahmiyya* are the classic statements), is not meant to be a puzzle but a reassurance: the One who receives your *duʿāʾ* is seated on the seat from which the cosmos is administered. That is why the verse proceeds at once to the casting of revelation — His sovereignty is exercised by speaking, not by pushing.

**Al-Rūḥ: What Is Cast, and From Where**

*Yulqī al-rūḥa min amrihi ʿalā man yashāʾu min ʿibādihi* — He casts (drops, throws down) the Spirit from His command. *Alqā* is used for something thrown from a height (the table of the heaven, 15:14; the garment, 12:93; the Book to Moses, 2:87). *Al-Rūḥ* here receives the four readings the source lists and others: (1) revelation itself — for God says *thus We revealed to you a spirit from Our command* (42:52), and *the Spirit of holiness descended it upon your heart, by Our leave* (2:97); (2) the angel Gabriel, *nazzala bihi al-rūḥu al-amīn* (26:193); (3) the Qur'an, as the *rūḥ* is named in 26:52; (4) the spirit of prophethood and its accompanying support, of the kind described in *God strengthened Jesus, son of Mary, with the Spirit of holiness* (2:87, 253). Al-Rāzī favors the first as the most coherent with the verse's purpose and notes that the ambiguity is beneficial: the "spirit" is whatever in revelation makes dead hearts live, and it is delivered by whatever carrier God elects.

The preposition *min amrihi*, "from His command," is the sūrah's own definition of prophecy's status: it originates in God's *amr*, which is why *mā kāna li-rasūlin an ya'tiya bi-āyatin illā bi-idhni Llāh* belongs to this chapter's argument (40:78), and why *li-yundhira yawma al-talāq* — to warn of the Day of Meeting — is the purpose of prophecy, a purpose the Makkan audience heard as a claim against them and the hadith transposes into consolation: *"Whoever does not wish to meet God, God does not wish to meet him"* (al-Bukhārī 6506; Muslim 2683), which is about the meeting as arrival, not about the necessity of the meeting itself.

**"Upon Whomever He Wills": Grace as the Structure of Election**

*ʿAlā man yashāʾu min ʿibādihi* — God's will selects the recipient of the most consequential communication in history. The verse's placement of this clause is the sūrah's answer to a question the chapter keeps asking: why Muhammad, why an orphan, why the Quraysh's least protected man, when the empires had all the qualifications? The Qur'an's reply is not that God's choice is arbitrary but that it is free: *God places His message where He wills* (6:124), and *your Lord creates what He wills and chooses; the choice is not theirs* (28:68). Ibn Kathīr notes that the clause simultaneously refutes those who claimed prophecy as an inheritance and those who demanded that a miracle be supplied on demand: a messenger is not an employee responding to a customer's specification.

**Yawm al-Talāq: the Day of Meeting Named for Its Encounters**

*Al-ṭalāq* is verbal noun of *lāqa*, meeting, and the sūrah's own use elsewhere shows the sense: *that is the Day of Meeting (al-talāq) for all the worlds* (83:2 — actually *yawmu al-talāq* in the recension of 37:20: *this is the Day of Recompense... that is the Day of Meeting*). Al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Qurṭubī enumerate the meetings: the first generations with the last, the oppressed with the oppressor, every soul with its deeds (cf. *every soul will find whatever good it did present*, 3:30; *and We will bring forth for him on the Day of Resurrection a book which he will meet open-faced*, 17:13), the inhabitants of the Garden with the inhabitants of the Fire (40:32 quotes the calling that passes between them), and ultimately the servants with their Lord (*on that Day you will be exposed, no secret of yours hidden*, 69:18; *that Day some faces will be radiant, looking at their Lord*, 75:22–23). Al-Rāzī remarks that a day named for *meeting* rather than for *judgment* is a warning of an unusual kind: what the damned fear most is not the verdict but the encounter — the sight of what they did to each other, the accountability to the weak (cf. *the mutilator of the slave-girl*, Bukhārī 2470, where the Prophet ﷺ warned of the reckoning and cited *your Lord is not unjust to anyone*, 41:46). That is the note on which the sūrah's eschatology is built, and it prepares the extraordinary scene of 40:16.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:16

> **16** *The Day they come forth — nothing of them is hidden from God. "Whose is the kingdom today?" "God's, the One, the Subduer.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Exposure Without an Exit**

*Yawma hum bārūzūn mā yakhfā ʿalā Llāhi minhum shay'* — the Day they are out, exposed; nothing of them hidden from God. *Barūz*, from *b-r-z*, is the state of being in the open, of a thing leaving cover: the *bāriz* is the champion who steps out of the ranks for single combat, and *barāz* is the open ground between armies. Resurrection, in this single word, is the moment when the whole human race is called out onto that ground, with no cover, no unit, and no line to retreat behind. The Qur'an's other images agree: *the Day when they come out of their graves, hurrying toward the Caller* (54:8), *and the sky will be opened and become gates* (78:19), *We will gather them all without leaving out one* (18:47). Then comes the verse's crushing parenthetical, in a present tense even though the Day is future — *mā yakhfā* — nothing is or will be hidden. Al-Ṭabarī glosses: their deeds, their states, their names, their lineages, their secrets, the marks on their faces (*they will be recognized by their marks*, 55:30; *We will know them by the marks on their faces*, 18:49), and — the clause's sharpest edge — what they concealed in their breasts, which verse 19 will name explicitly.

**The Question Asked on an Empty Plain**

*Li-man al-mulku al-yawm?* — "Whose is the kingdom today?" The sūrah's readers were hearing this in a city where *al-mulk* was precisely the claim of empires: Caesar and Khosrow, whose coins named their gods, and the Quraysh, whose prestige was their *ḥums* status around the sanctuary. The verse stages a day on which the question is asked of an audience consisting only of the powerless. Al-Bukhārī and Muslim transmit, in the wording of Abū Hurayra and of ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr, a tradition that makes the scene literal:

> *"God will grasp the earth and fold the heavens in His right hand, then say: I am the King; where are the kings of the earth? Where are the arrogant?"* (al-Bukhārī 7410; Muslim 2787)

and in the other authentic version:

> *"The people will be gathered on the Day of Resurrection barefoot, naked, and uncircumcised," and God will call out: "I am the King; the deeds are recorded; this day no soul is wronged in anything."* (al-Bukhārī 6527; Muslim 2860)

The two reports are the plainest commentary on 40:16: the question is not rhetorical for God's benefit but final for ours. Al-Qurṭubī records the variant readings — the interrogative intoned as a question and as an announcement — and notes the disagreement about who answers: some transmit that the whole assembly answers (Ibn ʿAbbās, al-Ḥasan), some that the angels answer, and some that God answers His own question because there is no one else whose answer would count. Al-Zamakhsharī prefers the last, and his remark is the most quoted: *when there is no respondent, the asking is for the sake of making the absence of respondents known.*

**Why the Question Is Not "Who Is Your Lord?"**

The Qur'an does ask other questions on that Day — *did you respond to the messengers?* (28:65), *where are My partners?* (28:62, 40:73) — but here the question is about ownership, not about loyalty, and that choice fits the sūrah's whole argument. Ghāfir has been dismantling, verse by verse, the claim that power is possessed by anyone other than God: Pharaoh's sovereignty is *this day* only (40:29), the disbelievers' freedom to come and go is an appearance (40:4), the idols have *no call* at all (40:43), the plotters' scheming is *astray* (40:25). The Day of the verse is the day on which the appearance is stripped: the only question a creature might have asked — who is running this? — has a one-word answer and no runner-up.

**Al-Aḥad al-Qahhār**

*Al-Wāḥid* — the One: a name the Qur'an refuses to leave without qualification in a chapter about *shirk*; *al-Qahhār*, from *q-h-r*, to subdue, to break the back of, to compel — a word used of the heavens that function without consent (*and He subdues all things* is not the idiom, but *the sky will be a molten metal*, 70:8, and the repeated *qahhara* of domination is: He compels all existence to be what it is). The pairing is the sūrah's answer to a fear: oneness could be loneliness, and powerlessness could be the truth of a solitary deity; here the One is the Irresistible, so the universe is neither self-running nor ruled by committee. The same combination occurs at 12:39 and 14:52 and 13:16, and it is used in the sūrah of Joseph as an argument against the plural lords of a household — which is precisely how the man of Pharaoh's house will deploy it here in Ghāfir's 40:42–43. Al-Rāzī notes: because He is *Qahhār*, His answer to the question needs no enforcement; the kings have nothing left to enforce with.

**The Day as "Today" — an Ethics in the Grammar**

The Arabic places *al-yawm*, "today," in a sentence about a future day, a feature the classical rhetoricians treat as *taḥqīq al-ghāʾib bi-wuṣūl mā lam yaqiʿ baʿdu*, presenting the absent as present because it is certain. Ibn Kathīr's pastoral inference is direct: whoever believes that the question will be asked of him will not spend his today behaving as though the kingdom were his. This is also the point the sūrah will make with the most graphic sentence in the chapter, at 40:46 — Pharaoh's house exposed to the Fire *morning and evening* while the world still runs as usual — and it is the reason the verse is placed immediately after the description of the Day of Meeting: exposure, then the question, then (v. 17) the reckoning.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:17

> **17** *That Day every soul is repaid for what it earned. No wrong is done that Day. Truly God is swift in the reckoning.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Sentences, One Theory of Justice**

The verse compresses the entire Qur'anic eschatology into a proposition, a qualification, and a speed limit. (1) *Yawmaʾidhin tajzā kullu nafsin bimā kasabat* — each soul repaid for what it *earned*; (2) *lā ẓulma al-yawma* — no wrong; (3) *inna Llāha sarīʿu al-ḥisāb* — God is swift at the reckoning. The three are mutually protective and answer the three classic objections to divine judgment: that it will be arbitrary, that it will be biased, and that it will be so slow as to be meaningless.

**Kasaba: the Verb That Makes the Self the Author**

*Kasb*, earning, from *k-s-b*, is the Qur'an's carefully chosen word for what a person does of himself, and the *tajzā* is its repayment: *she has what she earned, and against her what she perpetrated* (2:286). The grammar is repeatedly exact: *lahā mā kasabat wa-ʿalayhā mā iktasabat*, the simple verb for good and the intensive for evil, and the pronoun "for her" for the reward against "upon her" for the guilt. This is the verse from which the sūrah's whole doctrine of recompense runs, and the Muʿtazilī and Ashʿarī debates about *kasb* and * Khalq al-afʿāl* (the creation of acts) grew around precisely such verses: the Muʿtazilah insisted the servant creates his act so that God's *ʿadl* be intact; al-Ashʿarī's school held that God creates the act and the servant *acquires* it, thereby earning it — a position whose subtlety is often caricatured and whose aim is to hold both halves of this verse: the soul is repaid for what it *earned* (so it is truly its own), and *no wrong is done* (so the Judge is truly just). Al-Juwaynī and al-Rāzī both note that the verse's *kasabat* excludes two things at once: punishment for what one did not do, and the possibility of a soul's burden being transferred. The Qur'an is emphatic on the second: *no bearer of burdens bears another's* (6:164, 17:15, 35:18, 39:7, 53:38), and — against the idea that the sūrah's verdicts are collective — Ghāfir's own version of the same rule appears when the weak complain about their leaders (40:47–48).

**"No Wrong Is Done" — and the Punishment Comes From Them**

*Lā ẓulma al-yawma* is, as the source commentary observes, the verse's most important word for theodicy, and the sūrah elsewhere fills it out: *God does not wrong people in the least, but people wrong themselves* (10:44); *what We say does not admit of change, and We do no wrong to anyone* (50:29); *your Lord does no wrong to any servant* (18:46). Al-Qurṭubī cites the *ḥadīth qudsī* at length here, the great charter of Islamic moral realism:

> *"O My servants, I have forbidden wrong to Myself and made it unlawful among you, so do not wrong one another."* (Muslim 2577)

and the tradition continues with its corollary used by the source: *O My servants, these are your deeds which I record for you, then I repay them in full; whoever finds good, let him praise God; whoever finds other than that, let him blame no one but himself* — the wording being transmitted by Muslim in the longer form and the source's version appearing in Ibn Ḥibbān and Aḥmad's Musnad, from Abū Dharr. The theological consequence is stated clearly by Ibn Kathīr: the Fire is not God's spite; it is the shape that chosen wrongdoing takes when the shielding of mercy is lifted. That is why the sūrah's most horrifying statement about Hell — *the Fire, they are exposed to it morning and evening* (40:46) — is attached to a people whose own sin is described as a *plot* that enclosed them (40:45), a punishment literally self-inflicted, whose Arabic word *ḥāqa bi-him* means "went around them, enclosed them."

**Swift in the Reckoning: the Problem of Scale, Answered**

*Inna Llāha sarīʿu al-ḥisāb* answers an objection made in the Qur'an itself, in the sūrah of Abraham: *and God is swift in reckoning* is set there against *do not think God unaware of what the wrongdoers do; He only delays them to a Day when eyes stare fixedly* (14:42). Al-Ṭabarī reports the gloss: God will not need to rehearse, calculate, or deliberate, because He does not forget and nothing is obscure to Him; and the sūrah quotes the measure of that speed: *our command is only one word, like a blink of the eye* (54:50) and *it is only a single blast, and when they are all before Us* (36:53). The hadith preserves the scale in a single image: *"God attends to all of them on the Day of Resurrection as He attends to one of them"* (al-Bukhārī 7510) — the same *ḥisāb* that, in another report, is done *in half a day* for the believer, in the wording transmitted in the Musnad and Sunan with the chain graded variously, and the tradition in Muslim (2792) that the believer is shown his sins and God says: *I concealed them for you in the world, and I forgive them for you today.*

Al-Rāzī's logical note on the verse's placement is the last word: after the verse says "no wrong," some might infer a judgment so cautious that it is slow and the wicked get decades of impunity; the third clause forecloses the inference — the delay is mercy, not difficulty, and it has a deadline, which the sūrah will state with terrifying concreteness in the words of 40:46 and 40:84–85, the moment when the punishment is *seen* and belief becomes useless.

---
## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:18

> **18** *So warn them of the Day that is drawing near, when the hearts will be in the throats, choked with grief. There will be no close friend for the wrongdoers, and no intercessor who is heeded.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Command Restated With a Deadline**

*Wa-andhirhum yawma al-āsiya* — warn them of the Day of the Coming Event. After three verses of description (the Day of Meeting, the question of the kingdom, the reckoning), the sūrah returns to the Prophet's task in the imperative, because the whole purpose of Ghāfir's eschatology is not information but timing. *Al-āsiya*, from *a-s-y*, "to come into view, to arrive," is one of the Qur'an's names for the Last Day and is chosen here for its proximity: the Day is already on the road. Al-Ṭabarī glosses: "the Day whose arrival is certain," and al-Qurṭubī notes that the Arabs use *asā al-ʿaqīqa*, "the star came into view," of a sign appearing over the horizon — a name built out of the very experience of a Makkan traveller who knows that what he sees rising is already upon him. This is the sūrah's own analogy for *taqwā* applied to time: the danger that is described as "coming" changes the way the traveler walks.

**When Anxiety Becomes Anatomy**

*Idh al-qulūbu ḥanājira* — when the hearts reach to the throats. The Arabic is elliptical: the hearts *will be* in the throats, and the lexicographers explain *ḥanjara* as the throat-pipe itself, and the idiom *ballaghat al-qulūbu al-ḥanājira* as proverbial for terror that physically displaces the organ: fear drives the heart upward so that it is, as it were, strangling the man. Al-Rāzī, after quoting the linguistic point, adds what the word *ẓāmin* conveys: *kāẓimīn*, from *k-ẓ-m*, to suppress, to hold in, to close off — the Arabic for the man who bites back a scream; the same root is used of a water-skin tied shut and of rage *makẓūmīn*, "restraining" (5:63, in effect), and in the hadith of the strong man: *al-shadīd* is not the wrestler but the one who owns himself at anger (al-Bukhārī 6114; Muslim 2609). The verse therefore describes a double pressure: the heart forced up, and the mouth forced shut. Al-Zamakhsharī, never slow with a rhetorical stroke, remarks that God described the Day by the state of its audience rather than by its events, and that the single image does more than the catalogue — no scream, no pleading, no eloquence, only the sound of a man's own breath failing him. This is also a reply to the mockers of 40:4 and 40:35, whose defining trait was speech: endless disputation. Ghāfir's arc runs from argument to choking.

**No *Khalīl*, No *Sharīʿ*: the Two Supports Withdrawn**

*Lā khalīla lahum wa-lā shafīʿ yuṭāʿ* — for the wrongdoers no intimate friend and no intercessor obeyed. The two nouns are the two supports of a pre-Islamic (and any) person's security, and the verse removes both at once. *Khalīl*, from *kh-l-l*, is the friend into whose inmost (*khilāl*) affairs you enter and who enters yours — the word is used of God's relation to Abraham only, in the Qur'an's most stunning declaration: *and God took Abraham as a khalīl* (4:125), which means the intimate friend of God is the man who was willing to surrender his son and his homeland. The Arab pre-Islamic *khalāʾil* pacts — oaths sworn between men, the *ḥilf* that made the Quraysh's *ḥums* alliance (cf. the Prophet's speech at the conquest: *"there is no emigration, but forgiveness and goodwill"*) — are the social reality the verse negates. The Qur'an states the same denial repeatedly: *we will have no intercession, nor a khalīl near (to God)* (26:100–101); *and no friend and no intercession is for the disbelievers* (40:7); *on that Day there is no bargaining and no friendship* (2:254); *the intimate friends on that Day are enemies to one another, except the God-fearing* (43:67); *every man that Day has enough to occupy him* (80:37) and *no intimate friend will ask about his friend* (70:10). Al-Qurṭubī's inference is that the loss of friendship is itself part of the punishment: the damned are not merely tormented but *disassociated*, which is the exact inverse of what 40:7–9 grants the believers (angels as advocates, families reunited).

*Shafīʿ*, from *sh-f-ʿ*, is the one who joins his request to another's, making it two — the broker's favor. And the qualifier *yuṭāʿ* — "who is heeded/obeyed" — is the sūrah's careful limit on the doctrine of intercession, which the Qur'an both affirms and conditions: *who is it that can intercede with Him except by His leave?* (2:255); *on that Day intercession does not benefit except from one whom the Merciful permits and whose word He approves* (20:109); *and they do not intercede except for those He has approved, and they are of His awe* (21:28). The Muʿtazilah pressed these texts to deny intercession for sinful believers altogether; the Sunni majority, from al-Ṭabarī through Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī, read 40:7 as its proof for the believers and 40:18 as its negation for the obstinate disbelievers, harmonizing them: there is intercession, and it is entirely at God's disposal, and it excludes those whose defining act was to refuse the call that makes intercession available (cf. the *shafāʿa ʿuẓmā* of al-Bukhārī 4712 and Muslim 194, where the people come to Adam, then Noah, Abraham, Moses, Jesus, and finally the Prophet ﷺ, and God raises him to the *maqām maḥmūd* of 17:79).

**Who Is Warned, and Why It Still Reads as Mercy**

The pronoun *hum* in *wa-andhirhum* refers back to the disbelievers of the immediately preceding verses, but the sūrah has already established that the Prophet's function is warning, not coercion: *you are not a controller over them* (88:22), *so remind — you are only a reminder; you are not a tyrant over them* (88:21–22), *and say, "the truth is from your Lord; so whoever wills, let him believe, and whoever wills, let him disbelieve"* (18:29). The verse is thus a statement of God's fairness as much as a threat: they will not be seized before being warned (cf. 17:15, 28:59, 35:18, 6:19 — *this Qur'an has been revealed to me so that I warn you with it and whoever it reaches*). That last clause is the ground on which Ibn Kathīr insists this Day's terror is addressed even to a people who will not listen: the warning is not meant to produce compliance but to produce *evidence*, and the sūrah will put that in the mouths of the keepers of Hell at 40:50: *did not your messengers bring you clear proofs?* — *yes*. Then their own supplication is refused, because supplication was available and declined.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:19

> **19** *He knows the betrayal of the eyes and what the breasts conceal.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Single Sentence, the Whole of Moral Reality**

*Yaʿlamu khāʾinata al-aʿyuni wa-mā tukhfī al-ṣudūr* — God knows the *khāʾina* of the eyes and the secret in the chests. Structurally this clause stands between the choking silence of the Damned (40:18) and the verdict of a Judge who judges *bi-l-ḥaqq* (40:20), and it functions as the verse that makes the verdict fair: the judge is not relying on testimony, because He already has the facts, including the facts only the accused has. Al-Rāzī observes that the sūrah mentions knowledge of the *hidden* rather than power over the *visible* precisely here, because what is contested at the Day of Meeting (40:15–17) is intention, not behavior.

**Khāʾinat al-Aʿyuni: the Sin That Fits in a Blink**

*Khāʾina*, from *kh-w-n*, treachery, is the noun for a betrayal of a trust — the opposite of *amāna* — and the eye is called a trustee because the gaze is the sense through which desire enters uninvited. The phrase denotes, as the source records, two adjacent realities: (a) an eye that glances at what is forbidden — a look that the body takes while the owner pretends it is innocent, hence "treachery": the eye's movement is concealed even from its owner's public face; (b) a false claim about what was seen: "I saw," when one did not (IK, Q, Ṭs) — the testimony of the eye, which in Arab law was the strongest evidence there was, here marked as capable of being falsified. Both readings serve the verse's argument. If the eye can betray, then the most trusted sense is not self-authenticating; if the eye can lie, then no courtroom can reach the truth — and the only adequate judge is the one who does not need witnesses.

The Prophet ﷺ gave the first reading its canonical form:

> *"The zināʾ of the eyes is looking."* (al-Bukhārī 6241, 6243; Muslim 2657, from Abū Hurayra — in the full hadith the tongue's, the ear's, the hand's, and the foot's *zināʾ* are named, and *the heart desires and wishes, and the organ either acts on it or refuses*.)

That closing clause is the tafsīr of 40:19, and it is why the verse matters beyond purity law: the moral event happens before the act, in a place that no human authority can reach and no human record keep. The Qur'an's own narrative of the eye's *khāʾina* is the story of the Aziz's wife in Sūrah Joseph, where *khāʾin* is used in the very same scene: *and she desired him, and he would have desired her, if he had not seen the proof of his Lord* (12:24) — al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record the exegetical tradition that God showed him the image of his father Jacob restraining himself, and the verse's point survives the details: the same eye that can betray can also be the instrument of restraint, and the difference is what the breasts conceal.

**"What the Breasts Conceal" — the Heart as the Locus**

*Al-ṣudūr*, plural of *ṣadr*, is the chest, and the Qur'an consistently localizes intention, memory, and disbelief there: *and God has not made two hearts in your breast* (33:4); *so We removed what was in their breasts, flowing beneath them rivers* (7:43) — a purification that is literal and psychological at once; *do not take their oaths seriously, when they are ready to break everything, and they have made religion a play; and their hearts are in discord* (58:14, and cf. *they conceal in their breasts what they will not disclose to you*, 3:119); *and God knows what is in your breasts* (64:4, and 2:235, 284 — *whether you disclose what is in your breasts or conceal it, God will call you to account for it*). The verse at hand states the same omniscience, but in the register of the Day: He *knows* it now, so that at 2:284 that same knowledge is the basis of a hope, not only of fear — *God forgives whom He wills and punishes whom He wills*. The sūrah's own resolution of that tension is at 40:3 and 40:7: the One who knows the chest's contents is *Tawwāb*, and the angels ask forgiveness for those who repent.

The ethical radius is wider than the eye alone. A tradition in Muslim (2564, from Abū Hurayra) fixes the criterion of divine judgment:

> *"God does not look at your bodies or your appearances, but He looks at your hearts and your deeds."*

and the famous hadith on intention (al-Bukhārī 1) is its legal counterpart. Ibn ʿAṭiyyah's note on this verse is that it is the sūrah's most effective argument against the hypocrite and the reputational ascetic, both of whom manage appearances: the man of Pharaoh's house who *concealed his faith* (40:28) is the positive case of the same principle — his *sirr* was faith and it saved him; the courtiers whose public piety concealed ambition for Pharaoh's favor are the negative one.

**The Sound Heart and the Sick One**

The verse implies a taxonomy the sūrah never states but assumes: a heart may be *salīm* (sound) — the only luggage admitted to the Garden in Abraham's prayer: *the Day when neither wealth nor children will benefit, except one who comes to God with a sound heart* (26:88–89) — or *fī qalbihi maraḍ* (sick, 2:10), or sealed (40:35). The Prophet ﷺ mapped the same ground in the hadith the source quotes at 40:35: *"in the body there is a morsel of flesh; if it is sound, the whole body is sound; if it is corrupt, the whole body is corrupt — verily it is the heart"* (al-Bukhārī 52; Muslim 1599). The eye's betrayal is therefore a symptom, not the disease, and 40:19 is a warning about the disease. This is also why Ghāfir's damned, at 40:11–12, will confess with total accuracy and gain nothing: their knowledge of what their breasts concealed arrives too late to be repentance, which is defined by al-Nawawī and others in the words of the hadith as *al-nadāmu wa-l-ʿazm ʿalā al-ittibāʿ* — regret joined with resolve to follow (the regret alone: Ahmad 1/402 and Mālik, *al-Muwaṭṭaʾ*, with a chain the scholars discuss).

**How the Verse Reaches the Reader**

Nothing in 40:19 is addressed to the damned; it is a clause about God inserted into the sūrah's courtroom scene, and the reader is meant to feel both of its edges — exposure and intimacy. The man who has never admitted his worst intention to another living being is the one to whom this verse is, in fact, the most consoling in the chapter, provided he is the one who turns: the God who sees the eye's treachery is the God whose name, two verses earlier in the same paragraph, was *Ghāfir al-dhanb*. Al-Junayd's saying on this verse, quoted by al-Qushayrī in *al-Risāla al-Qushayriyya*, puts it as a definition of *tawba*: "the servant's acknowledgment that he has no power, and his seeing that all power belongs to God."

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:20

> **20** *God judges with the truth. As for those they call upon besides Him, they do not judge anything at all. Truly God is the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict of a God Who Does Not Need Evidence**

*Wa-llāhu yaqḍī bi-l-ḥaqqi wa-lladhīna yadʿūna min dūni-hi lā yaqḍūna bi-shay'* — the contrast is between two verbs of the same root with the same subject-position, and the predicate is not "judge falsely" but *do not judge anything at all*. The disbelievers' gods are not bad judges; they are not judges. In a sūrah that has described human courts (40:26, 28 — the court deciding whether to kill Moses) and divine judgment (40:12, 16), the verse establishes the single thing the courts lack: authority with knowledge. God *judges bi-l-ḥaqq*, and the preposition is read two ways by the commentators, both sound and both needed: "in accordance with truth" (never deviating from what is right) and "with the truth itself" (His verdict *is* reality, not a representation of it — as He says at 45:22, *and it was judged between them with truth, and it was said: praise be to God, Lord of the worlds*).

**Al-Ḥaqq: the Real, the Binding, the Due**

*Ḥaqq*, from *ḥ-q-q*, is that which is established, obligatory, and befitting — *ḥaqq* is the debt owed, the right one possesses, the thing whose existence does not depend on anyone believing in it. The sūrah's argument against the disputers (40:4, 35, 56, 69) is that they *fight the ḥaqq* with *bāṭil* (40:5), and here the verdict names the quality of the opposing party's court: their gods possess no *ḥaqq* at all, because they possess nothing. The Qur'an's most vivid statement of the same is the refrain of the *Ḥawāmīm*: *that is because God is the ḥaqq, and what they call upon besides Him is the bāṭil, and God is the ʿAlīy, the Kabīr* (22:62; cf. 31:30), and the verse of Jonah's people is paired with it: *would that there had been a town that believed and whose faith benefited it — except the people of Jonah... That is because their prophet... and We saved them and made them enjoy for a while* (10:98) — the exception that proves a true faith averts a true judgment, because it appeals to the One who judges with truth.

**The Idols' Incapacity, Argued From Their Composition**

Al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī gloss *lā yaqḍūna bi-shay'* with the argumentative tradition of Abū Musa al-Ashʿarī, Ibn ʿAbbās, and Qatāda: they judge nothing because they *hear* nothing, *see* nothing, *know* nothing, and *create* nothing — and the sūrah has just said it of the idols' worshippers' hopes at 40:43: *that which you call upon besides Him has no claim in this world or the next.* The classical *istidlāl* runs as a syllogism: judgment requires knowledge of the particular case and power to execute the verdict; idols are carved matter (cf. Abraham's question: *do you call upon that which neither profits you nor harms you? Woe to you and to what you call upon besides God! Do you not reason?* 21:66–67; and *do they have hands with which to act? Or feet with which to walk? Or eyes with which to see? Or ears with which to hear?* 7:195). Ibn Kathīr notes that 40:20 also negates the *aḥbār* and *awliyāʾ* whom the polytheists claimed as intercessors: whoever cannot judge is unfit to be trusted with a soul. Al-Zamakhsharī's barb, in *al-Kashshāf*, is that the verse puts the idols in the position of defendants who have no counsel: they cannot even deny the charge.

**The All-Hearing, the All-Seeing — the Pair That Closes the Courtroom**

*Inna Llāha huwa al-Samīʿu al-Baṣīr*. The pairing occurs around the Qur'an's most urgent moments of prayer and peril: *my Lord, indeed You hear my call* (14:39); *I will surely ask Your forgiveness, You are the most merciful of makers* — no, the pair's classic instance is *We heard a man calling* (46:21, the jinn) and *the two companies were about to meet; God was with them; God is All-Hearing, All-Knowing* (2:250 — here *ʿAlīm*, and the sūrah uses both variants). The name *al-Samīʿ* answers the idols' deafness to their worshippers' pleas (46:5 — *who is more astray than one who calls upon, besides God, what will not answer him until the Day of Resurrection, and they are heedless of their calling*) and *al-Baṣīr* answers their blindness to his servants' conduct (49:18 — *and God sees what you do*; cf. *I am with the two of you, I hear and I see*, 20:46). Applied to the Day, the pair converts 40:19's knowledge of hidden betrayal into a promise: the same two names mean that the *ḥujja* (plea) of the silent oppressed is heard and that their *ẓulm* is seen — which is why this verse stands immediately before the sūrah's long passage on the oppressed and their oppressors (40:21–50), where the man of Pharaoh's house, unable to be heard in the court, is heard by *al-Samīʿ al-Baṣīr*.

**Practical Reach: Two Courts at Once**

The verse teaches the believer a habit of double bookkeeping. Whatever case goes against him in the court of appearance — reputation, patronage, the "obvious" verdict of the powerful — is filed in a second court whose judge has full evidence and no need of it, and whose verdict is *bi-l-ḥaqq*. The Qur'an's example is the Prophet of this sūrah's own persecution, comforted precisely by such framing (4:105 — *do not plead on behalf of the treacherous; God does not love the treacherous sinner* and 4:107 — *God is All-Knowing, All-Wise*). But the verse cuts inward too, and the sūrah is careful not to let the reader forget it: whoever trusts that God judges with truth should not want to be judged by his own standard of appearances, since 40:19 has already said what His standard covers.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:21

> **21** *Have they not travelled in the land and seen how the end of those before them was? They were stronger than them in power and in the traces left in the land; so God seized them for their sins, and they had no protector from God.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Scripture's Command to Take a Trip**

*Afalam yasīrū fī al-arḍi fa-yandhurū kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu lladhīna min qablihim* — the sūrah's second travel-verse (the refrain occurs at 12:109, 30:9 and 42, 35:44, 40:82, 47:10) and, for its first audience, a literal instruction: the Quraysh caravans to Syria and Yemen passed within sight of the ruined settlements of the ʿĀd and Thamūd and the towns of Lot; the *ḥaram* trade routes of 106:1–2 made archaeology part of the ordinary economy. The sūrah of al-ʿAnkabūt states the method: *travel in the land and see how He began creation; then God will bring into being the final creation* (29:20) — that is, the empirical and the eschatological are learned in the same journey. And the verse's grammar is deliberate: *fa-yandhurū* is a consequential jussive ("so that they look"), not a second imperative — the travelling is for the looking, and the looking is for the *kayfa kāna ʿāqibatu-hum*: not "what happened," but *how the ending came out*, since *ʿāqiba* is from *ʿ-q-b*, what follows after.

**ʿĀthār: the Traces that Outlive Their Owners**

*Ashadda minhum quwwatan wa-āthāran fī al-arḍ* — stronger in might and in footprints in the land. *Āthār*, plural of *athar*, is exactly that: remaining marks, buildings, inscriptions, irrigation works, the carved tombs of the Nabataean Hegra that a traveler in the Hijaz passes daily (cf. 7:74 — *and you carve out of its mountains houses, being skilled*, and 15:82 — *a sign for those who believe*; 26:148–149 — *gardens and springs and treasuries and a noble station*, which 44:53–56 follows with "so, and We made another people inherit them"). The Qur'an's point is not that ruins are moral poetry; it is a logical observation about the direction of the evidence. The destroyed were *more* durable than the Quraysh, and their durability did not save them; therefore durability is not a verdict. Al-Rāzī makes this explicit as a *burhān*: if permanence proved divine favor, nothing would have perished, and the ruins refute the premise. The same reasoning stands behind Ghāfir's own 40:82 with the added element of numbers, and it is aimed at the fallacy the sūrah diagnosed at 40:4 — the reading of mobility and prosperity as evidence.

**Seized for Their Sins — and Why the Verse Adds "No Protector"**

*Fa-akhadhahumu Llāhu bidhunūbihim wa-mā lahum min Llāhi min wāqin* — God seized them for their sins, and they had no one shielding them from God. *Wāqin*, from *w-q-ʾ*, the same root as *taqwā*, is a shield or a protector: the verse's last words say that the very faculty the Qur'an praises in the believer — *ittaqū*, take a shield — was unavailable to them when it mattered, because *taqwā* is a shield taken *before* the blow, not after. Ibn Kathīr draws this out and cites, in the same sense, *and you will not be able to protect yourselves from God* — and the phrase "from God" is essential: in the sūrah's system nothing stands between a creature and its Creator, not a tribe, a fort, a tower, an alliance, or a king (40:26, 36–37). The clause *bidhunūbihim* — "for their sins" — is repeated almost verbatim from 40:5 and is the sūrah's insistence, against any reading of history as fate or whim, that the seizure was *caused*; this pairs with 40:17's *lā ẓulma* to produce the chapter's theodicy in four short verses: 40:20 (a true judge), 40:17 (no wrong), 40:5 and 40:21 (seizure *for* sins), 40:6 (the word already due).

**The Travel as Spiritual Technology**

Al-Ghazālī, in the *Iḥyāʾ*'s book on travel, and Ibn Rajab, in *Faḍlu Riḥlat al-ʿIlm*, both treat this verse as the scriptural basis for a discipline: to travel with the specific intention of reading the *ʿāqiba*, so that the traveller returns with a resized sense of what endures. The Prophet ﷺ is reported to have discouraged a journey that was mere movement:

> *"Travel is a piece of torment: it withholds from one of you his food, his drink, and his sleep; so when one of you has finished his business let him hasten to return to his family."* (al-Bukhārī 1804; Muslim 1979)

and the two together frame what Ghāfir's verse asks for: not tourism but autopsy — a reading of endings. The modern reader has an easier time than the Makkan: a phone holds images of every ruined palace and collapsed bank on earth, and the same fallacy — the inference from scale to legitimacy — is available to be corrected at no cost of travel, if the seeing is a *nudhra* and not a scroll.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:22

> **22** *That was because their messengers used to bring them the clear proofs, but they disbelieved; so God seized them. And He is Strong, terrible in requital.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Causal Sentence That Forecloses Complaint**

*Dhālika bi-anna-hum kānat ta'tīhim rusuluhum bi-l-bayyināti fa-kafarū fa-akhadhahumu Llāh* — the verse repeats 40:21's verdict and now supplies the reason in full. The imperfect *kānat ta'tīhim* is the key to its tone: the messengers *kept coming* to them, repeatedly. This is not a single refusal of a single message; it is a settled way of living with persistent evidence. Al-Ṭabarī notes the past-imperfect as marking continuity, and the same idiom of habitual divine address recurs in the sūrah's own *kānū yufsidūna* (they *used to* corrupt) and *mā kanū yastaṭīʿūna samʿan* (41:22). Ghāfir thus builds its case in a legally fair sequence: the seizure followed proofs, and the proofs were not withdrawn.

**Al-Bayyināt: Evidence as the Unit of Accountability**

*Al-Bayyina* is that which makes its own case clear — from *b-y-n*, to be separate and evident (the *baynūna*, the divorce document, is named for making separation explicit; the *bāʾina* is a divorce that needs no further act). The Qur'an reserves the plural for revelation and its accompanying miracles and applies it at the crucial junctures: *there has come to you a light from God and a clear Book* (5:15); *there has come to you the clear proof* — the same phrase the sūrah uses of the man of Pharaoh's house's argument (40:28, 34, 83). Its function in this verse is epistemological ground-clearing: no punishment in the Qur'an attaches before clarity, because *We never punish until We send a messenger* (17:15), and *when We destroy a town, We first send to its elite, who then corrupt it; so the word comes due upon it and We destroy it entirely* (17:16; cf. 28:59 and 6:131). Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī both add the point that God's seizure was *li-ʿinādi-him baʿda al-bayān* — for their stubbornness after clarity, and they cite for it the sūrah's own later summary: *they were convinced in themselves, but out of wrongdoing and haughtiness they rejected it* (27:14).

**"He Is the Qawiyy, Severe in Requital" — the Attribute Named, Not the Anger**

*Wa-huwa shaʾidu al-ʿiqāb*. Two observations. First, the sūrah uses *qawiyy* (Strong) rather than *ʿazīz* here, and *al-Qawiyy* belongs in the Qur'an to a specific register — it is used of the created as well as the Creator (11:66, 18:83, 54:20, 76:21 — *Dāwūd, the one of [divine] strength*, and 50:8), which is why the classical theologians stress that God's strength is *dhāti* (essential, uncaused) and not acquired, whereas the strongman of 54:20, the wind, is *qawiyy* by commission. Second, *shadīd al-ʿiqāb* returns verbatim from 40:3, bracketing the sūrah's first section with the same name it opened with, and this is one of the structural features the sūrah's later section will make unbearable: the sūrah began with *Ghāfir al-dhanb, Tawwāb, shadīd al-ʿiqāb, Dhū al-ṭawl*; it now repeats the severity alone, without mercy named alongside, because the people under discussion have excluded themselves from the terms in which mercy operates (*tawba* and *ittibāʿ*, 40:7).

That is the doctrinal edge, and it is why the verse is quoted in the *kabāʾir* (major sins) literature. Al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr both record the debate: the Muʿtazilah cite such pairings to argue that God's threatened punishment must be executed, and the Sunni answer is that *shiddat al-ʿiqāb* is real and yet His will may forgive whom He will for whom He wills, on the basis of 4:48 (*God does not forgive that partners be associated with Him, and He forgives what is below that for whom He wills*) and 40:3's own *Ghāfir al-dhanb* — and that the sūrahs naming severity without pardon address only those who persisted until the seal (40:35). Practically, the verse supplies the sūrah's answer to a modern question that arises at every disaster: *is this punishment?* Ghāfir's structure forbids the casual yes (40:4's warning against inferring verdicts from prosperity applies in reverse) and forbids the evasive no (40:22's *fa-akhadhahumu Llāhu bidhunūbihim* is stated as fact for the peoples of revelation). What the chapter permits is the judgment of a case where the evidence was known and refused — and the certainty that no case ends until it reaches that standard.

---
## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:23

> **23** *And indeed We sent Moses with Our signs and a clear authority,*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah Moves From Annals to a Case Study**

The chapter has quoted the nations destroyed (40:5), the verdict on the damned (40:6–20), and the argument from ruins (40:21–22). Now it stops summarizing and tells one story at length: verses 23–50, more than a third of the sūrah, on Moses, Pharaoh, and — uniquely in the Qur'an — on a believer inside Pharaoh's own household. The source commentary notes that Moses is the most frequently named prophet in the Qur'an, and that the accounts of him are given as *ʿibra* (moral example) rather than chronicle (cf. 43:46–56; 44:17–31; 51:38–40; 79:15–25). The reason the sūrah needs a case study is structural: everything asserted about *mukādara* (40:4) and *ḥujja* (40:35, 56) can be proved abstractly, but the fate of a prophet's people in a specific court with a named tyrant, a named minister, and a named dissenter is the kind of evidence a frightened minority can hold on to.

**"With Our Signs" — the Plural of Belonging**

*Wa-laqad ārsalnā Mūsā bi-āyātinā wa-sulṭānin mubīn*. The *bi-* of accompanying, plus the plural *āyāt*, means the mission was not accompanied by one spectacular act but by a package of evidence, and the possessive "Our" marks their source: the signs are God's, not Moses' — a distinction the sūrah will enforce at 40:78 (*it is not for a messenger to bring a sign except by God's leave*). The signs are enumerated elsewhere: *We gave Moses nine clear signs* (17:101), and the list at 20:17–23 (the staff, the white hand), 27:10–12, 28:31–32, and the ten plagues implied at 7:133 (flood, locusts, lice, frogs, blood). Al-Qurṭubī notes that *āya* here carries both senses at once: a mark that compels assent and a lesson that repays reflection — which is why the Pharaohites keep seeing and keep not learning, the sūrah's running irony.

***Sulṭān Mubīn*: a Document, Not a Trick**

A *sulṭān* is authority with the standing to enforce — from *s-l-ṭ*, to be above, hence mastery; in Islamic legal usage *sulṭān* is a warrant or evidence sufficient to compel a judge's ruling. The phrase *bi-sulṭānin mubīn* occurs at 7:71, 10:78, 11:53, 11:96, 16:33, 17:101, 23:45, 26:16, 27:12, 40:23, 40:78, 51:38, 54:42, 67:30, 71:15, 72:25 — always in argumentative contexts, and always about the credentials of a claim. Al-Ṭabarī records the two glosses: the Torah, i.e., a revealed scripture with binding law (Q), and the evident miracle (Ṭs, Z); Ibn Kathīr says both are true, because *mubīn* means "making manifest," and both the Book and the miracle make the sender's claim manifest. The choice of word defeats the accusation that will come in the next verse: a magician impresses, but a *ḥujja* proves, and proof is a public object open to cross-examination. The sūrah is setting up its courtroom, in which a defense counsel will speak for the accused in verses 28–45 and the "judgment" will be the drowning.

**Why 40:23 Verbatim Repeats 11:96**

Verse 23 is almost word-for-word 11:96 (*wa-laqad arsalnā Mūsā bi-āyātinā wa-sulṭānin mubīn*), and the identical wording is one of the Qur'an's pedagogical devices: the Makkan hearer who knows one of the two sūrahs knows the other, and Ghāfir's addition — the story of the court — is the *ziyāda* (addition) on which the chapter teaches. Al-Biqāʿī built his whole science of *Naẓm al-Durar* ("the arrangement of pearls") on precisely this: that each repetition in the Qur'an is placed where a specific structural need requires it. Here the need is to demonstrate a *sulṭān* under examination, and so Ghāfir retells the sending not as rescue narrative (as in Ṭā Sīn and Qaṣaṣ) but as a trial.

**The Verse as a Mirror for the Prophet ﷺ**

Every clause is a compliment and a comfort to Muhammad ﷺ, whose own audience had used the same two objections — *sihr* (40:24) and *kidhb* (40:28): you too were sent *with signs and a clear authority*, namely the Qur'an itself (*this is the clear Book*, and Ghāfir has named it *tanzīl min ʿindi Llāh*, 40:2). The sūrah then lets the Pharaohite verdicts stand as the future tense of the Quraysh's. Al-Rāzī's summary of the section is worth keeping in mind for the next twenty-five verses: *God mentions Moses' story in this sūrah so that the Prophet may know that what he suffers is the way of God with His messengers, and so that the man who concealed faith in the house of tyranny be an argument against those who conceal faith in the house of safety.*

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:24

> **24** *To Pharaoh, Hāmān, and Qārūn; and they said: "A lying sorcerer.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Men Who Represent Every Way of Rejecting Revelation**

*Ila Firʿawna wa-Hāmāna wa-Qārūna*. The triad is a study in the division of power, and the sūrah will show each of them fall separately: Pharaoh by drowning, Hāmān by the tower (28:38–40 — *so We seized him and his soldiers and threw them into the sea*), Qārūn by the earth (28:81 — *We swallowed him and his house into the earth*). Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr transmit from Ibn ʿAbbās and Qatāda that Pharaoh was the king, Hāmān the executor of his will (the one in charge of building, or, as the source puts it, of the quarries and kilns), and Qārūn the treasurer, the wealthy relation who bought the loyalty of the public. Together they are the state, the bureaucracy, and capital. Ghāfir's audience, facing exactly those three pressures in Makkah, is told that all three were on the same side against the same man, and that all three are in the next verse described by the same two words: *sāḥir kadhdhāb*.

**Firʿawn: the Title That Became the Name**

*Firʿawn* is used in the Qur'an seventy-four times, always as the villain, and never once as a personal name — a fact the commentators noticed before Egyptology confirmed it: the Arabs called the king of Egypt by his title, and the title was "the Great House," *per-aa*, from which Greek *Pharaō* and Arabic *Firʿawn* derive. The sūrah is thus more scrupulous than the modern habit of turning the title into a proper noun; the namelessness is part of the point, since the man who declared "I am your lord, most high" (79:24) is denied even an identity in revelation. Al-Qurṭubī records the report that his name in the Torah was *al-Walīd*, and that some said he was the Pharaoh of Joseph and some the Pharaoh of the Exodus; the sūrah itself refuses the distinction, and it is safer to refuse it with the sūrah. The important detail is theological: the sūrah calls him "Pharaoh" as a class — every *firʿawn* who claims divine immunity.

**Hāmān: a Name, a Function, and a Misused Name**

*Hāmān* appears six times in the Qur'an (28:6, 8, 38; 29:39; 40:24, 36) and each time as Pharaoh's henchman — never, at any point, in the story of Moses and Israel in Egypt in any other capacity. The name in the Bible (Esther 3) belongs to Agag the Amalekite, Haman the son of Hammedatha, the minister of Ahasuerus who plots the genocide of Persian Jewry, and it is precisely there that the well-known polemic against the Qur'an locates a "borrowing" (the claim appears in Ignatius of Antioch's circle, in the anti-Qur'anic writings of the Syriac tradition, and in modern orientalist works). The classical Muslim answers are three, and none of them depends on accepting or rejecting the polemic: (1) the two figures may be two individuals with the same name or title, since *Hāmān* was glossed in Arabic lexica as a rank of nobility; (2) the name is attested in the region's records independently of the Book of Esther — the inscription of the temple of Ptah at Memphis has been read as naming a chief of the building works (*Haman*), which is why many modern readers note the title fits an overseer of quarries, exactly as 28:38 and 40:36 require; and (3) the Qur'anic datum is a claim about the Egyptian court, not about the Persian one, and the accusation assumes the Qur'an's audience was reading Esther, when the Talmudic-Purim association with *Agagite* and the "blotting out of Amalek" is a matter of Jewish liturgical life, not of Meccan hearing. The verse's plain function, in any case, is to name the man who will be told to fire the kilns: the bureaucrat who turns a tyrant's fantasy into public works.

**Qārūn: Wealth as a Third Kind of Argument**

*Qārūn* (Korah) is described elsewhere as being *from his people* (28:76) — an Israelite insider, a cousin of Moses according to most reports (al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr), whose keys were "a burden to a company of strong men" (28:76) and whose wealth made him a rival authority to prophecy itself. His famous line, quoted in the sūrah of the Story, is the sūrah's own diagnosis of the disputers: *God has given me this only because of a knowledge I have* (28:78) — the meritocratic apology for inherited advantage, answered there by *does he not know that God destroyed before him generations stronger in power and richer in wealth?* The verse's implication for Ghāfir is exact: the accusation against Moses is not made only by the king; it is made by the state, the media of its public works, and the philanthropist whose piety is his portfolio. The three together give the lie a weight that no single tyrant could.

**The Slander: Two Words That End Arguments for a Living**

*Wa-qālū sāḥirun kadhdhāb*. Sorcery plus lying: the charge is not "he is mistaken" but "he is a professional manipulator," which is the only accusation that excuses a court from examining a *sulṭān mubīn*. The Quraysh deployed the identical formula (10:2; 11:8; 20:69; 21:3; 26:27, 37, 51; 27:13; 34:43; 37:109; 38:4; 43:30; 46:7; 51:52; 52:25; 54:2, 9, 42), and the sūrah of al-Qamar makes the diagnosis a rule: *"they said: an enchanter, a madman."* The two words matter separately. *Sāḥir*, from *s-ḥ-r*, is one who turns the appearance of a thing (*sāḥara al-ard*, the mirage that turns desert into water), so the charge is that the prophet produces a compelling *appearance* with no substance, and the Qur'an's answer in this very sūrah is that Moses came with *bayyināt* and a *sulṭān* — evidence and standing, the two things a magician does not need and cannot produce. *Kadhdhāb* is the intensive form of liar: not one who errs, but one whose speech is constitutively false — and the sūrah will use the same word for the type God does not guide (40:28: *inna Llāha lā yahdī man huwa musrifun kadhdhāb*). Al-Rāzī notes the irony that the two accusations cancel each other, and that the Qur'an records the cancellation elsewhere: *see what similitudes they coin for you; they are astray and cannot find the way* (17:48; cf. 25:9, 38). A man cannot be simultaneously a supernaturally aided deceiver and a raving liar whose claims are baseless, and the Quraysh knew it.

**A Note on the Charge's Afterlife**

The verse is the sūrah's first demonstration that accusation substitutes for argument — and it is worth pausing, because this is the mechanism by which all of Ghāfir's tyrants operate. Pharaoh's court, the Quraysh's *malāʾ*, and every subsequent establishment that meets a reformer with the vocabulary of *sāḥir kadhdhāb* (in our idiom: demagogue, fantasist, foreign agent, madman) are performing the same move: discredit the source so the *sulṭān* never has to be read. The Qur'an's answer is procedural, and the whole of 40:28–45 is an illustration of it: bring the case back to the evidence, and let a single honest voice state it: *he has brought you clear proofs from your Lord.*

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:25

> **25** *So when he brought them the truth from Us, they said: "Kill the sons of those who believed with him, and let the women alive." Their scheming is nothing but in loss.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Insult to Industrial Murder**

*Falammā jāʾahum bi-l-ḥaqqi min ʿindi-nā qālū-qtulū abnāʾa alladhīna āmanū maʿahu wa-istanyū nisāʾahum*. The *fa* after verse 24's accusation marks escalation: the charge of sorcery is followed, when the truth keeps arriving, by a policy. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr explain that this is Pharaoh's second decree against the Israelite boys: the first, before Moses' birth, was to kill them in the hope of preventing the prophesied downfall of his house (28:4); the second is collective punishment for following Moses, and its wording in 7:127 is reported as the response to the courtiers' complaint: *Will you let Moses and his people corrupt in the land and abandon you and your gods? He said: we will kill their sons and keep their women alive, and we are above them, dominant.* The Qur'an thus preserves the internal logic of the state's decision-making: atrocity announced as a security measure.

**Why "the Sons," in the Singular**

The Arabic is *aqtulū abnāʾa* — kill the *sons*, and the sūrah elsewhere uses a strikingly different construction, in the passive, about the same event: *while the sons of those who believed with you are being slaughtered and their women left alive* (7:141). The classical rhetoricians note the Qur'anic habit of using the singular *ibn* collectively for *banūn* — a trick of compression, in which each father's boy is counted separately, so the massacre is rendered as a series of family losses rather than a census. Al-Zamakhsharī, in *al-Kashshāf*, also observes the choice of "the sons of those who believed *with him*," the qualifier tying the death-sentence to the faith rather than to ethnicity — that is, this is not anti-Israelite policy at this point but religious persecution, and the sūrah will make the same charge against the *ashāb al-ukhdūd* at 85:4: *slain were the people of the Ditch, the fire with its fuel, when they sat by it and witnessed what they did to the believers — and they resented nothing but that they believed in God, the Mighty, the Praiseworthy.* Ghāfir's verse is thus a link in a chain of verses that define tyranny as the anger at belief itself.

**"Let the Women Alive": Mercy as a Weapon**

*Wa-staḥyū nisāʾahum*, from *ḥ-y-y*, to keep alive — and al-Ṭabarī and al-Qurṭubī note the intent recorded in the commentaries (Ṭ): they were spared not from pity but for servitude, so that the women would be held, used, and degraded, and the community's structure would be dissolved. This is why 14:6 describes the same act with the word *sūʾ al-ʿadhāb* — "the worst of torment": *and when your Lord announced that He would surely afflict them with the worst of punishment, and that the Fire of your Lord is indeed severe.* A policy designed to end a people's future and humiliate their present is not "merely" violent; it is precisely the erasure of a people's continuity, which is why the Qur'an frames it as the greatest of Pharaoh's crimes in the sūrah that also says *God does not wish wrong for His servants* (40:31) and *He wronged them not, but they were wronging themselves* (14:45).

**How the Verse Ends the Matter: Their Scheme is Waḥīd al-Talāqi**

*Wa-makāru lladhīna kafarū illā fī ḍalāl*. The *makr* of 40:25 is the same *makr* the sūrah attributed to *every community* against its messenger at 40:5, and the verdict is identical in structure to the Qur'an's universal formula, which appears in this very chapter twice: *they schemed, and God schemed, and they did not perceive* (68:27's pattern; cf. *their scheme did not harm you at all*, 4:176's context — actually 3:176's *lan yaḍurrū-kum illā adhan*), and above all: *and Pharaoh's scheme was nothing but in loss* (40:37) — the sūrah's ring-composition. The word *ḍalāl* (error, going astray, loss of the road) is used of *makr* because a plot is a road the plotter walks toward an objective, and the Qur'an's claim is that the road ends nowhere. The grammarians note the *ḥaṣr* in *illā*, which makes the sentence categorical: no variant, no exceptions, no temporary successes included. The promised failure is demonstrated inside the narrative itself: the second killing decree is never reported as having been executed, and the source commentary records al-Qurṭubī's and al-Rāzī's remarks that God turned Pharaoh's attention with the plagues (Q), and that in any case the first decree had failed — the boy meant to be killed grew up in the palace (cf. 28:7, where Pharaoh's household takes in the very child whose death-sentence he signed). Al-Bukhārī (3394) and Muslim (2371) transmit the same point as a warning to tyrants:

> *"The people of Paradise will see the people of the Fire, and a man from the people of Paradise will shine, and he will say: I know him — he is my teacher, and I was only saved from the Fire because of him; and he will mention a Jew or a Christian he had wronged, or a person whose death he had hastened..."*

and the more exact text is the famous tradition in Muslim (2578) from Abū Hurayra about the one who wrongs people — with the *qissāṣ* settled on a Day on which no scheme avails.

**The Structural Turn: Why Verse 28 Follows Verse 25**

A court that votes for genocide is a court with a conscience problem, and the sūrah supplies it. Within three verses an insider will object, and the objection's opening words are a question of law: *a-taqtulūna rajulan an yaqūla rabbiya Llāh* — "will you kill a man for saying 'my Lord is God'?" (40:28). The verse's placement is therefore itself an argument about tyranny: an extermination decree requires unanimous compliance, and it is broken by one man who had access to the room. Ghāfir is teaching that the decisive variable in the history of evil is not the number of its victims but the number of people inside the room who refuse.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:26

> **26** *Pharaoh said: "Leave me, I will kill Moses; let him call upon his Lord. Truly I fear that he will change your religion or bring corruption in the land.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Leave Me": a King Who Must Ask Permission**

*Wa-qāla Firʿawnu dharūnī aqtul Mūsā wa-li-yadʿu rabbah*. Pharaoh — the man who claimed divinity (79:24) — speaks in the register of a petitioner. Al-Ṭabarī records from Ibn ʿAbbās and the *salaf* that Pharaoh had resolved to kill Moses and his courtiers restrained him, and that this verse is his appeal to them; the same tradition explains 28:35's *they will not believe in me* and 43:51's *the people obeyed him* — the king was powerful because the room obeyed.
