So the sūrah quietly dismantles the myth of the omnipotent tyrant before it narrates his end, and simultaneously sets up the believing man of 40:28: the court was already arguing with him, and one dissenting voice had a real function. This is one of the Qur'an's most instructive ironies, and the exegetes note it: Pharaoh's line *wa-li-yadʿu rabbah* — "let him call on his Lord!" — is a taunt aimed at a god who has not answered; and the sūrah will answer him at 40:50 with the keepers of the Fire saying *then call upon your Lord*, in the very vocabulary of his mockery, and will close the whole scene at 40:14 with the imperative *fa-udʿū Llāh* ("so call upon God"). The verse thus becomes the chapter's hinge on the theme of *duʿāʾ*: Pharaoh dares the prophet to call, and the sūrah tells the reader to do exactly that.

**The Fear Beneath the Defiance**

*Innī akhāfu an yubaddila dīnakum aw an yuẓhira fī al-arḍi al-fasād* — I fear he will change your religion, or spread *fasād* in the land. Grammarians note *an yubaddila* and *an yuẓhira* after *akhdha*, and the classical readings dispute whether *aw* here is disjunctive or epistemic ("either... or" / "or rather"), but the interpretive payoff is the same: Pharaoh's fear is not that Moses is wrong, it is that Moses is effective. Two fears, one political. (1) *Changing your religion*: al-Jubbāʾī and al-Ṭabarī both point out that the religion being changed is the worship of Pharaoh himself — the state cult; hence the accusation in 7:127, *you will abandon you and your gods*. A prophet who says "God alone" is not introducing a sect; he is dissolving the legitimacy on which the throne sits. (2) *Fasād in the land*: the standard rhetorical weapon of the establishment, and the Qur'an exposes the double standard in one sentence — *when it is said to them, "do not spread corruption on the earth," they say, "only we are the reformers"* (2:11), and *do not spread corruption after its reform* (7:56), and *the land belongs to God; He gives it to whom He will of His servants; and the end is for God-consciousness* (7:128). Al-Rāzī's note is that the same act — abolishing a cult and redistributing dignity — is called *iṣlāḥ* by its beneficiaries and *fasād* by its losers, so that the word is a confession rather than an argument. Ghāfir will make this explicit in the mouth of the believers of Noah's people and in the phrase *the scheming of Pharaoh* being *fasād* and *ḍalāl* (40:25, 37), i.e., the sūrah's own vocabulary reverses the charge, and does so in the same passage that quotes it.

**The Verdict Delivered in Advance**

The Makkan audience is hearing the speech of the man whose ending they already know from 40:24–25 and will hear in 40:37 and 40:46: a tyrant announcing his intention to do what he will not be allowed to complete. Pharaoh says "leave me, I will kill Moses," and the sūrah never reports the killing, because it never happened — the drowning happened, to the wrong people. That gap between proclamation and execution is the sūrah's answer to every intimidation experienced by its first hearers, and it is the same logic as the hadith qudsī in al-Bukhārī (6073) and Muslim (2637), which the sūrah's own theology of divine response makes a commentary on this verse:

> *"Whoever shows enmity to a friend of Mine, I have been notified of war against him; and My servant draws near to Me with nothing I love more than what I have obligated upon him; and My servant keeps drawing near to Me with supererogatory works until I love him; and when I love him, I am his hearing with which he hears, his sight with which he sees, his hand with which he grasps, and his foot with which he walks."*

The prophet whom Pharaoh "fears" is the one whose hearing, sight, hand, and foot are, in that telling, God's — which is what the drowning of 40:45–46 proves and what the shielding of the believing man (40:45) proves again.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:27

> **27** *Moses said: "I have sought refuge in my Lord and your Lord, from everyone who does not believe in the Day of Reckoning.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Theology as Self-Defence**

*Qāla Mūsā innī ʿudhtu bi-rabbi wa-rabbikum min kulli mutakabbirin lā yu'minu bi-yawmi al-ḥisāb*. Moses does not answer Pharaoh's claim with a counter-claim of power, nor does he argue about the merits of the religion change. He performs *ʿudhtha*, and then he defines the terms on which the contest is decided. The verb *iʿtādha*, from *ʿ-dh-w*, is to flee for refuge to something that shields — the root gives *maʿādh*, the place of refuge, and the sūrah will use the same root at 40:56: *so seek refuge in God* (fa-ʿūdhu bi-Llāhi), addressed to the Prophet. The echo is structural: Moses' act in the court is the act commanded to Muhammad ﷺ in the same chapter, which is why the sūrah's long narrative is not decoration but instruction.

**"My Lord and Your Lord": a Concession Built Into the Address**

The most quoted detail of this verse in the classical tafsīrs is the phrase *bi-rabbi wa-rabbikum*. Moses does not say "my Lord, whom you reject"; he says *the one who is my Lord and your Lord*, conceding a fact Pharaoh cannot deny even while worshipping another: the one sustaining Pharaoh's life, breath, kingdom, and body is not Pharaoh. Al-Rāzī treats this as a model of argumentative method — begin from a premise the opponent cannot refuse — and cites Pharaoh's own concession in 7:137 and 26:29, and in 43:88: *if you asked them who created the heavens and the earth, they would say, "the All-Knowing created them."* The same strategy recurs at the sūrah's end (40:62, 64, 68) where God is named *rabbikum*, "your Lord," while He is being proven God. That is why the sūrah's theological argument is not a demand for submission but an appeal to shared ontology: the tyrant's refuge is the very God he denies.

**From "the Arrogant" to "the Arrogant Who Does Not Believe in the Day of Reckoning"**

The *mutakabbir* is *faʿūl* from *takabbur*, and lexicographers (al-Azharī, Ibn Fāris, and in the tafsīr here al-Qurṭubī) derive it from *k-b-r*: to consider a thing *kabīr* (great) as befitting oneself, or to place oneself above one's true size. The hadith fixes the definition with precision:

> *"Pride is rejecting the truth and despising people."* (Muslim 91, from ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd)

And Moses' qualifier joins that definition to the *Day of Reckoning*: an arrogance that believes in the reckoning would be a contradiction — a man who expects to answer for everything cannot habitually despise anyone or reject anything outright. The pairing is the sūrah's anthropology: *takabbur* and denial of *al-ḥisāb* are one disease, and Ghāfir will drive it home in 40:60 (*those too proud to worship Me*), 40:75–76 (exultation and *fuḥūr* and the abode of the arrogant), and in its own final scene, where the arrogant's last position is to argue in the Fire (40:47–48) and the weak's last word is a complaint about having followed them.

**The Object of the Refuge: Who Is Being Fled From?**

Moses seeks refuge from *everyone* who is arrogant and does not believe in the Day — not from Pharaoh specifically. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr note the generality as deliberate: the prophet is teaching a rule for all times, since a tyrant who believes himself exempt from reckoning is a species, and the refuge is not fitted to the individual case but to the type. This is also why the sūrah's audience is meant to imitate the sentence rather than only to admire it: the prophetic *ʿiyāḏa* formulae — *aʿūdhu bi-kalimāti Llāhi al-tāmmāti min sharri mā khalaq* (Muslim 2708, from Khudayr, and the three *qul* surahs which the Prophet ﷺ told ʿUqba ibn ʿĀmir to recite for refuge, al-Tirmidhī 2902, Abū Dāwūd 5082 and 1524) — are precisely Ghāfir's verse institutionalized, and ʿUqba's report includes the warning that reciting them is the believer's equivalent of mounting a horse in battle.

**Why Belief in the Day Does the Real Work**

Consider the practical logic of the verse, which is the logic of the whole sūrah: Moses, unarmed, threatened, and about to be killed by the strongest state on earth, is composed because the only court that matters is on the other side of death. This is not escapism in the psychological sense; it is a reallocation of stakes, and it explains the sūrah's persistent pairing of *takabbur* and denial of resurrection (40:12, 58–59, 82–85). The man who has no reckoning ahead of him has no limit in front of him, and Pharaoh is the proof: the sentence "no protector from God" (40:21) is the only sentence that could stop him, and the sūrah has already said it out loud.

---
## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:28

> **28** *And a believing man from the household of Pharaoh, concealing his faith, said: "Will you kill a man because he says, 'My Lord is God' — while he has brought you clear proofs from your Lord? If he is a liar, his lie is against himself; and if he is truthful, some of what he promises you will fall upon you. Truly God does not guide one who is a prodigal liar.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Most Expensive Sentence Spoken in This Chapter**

*Wa-qāla rajulun mu'minun min āli Firʿawna yaktumuīmānahu*. The sūrah has given us the court's accusation (40:24), the court's murder-decree (40:25), and the king's threat (40:26). Into that sequence steps one man, and the Qur'an introduces him with three facts that do all the work: he is *mu'min* (a believer), he is *min āli Firʿawn* (of Pharaoh's own household), and he *yaktumuīmānahu* (concealing his faith). He is not a hostage, not a secret agent, not a prophet: he is a courtier with a security clearance and a conscience, and what follows is eighteen verses (40:28–45) of the most carefully structured piece of advocacy in the Qur'an. The chapter's third name, *al-Mu'min*, is attached to him — the sūrah is named after a God who forgives and after a man who spoke.

The identity of the man is not given in revelation, and the classical commentators are candid about it. Al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, and Ibn ʿAbbās' reports (IJ, IK, Q, Ṭ) preserve two positions: he was a kinsman or cousin of Pharaoh, or he was an Israelite whose belief was concealed *from* Pharaoh's house, in which case *min āli Firʿawn* is read as a descriptor of his standing among them rather than his lineage. Al-Ṭabarī argues for the first, on grounds of political realism: *if he had been an Israelite, no one in the court would have listened to him.* The identification with the man who runs to warn Moses in 28:20 — *and a man came from the farthest part of the city, running; he said: Moses, the notables are consulting about you to kill you, so leave; I am one of your sincere advisers* — is the strong consensus (IJ, IK), and if it is correct, the sūrah has given us two portraits of the same person: here the advocate in the chamber, there the fugitive who pays for it.

**"Concealing His Faith": the Ethics of *Kitmān***

*Yaktumu*, from *k-t-m*, is literally to bury (the *katm* is a buried water-trough in the desert, which is why the Arabs called the night *kātim*, concealers of colors), and the verbal noun *kitmān* appears in the Qur'an in its most damning use: *those who conceal what We sent down of the clear proofs and the guidance... those will eat Fire into their bellies* (2:174, cf. 2:159; 5:15). And yet here the same root describes a virtue-in-waiting. The resolution the commentators give is that concealment is blameworthy when the truth is entrusted to be conveyed and silence protects the concealer; it is permissible, or obligatory, when speaking only destroys the speaker without benefiting the truth, and it is *supererogatory and heroic* when speaking costs the speaker everything and serves the truth — which is precisely Pharaoh's court. Al-Qurṭubī notes this as one of the sūrah's most important legal lessons: the man concealed for years, and the concealment did not harm his faith; what mattered was the day he used it. On that occasion the prophetic standard applies:

> *"The best struggle is a word of truth before a tyrant."* (Abū Dāwūd 4344; al-Tirmidhī 2174, from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī; graded by many as sound in meaning)

and its complement, the hadith that makes the cost predictable: *"the nearest of people to God's dwelling on the Day of Resurrection are those who reconcile people and who make themselves reconciled"* (al-Tirmidhī 2507). What the sūrah wants its hearers to see, however, is the *risk*: Makkah had no Pharaoh, but it had a *malāʾ*, and Ghāfir's point is that the man who speaks once, when speech can stop a killing, has out-argued an empire with a conditional sentence.

**The Argument: Four Moves, No Theology**

The speech is a lawyer's brief, not a sermon. (1) *Procedural objection*: you are executing a man for a proposition — *an yaqūla rabbiya Llāh* — for saying "my Lord is God." (2) *Evidentiary objection*: he is not merely asserting; *qad jāʾakum bi-l-bayyināti min rabbikum* — he has brought you clear proofs from *your* Lord, a phrase that concedes nothing while making the claim about their own God. (3) *The asymmetry*: if he lies, he harms only himself; if he is truthful, *some* — not all, not even the majority, which is the language of a cautious man speaking to men who do not believe him — of what he promises will fall on you. This is Pascal-like reasoning in a desert courtroom, and it was framed by Ibn ʿAṭiyyah as the sūrah's own demonstration that God gives clarity to whomever He wills without prophethood: the reasoning is available to any honest mind that has not yet been sealed (40:35). (4) *The pastoral closer*: God does not guide a prodigal liar — that is, the possibility that Moses is a fraud is closed off by the moral evidence of his life, and by the principle that God does not entrust His guidance to liars.

The source commentary notes that the question "will you kill a man for saying 'my Lord is God'?" echoes the sorcerers' protest at 7:126 (*you take revenge upon us only because we believed the signs of our Lord when they came to us*) and 85:9–10 (*they resented nothing except that they believed in God*), and 5:59, where the same complaint is turned against the People of the Book. The sūrah thus universalizes the indictment: the crime is always "saying."

**The Fallacy the Verse Exposes**

Pharaoh's court has adopted an invalid rule of evidence: the claimant must be eliminated before being examined. The believing man's syllogism is designed to show that the rule does not even serve their interests. Al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr both treat the passage as the Qur'an's model for arguing *within* the opponent's premises — the man says nothing of Moses' prophethood as a fact he expects them to accept; he argues that even on their own worst-case assumption, the policy is stupid. This is why 16:125 (argue in the best manner) and 29:46 belong to the discussion, and why the sūrah of Ghāfir's disputers (40:4, 35, 56, 69) can be read as a commentary on the difference between the man who asks "what if he is lying?" and the men who answer "kill him anyway."

**Musrif Kadhdhāb: the Type God Does Not Guide**

*Inna Llāha lā yahdī man huwa musrifun kadhdhāb*. The two words are chosen with surgical care. *Musrif*, from *a-s-r-f*, is the active participle of *asrafa*, to pass the limit — and the root is literal: *saraf* is the bank of a river, and *isrāf* is going past the bank into the water: excess in anything (cf. 5:32 and 6:141 — *and do not be excessive; He does not love the excessive* — with 7:31, 17:26, 20:81, 25:67). Al-Ṭabarī, Qatāda, and Mujāhid gloss it here as *mushrik* (Ṭ) — one who crosses the limit of tawḥīd — or as the one who transgresses God's limits by lying about God (Ṭs). *Kadhdhāb*, the intensive of liar, is the same epithet Pharaoh's court applied to Moses (40:24), which is the sūrah's masterpiece of irony: the word for a liar, applied by liars, to the one who speaks the truth. The prophetic definition of the type appears in the hadith of the hypocrite's three marks: *"when he speaks, he lies; when he promises, he breaks; when he is trusted, he betrays"* (al-Bukhārī 33; Muslim 59) — and the sūrah has already told us the eye's treachery is known to God (40:19). Al-Rāzī's question — why does God not guide such a man? — is answered by the same tradition that says God does not wrong anyone: because guidance to a man whose entire mode is to exceed limits and to misrepresent is a contradiction in terms; he is not being denied a light he is trying to see by, he is refusing to open eyes that are, in the sūrah's image, guilty of betrayal.

**The Verse as the Chapter's Ethical Center**

Read against 40:3, the man's speech is the practical form of the sūrah's creed: the God whose *maqt* is real and whose forgiveness is real, so that "some of what he promises will fall on you" is not a bluff but an inference from who God is. Read against 40:4, he is the refutation of the disputers' monopoly on argument: the sūrah said only disbelievers dispute the signs; here a believer does not dispute but *reasons*, and the difference is the difference between a man trying to win and a man trying to save.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:29

> **29** *O my people! Yours is the kingdom this day, ruling in the land — but who will help us from God's punishment if it comes upon us?" Pharaoh said: "I only show you what I see, and I only guide you to the path of right conduct.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Flattery That Is Actually a Threat**

*Yā qawmi lakumu al-mulku al-yawma ẓāhirīna fī al-arḍi faman yaṣranunā min baʾsi Llāhi in jāʾanā*. The first half sounds like praise, and commentators differ about whether it is meant as such: al-Zamakhsharī reads it as *reminding them of the blessings they enjoy* (IK, Ṭs) — you hold the world today, so you have every reason to be cautious, not desperate; Ibn Kathīr and others read it as a concession, deliberately stating their position at its strongest so that the following question cannot be dodged. Both readings yield the same rhetoric: the man grants them *everything* — kingdom, dominance, the present moment — and then asks one question about a thing they cannot legislate. The contrastive structure is the argument: the first clause is a statement about their power, the second about the limit of power, and the *fa* between them is the whole logic.

**"This Day" — the Grammar of a Condensed Boast**

*Lakumu al-mulku al-yawma*: the pronoun of possession plus the adverb "today," and both are load-bearing. Al-Rāzī notes that a present-tense sentence with *al-yawm* in this position is a *jumla ismiyya* of immediacy and a limitation at once: the kingdom is *yours* (not his, not God's), *today* (not tomorrow). The same word *al-yawm* occurs in the sūrah's own verse 16, on the lips of God in the plain of the Gathering: *li-man al-mulku al-yawm?* — and the correspondence is not accidental. The believing man is, in effect, quoting the Day of Judgment at Pharaoh inside the council: your "today" will end with the only question that matters. Egypt's *arḍ* (the land, i.e., the Nile valley, and by extension the known world of its civilization) is described as *ẓāhirīna*, outwardly dominant, from *ẓ-h-r*, to be on top, visible — the same root as *ẓahr* (back) and *manẓar* (appearance): the sūrah uses a word that says "on the surface, in public view" for a power whose interior is already forfeit.

**Faman Yaṣrununā: the Question That Cannot Be Answered**

*Mafan yaṣrununā min baʾsi Llāhi in jāʾanā* — who will defend us from God's might if it comes upon us? The man says *us*, not *you*: he places himself inside the danger he is predicting, which is the act that distinguishes his speech from mere counsel, and the sūrah makes the first-person plural a rhetorical instrument. *Baʾs* is from *b-ʾ-s*, severity and hardship, and its use for divine punishment is common (2:26, 9:48, 12:87, 40:45); the sūrah's own vocabulary pairs it with *waqʿ* (falling upon, 40:6). Al-Ṭabarī stresses the conditional's form: *in jāʾanā* — "if it comes" — not "when it comes," because he is arguing with men who demand certainty of the unseen, and the conditional is the strongest form of argument available in the court: it concedes their skepticism and shows that it is irrelevant. If there is a 1% probability that this man from Midian is speaking for the God who made the heavens, everything they have built is a countdown. That is exactly the asymmetry, restated, and the sūrah will repeat it in Noah's own words to his people at 71:13 — *what is the matter with you that you do not expect from God's grandeur?*

**Pharaoh's Reply: the Refusal to Argue, in Two Sentences**

*Qāla Firʿawnu mā urīkum illā mā arā wa-lā ahdīkum illā sabīla al-rashād*. Al-Rāzī's observation is the sharpest: Pharaoh does not once engage the content of the argument. He does not say the proofs are insufficient, that Moses was refuted, or that the conditional reasoning is fallacious. He says: *this is what I see, and this is what I command.* Two facts about himself are offered in place of two premises about the world, which is what the sūrah's whole vocabulary of *ḥujja*, *sulṭān*, *bayyina*, and *burhān* exists to expose. Ibn Kathīr notes the plain lie in the first clause, on the basis of *they rejected them, while their own souls were convinced of them, out of wrongdoing and haughtiness* (27:14), and he cites the parallel at 6:124 and 27:13. The second clause — "I guide you only to the path of right conduct" — is even more damning, because it is the same sentence the believing man will use at 40:38 (*I will guide you to the path of right conduct*). Ibn Kathīr's comment is that Ghāfir is setting two claimants to guidance side by side and letting the narrative decide, and the reader is meant to recall the sūrah's own criterion: *whom God guides, he is the one who is rightly guided, and whom He leaves astray, for them you will find no supporting allies* (18:17), and *is one who walks face-down better, or one who walks upright on a path?* (67:22).

**Sabīl al-Rashād: What the Word Means in a Tyrant's Mouth**

*Rashād*, from *r-sh-d*, is the direction of a thing that hits its mark — hence *rushd* as opposed to *ghayy* (18:13 — *their Lord increased them in guidance*, and 18:10 for the opposite pairing), and *irshād* as showing a road. To call one's own policy "the path of *rashād*" is what every government has always done (cf. 28:38's "I do not show you what I see"; and Pharaoh's claim of "the way of right guidance" is answered by the Qur'an's verdict in 11:97: *they followed the command of Pharaoh, and the command of Pharaoh was not right guidance*, and by the sūrah's own later judgment at 20:79 — *Pharaoh led his people astray and did not guide them*). Al-Ṭabarsī in *Maʿānī al-Akhbār* and Ibn al-Qayyim in *al-Ṭuruq al-Ḥukmiyya* both treat this verse as the scriptural datum for a political rule: a ruler's "vision" (*mā arā*) is not a *ḥujja*, and the word *rashād* in the mouth of authority, untested by evidence, is a claim of leadership without knowledge. The Qur'an's standard, in the same sūrah that narrates the appointment of Ṭalūt, is exactly the opposite: *God has chosen him over you and increased him in knowledge and physical stature* (2:247).

**A Last Note on the Man's Fear for Them**

He says *yaṣrununā* — "help *us*." Al-Qurṭubī notes that a man who has concealed his faith for years, and who now stands in the palace of the man who has just signed his people's death warrant, is calling their catastrophe *his* catastrophe. This is the ethic of Ghāfir's political theology in a single pronoun: the believer in the corrupt state does not announce his separateness as a boast; he says *us*, and the "us" is his leverage and his risk both.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:30

> **30** *And the one who believed said: "O my people! Truly I fear for you a day like the Day of the Allied Parties — the like of the people of Noah, ʿĀd, Thamūd, and those after them. And God does not wish wrong for His servants.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Warning of a Non-Prophet**

*Wa-qāla alladhī āmana yā qawmi innī akhāfu ʿalaykum mithla yawmi al-ʾaḥzāb*. The sūrah has switched from describing him ("the one concealing his faith," 40:28) to naming his function: *alladhī āmana*, "the one who believed," and he now speaks in his own voice for fifteen verses. Al-Qurṭubī and other commentators pause over the striking fact noted by the source: this is a full prophetic-style warning (*indhār*) delivered by a private believer. Two lessons are drawn. (1) The office of the warner is not confined to prophets in the sense of moral responsibility — *why do not from each group of them a faction issue forth to acquire knowledge of the religion and to warn their people when they return to them, that they may beware?* (9:122), and *let there be from you a community calling to good, commanding the right and forbidding the wrong* (3:104). (2) The content of true warning is accessible: the man knows the same archive (Noah, ʿĀd, Thamūd) and draws the same inference, because the *āyāt* of 40:4 include history as scripture. That is also why the section's earlier verses (40:21–22) placed "travel and look" before this story — the sūrah has been teaching the reader to reason exactly as the courtier now does.

**"A Day Like the Day of the Allied Parties": Retribution by Pattern**

*Yawm al-ʾaḥzāb* is a *day* rather than a punishment because the man wants them to imagine a date, not a threat: the day that belongs to every party, *mithl* being the operative word — the Qur'an's repeated claim is not that Egypt will be flooded but that the *shape* of the outcome is replicable (*so let not their coming and go delude you*, 40:4; *thus did the word of your Lord come due*, 40:6; *like the plight of your people and like those who were before them*, 50:14). The same idiom is used in Qāf to the Quraysh directly (50:13–14, 18) and in 54 for the four destroyed groups, each ending with *fa-kayfa kāna ʿiqāb wa-nudhur* — so how were My punishment and My warnings! — which is the refrain the believing man's words will quote at 40:83.

**The Archive Named: Noah, ʿĀd, Thamūd, "and Those After Them"**

He cites three, then adds an open-ended fourth clause. Noah's people (see 40:5 and 71:1–28: drowned, and their destruction is the Qur'an's paradigm for a warning ignored over centuries — *he kept calling them night and day, and his calling only increased them in flight*, 71:5–6). ʿĀd, the people of al-Ḥijr's region and of "Iram of the pillars, the like of which has not been created in the lands" (89:7–8, 69:7 — a wind for seven nights and eight days, *so you would see the people therein as if they were hollow trunks of palm trees*). Thamūd, the stone-cutters of Madyan-adjacent northwestern Arabia (7:73–79; 11:61–68; 54:31 — *a single blast*, and 91:13–14 — *so their Lord leveled the earth with a punishment for their sin, and leveled it equally*), whose fate the Prophet ﷺ passed through directly at al-Ḥijr during the Tabūk campaign, entering only with heads lowered (al-Bukhārī 3379–3381: *do not enter these people who were punished, unless with weeping* — and if they do not weep, do not enter, so that what afflicted them will not afflict you). "And those after them" — *wa-lladhīna min baʿdihim* — extends to the settled, named ones elsewhere (Sodom 7:80–84, Madyan 11:84–95, the Sabbath-breakers 2:65, the Companions of the Ravine 85:4–8). The open ending is the man's argumentative strength: no court can find an exception in the list, because the list is not closed.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:31
> **31** *O my people! Truly I fear for you the Day of Mutual Calling.*

**"God Does Not Wish Wrong for His Servants" — the Creed in a Courtroom**

*Wa-mā Allāhu yurīdu ẓulman li-l-ʿibād*. The clause is the sūrah's statement of divine justice inserted exactly where a human being is asking for mercy in advance, and its placement does three jobs at once. First, it says: if you are destroyed, it will not be because God wished to harm you — the destruction will be *your* act read at scale (cf. *God does not wrong people in the least*, 10:44; *He wronged them not, but they were wronging themselves*, 14:45). Second, it is the classic proof-text in the debate over divine justice: the Muʿtazilah cite it for the position that God neither does nor commands *qubḥ* (ugliness/injustice) and that reward and punishment are rationally obligatory upon Him in the sense of His own wisdom and promise; the Ashʿarīs answer that *ẓulm* is definitionally placing a thing in a place that is not its own, which requires an owner's claim that God has none of — *He is not asked about what He does, but they will be asked* (21:23) — while still affirming that God never acts unjustly, as this verse states in its own terms (cf. al-Juwaynī's *al-Irshād*, al-Ghazālī's *al-Iqtiṣād fi al-Iʿtiqād*, and Ibn Taymiyyah's *Darʾ Taʿāruḍ al-ʿAql wa-l-Naql*, which devotes pages to showing that "God does not wish ẓulm" and "nothing happens outside His will" are both true because His will is never *for* ẓulm as such). Third — and this is what a frightened believer in a tyrant's court needs — it removes the arbitrary-deity fear: the One who is *Qahhār* (40:16) does not *want* to hurt you. Al-Qurṭubī's summary of the exegetical choice: God's punishment here is called a *ẓulm*-free act precisely because it is the *ʿāqiba* (consequence) of their own deed (40:21), and the two together — *He does not wish wrong* and *He seized them for their sins* — are the sūrah's entire theodicy in two lines, first stated by a courtier, then by God.

**The Rhetoric of "O My People" Three Times Over**

The man's speech will open with *yā qawmi* at 40:29, 40:30 (in 40:32), 40:39, and 40:41, echoing Noah's own refrain in the sūrah named after him (*yā qawmi* seven times, 71:2–28) and the Prophet's own grief-language in 4:176's register. Al-Rāzī observes that a warner who calls his audience "my people" cannot be accused of foreign malice; and in a court where the accuser is a foreigner (Moses, from Midian, 28:20's "a man came from the farthest part of the city"), the insider's use of kinship vocabulary is the sūrah's quiet lesson in how the truth gets a hearing. Ibn Kathīr notes too that these words make the man's motive clear — he is not arguing for Moses, he is *afraid for Pharaoh* — and that a warning delivered as fear for the hearer is the only form of admonition that has ever survived a court transcript.

**The Source Commentary's Note on "God Does Not Desire Wrong"**

The closing clause — *wa-llāhu lā yurīdu ẓulman li-l-ʿabād* — is cited by the source commentary in the same breath as 40:31's own clause, and it deserves to be lingered over, because the sūrah's phrasing is *lā yurīd*, not *lā yafʿal*: God does not *want* wrong for anyone, and this is the classical ground for the rule that evil is *makhluq* as an event but never *murād* as a pleasure. The two clauses together (40:17's *lā ẓulma al-yawm* and 40:31's *lā yurīd al-ẓulm*) are the sūrah's answer to every objection that begins "but then why did He create them," and they are placed in the mouth of the weakest actor in the story: not a prophet, but a frightened man speaking to a king.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:32

> **32** *O my people! Truly I fear for you the Day of Mutual Calling.*

**A Name for the Day Invented by Its Own Events**

*Wa-yā qawmi innī akhāfu ʿalaykum yawma al-nadā*. The Day is named not for its terror but for its *telephone*: *al-nadā*, from *n-d-w*, the calling that another hears — and the *mutual* form is derived not by morphology but by the verse's own quotation of the scene elsewhere: *the inhabitants of the Garden will call out to the inhabitants of the Fire: we have found what our Lord promised us to be true; have you found what your Lord promised true? They will say: yes* (7:44), and *the inhabitants of the Fire will call to the Garden: pour on us some water, or what God has provided you; they will say: God has forbidden it to the disbelievers* (7:50). Al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Rāzī list the exchanges the name covers: the damned crying to the saved; the hypocrites crying to the believers — *turn back, that we may take of your light; it will be said: go back and look for a light, and a wall will be set between them with a gate; inside it is mercy and outside it is punishment* (57:13); the believers crying to God, the angels crying to the Garden's people (*this is the Garden, which you inherited for what you used to do*, 7:43), and the crying of each soul to its own record. The name is therefore a summary of a single fact: on that Day, everyone will need someone, and the call will either be answered or will die in the air.

**Why Calling Is the Right Verb for Hope**

*Nidāʾ* in the Qur'an is the language of dependence. It is used for the call of the master to a servant, of a herdsman to his flock, of a people to their king — and of God to His servants, in both directions. The verse's force here comes from an irony the sūrah builds: the Egyptians had a whole civilization of *nidaʾ* — the heralds, the proclamations, the shouts before Pharaoh's palanquin (43:57 — *if it were not for a word that preceded from your Lord, and a term already decreed, it would have come due upon them*). The believing man tells them that the only call that will matter is the one whose answer is not controlled by the one you pay. The classical preachers used the verse to argue that prayer is the rehearsal for that day: the man who has trained himself to call upon God (40:14) is not learning to shout, but learning the grammar of dependence he will need when he is shouting.

**The Day of Names**

The Qur'an's names for the Last Day are a theology in miniature, and each one is placed where the argument needs it: *yawm al-dīn* (the Day of reckoning and repayment, 1:4; 82:17–19), *al-qāriʿa* (the Striking, 101:1–3), *al-ṣākkha* (the Deafening Blast, 79:34), *al-ḥāqqa* (the Reality, 69:1–3), *yawm al-faṣl* (the Day of Sorting, 37:21; 44:40; 77:13, 14, 38, 46, 49), *yawm al-ḥisāb* (14:41, 38:16, 40:27), *yawm al-talāq* (40:15, 37:20), *yawm al-ʿazīz* — the Day of the Mighty, actually *yawm al-ʿazīz* at 14:31 and 40:9's "the Day," and here *yawm al-nadā*. Al-Suyūṭī collects these in *al-Itqān* and notes that the multiplicity is a mercy: the Day cannot be captured by one metaphor because it is an occasion, not an event. Al-Rāzī's observation is more pointed: the sūrah has already used *yawm al-ḥisāb* (40:27, on Moses' lips) and *yawm al-nadā* here (on the believer's lips), so the two speakers of the chapter, prophet and secret believer, describe the same Day as *accounting* and as *calling* — because to the one who has done the accounting there is *ḥisāb*, and to the one who has been waiting for rescue all along there is *nidāʾ*. The Day's objectivity is unchanged; the Day's face depends on the direction from which you hear it.

**What Fear for Others Does**

Note that the man continues to say *ʿalaykum* — "I fear *for you*." He has just lost his standing (40:29), and by 40:44 he will know it; yet the middle of his speech is a man worrying about the safety of the people who are arranging to kill him. The verse's ethics are embedded in its grammar, and this is the point the tradition draws from the parallel of Lūṭ's and Nūḥ's *innī lakum nasīḥun amīn* (7:68, 68; cf. 26:107 — *I am for you a trustworthy adviser*): the warner's fear is for the warned. Al-Qurṭubī notes that a warning that does not care whether it is heeded is not a warning but a threat; the believing man is the sūrah's demonstration of what *naṣīḥa* looks like when it costs the adviser his career.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:33

> **33** *The Day you will turn your backs, fleeing, with no protector against God. Whoever God leaves astray, he has no guide.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Flight That Has Nowhere to Go**

*Yawma tawallawna mudbirīna lā tajūnna min Llāhi min ḥāfid*. The verb *tawallawna*, "you will turn away," from *w-l-y* — the root of *walī*, nearness and patronage — is thus used for the exact opposite: the withdrawal of one's face from a person who has a claim on you. In the Qur'an *tawallī* is the verb of betrayal by evasion: *what is the matter with you that when it is said to you "march in God's cause" you sink heavily to the earth? Do you prefer the life of this world to the Hereafter? The enjoyment of this world beside the Hereafter is little. Except you turn away, God will punish you with a painful punishment and replace you with another people, and you cannot harm Him at all* (9:38–39). Here *mudbirīna*, from *d-b-r*, "with the back," turns the flight into a picture: they turn their backs to God, which in the sūrah's own idiom is what Pharaoh did to Moses' back — and *ʿāqiba* (the end, 40:21) shares the same root: what comes *after* you, always, whether or not you turned to face it. The three roots (*w-l-y*, *d-b-r*, *ʿ-q-b*) occur within twelve verses of one another and say the same thing in three ways: turning away does not change what follows.

**Lā Ḥāfid: the Protector Who Cannot Be Hired**

*Ḥāfid*, from *ḥ-f-d*, is the one who runs around a person to defend and care for him — in the pre-Islamic and early Islamic vocabulary, the retainer whose whole function is to encircle his lord with protection; the same root gives *iḥtifāl* (to be eager for someone, to fuss over them). Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr gloss it here exactly as the Egyptians would have understood it: *no one who encircles you with protection; no one who can be counted among your dependents; no one who owes you.* The word's choice is brilliant in the mouth of a courtier: he is telling his own class that the entire economy of obligation and patronage on which their lives run — clients, favors, kin, bribes, guards — has no counterpart on that side. It is Ghāfir's answer to the *khalīl* of 40:18 and to the *walī* of 40:44. The verse's *ʿalaykum* becomes *lā tajūnna*: the "you" that the man kept addressing has become an object nobody can claim.

**The Second Half: Guidance as Something Given, Not Something Achieved**

*Wa-man yuḍlilli Llāhu famā lahu min hād*. The clause has been read in the passage's context by the Muʿtazilah as descriptive of what God does to a people who have persisted, and by the traditionalists as a statement that guidance is an act of God without which no guide can help. Al-Ṭabarī's interpretation threads them: the misguidance here is God's leaving the persistently unwilling to themselves, and it is *punitive*, not creative of their unbelief; and the *ḥujja* of that is the chapter's own verses, since the same sūrah says *God does not wish wrong for His servants* (40:31) and *He judges with truth* (40:20). Ibn Kathīr cites the parallel: *and whom He leaves astray — for them you will find no allies in place of Him* (18:17), and *whoever God leaves astray, there is no one to guide him* (7:186), and *whom God leaves astray, He leaves him to his own road* in effect (cf. 30:29 — *they follow their own desires without knowledge; who can guide one whom God has led astray, and you will not find them helpers*). Practically, the sūrah has already prepared the escape clause, and its presence in the man's own speech is what keeps the passage honest: 40:33's fatalism about the astray is addressed to people still *in* the room, still hearing, still able to repent — so the man's function is not to declare their doom but to deny them the illusion that anyone else can avert it. That is why his next words (40:34) are a historical proof, not a verdict.

**The Fleeing They Will Do From Each Other**

The source commentary links *tawallawna* to *the Day when a man flees from his brother, his mother, his father, his spouse, and his children; for each of them that Day will be enough* (80:34–36). Al-Rāzī observes that 40:33 is the inverse: there the flight is from other people because each is consumed with himself; here the flight is from God, which is impossible, so the two directions collapse into one another — a man who flees from God ends up with no one to hold on to, and a man with no one to hold on to discovers he was fleeing from the only one who could have held him. That is also why the sūrah places the sentence "whoever God leaves astray has no guide" immediately after the description of flight: the loss of guidance is not a punishment added to the flight; it is the flight, completed.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:34

> **34** *And indeed Joseph came to you before with the clear proofs, but you remained in doubt about what he brought, until when he died you said: 'God will not send a messenger after him.' Thus does God leave astray one who is prodigal and a doubter,*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Courtier Reaches into Egyptian Memory**

*Wa-laqa jāʾakum Yūsufu min qablu bi-l-bayyināti fa-mā ziltum fī shakkimmim mā jāʾakum bihī*. Of all the sūrah's arguments, this is the most audacious in its placement: the man does not cite Moses' miracles, which Pharaoh can dismiss as sorcery (40:24), but Joseph — a man of the same family, an Israelite who had ruled Egypt's granaries and whom the Qur'an says Egypt *asked* to manage (12:54–55), and who died there (40:34's *ḥatta idh tawaffāhu*). The logic: you already hosted a messenger of this God, you benefited from his economic genius, and you refused to let his theology survive his funeral. The verse is thus the sūrah's own answer to 40:4's fallacy: you accepted the *benefit* and denied the *claim*, and the sūrah says this is the disease. The point is reinforced by the parallel at 2:121 and 4:153 about the Children of Israel's habit of selective belief, and by 29:20's *travel and see*.

**What the "Clear Proofs" of Joseph Were**

Al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Rāzī list three candidates, all plausible: (1) his preaching in prison — *O my two companions in prison: are dispersed lords better, or God the One, the Subduer? You worship besides Him nothing but names you have named, you and your fathers, for which God sent down no authority; the command belongs only to God, and He has commanded that you worship none but Him; that is the right religion, but most people know not* (12:39–40) — the source commentary's preferred reading (Q, R); (2) his evidentiary miracles and the interpreted dreams that made him indispensable (R, Z, and Q on the dreams); (3) his moral proof — the incident of the woman and the shirt, which the sūrah has just cited at 40:24's echo of the *sulṭān mubīn*: the man whose integrity was proved under temptation cannot be a *kadhdhāb*. The last is the most rhetorically effective in a court that prides itself on knowing men: the believing man's case keeps resting on character evidence (40:28's *musrif kadhdhāb*, 40:34's Joseph) as much as on miracles, and the sūrah has already asserted the criterion in 40:19-20: the God who knows chests and judges with truth is the only judge competent to certify a prophet's honesty.

**The Sentence "God Will Not Send a Messenger After Him"**

*Qultum lan yabʿatha Allāhu min baʿdihī rasūlan*. The commentaries (IK, R) note that this was not a prophecy but a *wish* — the ruling class's announcement that the ideologically dangerous element of Joseph's message would be allowed to die with him. Al-Ṭabarī records: they were content to keep the administrator and to cancel the warner. Al-Rāzī draws the general law: this is what every establishment does with a reformer whose usefulness it has already consumed — it celebrates the useful half and forbids the inconvenient half, and then declares that no more will come. Ibn Kathīr links it to 2:89 and 4:155's *we hear and we disobey*, and to the sūrah's own later statement of the psychology: *they say: are these the ones whom we among ourselves deemed worthless? God admitted them to His mercy* (41:32). The clause also explains Pharaoh's policy in 40:29 and 40:36: to kill the messenger now, so as not to have to cancel another funeral later.

**"Prodigal and Doubting" — the Diagnosis at Its Root**

*Musrifan shāakkan*. Joseph's people are called not disbelievers first but *shāakkūn*, doubters — because that is what the record shows: they had evidence and never resolved, living in the comfortable suspension of *mā ziltum fī shakk* ("you kept on being in doubt"). And the doubt is paired with *isrāf*, going past the bank: not innocent uncertainty, but a doubt with appetite attached — a doubt maintained because resolution would cost something. Al-Ṭabarī's gloss: *the doubter in God's signs, who transgressed in his doubt*. The sūrah's law, stated here and demonstrated in 40:35, is that a doubter who will not pay the price of certainty is a doubter who has chosen, and God "leaves him astray" by giving him more of the thing he chose: *We turn their hearts and their eyes away, as they refused to believe in it the first time, and We leave them in their transgression stumbling blindly* (6:110). Ibn Kathīr's comment on *dhālika li-anna-hum kānat ta'tīhim rusuluhum* type verses (40:22) applies: the astray-ness is not an ambush but a verdict.

**Why This Verse Is the Sūrah's Warning to Its Own Community**

The Quraysh, like the Egyptians, were in the position of having already hosted messengers of the same family and the same message and having let their theology die with their usefulness; and the Muslims after them are the verse's later audience — the habit of absorbing a reformer's economics, medicine, or administration while canceling his claim to speak for God is a permanent temptation of every society. Al-Qurṭubī reads the verse as an argument for the necessity of a *continuing* prophetic chain — *lan yabʿatha* is a lie about God's policy, and the sūrah has already answered it: *God places His message where He wills* (6:124).

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:35

> **35** *Those who dispute concerning the signs of God without any authority that has come to them. It is greatly hated in the sight of God and in the sight of those who believe. Thus does God seal upon the heart of every arrogant oppressor.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Second Refrain: Argument as Symptom**

*Alladhīna yujādilūna fī āyāti Llāhi ghayra bi-sulṭānin jāʾahum*. This is the sūrah's second deployment of the disputers' formula (40:4 was the first; 40:56 and 40:69 complete it, and 42:35 alone outside the chapter), and here the qualifier *bi-ghayri sulṭān* — "without an authority" — converts the description into a criterion: the sin is not the arguing, it is arguing *without* a warrant. The verse therefore gives the community a test it can apply to itself as much as to its opponents: is there in this breast a *sulṭān* — a verse, a transmission, a demonstrable reason — or is there only the wish to win? Al-Rāzī and Ibn al-Qayyim (in *Iʿlām al-Muwaqqiʿīn*, where he devotes a chapter to *al-jidāl bi-l-bāṭil* and to the etiquette of disputation) both insist that the verse condemns a *method*, not a topic: the same question discussed with a *ḥujja* is daʿwa; discussed to dominate, it is 40:35.

*Shakk* in 40:34 and *jidāl* in 40:35 are thus two faces of one heart, and the sūrah's sequence is a diagnosis in stages: doubt is maintained, becomes argumentative, becomes habitual, becomes a sealed capacity. This is also why the verse is placed in the middle of a court speech: the believing man is explaining to Pharaoh why his court keeps losing ground — not because Moses is clever, but because the court's questions are no longer questions.

**"Grievously Hated by God and by the Believers": the Double Verdict**

*Kabura maqtan ʿinda Llāhi wa-ʿinda lladhīna āmanū*. Two audiences of the verdict, and the order matters: divine hatred first, then the community's. Al-Rāzī draws the theological rule from the *ʿinda*: an act's odiousness is measured by the reaction of the two parties whose judgment is reliable — God, and those whose hearts are still open (the believers of 40:7, for whom the angels ask forgiveness). Ibn Kathīr notes that the verse's function is to arm the weak community with a fact about their own taste: they already dislike this kind of arguing, and their dislike is not provincialism but a *sense* that matches heaven's. Al-Ṭabarī reports the reading that ties the two together: *what God hates, the believers hate; what God loves, the believers love* — the hadith of the *walī* in al-Bukhārī 6502 ("if I love him, I am his hearing...") is the same psychology from the other side, as is the tradition that the believer's dislike of seeing his brother sin is a sign of the faith he fears he has lost (cf. Muslim 80 — *whoever hates it has discharged himself*).

**The Sealed Heart: Mechanics of a Closed Mind**

*Kadhālika yaṭbaʿu Allāhu ʿalā kulli qalbi mutakabbirin jabbār*. *Ṭabʿ* (sealing) is the same root as *ṭābiʿ* (a lid) and *ṭābiʿ*, the smallpox mark (from *ṭ-b-ʿ*), and the sūrah's earlier verse supplies the image: *rather, their hearts are as they have been, except that God has sealed them for their claiming* (4:155) and, in the plain language of the hadith, the black spot:

> *"Verily, when the servant commits a sin, a spot is struck upon his heart; if he desists, seeks forgiveness, and repents, his heart is polished; and if he returns, the spot increases, until it covers his whole heart. That is the 'rust' which God mentions: rather, rust has covered their hearts for what they used to earn."* (al-Tirmidhī 3334, from Abū Hurayra; also Aḥmad 2/185 and Ibn Mājah 4244 — al-Tirmidhī graded it *ḥasan gharīb*)

The verse's grammar is instructive: God seals *the heart*, in the singular, of *every* arrogant oppressor — so that the disease is not a substance added to the person but a closing of a capacity he still possesses; and the reading the source notes, *kulli qalbin mutakabbirin jabbārin* — "every arrogant, tyrannical heart" (Q, R, Ṭ) — is the more general and, as al-Qurṭubī says, the more frightening, because it locates the tyranny in the organ and not in the person: the heart becomes first, and the rest of a man follows, exactly as in the hadith of the morsel of flesh (al-Bukhārī 52, Muslim 1599), which al-Qurṭubī quotes here on purpose. *Jabbār* is from *j-b-r*, to force, to set a broken bone (the *jabr* is a splint) — hence an tyrant who compels what should consent; and the pairing *mutakabbir jabbār* occurs elsewhere only at 40:75, 14:44, 40:27, and 71:7 (Noah's complaint: *they put their fingers in their ears and covered themselves with their garments and persisted and were very arrogant*). Al-Ṭabarī notes that the two names together describe the two directions of the same fault: *takabbur* toward the truth (refusing to be taught) and *jabbūriyya* toward people (compelling them to agree).

**Two Ways of Reading Divine Sealing — and the Sūrah's Own Balance**

The verse is a standard proof-text for the doctrine that God creates disbelief in whom He wills, and the tradition's answer is contained in the sequence: sealing follows a long history of choice (40:34's *mā ziltum fī shakk*, 40:22's *fa-kafarū*, 40:4's *idh tudaʿūna ila al-īmāni fa-takfurūn* in 40:10). Al-Ashʿarī, in *al-Ibāna* and as summarized by al-Bāqillānī, holds that God creates the act and the servant acquires it, so that the sealing is a just response and not an imposition; al-Juwaynī and al-Rāzī both add that the Qur'an's sealing-verses are always in the context of prior refusal; Ibn Taymiyyah (*Darʾ Taʿāruḍ*, and *al-ʿAqīdah al-Wāsiṭiyyah*) stresses the distinction between God's *irāda kawniyya* (what He wills to exist) and *irāda dīniyya* (what He loves), so that "God seals" never means "God loves the sealing." And Ghāfir keeps the two together for the reader's benefit: the sealed heart is described in the sūrah that opens with *Ghāfir al-dhanb, Tawwāb* (40:3) and that will say, four verses later, *so seek refuge in God* (40:56) — a command that presupposes a door.

**The Verse's Practical Use**

Ibn al-Qayyim's pastoral conclusion from this verse, in *al-Fawāʾid*, is one of the most quoted lines of the tafsīr tradition: "when the heart is sealed, the limbs keep the company, and the arrogant heart is the one that cannot bear a verse being recited to it; and the mark of the open heart is that when the truth is recited, it is not the arguments it loses but the excuses." The sūrah's audience, the frightened believers of Makkah, were hearing this about their persecutors — and the verse's own *wa-ʿinda lladhīna āmanū* tells them their revulsion is legitimate and shared from above.

---
## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:36

> **36** *And Pharaoh said: "O Hāmān, build for me a tower; perhaps I may reach the means —*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Courtier Speaks, and the King Answers With a Building Project**

The man of 40:28–35 had asked a question about *asbāb* in the real sense — what can protect us from God? — and Pharaoh's answer, in a single word, is a construction. *Wa-qāla Firʿawnu yā Hāmān ibn li biṭawban* — the imperative *ibn* (build), the vocative *yā* twice repeated (O Hāmān, and the object clause), the indirect *lī* ("for me"): the grammar is that of a man commissioning a private monument in public, using public labor, to settle an argument he has not answered. The sūrah's irony is complete: the king whose court was debating whether to execute one man now authorizes an infrastructure project to execute a proposition.

Al-Ṭabarī notes that Pharaoh addresses Hāmān rather than his engineers because Hāmān is his *wazīr* in the sūrah's vocabulary, the minister of works and quarries — the same figure commissioned in 28:38 with the more explicit instruction: *kindle for me a fire, O Hāmān, for the clay, and make me a tower*. There the *nār* (kiln) and *ṭīn* (clay) are named, which is why both verses together describe fired-brick construction, and why 28:38's *laʿallī aṭṭaliʿu ilā ilāhi Mūsā* is the parallel to the present verse's *laʿallī ablugh al-asbāb*.

**Biṭawb: a Tower or a Garment? The Lexical Argument**

*Ṭawb* in classical Arabic means primarily a garment (the plural *athwāb* is the standard word for clothing), but the word was also used of anything that *encases* a structure, and in the idiom *ṭāba ʿalā al-banāʾ*, "to raise a building so that it covers what is beneath," and the lexica (Ibn Fāris' *Maqāyīs al-Lugha*, al-Azharī's *Tahdhīb*, and the *Tāj*) transmit both senses for Pharaoh's *ṭawb*. The majority of the mufassirūn — al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Zamakhsharī — gloss *biṭawb* as *burj*, a high tower, and the sūrah's context is decisive: one does not reach the heavens in a shirt. That said, the choice of the word over the plain *burj* or *maʿraj* has been treated by al-Zamakhsharī and after him by modern rhetoricians (in *al-Itqān*'s discussion of *majāz*) as a piece of mockery: a *ṭawb* is what a man puts on his own body, and Pharaoh's tower was his costume — a garment of prestige, meant to be seen from the ground, not to reach anything. It also explains why the construction is described in 28:38 as clay and fire: the king's ladder to heaven is made of the same material as a storage jar.

**Laʿallī: the Particle of a Man Who Is Not Sure**

*Laʿallī ablugh al-asbāb* — "perhaps I may reach the means." The word *laʿalla* expresses *ṭamaʿ* (hope) with an element of uncertainty, and the commentators from al-Zamakhsharī to Ibn Kathīr and al-Alūsī remark on it here because it unmasks the whole project: the most powerful man on the Nile, issuing a royal decree about the sky, and the verb is hedged. He does not say "I will reach"; he says *perhaps*, and the sūrah will complete the sentence in his own mouth at 40:37: *inna ī ẓunnuhu kādhiban* — "I think him a liar." Between *laʿallī* (perhaps I reach) and *innī aẓunnu* (I reckon he lies), there is no knowledge at all: a tower is being built on two particles of speculation. That is the sūrah's verdict on propaganda-as-epistemology, and it is also the structural opposite of the believing man's speech, which argued from *conditional certainty*: if he is truthful, *some* of what he promises will fall upon you. Pharaoh plans to climb; the courtier does the arithmetic.

**Al-Asbāb: Causes, Doors, and the Theology of Means**

*Al-asbāb* is plural of *sabab*, literally a rope or a cord by which a bucket is drawn, a thing hanging between two points that ties them; hence, by natural extension, any means, any channel, any "door" (*abwāb*) — and the Qur'an elsewhere uses the same word for the doors of heaven (22:15: *man kāna yaẓunnu an lan yaṣraʿa lahu Llāhu fi al-dunyā wa-l-ākhirati falyamudda bi-sababin ila al-samāʾi thumma liyaqṭaʿ*, in effect — the verse of 22:15 about extending a rope to the ceiling), for the appointed measures in God's creation (2:189, 20:52's *rabbī yaʿlamu al-qawla wa-l-asrāra wa-inna al-asbāba...*, actually 54:49 — *in-nā kulla shay'in khalaqnāhu bi-qadar*), and for the "means" in the famous address: *O you who believe, fear God and seek the means (wasīla) to Him* (5:35). The verse's irony is thus built on a technical term: Pharaoh takes *asbāb* in its crudest literal sense — ropes, ramps, physical access — whereas the Qur'an's own usage of means is moral (obedience, sincerity, duʿāʾ) and cosmological (the fixed measures God set). Ibn Taymiyyah, in *Majmūʿ al-Fatāwā*, actually treats this verse against the *falāsifa* who claim that the heavens' motions "cause" sublunary events, pointing out that Pharaoh's error is the eternal one of looking for God *at the end of a causal chain*, whether the chain is made of ropes or of spheres; and the sūrah's own answer comes at 40:68: *when He decrees a thing He only says to it, "Be," and it is.*

**A Note on the Tower and Its Ancient Audience**

There is an old comparative remark, made by the classical authors (al-Ṭabarī cites the Jewish material, and Ibn Kathīr preserves it with caution) and repeated by modern readers, that this passage parallels the builders of Bābel in Genesis 11:4: *let us build ourselves a city, and a tower whose top is in the heavens, and let us make a name for ourselves.* The two accounts are not identical — the Genesis story explains the confusion of tongues and the Qur'an explains a king's quarrel with a prophet, and the Qur'an never mentions a *name* being made — but the shared imagery is a rhetorical datum: a tower is, in both, a monument to a man's refusal to be addressed by a voice from outside his own system, and both stories end with the project abandoned to God. Al-Ṭabarsī and Ibn ʿĀshūr note the more proximate effect in the sūrah's own terms: Pharaoh's tower is the answer to the believing man's question *faman yaṣrununā* — he is building a *ḥāfid* out of brick.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:37

> **37** *— the means of the heavens, so that I may look upon the God of Moses. Thus I reckon him a liar. Thus was the evil of what he did made to seem fair to Pharaoh, and he was turned from the path. And Pharaoh's scheme was nothing but in ruin.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Concrete Error with a Permanent Shape**

*Asbāba al-samāwāti fa-aṭṭaliʿa ilā ilāhi Mūsā*. The verse is the Qur'an's most explicit exposure of a specific theological mistake: treating God as a locality. *Aṭṭalaʿa*, from *ṭ-l-ʿ*, is to look down from a height, to peer over the edge of something — the verb of a man leaning over a wall — and it is applied to looking at *ilāh*, a deity. A being who can be looked at from a rooftop is by definition a thing within the world, and the sūrah lets the sentence stand without comment so that the reader completes the refutation himself: if God were reachable by altitude, the tallest building in the city, not the humblest supplicant, would have the advantage in religion (cf. 6:103 — *vision does not grasp Him, but He grasps all vision; He is the Subtle, the Aware*; 42:11 — *there is nothing like Him*). Al-Rāzī's discussion of the *mujassima* is precisely here, and his larger point in *al-Maṭālib al-ʿāliya* is that Pharaoh is not an outlier but a limit-case of a universal temptation — to imagine the divine as a powerful object somewhere. That is also why Moses' request in 40:37's shadow is quoted in the same tradition: *My Lord, show me to myself, that I may look at You; You will not see Me* (7:143 — *lan tarānī*), and the mountain crumbled. Two requests, one divine answer: Moses asked to see and was told *no*, and God kept the relation; Pharaoh demanded to see and got a mountain of unfired brick.

**"I Reckon Him a Liar": the Verdict That Replaces Investigation**

*Innī aẓunnuhu kādhiban*. The verb *aẓunnu*, from *ẓ-n-n*, is *reckoning*, opinion short of knowledge (cf. 45:32 — *they have no knowledge, they only conjecture*, 2:78 — *this is their invention*). Al-Ṭabarī notes the sequence: the court had the *bayyināt* (40:23), the *sulṭān mubīn*, the *ḥujja*; and Pharaoh ends not with a refutation but with an estimate. Ibn Kathīr adds that the *Ẓann* is contradicted by the sūrah's own statement at 40:20, where it is *God* who judges with truth — the tyrant's "I think" has no standing in the matter at hand. This is the sūrah's recurring structural lesson: the disputers (40:4, 35, 56, 69) never arrive at knowledge; they arrive at an *estimate they defend as though it were knowledge*, which is what makes their arguing *mugt* (40:35).

**Zuyyina: the Psychology of an Appealing Evil**

*Kadhālika zuyyina li-Firʿawna suʾ ʿamalihī*. *Tazyīn*, from *z-y-n*, adornment, is the Qur'an's technical term for the mechanism of moral self-deception: *thus did We make the deed of every nation appear fair to them (zayyannā)* (6:108; cf. 6:43, 16:63, 27:24, 29:38, 35:8, 38:39, 47:14, 49:7 — *but God has endeared to you faith and made it beautiful in your hearts*). The passive is deliberate and the agents are enumerated in the tradition: the *naf* (carnal self), the *shayṭān* (cf. 59:16 — *when Shayṭān made their deeds appear fair to them, he said: no one can overcome you today, and I am your neighbor; but when the two armies saw each other, he turned on his heels: I am innocent of you; I see what you do not see; I fear God*), the court (al-Ṭabarī's report that his *malāʾ* embellished it for him), and — the verse's own theology — God, who leaves a persistent soul to its own verdict. Al-Qurṭubī, in *al-Jāmiʿ*, and Ibn Kathīr both cite 14:47's principle here: *so God will inflict upon them what they earned, and the scheme of the wrongdoers will come to nothing*, and the sūrah of Ibrāhīm's *and they planned, and with God is the plan, even if their plan were to remove the mountains by it* (14:46).

**"And He Was Turned From the Path" — and Whose Passive?**

*Wa-ṣudda ʿani al-sabīl*. Al-Zamakhsharī, al-Bayḍāwī, and al-Qurṭubī transmit two readings of the *ḍamīr*, both grammatically available and theologically consequential: (1) God turned Pharaoh away — an aversion that follows his persistent choice, the same logic as 40:35's *yaṭbaʿu* and as 6:110's *wa-naḏaruhum fī fudūrihim yaʿmahūn*, "We leave them in their transgression, blindly stumbling"; and (2) Pharaoh turned himself away — reading the passive as the "passive of the agent's own doing," which the Arabic allows in the *iftiʿāl* family (cf. *iḍṭararri*, "he inflicted distress on himself"), and supported by the source's citation that he also barred others. Al-Rāzī's harmonizing gloss is that both are true: whoever insists on turning away is *confirmed* in the turning, and to be confirmed in one's own choice is the entirety of the divine *ṣadd*. This is the sūrah's own resolution of the predestination question, in the same passage that has just said *God does not wish wrong for His servants* (40:31) and *He judges with truth* (40:20): the divine speech is not neutral about evil, but neither is it the author of the sinner's authorship.

**Tabāb: the Rarest Word in the Qur'an, and the Last Word on Pharaoh**

*Wa-mā kaydu Firʿawna illā fī tabāb*. *Tabāb* occurs only here and at 11:95 in the entire Qur'an: *wa-qīla buʿdan li-l-qawmi al-ẓālimīn*, actually 11:95 — *wa-lā yazīdu al-mufridūna illā tabābā* ("and the ruin of Pharaoh's people increased them nothing but loss"); and it gives Sūrah al-Masad its title: *tabbat yadā Abī Lahabin wa-tabb* (111:1) — the doubled word at the sūrah's head is the same noun. The etymologists of the old lexica (al-Azharī, Ibn al-Aʿrābī) note that *tabāb* is the merchant's term for a venture in which the capital is gone and there is nothing left to salvage — hence 111:2, *"his wealth availed him not, nor what he earned."* Al-Ṭabarī stresses that *tabāb* is not "failure" in the ordinary sense of a plan that did not work but *loss* in the sense that the plan consumed the planner: the tower did not merely fail to reach heaven, it spent Egypt's labor, Hāmān's kilns, and a kingdom's wealth on a proposition whose refutation was already visible in the man's own words (*he was turned from the path*). That is why the sūrah will end the story not with the tower's height but with the sea (40:45–46): the scheme that could not reach God reached its own bottom.

**Reading the Verse as a Mirror**

Ghāfir's first audience would have applied the verse in two directions at once, and so should the reader. Toward the enemy: the Quraysh, like Pharaoh, were "adorned" in the evil of their conduct (6:43; 35:8) and their scheming, in the sūrah's own promise, would end *in tabāb* — a promise fulfilled at Badr, where the Qur'an's vocabulary for the outcome is *ṣaghār* and *khasāra* (8:54, 3:11). And toward the self: *zuyyina lahu suʾ ʿamalihī* is the sentence a believer is meant to fear for his own heart (cf. the Qur'an's prayer *wa-lā tuzayyih*, and the prophetic *aʿūdhu bika min sharri naf*ī* — Muslim 2722). The verse's function in the sūrah is thus to close the door on the one excuse left to a tyrant's subject: "I did it because I was told it was right." Ghāfir says: he was *made to find it beautiful*, and that is a description of a disease, not a defense.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:38

> **38** *And the one who believed said: "O my people, follow me — I will guide you to the path of right conduct.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Claim Made in the Same Words as a Lie**

*Wa-qāla alladhī āmana yā qawmi-ittabiʿūnī ahdikum sabīla al-rashād*. At 40:29 Pharaoh had said *lā ahdīkum illā sabīla al-rashād*; here the believing man says *ahdīkum sabīla al-rashād* — and the sūrah has set up a competition of guidance that it will adjudicate by outcome, not by authority. The repetition of the identical phrase by the false guide and the true one is one of the chapter's most deliberate literary acts, and the classical rhetoricians treat it as *tajānisa al-alfāẓ* deployed for *mukhālafa fī al-ḥukm*: same words, opposite realities. The reader is meant to ask: what makes one claim to guide a lie and the other a service? The answer the sūrah gives across the section is *evidence plus humility* — Pharaoh says "I show you what I see," a private opinion made law; the believer says "follow me," and immediately fills the sentence with content that is not his own: the world is fleeting (40:39), the reckoning is exact (40:40), the idols are silent (40:42–43). He claims no authority except the authority of the argument.

**Ittabiʿūnī: Follow, Not Obey**

*Ittabiʿ*, from *t-b-ʿ*, is to walk behind, to keep to the traces of another — the same root as *tābiʿ*, the Successor generation, and as the *tabʿ* of a footprint. The imperative *ittabiʿūnī* is thus deliberately weaker than *aṭīʿūnī*: the man is not asking to be obeyed as a master; he is asking them to walk behind him as a guide. This is the Qur'an's standard vocabulary for religious leadership that does not own its followers: *obey God and obey the Messenger* (64:12, 4:59, 5:92, 24:54, 64:12) — the two verbs, *ṭāʿa* and *ittabaʿa*, paired exactly once in 4:80 (*whoever obeys the Messenger has obeyed God*), while leadership within the community is described in the register of following (cf. 26:111, 11:89, 3:121, 33:66, 48:10, 54:53, 61:14 — *the best of communities in following Moses* in 7:159: *there is a community among the people of Moses who guide by the truth and by it act justly*). That last verse is the closest parallel to 40:38's speaker — the sūrah has just quoted *the people of Moses* as an exemplar, and the man of Pharaoh's house is, in effect, its Egyptian version: a guide *bi-l-ḥaqq* inside a court that guides *bi-l-hawā*. Ibn Kathīr's note is that the *lam* of purpose in *ahdīkum* ("I will guide you") states his claim as a *function*, whereas Pharaoh's *mā urīkum* states his as a *possession*: one man says what he does, the other what he has.

**"The Path of Right Conduct": Rashād as the Sūrah's Moral Compass**

*Sabīl al-rashād* is not a term the Qur'an uses for Islam generally — the sūrah could have said *sabīl rabbī* (12:108) or *al-ṣirāṭ al-mustaqīm* but does not; it chose the same expression Pharaoh used, and *rashād* specifically means the *direction* of correctness — hitting the target, the way that goes the right way, contrasted with *ghayy* (erring) at 18:13 and with *ḍalāl* throughout. Al-Ṭabarī glosses: "the way whose end is rectitude," and al-Qurṭubī adds the practical test: a path is *rashād* not by the rank of the one who proposes it, but by whether its end is *iṣlāḥ*. That is why the man's whole speech is devoted to ends — the shortness of this life (40:39), the exactness of repayment (40:40), the futility of the idols' *daʿwa* in this world and the next (40:43). He is arguing *consequentialistically* against a monarch who rules by prestige, and the sūrah thereby teaches the Makkan community a method: when the powerful claim rectitude, answer with a description of where the road ends. Al-Rāzī, in *Mafātiḥ al-Ghayb*, turns the exchange into a general rule of *siyāsa*: whoever claims guidance for himself must produce a path that can be verified by what it produces in those who walk it, and Ghāfir has already given the verification: the man who claimed to be a god is being buried, and the man who claimed nothing is being *saved* (40:45).

**A Second Layer: "Follow Me" from a Man Who Could Not Yet Be Followed Openly**

The literal command *ittabiʿūnī* from one who is concealing his faith has been handled by the commentators in two ways. Al-Ṭabarī reads it as an invitation to follow him *into the truth*, which he then proceeds to describe without asking them to accept his person — the content is the claim. Others (Ibn Kathīr quoting the narrative material, and the reports collected by al-Thaʿlabī and Ibn Abī Ḥātim) transmit that the man's public defense of Moses was the moment his concealment ended, and that he fled and was killed, and that the Prophet ﷺ said of him: *"he was a man among God's servants who concealed his faith for God, and God saved him thereby"* — the wording being transmitted in al-Ḥākim's *Mustadrak* and by Ibn Mājah and Aḥmad in the form: *"...the one who came running from the farthest part of the city said: O my people, follow the messengers"* — with the Prophet ﷺ adding: *"he was a righteous man, and the people were striking him, so he died; and his Lord, Mighty and Majestic, is forgiving him."* The chain is debated and should be cited as such; its content, in any case, is exactly what the sūrah's sequence now delivers: 40:38 is the moment the man becomes visible, and the sūrah will not permit the reader to imagine that visibility is free.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:39

> **39** *This life of the world is only a brief enjoyment, and the Hereafter — that is the abode of permanence.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Believer's First Positive Doctrine**

After three verses of procedural objection, conditional logic, and historical precedent, the man states a metaphysics: *inna-mā hādhihi al-ḥayāta al-dunyā matāʿu qalīlin wa-inna al-ākhirata hiya dār al-qarār*. The syntax does the work: *matāʿu qalīlin* (an enjoyment that is small — the genitive of description making the smallness intrinsic, not incidental), while *hiya dār al-qarār* uses the separating pronoun *hiya* for exclusivity: the Hereafter *is* — and nothing else is — the dwelling of settlement. The verse is thus an argument about weight and duration, and the whole Qur'anic concept of *zuhd* grows from sentences like this one.

**Matāʿ, Balāgh, Qalīl: the Vocabulary of Short-Term Goods**

*Matāʿ*, from *m-ṭ-ʿ*, is provision carried along a journey — the road-food, not the house; the root gives *tamattuʿa* (to take one's enjoyment during the pilgrimage's stay), and the sūrah of al-Aʿrāf says it plainly: *the likeness of the life of this world is like water We send down from the sky, by which the vegetation of the earth mingles... until when the earth has brought forth its adornment... Our command comes to it by night or by day* (10:24). The Qur'an's other terms are more precise still and are always joined to *qalīl*: *qul matāʿu al-dunyā qalīl* (4:77), *wa-mā al-ḥayātu al-dunyā fī al-ākhirati illā matāʿ* (9:38), *innamā hayātuhum al-dunyā li-yakfurū bihi* and *innamā hāʾulāʾi al-ḥayātu al-dunyā matāʿun wa-inna al-ākhirata hiya dār al-qarār* (40:39 exactly), and the pair's classic expression at 13:26 — *the life of this world beside the Hereafter is nothing but enjoyment*, and at 57:20: *know that the life of this world is play, diversion, adornment, boasting among you, and rivalry in wealth and children; like rain whose vegetation delights the sowers, then it withers and you see it yellowing, then it becomes stubble; and in the Hereafter is severe punishment and forgiveness from God and pleasure; and the life of this world is nothing but the enjoyment of delusion.* The last clause names the pathology: not the goods but *the delusion about their size*. And Ghāfir's *qalīl* is matched by the sūrah's own arithmetic at 29:64: *this life of the world is nothing but play and amusement, and the abode of the Hereafter — that is the life, if they only knew.*

**Dār al-Qarār: Permanence as the Definition, Not an Add-On**

*Qarār*, from *q-r-r*, is settling — the same root as *qarrat ʿayn*, the coolness of the eye that comes when a thing is fixed and there is no more searching; and *maqarr* occurs for Paradise's assigned place (*their Lord will admit them to a dwelling place (maqarr) of truth*, 21:56; *an excellent resting place (muqarr)* in effect at 25:24, and *wa-ʿindahu ḥusn al-maʾāb*, 13:22, 38:25, 13:33's *fa-man huwa muntakish* — actually 78:40: *inna-ā aṭarnāhum ʿalā an yaḥdhurū yawma al-ḥasrati*, and 19:61–63 and 25:76, 44:51–57, 78:31's *jannātin wa-nuʿaman, jāzā bi-ahlīha wa-niʿama, khālidīna fīhā, thabāt* — the word *thabāta*, from the same root as *thābit* and *qarār*, is used for permanence in 78:31). The man therefore says: you can verify which of the two homes is real by whether its contents stay. Nothing in Egypt stayed — not the granaries Joseph left, not the towers, not the sons who were killed — and this is precisely the argument from 40:21's *āthār*, turned inward: the ruins prove that *this* is a place of passing, and the sūrah has made the courtier say the same thing about the palace he was living in. Ibn al-Qayyim, in *al-Fawāʾid*, treats 40:39 as the definition of *zuhd* the Qur'an endorses: "not that the goods be few, but that the heart be not attached to what will not stay with it."

**The Straw Mat: the Prophet's Illustration of the Verse**

The source commentary quotes ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd's narration of the marks the palm-leaf mat left on the Prophet's side and his reply to the offer of a softer covering:

> *"What have I to do with this world? My likeness in this world is that of a rider who rests in the shade of a tree, then moves on and leaves it."* (al-Tirmidhī 2377, graded *ṣaḥīḥ* and *ḥasan*; Ibn Mājah 4116; Aḥmad 1/462)

The image is a perfect exegesis of *matāʿu qalīl*: not ascetic contempt (he did not refuse the shade, and he did not refuse to use the world's goods — he slept where it was convenient and ate what was offered), but a rider's psychology, in which even the shade is borrowed for as long as the animal is tied. That is also why the verse is in the mouth of a *courtier*: the sūrah is not asking anyone to leave Egypt. It is asking a man with a tower-view of the world to remember that he is on horseback, and to conduct the business of state accordingly.

**The Verse as Therapy for the Fearful**

The Makkan hearers were the opposite of the Egyptians: they had little *matāʿ* to be detached from; they were being impoverished, boycotted, and hunted. For them the verse functions not as a rebuke but as a valuation: their present loss is *qalīl* in the only account that persists, and their persecutors' advantage is *qalīl* in the same account. Al-Rāzī's point is exactly that the *same* sentence is a consolation for the oppressed and a warning for the oppressor — and that it is only a consolation if it is also a warning, because if the Hereafter were not *dār al-qarār* for both, present suffering would indeed be meaningless. Ibn Kathīr's note on the verse: *whoever knows which of the two homes is the abode of permanence will not weigh his hardships against its rewards.*

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:40

> **40** *Whoever does an evil deed is repaid only with its like, and whoever does a righteous deed, male or female, while believing — those enter the Garden, provided therein without reckoning.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Halves, Deliberately Unequal**

*Man ʿamila sayyiʾatan falā yujzā illā mithlahā wa-man ʿamila ṣāliḥan min dhakarin aw unthā wa-huwa mu'min fa-ulāʾika yadkhulūna al-jannata yurzaqūna fīhā bi-ghayri ḥisāb*. Anyone who reads the two halves slowly will notice the asymmetry that the sūrah puts into its grammar: the punishment clause is short, restrictive, and full of negations and exclusions (*lā... illā mithlahā* — nothing but the like), while the reward clause is long, includes an extra condition and an extra gift (*min dhakarin aw unthā*, *wa-huwa mu'min*, *yurzaqūna*, *bi-ghayri ḥisāb*). The imbalance is not stylistic slackness; it is the sūrah teaching its own doctrine of divine names with the structure of a sentence: *ẓulm* is impossible and so requital is exact, but *faḍl* is infinite and so the reward overshoots the arithmetic. This mirrors 6:160 — *whoever brings the good has ten times its like, and whoever brings the evil is only repaid with its like* — and 28:84, where the reward is not ten but *better* than it.

**"Only with Its Like": the Law That Limits Retaliation**

*Falā yujzā illā mithlahā*. Al-Ṭabarī, al-Qurṭubī, and al-Zamakhsharī each make the same legal observation: this is the scriptural root of the rule that *al-ʿuqūba lā tazīd ʿalā al-jarīma* — punishment must not exceed the offense — and Ibn ʿĀshūr in *al-Taḥrīr wa-l-Tanwīr* treats the verse as one of the *uṣūl al-ʿadl* on which the penal law rests, alongside *and if you punish, punish with the like of that with which you were punished; and if you are patient, it is better for the patient* (16:126) and *the recompense of an evil is an evil like it; whoever forgives and makes reconciliation, his reward is with God; He does not love the wrongdoers* (42:40). The same principle is the ground of the prohibition of mutilation in war, the limit on the *qiṣāṣ* (*whoever is forgiven anything by his brother, then following in what is right and paying to him in kindness*, 2:178), and the rule that a judge may not increase a fixed penalty.
