The sūrah's placement here, in the speech of a man arguing with a court that has just decreed the killing of children, is the point: the verse says *evil is repaid with its like*, and therefore the court's plan is already, on its own terms, a transgression of the measure — no crime of the Israelites justifies a generation of their boys. And the readers are told that God Himself will not exceed the measure with them, which is the ground of hope the next verses will exploit.

**The Multiplication of the Good, and Its Conditions**

Two conditions qualify the reward: *min dhakarin aw unthā* and *wa-huwa mu'min*. The first is the Qur'an's standard declaration of the irrelevance of sex to moral and eschatological standing, and the sūrah in which the phrase occurs in this exact form is Ghāfir and Āl ʿImrān 3:195, al-Nisāʾ 4:32 and 124, al-Naḥl 16:97, al-Aḥzāb 33:35 (which enumerates the categories for both: *the men who submit and the women who submit... God has prepared for them forgiveness and a great reward*), al-Muʾminūn 23:11, and 57:12. The second — *while believing* — is the condition on which all the schools insist: *bi-ghayri ḥisāb* is not given to virtue in the abstract, because the sūrah's own preceding verses have denied that deeds without *īmān* have a standing in the final account (*those whose labors have become futile in this world and the next*, 11:16; *they will have no portion in the Hereafter*, 3:77; and the hadith of those dragged to the Fire in Muslim 1905 for reciting and fighting for a name). Yet the two conditions are not a contradiction but a sequence: the *īmān* is what makes a deed *ṣāliḥ* rather than a mere good act, and the sūrah's own *mukhliṣ* (40:14, 65) supplies the interior of that definition.

**Bi-ghayri ḥisāb: the Provision That Escapes Measurement**

The phrase is the verse's most contested and most consoling. The commentators offer four senses, all compatible: (1) in unmeasured, unlimited quantity — *your Lord says: ask Me and I will answer you; those too proud to worship Me will enter the Fire debased* (40:60), and *whatever you have with you runs out, and what is with God endures* (16:96); (2) without any reckoning being imposed on them — for the reckoning is described in the sūrah of Abraham as being done *in half a day* for the believer, and *so he will be given his record in his right hand; he will say: here, read my record* (69:19–21), and the hadith in al-Bukhārī (6573) and Muslim (2791) about the seventy thousand who enter the Garden *bi-ghayri ḥisāb*: from the *rifāʿa* tradition, the wording being: *"they are the ones who do not cauterize, do not take augury, and do not seek ruqya, and upon their Lord they rely"*; (3) that the reward for evil requires an *exact* reckoning while the reward for good is *generosity*, which is al-Rāzī's reading and the one that fits the verse's own two halves; and (4) the reading attributed to al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Zayd: were people repaid for their good deeds strictly according to desert, there *would* be a reckoning — the "without reckoning" is a gift of God's *faḍl* and not the arithmetic of the deed's due (the same idea as in the hadith: *"none of you will be saved by his deeds; they said: not even you, Messenger of God? He said: not even me, unless God envelops me with His mercy"*, al-Bukhārī 5355; Muslim 2831). Al-Rāzī notes that the phrase's presence in a legal sentence is the sūrah's own way of saying: law and grace are not opposed; grace is what law, honestly applied, leaves over.

**Rizq in the Garden, and Why the Sūrah Chooses the Word for Food**

*Yurzaqūna fīhā*: the root *r-z-q* is used in 40:13 for rain sent down from the sky, and here for Paradise's provision, and the sūrah has made the two the same category already (*God shows you His signs and sends down provision for you from the sky*, 40:13). The continuity is the argument: the provision you eat today, which arrives without your asking the cloud, is the first installment of the provision you will eat in a home whose doors are not even counted. Al-Qurṭubī's comment on 40:13 and this one is worth keeping: *whoever recognizes the Rāziq will not be consumed by the rizq.* That is the sūrah's answer to Pharaoh, whose entire anxiety was economic and political: the man who fears that Moses will *change your religion* is a man who owns a country and fears a loss; the *bi-ghayri ḥisāb* clause says, to the persecuted, that their provider is not the state they are being expelled from.

---
## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:41

> **41** *How is it that I call you to safety while you call me to the Fire?*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Inverts the Courtroom**

*Wa-ya qawmi mā liya adʿūkum ila al-najāti wa-tadʿūnanī ila al-nār*. The man has moved from argument to astonishment, and the sūrah marks the shift by making the sentence an exclamation of bewilderment rather than a proposition: *mā liya... wa-*, "what is the matter with me that...," a construction of incredulity that the Qur'an puts in the mouths of prophets repeatedly (cf. *what is the matter with you that you do not expect from God's grandeur?* 71:13, and *what is the matter with you, when it is said to you "march in God's cause" you sink heavily* 9:38). The rhetorical work is done by the reversal of the two verbs' directions: *I call you* (to safety) against *you call me* (to the Fire). A court trying a criminal, in the middle of the world's strongest state, is told that it is the one conducting an extradition to the Fire, and that the defendant is the one running an asylum.

**Two Directions, Two Names for the Same Reality**

*Najāt* and *nār* are the sūrah's coordinate opposites. *Najāḥ* (from *n-j-w*, to be at a distance from the danger, as a *nājiya* is a saved man on high ground away from the flood) is the word the Qur'an uses for being rescued from the Fire and admitted to the Garden, and its root supplies the sūrah of Nūḥ and the phrase *wa-najjaynāhu wa-man fi al-fulki* (29:15, 54:15). It is not, in the Qur'anic usage, an individualistic category: Noah's *najāt* includes his followers, and Jonah's does (21:88), and Pharaoh's household loses theirs *as a class*. So when the man says *I call you to safety* — the plural *kum* is the point: he is offering them exit as a community, not as private souls, and this is the political dimension of the sūrah's *duʿāʾ*: the salvation he proposes is not escape from Egypt but escape from what Egypt is about to become. And *al-nār* here is not a threat added to an argument; it is the argument, because the sūrah has already described the Fire as the *ʿāqiba* of the peoples he named at 40:30–31: a known outcome, not a hypothesis.

**A Single Sentence That Exposes a Whole System of Values**

Look at the inversion in the clause *wa-tadʿūnanī ila al-nār* — they are *inviting* him. The Qur'an's most sustained description of how ideological pressure works on an honest person inside a corrupt establishment is not by threat but by *invitation*: you are invited to stop asking questions, to accept the framing, to call disbelief "prudence" and murder "security," and the invitation is polite, repeated, and socially expensive to refuse. The sūrah will later name the same mechanism in the exchange between the weak and their leaders: *and when they argue in the Fire, the weak will say to those who were arrogant: we were your followers; can you then bear for us a portion of the Fire?* (40:47). The man's question at 40:41 is the inverse of that scene: *you are inviting me into the very thing you are about to enter, and you are calling that an honor.* Al-Zamakhsharī notes that the verse's astonishment (*mā liya*) is the only appropriate response to a society that has inverted the vocabulary: the one who calls to *najāt* is treated as a criminal, and the one who calls to the Fire is the king.

**Why "to the Fire" and Not "to Unbelief"**

The verse's next line (40:42) will name the crime — disbelief and association of partners with God — so 40:41 does not. That is the man's method: he argues to Pharaoh's court in terms of *consequences they cannot deny* rather than in terms of *doctrines they refuse to hear*, exactly as he did at 40:28 with the conditional (*if he is truthful, some of what he promises will fall on you*). Ibn Kathīr remarks that a wise advocate does not begin with the contested premise but with the agreed consequence; the Qur'an then shows the method reaching its limit at 40:37 and 40:45 — the method is sound, the hearers are sealed — and provides the believer with the necessary companion virtue at 40:44, *I entrust my affair to God.*

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Answer to Its First Verse**

The chapter opened with *Ḥā. Mīm.* and an assertion that the Book is *tanzīl* from *al-ʿAzīz al-ʿAlīm* (40:2), whose purpose is *li-yundhira yawma al-talāq* (40:15). Here, deep inside the narrative, the chapter shows what such a warning looks like in practice: a man inside the doomed city, calling to *najāt* and being called to the Fire. The sūrah's own claim — that warning is the purpose of revelation — is thereby demonstrated rather than asserted, and the audience is asked which side of the sentence they are on. This is also the most economical place in the chapter for the famous hadith about the cost of speaking: *"None of you should keep back from commanding right and forbidding wrong when he sees it, except that he does so with his hand if he can, and if not with his tongue, and if not with his heart, and that is the weakest of faith"* (Muslim 49, from Abū Saʿīd), inasmuch as the man had exhausted the first two options years before this verse and is now paying for the second.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:42

> **42** *You call me to disbelieve in God and to associate with Him what I have no knowledge of, while I call you to the Mighty, the Forgiving.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Naming the Two Calls for the First Time**

*Tadʿūnanī li-akfura bi-Llāhi wa-aushrika bihi mā lā ʿilma lī bihī wa-adʿūkum ila al-ʿAzīzi al-Ghaffār*. After the abstract polarity of 40:41 (*najāt* / *nār*), the man states the content on both sides: their invitation is to two acts — *kufr* and *shirk* — and his is to two names of God — *al-ʿAzīz* and *al-Ghaffār*. The compression is exact: the whole of *kufr* is refusing to acknowledge what He has revealed, and the whole of *shirk* is inventing an intermediary for what He has not authorized; and the whole of religion is that a Mighty God exists whose might is matched by His willingness to forgive.

**The Epistemic Crime: "What I Have No Knowledge Of"**

*Mā lā ʿilma lī bihī* — the qualifying clause is the sūrah's own criterion of authority, and it is the reason the verse belongs here rather than in a purely creedal context. The accusation against Pharaoh's religion is not "I dislike it" but "it is unsupported": no knowledge — neither revealed nor demonstrable — stands behind the partners. The Qur'an's standard, stated with unvarying consistency, is *qul hal yaʿlamūna awlā min an yuqrira... say: bring your proof, if you are truthful* (2:111), *and say: God is a witness between me and you; this Qur'an was revealed to me so that I might warn you with it and whoever it reaches* (6:19), and the foundational verse of the epistemology: *do not pursue that of which you have no knowledge; indeed the hearing, the sight, and the heart — all of those will be asked about* (17:36). Ibn Kathīr and al-Ṭabarī both gloss *mā lā ʿilma lī bihī*: "in neither revelation nor intellect is there anything establishing it." Al-Rāzī's extension is the important one for the sūrah's original listeners: the same criterion excludes *their* accusation of sorcery and lying (40:24), for they too had "no knowledge" of it, and the man's words are a mirror — a claim unsupported by knowledge cannot support an execution.

Note too the humility of the wording: he does not say *mā lā wujūda lahu* ("what does not exist") but *mā lā ʿilma lī bihī* — "what I have no knowledge of." The negation is attached to the subject's epistemic position, not to an absolute metaphysical assertion, which is the Qur'an's own manner of arguing about the unseen: the believer is not asked to claim omniscience, only to be certain of what God has told him and to refuse what nobody can show him.

**"The Mighty, the Forgiving": Two Names Chosen Under Torture**

*Al-ʿAzīz al-Ghaffār* occurs only a handful of times in the Qur'an (here; 38:66; 40:42), and *al-ghaffār* in combination with *al-ʿazīz* is otherwise approached at 39:5 (*al-ʿAzīz al-Wahhāb*), 15:49–50 (*al-Ghafūr al-Raḥīm... wa-anne ʿadhāba-hu huwa al-ʿadhābu al-alīm*), and 50:28. The choice is not free variation. A man about to be killed by the strongest state on earth is offering his persecutors a God whose two relevant properties are: nothing can compel Him, and nothing is too great to be covered. That is an entire response to the logic of terror: fear of the *ʿazīz* dwarfs fear of Pharaoh, and hope in *al-ghaffār* dwarfs fear of the crime of having believed in secret for years. Al-Zamakhsharī's remark on this verse in *al-Kashshāf* is that the two names *cancel the court's two weapons* — coercion and the guilt of the accused — and Ibn Kathīr adds that the sūrah has already established the pairing in its own opening (40:3), so the man is quoting the chapter's creed in the chapter's vocabulary.

There is a further, quietly devastating point: he names God *al-Ghaffār* to men who have just resolved to murder children. The implication, drawn by al-Qurṭubī, is that the same forgiveness on offer to them is what their policy excludes; a court that executes the innocents cannot afterwards ask to be covered, since covering is granted to those who ask, and they have not asked, they have decreed. This is precisely the sūrah's later statement of why Pharaoh's last-minute *īmān* fails (40:84–85), and it is why 40:42's two names are placed not as consolation but as an indictment.

**The Grammar of the Two Invitations**

The sentence keeps the *tadʿūnanī / wa-adʿūkum* parallel from 40:41, and adds a striking asymmetry in the objects: their call is toward *an actions* (akfura, ushrika — verbs, things they want him to *do*, and by implication things he would have to keep doing), while his call is toward *a Person*. The commentators note the rhetorical principle: the religion of fear and favor is a set of behaviors performed for patrons; the religion of tawḥīd is a relationship with One who is described, not a set of transactions with many. Al-Rāzī uses the verse in his argument that *duʿāʾ* is addressed only to God because calling implies the assumption that the one called can hear, know, help, and forgive — and four such attributes cannot be distributed among statues without contradiction, exactly as the sūrah's 40:20 and 40:43 will state.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:43

> **43** *Truly there is no doubt that whatever you call me to has no call in this world or in the Hereafter, and that our return is to God, and that the prodigal are the companions of the Fire.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Propositions, Graded by How Much They Can Be Denied**

*Innā maʿa dʿūna ilayhi lā daʿwata lahu fī al-dunyā wa-lā fi al-ākhirati wa-anna maraddanā ila Llāhi wa-anna musrifīna hum aṣḥābu al-nār*. The man's sentence is built like a legal brief with three counts, each harder for the court to reject than the last: (1) the object of their call has no standing *at all*, in this world or the next; (2) the destination of everyone in the room is God; (3) those who exceed the limit will end up in the Fire. Al-Rāzī observes that the sequence is an *argument in stages* because the first is verifiable by experience, the second is a claim about the future that even a materialist of that age conceded as a possibility, and the third is the threat — and the sūrah leaves each proposition to do only the work its evidence supports.

**Lā Daʿwata Lahu: the Wordplay That Carries the Argument**

The phrase is the verse's jewel, and the source commentary preserves both readings, which is what makes it double-edged. *Daʿwa* as "call/invitation": the thing they invite him to has no *call* of its own — that is, it never issued an invitation, was never sent for, and never made a claim; the idols (and by extension the entire patronage-religion of the court) do not even *ask* to be worshipped: *they are heedless of your calling* (46:5), and *they have not tasted death* (27:40's logic), and *do they have hands to strike with... ears to hear with?* (7:195). And *daʿwa* as "claim, right of advocacy, standing in a lawsuit" (*daʿwā* in legal Arabic is a claim, and the *muḍṭarib bi-l-daʿwā* is one who presses a suit): the idols have no *claim* — no right, no title, no standing in any court in this world or the next. The wordplay is the argument, because in Arabic the *daʿwa* and the *daʿwā* are the same root: what cannot call cannot claim, and what cannot claim cannot rescue — which is the exact reason the sūrah has already denied the damned any *khalīl* or *shafīʿ* at 40:18.

The clause *fī al-dunyā wa-lā fī al-ākhirati* is an *a fortiori*: if the idols cannot answer now, they will not answer later; the world is a place of at least some response, and the sūrah has already argued from that principle in 40:14's and 40:60's *duʿāʾ*, where God says *I will respond*. Ibn Kathīr cites 46:5–6 as the sūrah's own gloss: *and who is more astray than one who calls, besides God, that which does not respond to him until the Day of Resurrection, and they are heedless of their call? And when the people are gathered, those [idols] will be enemies to them and deny their worship.* Al-Ṭabarī notes the added sting in *wa-lā fi al-ākhirati*: not only do they not help, they will *disown* — which is precisely the scene the sūrah is about to narrate at 40:73–74 with the partners answering the damned's question "where are they?" — *they have vanished from us; rather, you did not call upon anything before.*

**"Our Return Is to God" — the Sentence That Ends the Argument**

*Wa-anna maraddanā ila Llāh*. The man's second proposition is the sūrah's own third verse, restated in the first person plural: *unto Him is the journey's end* (40:3). This is deliberate and it is the theological engine of the whole speech: once return to God is conceded, the court's verdict ceases to be final, and every decision in the case is provisionally suspended in favor of a hearing to which Pharaoh has no credentials. The Qur'an uses the same sentence as its universal closer — *and to God is the return* occurs at 3:109, 4:172 (in effect), 5:18, 5:26, 7:164, 11:4, 14:2, 22:5, 24:42, 29:20, 31:14, 35:4, 42:53, 57:29, 58:8, 64:4, 65:7, 75:12, 84:6 — and al-Rāzī notes that its power is that it needs no defense: even the polytheists of Makkah affirmed a return to God (31:25, 43:9: *if you ask them who created them, they will say God*), so a man who states 40:43's second proposition has already won the point, and the sūrah knows it (cf. 43:87).

The practical lesson here is worth isolating for the reader: a *shared* eschatological premise is stronger ground than a contested Christological or theological one, and Ghāfir's advocate uses it that way. He does not begin by asserting resurrection, he asserts *return* — a datum the court already owns — and then lets it carry the verdict.

**The Prodigal, the Fire, and the Third Proposition's Function**

*Wa-anna musrifīna hum aṣḥābu al-nār* — and the *musrifūn* are the companions of the Fire. He does not say "you," and the substitution of the class-name for the pronoun is the man's last act of courtesy and his sharpest stroke: *the ones who go past the bank* are the companions of the Fire — you may inspect your own position in that category, and if you are not *musrif*, you have nothing to fear from the verse. Al-Ṭabarī records the gloss: *al-musrifūn*: the polytheists and the unjust, and *here* the killers of the Israelite children, who exceeded every limit that even pagan custom respected. The pairing of *musrif* (40:28, 43, 77's context, 6:141) with *aṣḥāb al-nār* (40:6, 48, 71) closes the sūrah's logic of *isrāf* as the master-sin: not a particular act but the disposition of a being who treats its limits as suggestions.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:44

> **44** *Remember what I say to you — I commend my affair to God. Truly God is All-Seeing of His servants.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Ending the Debate Without Winning It**

*Wa-uwardiku alladhī qultum fa-usallimū; inna Llāha basīrun bi-l-ʿibād*. The speech ends on a note no other verse in this sūrah has prepared: the advocate concedes that he has not persuaded them, refuses to repeat himself, and hands the case over. The verb *udhkurū* (from *dh-k-r*, to make present to mind, the opposite of *nisyān*) is not "remember me" but "remember this"; and the imperative's future tense — *sa-tadhkurūna*, the source's "you will soon remember" — attaches the remembering to the hour when it will do no good, a Qur'anic pattern (cf. 38:88, *you will know about it*; 67:6, *they will ask: did no warner come to you?*; 40:29 and 40:83). Al-Rāzī notes the rhetorical form: the sentence is an *ī'jāz bi-l-qaṣr* (compression by ellipsis) — "you will remember, and it will not profit you" — with the second half suppressed because the court already knows it and a man about to be executed does not need to say it.

**"I Commend My Affair to God": *Tafwīḍ* as the Final Act of a Public Conscience**

*Ufawwiḍu amri ila Llāh*. This is one of the Qur'an's most quoted sentences, because it is the *sunna* of a prophet in a crisis — Yaʿqūb's *and I commend my affair to God; indeed the tyranny comes only from God* (12:67) and *in God is the verdict* (12:67 again, 12:101's *tawaffanī musliman wa-awfiṇī bi-ṣaḥbi-ka*), and Noah's *tawakkaltu ʿalā Llāhi fa-jmaʿū kaydakum wa-shurakāʾakum* (10:71), and Hūd's *I call God to witness, and you, that I am innocent of what you associate besides Him; so plot against me all of you and grant me no respite; I place my trust in God, my Lord and your Lord; there is no creature whose forelock He does not hold* (11:54–56)., and because it is the practical definition of *tawakkul*: action completed, outcome released. The classical distinction the source commentary preserves — *tafwīḍ* before the decree is executed, *taslīm* after it — is transmitted in al-Bayhaqī's *Zuhd*, in al-Sulamī's and al-Qushayrī's *Risāla*, and in al-Harawī's *Madārij al-Sālikīn* (Ibn al-Qayyim's *al-Fawāʾid* repeats it), and it is a fine one: *tafwīḍ* is to give the case to God while it is still undecided, *taslīm* is to accept it after the verdict. The distinction matters because the man is doing the first, not the second: he has not yet been punished, and his act is a *transfer of risk*, not a fatalistic shrug.

The two sayings the source preserves are the most cited on this topic, and both are worth reproducing exactly, since they are among the sharpest lines in the Arabic literature of trust. Dhū al-Nūn al-Miṣrī, asked "when does a servant *tafwīḍ* his affair to God?": *"when he despairs of his own action and his own self, takes refuge in God in every one of his states, and finds no rest in anything other than his Lord."* And Rūzbihān al-Baqlī: *"the perfection of tafwīḍ is that you see no one and no thing possessing benefit or harm, and that you see God through the existentiation of existence in every breath."* (Both quoted in al-Qurtubī's and al-Rāzī's transmission of the Sufi material and in Ibn al-Arābī's *al-Futūḥāt* on the verse.) Neither mystifies the act into passivity; both define it as the extinction of *self-reliance* as a source of security — which is precisely what the believing courtier needed in order to speak at all.

The prophetic corollary is a hadith the sūrah's audience would have recognized as the practical form of this verse:

> *"If you were to rely upon God with true reliance, you would be provided as the birds are provided: they go out hungry and come back full."* (al-Tirmidhī 2344, from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb; graded *ḥasan* by al-Tirmidhī)

The birds *go out*. *Tawakkul* is not staying in the nest, and the man goes out to speak, then commends the result — the two halves the hadith keeps inseparably together, against every fatalism that misreads 40:44 as permission for silence.

**"God Is All-Seeing of His Servants" — the Name That Guarantees the Rest**

*Inna Llāha basīrun bi-l-ʿibād*. The man could have ended with "God suffices me" (cf. 3:173, 8:2, 9:129 and 33:3, 39:38, 73:9: *ḥasbuka... wa-man tawakkala fa-inna-ka ʿazīzun ḥakīm*). He ends instead with *baṣīr*, seeing, because his problem is not primarily protection but *witnessing*: he will be punished by men who will never know what he actually said or why, and the verse is the sūrah's answer to the fear of being misremembered. Al-Qurṭubī notes the theological weight of *ʿibād* here: the one who sees is called *rabb* and the ones seen are *servants*, so the seeing is not surveillance but custody — the way an owner looks after what is his (and cf. the sūrah's 40:15, *upon whom He wills of His servants*, in the context of revelation, and 40:6, *possessor of the Throne*). That the sūrah closes this speech on *basīr*, having begun with "He knows what the breasts conceal" (40:19), makes the section a ring: the whole of the man's ordeal is enclosed in the assertion of a sight that reaches inside the palace walls.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

The Qur'an never reports what happened next. Not one verse in Ghāfir tells us the man was killed, imprisoned, or saved; the narrative resumes at 40:45 with *so God protected him from the evil of what they devised*, and the details belong to transmitted reports whose chains the hadith scholars do not certify (see below on 40:45). That reticence is itself the verse's final lesson: a man who *commends his affair to God* does not get a hagiography in the Qur'an. His reward, as far as scripture is concerned, is the fact that God protected him and the fact that his sentence is recited — which is, historically, a form of immortality that no pharaoh's tower has ever matched.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:45

> **45** *So God protected him from the evil of what they devised, and the people of Pharaoh were encompassed by the worst of punishment.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Verse That Ends a Story and Starts Another**

*Fa-wqāhu Llāhu sayʾa mā makarū wa-ḥāqa bi-āli Firʿawna sūʾu al-ʿadhāb*. The sūrah has spent eighteen verses inside a council chamber; in this single sentence it closes the chamber and opens the sea. The conjunction *fa* here is not merely sequential but consequential: *so* — because of what he said and because of whom he entrusted it to (40:44) — *God protected him*. Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr both stress the causality: the *wiqāya* (protection) is a direct answer to a specific conduct, and the sūrah's first Makkan listeners were being told in the most economical way possible that God's protection of a single honest voice inside a tyranny is a *policy*, not a lucky break.

**Wiqāya: the Shield, and the Punishment as the Thing Warded Off**

*Wa-qāhu*, from *w-q-y*, is to place a shield between a thing and what would strike it; the *wiqāya* is the guarding itself, and the *taqwā* that the Qur'an commands 250 times is its everyday human form. The verb occurs in the Qur'an for deliverance from death (12:110 in effect; *and We protected him from the plot*, 12:33 — Joseph's prayer *wa-illā taṣrif ʿannī kaydahunna aṣbu ilayhinna*, 12:33, is the closest parallel: a prophet who asks to be *turned* from temptation and is answered with the same verb), and for protection on the Day: *but the Fire will not touch them... they will be protected from the evil of that Day* (76:10, actually 76:11 — *fa-wqāhum Llāhu sharra dhālika al-yawmi wa-qaḍḍā fī wujūhihim niḍran wa-surūran*), and the sūrah has already used the same root in the angels' petition: *wa-qihim al-ḥamīra... wa-qihim al-sayyiʾāt* (40:7, 9). So the word links the angels' prayer to this moment: the man of Pharaoh's house is one of the people *for whom those who bear the Throne asked forgiveness and protection*, and 40:45 is the receipt. That intertextual chain is the sūrah's proof that its eschatology and its history are the same system — the angels ask, God guards, and a man walks out of a palace alive.

**Ḥāqa: Encirclement as the Shape of a Self-Inflicted Doom**

*Wa-ḥāqa bi-āli Firʿawna sūʾu al-ʿadhāb*. *Ḥāqa*, from *ḥ-w-q*, means to encircle, to be fixed upon, as the *ḥawqa* of a fortified enclosure; the idiom is the Arabs' own for a doom that closes like a ring. Al-Rāzī, al-Qurṭubī, and the source commentary all note the sūrah's systematic use of it: *their Lord says, so their Lord burdened them with the punishment of the earthquake... they will be encircled by what they used to ridicule* (69:11's wording and 15:17; cf. *wa-ḥāqa bihim mā kānū bihī yastahziʾūn* at 45:33, 39:48, 16:34, 11:8 — *the punishment came to them*, 40:83). The point is not simply poetic: the ring is the geometry of the sūrah's justice. The evil they devised (*makarū*) was designed to close around Moses and the Israelites (40:25's killing of the sons, 40:26's killing of the prophet, 40:36's tower meant to reach heaven and to fence the believers in); and the sūrah's verdict is that the enclosure contracted around its builders. Ibn Kathīr cites as its rule 35:43: *the evil plot envelops only its own people*, and 40:21's *they had no protector from God*: the ring, once complete, has no inside.

**The Worst of Punishment: Two Readings**

*Sūʾ al-ʿadhāb* is taken in the tafsīr literature in two senses, both present in the wording. First, it refers to the drowning in the sea, which is the narrative's immediate sequel (10:90 — *until when drowning overtook him, he said: I believe that there is no god except the One in whom the Children of Israel believe*); second, it refers to the exposure to the Fire described in the very next verse (40:46), which is the *sūʾ* that persists after the drowning, so that "the worst of punishment" is precisely the combination — physical destruction now and the *ʿadhāb* that follows in the *barzakh*. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the sūrah's use of *āli* (household, clan) rather than *qawm*: the judgment falls on a *house*, a system of clients and dependents, which is why 40:46's "the people of Pharaoh" (not "Pharaoh") includes everyone who kept the house running — a point the sūrah's theology of collective responsibility will qualify at 40:47–48.

**What the Sūrah Does Not Report — and Why That Is Safer**

The details later readers want (the man's name, whether he escaped to join Moses, whether he was killed and buried in the Nile mud) come from *Isrāʾīliyyāt* and weak historical reports: *Ḥabīb al-Najjar*, "the carpenter," is named in some transmissions cited in al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr, as is the claim that he was seized, beaten, and died, with the Prophet ﷺ reported in a narration graded as weak by many to have said: *God has mercy on the brother of Pharaoh's people; he came to his people with the best of exhortation and was struck in God's cause, and God gave him martyrdom.* Al-Ḥākim and Ibn ʿAbbās material in the *Mustadrak* and Aḥmad's Musnad transmit versions with chains that the muḥaddithūn (al-Dhahabī among them) criticized. The correct discipline, which the sūrah itself models, is to stop at the verse: God protected him from the evil of their plan. Whatever else happened, the Qur'an has decided that the man's function is to be an *example of speech*, not a legend of martyrdom — and it is worth noting that the sūrah's silence is the reason 40:45 remains one of the most morally productive sentences in scripture: the reader is not permitted to make an exception out of the rescue.

---
## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:46

> **46** *The Fire: they are exposed to it morning and evening. And on the Day when the Hour comes — "Cast the people of Pharaoh into the worst of punishment.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verse That Established a Doctrine**

*Al-nāru yuʿraḍūna ʿalayhā ghuduwwan wa-ʿashiyyan*. Grammatically, *al-nāru* is a nominative either of exposition after the general *sūʾ al-ʿadhāb* of 40:45 ("the worst of punishment — namely, the Fire") or of a new sentence whose subject is the exposure; both readings are transmitted and the doctrine is identical. The passive *yuʿraḍūna*, from *ʿ-r-ḍ*, is the verb of being brought out for inspection: of armies reviewed (*wa-l-ʿarḍ*, the Day when you will be exposed and nothing of you is hidden, 69:18), of deeds laid open (*and the record will be placed, and you will see the criminals afraid of what is in it*, 18:49), and of treasures displayed (28:42 — *and We displayed the treasure*). The sūrah therefore uses a word whose ordinary associations are *parade, audit, and exhibition* for a punishment, and the effect is a humiliation more precise than pain: the people of the most ostentatious court in history are made into a display for onlookers.

**Morning and Evening: the Time That No Longer Measures Anything**

*Ghuduwwan wa-ʿashiyyan* is the Arabic idiom for regularity — the two shifts of a working day, and the sūrah of Ghāfir uses the same pair for the *dhikr* commanded to the Prophet at 40:55. That is the most terrible joke in the verse: the household that was commanded to praise God at eventide and at dawn (40:55) is now *scheduled* for exposure at eventide and at dawn. The rhythm of devotion and the rhythm of punishment use the same words, and the sūrah is telling the reader that a life's cadence is not neutral: what fills your mornings and evenings in this world is what will fill them in the next. Ibn Kathīr makes this explicit, and adds the report that the exposure in the grave is continuous but measured out in the units of the world the dead once lived by, since *dahr* (time) for the *barzakh* is the residue of the life that made it.

**The Grave, the *Barzakh*, and the Argument from This Verse**

This is the sūrah's primary proof-text for *ʿadhāb al-qabr* and for the intermediate state, and the history of that argument is instructive. The traditional reading — al-Ṭabarī's, al-Qurṭubī's, Ibn Kathīr's, and the position of *ahl al-sunna* as al-Ashʿarī states it in *Maqālāt al-Islāmiyyīn* and al-Bāqillānī in *al-Tamhīd* — is that the verse describes a punishment *prior to* the Hour, because 40:46 explicitly separates the two: exposure *morning and evening*, and *then*, on the Day the Hour comes, "cast the people of Pharaoh into the worst of punishment." A punishment mentioned before the Day cannot be the Day's punishment, and al-Qurṭubī argues that the plain sense is being in the *barzakh*; al-Rāzī, while noting the Muʿtazilī and Khārijī objections, concedes that the verse is the strongest textual support for the two-phase eschatology. The dissenting reading, associated with Abū Muslim al-Iṣfahānī in *Taʾwīl al-Maʿānī* and developed by Qāḍī ʿAbd al-Jabbār, takes *ghuduw wa-ʿashiyy* as an idiom for ceaseless recurrence and holds that the exposure belongs to the Day itself, with the "worst punishment" as its intensification. The hadith literature, however, supplies the explicit frame that most readers accept, and it is quoted by the classical commentators at this verse:

> *"When the servant is placed in his grave and his companions turn away, he hears the sound of their sandals; two angels come and sit him up and ask: what did you used to say about this man? As for the believer, he says: I bear witness that he is God's servant and His Messenger; it is said to him: look at your seat in the Fire — God has replaced it for you with a seat in the Garden... and for the hypocrite or disbeliever: I used to hear people say something and I said the same; I do not know. It is said: you did not know and you did not follow; and he is struck between his eyes with an iron hammer, and he cries out, and every creature hears it except the two categories of jinn and humankind."* (al-Bukhārī 1338; Muslim 2870, from Thābit al-Bunānī from Anas)

and the complementary report:

> *"The grave is either a garden from the gardens of Paradise or a pit from the pits of Hell."* (al-Tirmidhī 2460, from Abū Saʿīd; graded *ḥasan* by al-Tirmidhī and *ṣaḥīḥ* by Ibn Ḥibbān and al-Ḥākim)

and, most exactly on the *ʿaraḍ* itself, in Muslim (2866) from ʿĀʾisha and Usama ibn Zayd:

> *"No people were punished but that they are exposed in their graves, so that they know the measure of their punishment."* (the wording in Muslim's chapter on the fitna of the grave and the Dajjāl, with the Prophet ﷺ ceasing to pray over the dead after it was revealed)

The doctrine that results is not morbid but structural: death is not a pause, it is a continuation, and the sūrah's audience is being told that Pharaoh's story did not end at the shoreline.

**"Cast the People of Pharaoh Into the Worst of Punishment"**

*Awwidi qawma Firʿawna ashadda al-ʿadhāb* — the imperative is addressed to the angels of punishment, in the sūrah's characteristic style of reporting the divine command rather than the act (cf. 8:12, 37:62, 39:71, 14:15's *fa-idhā jāʾa amru rabbika...*, and 40:45's passive construction). *Awid* is from *waʾīd*, the threat; the verb means "make them taste what was threatened," and the sūrah has already used *dhūq* (taste) for the same moment at 40:52 and 3:166, 7:39, 45:34 — the vocabulary of tasting stresses immediacy and personal experience rather than duration. And the superlative *ashadd al-ʿadhāb* answers 40:46's earlier *sūʾ al-ʿadhāb*: the worst of it is not the exposure, it is the permanence without relief; the sūrah's own phrase for that elsewhere is *lā yufaṭṭaru ʿanhā* (they will not be released from it, 41:24's context — actually 41:23 and 2:167's *wa-mā hum bi-khārijīn*, and 4:168 — *whom God curses, he has no helper*, 4:52 and 4:173's *wa-ʿadda hum ʿadhāban muhīnā*, "a humiliating punishment," which is the word that recurs from 40:27's *mutakabbir* to 40:60's *dākhurīn*, "debased," and 40:66 and 40:76).

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Answer to Its Own Opening Claim**

Sūrah Ghāfir opened by naming God *Ghāfir al-dhanb* (40:3) and by asserting that the religion is to be devoted entirely to Him (40:14); 40:46 shows what happens when a *household* organizes its entire life around a human being instead. Al-Ṭabarī's comment is the moral of the section: God gave the believing man *wiqāya* and the people of Pharaoh *ḥawqa* in one sentence, so that no reader could suppose the difference lay in anything other than the direction of their *duʿāʾ*. The Makkan hearers, some of whom had families still inside the persecuting establishment, were also told something pastorally sharp: the fate of the household is decided by each member's own answer to the question in the grave — *what did you used to say about this man?* — and not by the surname on the door.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:47

> **47** *And when they argue in the Fire, the weak will say to those who were arrogant: "We were your followers — can you then bear for us a portion of the Fire?" Those who were arrogant will say: "We are all in it. God has judged between His servants.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Trial Inside the Punishment**

*Wa-idh yaḥtajju al-ẓalāmu fī al-nāri fa-qāla alladhīna istuḍʿifū li-lladhīna-ktarū inna kum kānā tabāʿakum fa-hal anṭum ʿannā min ʿadhābi Llāhi min shay'in qāla alladhīna-ktarū in inā kul fīhā inna Llāha qad ḥakama bayna al-ʿibād*. The scene is a cross-examination inside a prison: the leader–follower relationship, which organized every hour of the world, is now the only thing left to argue about. Al-Rāzī observes that the sūrah does not report an argument about God's justice, about prophecy, or about the Qur'an — the disputes of 40:4, 35, 56, and 69 are over — but an argument about *who owes whom*. The questions the disputers refused to answer are gone; the question they never thought to ask — whether the leader has any power at all — is the last question on the docket, and it answers itself in the negative.

**The Vocabulary of Hierarchy, Undone**

*Al-ẓuʿafāʾ* (the weak) and *al-mustakbirūn* (those who consider themselves great) are the sūrah's two standing classes, and their pairing here is the resolution of everything the chapter has been building since 40:27 and 40:35 (*mutakabbir jabbār*). *Istauḍʿafa* is the causative: the strong *treat* the weak as weak, which is an action performed *on* people, not a description of them — and the sūrah elsewhere makes it the defining crime of Pharaoh's regime: *he treated the people as weak, and they obeyed him* (28:4; and 40:29's *ẓāhirīna fī al-arḍ*). In the Fire the causative collapses: the *ẓuʿafāʾ* are no longer weak, because they are addressing their former masters as creditors, and the *mustakbirūn* are no longer great, because their first reply is an admission of shared imprisonment. This is Ghāfir's sociology in a single *ism fāʿil* and one *istifʿāl*: rank is a relational act, and it requires the world's cooperation to exist.

**"We Were Your Followers": the Defense That Convicts**

*Innā kunnā lakum tabāʿa*. *Tabīʿ*, from *t-b-ʿ*, is that which follows — the same root as the *tābiʿūn* — and the phrase is the most honest sentence in the Qur'an's catalogue of excuses, because it is entirely true and entirely useless. The weak are not claiming they were deceived against their will; they are claiming that their *following* was the relevant fact about them. The Qur'an's answer, in the parallel scene, is brutal and precise: *and when the souls are paired, their allies will say: you were not believers; there is no authority of ours over you; rather, you yourselves were a people transgressing; so the word of our Lord has come due upon us, and we have tasted it* (37:62–63, in effect; cf. 14:21–22), and *they say: our Lord, we obeyed our masters and our leaders and they led us astray from the path — our Lord, give them double punishment and curse them with a great curse* (33:67–68). Al-Qurṭubī notes the escalation across the scenes: here the weak *ask* their leaders to carry a portion; at 14:21 they *blame* them; at 33:67–68 they *ask for their punishment to be increased*. The trajectory of the verse, he says, is the progressive dissolution of every human solidarity that was not built on *taqwā*, and its end is the only statement that matters: *rather, you yourselves were a people transgressing* (37:64).

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:48
> **48** *And those in the Fire will say to the keepers of Hell: "Call upon your Lord to lighten for us a day of the punishment." They will say: "Did not your messengers come to you with the clear proofs?" They will say: "Yes." They said: "Then call — and the call of the disbelievers is nothing but in perdition.*

**The Leaders' Reply: a Jurisdictional Deflection**

*Innā kullun fīhā inna Llāha qad ḥakama bayna al-ʿibād*. The leaders concede everything and change the venue in one breath: "we are all in it" (there is no rescue for us either, so the request is void) and "God has judged between the servants" (the case is closed and there is no appeal). Al-Zamakhsharī's comment is that the leaders speak with the tone of a defendant who has stopped pretending to be innocent and is now only arguing that the sentence is final — which is exactly what the sūrah wants from its living audience: a plea that sounds reasonable on the last day, and is worthless, is a plea of the same kind today. Note also the absence of any counter-accusation of God: the damned here are *not* told "you should have thought for yourselves" by their leaders, because the Qur'an reserves the real rebuke to another place (2:167; 14:22 — *I had no authority over you except that I called you and you responded to me; so do not blame me, blame yourselves*). Ibn Kathīr notes that at 40:48 the leaders have already lost the appetite for leadership even in argument.

**"Bear for Us": the Theology of Substituted Punishment Denied**

*Hal antum ḥāmilū ʿannā* — the verb is *ḥamala*, to carry a load, and the request is precisely the request that the Qur'an denies everywhere: *no bearer of burdens bears the burden of another* (6:164, 17:15, 35:18, 39:7, 53:38). Yet the same Qur'an says *they will bear their own burdens and other burdens with them, for what they used to say* (16:25) and, in a verse whose lesson Ghāfir's audience knew by heart: *and We will certainly test you until We know those among you who struggle and are patient, and We will try your reports* (47:31), alongside 29:13 — *and those who disbelieve will say to the believers: follow our road and we will bear your sins; they will bear nothing of their sins.* The reconciliation the commentators give (al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Rāzī) is that *bearing others' burdens* means bearing the *consequences of having caused* others' sins, not bearing their guilt; each bears the full weight of his own act, plus the weight of the act he taught — which is exactly what the hadith states:

> *"Whoever calls to guidance, he has a reward like the rewards of those who follow him, without diminishing their rewards in anything; and whoever calls to misguidance, he has a burden like the burdens of those who follow him, without diminishing their burdens in anything."* (Muslim 2674, from Abū Hurayra)

and its companion: *"Whoever introduces in Islam a good practice, he has its reward and the reward of those who act by it after him... and whoever introduces an evil practice..."* (al-Bukhārī 2324; Muslim 1017). The verse thus does two things at once: it abolishes vicarious punishment and it multiplies the responsibility of leaders — the only asymmetry that is also visible in 40:47–48's structure, where the weak ask for mitigation and the leaders are told their judgment has come due.

**Al-Laʿna: Distance, Not Anger**

*Fa-ʿalayhim laʿnatu Llāhi*, and the source commentary's gloss — the curse is "God's banishment of them from His mercy" — is the right way to read every *laʿna* in the Qur'an, including this one: the word names a *distance* that is also a *judgment*, because the root *l-ʿ-n* means remoteness (al-ʿanā, "the far place"). That is why the verse's second sentence is a verdict and not a wish: *God has judged between His servants* — the *q-ḍ-ʾ* of 40:20's and 40:78's *quḍiya bi-l-ḥaqqi*, which the sūrah uses so that the curse is understood as *jurisdiction*, not as temper. Al-Rāzī's question is why the leaders' own *laʿna* (40:48) should be added to the people's, since they had already said "we are all in it," and his answer is that the admission of common fate is the very thing that makes mutual cursing rational: the *laʿna* is what *ḥizb* becomes when it loses its leader, and the verse is a clinical note on the sociology of collapsed parties.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:49

> **49** *And those in the Fire will say to the keepers of Hell: "Call upon your Lord to lighten for us a day of the punishment." They will say: "Did not your messengers come to you with the clear proofs?" They will say: "Yes." They said: "Then call — and the call of the disbelievers is nothing but in perdition.*

**Downgrading the Intercessor: From Idols to Jails**

In the world they worshipped partners; at 40:47 they asked their *leaders* to carry their punishment; here they ask the *keepers of Hell*. The sūrah's sequence is a ladder of failed address, and each rung is a step in the discovery that no one else is in charge. *Khazana* in the Qur'an is used of custodians of the heavens and earth (42:27's *khazāʾinu al-samāwāti wa-l-arḍ*), of treasure (3:14 in effect; 12:55 — Joseph says *keep me [ḥifẓan] over the storehouses of the land*), and of the Fire's angels (74:30–31, *I have set over the Fire none but angels, and We made their number nothing but a trial for those who disbelieved*). Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record from Mujāhid and others that they are nineteen, chief among them Mālik, and that the report of their number comes with 74:30 — and the Quraysh's reaction to that number is preserved in the sūrah of al-Muddaththir itself: *their Lord said to them: He has nineteen... We have made the number of them nothing but a trial*, and the weak-kneed demand that a messenger bring a *ḥujja* (74:31–36). The request here is therefore doubly absurd, and the angels' reply does not even mention their number: they answer by producing the record of what happened in the world.

**"A Day of the Punishment": the Negotiation for Less**

*Rabbanā akhaff ʿannā yawman min al-ʿadhāb*. Their request is not "save us," not "forgive us" — they have stopped asking for the impossible — but "one day, a little of it," a reduction in intensity. Al-Rāzī treats this as one of the Qur'an's starkest proofs that the punishment of the persistently impenitent has no exit condition at all, because at this point the request has been stripped to its minimum and is still refused. He also notes the mercy implied in the refusal: had God accepted a *lightening*, the demand for it would have been the last hope; the sūrah will state the closure in 23:108: *be despised in it, and speak not to Me*.

The irony of the phrasing is not lost on the classical commentators: they ask to have *one day* lightened, in a place where 40:46 has already described exposure *morning and evening*, and the sūrah's whole audience knows that the unit they are haggling over is the one they wasted in 40:39 (*matāʿu qalīl*). The commodity of the next life — a *day* — is the same commodity of this one, and every day of *ḥubb al-jāh* and *takdhīb* was one of the days they now beg back.

**The Angels' Counter-Question: the Case Reopened on Its Only Relevant Point**

*Awa lam takun jāʾatkum rusulukum bi-l-bayyināti? Qālū balā*. The angels, rather than answering the petition, put the question that 40:17 and 40:20 established as the only one on which a verdict can rest. Ibn Kathīr notes that *rusulukum* — "your messengers," with the possessive — is used here, as in 40:22, to close every claim of unfamiliarity: the message was addressed to *them*, in their own language, by their own kind (cf. 7:59, 65, 73, 85, 96 and 14:11 — *messengers from among them*). Al-Qurṭubī's observation: no punishment is ever discussed in the Qur'an without the *bayyināt* being named first — a rhetorical discipline that keeps the theology of justice intact even in the Fire. And the answer is one word: *balā*, "yes," the word that affirms against a negative, which in Arabic is used when one confirms what the questioner expected to be denied — a linguistic mark of admission forced out of them, not volunteered. The sūrah elsewhere records the same admission and its futility: *they will say: yes, indeed — but the word of torment has come due upon your Lord, and they have become desperate* (7:44), and 67:8–9 — *how is your state? They will say: our worst state, and You were sending no warner to us* — and 67:10–11, the closest parallel, where they name three causes of their ruin: *and they will say: if we had only listened or understood, we would not be among the companions of the Blaze; so they will confess their sin* — that is, *samʿ* and *ʿaql*, hearing and reason, are the two faculties the sūrah has insisted they had (40:20's *al-Samīʿ al-Baṣīr*, 40:13's *yatafakkaru*, 40:58's *a-ʿamī wa-l-baṣīr*), and confession that comes after the loss of both is called a confession *of the limbs*: *until, when they reach it, their hearing, their eyes, and their skins testify against them* (41:20).

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:50
> **50** *Truly We will help Our messengers and those who believe in the life of this world and on the Day when the witnesses stand forth.*

**"Then Call!" — and Why It Is Nothing but *Talāq***

*Qālū fa-dʿū*. The angels' response is the cruelest sentence in the sūrah because it is a *concession*: call, and see. Ibn ʿAbbās and Qatāda, as al-Ṭabarī transmits, explain: they would call and no answer would come; the angels are telling them to test the claim themselves, which is the Qur'an's repeated method of *imitḥān* in this world (*or whom do you call upon, besides Him, that, if God's punishment comes to you or the Hour comes to you, you would call upon other than God? No — it is He you would call upon, and He would remove that for which you called, if He willed*, 6:41; cf. 10:22, 17:67, 29:65 — and 35:14 — *if you call them, they do not hear your call, and if they heard, they would not answer you; and on the Day of Resurrection they will disown your association*). Al-Rāzī's analysis of the logic is the sharpest: an appeal to a being that cannot hear, and to a God who has already stated the term of the deadline, is not a supplication but a *performative contradiction*, and the verse's verdict — *wa-mā duʿāʾu al-kāfirīna illā fī ḍalāl* — is that the misdirection is in the act itself. *Ḍalāl*, from *ḍ-l-l*, is the loss of the road (a *muḍill* is one who loses his camel in the desert), and the sūrah has used the identical phrase twice already for human projects: *the scheming of the disbelievers is nothing but in ḍalāl* (40:25) and *the scheme of Pharaoh was nothing but in ḍalāl* (40:37, with *tabāb*). So the sūrah's law is uniform: whatever is done *away from* God is not merely unsuccessful, it is *mis-aimed* — duʿāʾ, *makr*, and *kayd* all share the same ending, and this is the reason 40:60 immediately follows the sūrah's cosmic signs with *wa-qāla rabbukumu-dʿūnī astajib lakum* ("and your Lord said: call upon Me and I will answer you"): one *duʿāʾ* is in *ḍalāl*, one is answered, and the difference is not the eloquence of the petitioner but the object.

**The Pastoral Point of the Pairing**

The sūrah ends its Moses-cycle (40:50) with a *duʿāʾ* that is void, and begins its next section (40:51) with God's own oath-like promise: *We will help Our messengers*. The Makkan reader, who has been hearing about the persecution and drowning of others for twenty-eight verses, is given the chapter's conclusion in two adjacent sentences: supplication offered to a human court in fear is *ḍalāl*; supplication offered to God is *istijāba*. That is also the last lesson the believing man of 40:44 taught by example, and it is why the sūrah will, three verses later, command patience and *istighfār* at eventide and at dawn (40:55) — the rhythm that 40:46 has just shown being used for exposure instead.

**The Pastoral Sentence Inside a Threat**

The source commentary's point about *wa-mā duʿāʾu al-kāfirīna illā fī ḍalālin* — "the loss is complete, and their supplications are utterly futile" — is best felt in reverse, in the sūrah's other half, where the same words describe the living: the Makkan believers were, at this moment, the people whose *duʿāʾ* also seemed unanswered, and 40:50 tells them that *inḍalāl al-duʿāʾ* (dissipation) has a diagnostic shape: it is what happens to a call made to a *rebellious* God while *turning away* (istikbār, 40:60) is still the stance of the caller. The verse's lesson to the persecuted is therefore not "your prayers are fine and theirs are not" but "there is one condition under which a call is lost, and it is the condition of *istikbār*, which you do not have." That is why the sūrah's most consoling sentence (40:60) is three verses away rather than three chapters.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:51

> **51** *Truly We will help Our messengers and those who believe in the life of this world and on the Day when the witnesses stand forth.*

**An Oath-Shaped Promise in the Middle of the Chapter**

*Innanā lananṣuru rusulana wa-lladhīna āmanū fī al-ḥayāti al-dunyā wa-yawma yaqūmu al-ʾashhād*. The verse is built for emphasis in three layers at once: the *inna*, the *lām* of the verb, and the *nun* of the imperfect — a triple asseveration, which in the Qur'an is reserved for moments when the audience is at its weakest. The Makkan community at this point had no army, no treasury, and no prospect; the sūrah has just spent a quarter of itself on the drowning of a world-power, and now it commits God to a promise in the most emphatic Arabic available. Al-Ṭabarī glosses *lananṣuranna*: "We will certainly avenge them upon their enemies and establish them, and We will make them the winners in the argument and in the sword."

**Two Arenas of Victory, and a Rule About Which One Comes First**

*Fī al-ḥayāti al-dunyā* — the promise includes this world, and the sūrah's own commentators enumerate its historical realizations without straining: David and Solomon enthroned, Moses drowning Pharaoh, Noah inheriting the earth after his people's drowning, Jesus' religion carried by his followers after his ascension (Ṭ). The Qur'an's general law is stated at 9:40, 9:14, 22:38–39, and in the *sunnah* formula: *We have written in the Psalms, after the Reminder: My righteous servants will inherit the earth* (21:105), and *the earth belongs to God; He gives it as an inheritance to whom He will of His servants, and the end is for God-consciousness* (7:128). But the second arena — *yawma yaqūmu al-ʾashhād* — is the guarantee that makes the first one meaningful even when it is delayed, and the sūrah's order is careful: this world *and* that Day. Ibn Kathīr notes that the conjunction promises *both*, and that no verse in the Qur'an promises worldly victory unconditionally; the condition is stated in 47:7 in the other direction: *if you help God, He will help you and make your feet firm*. The promise is not a timetable; it is a certainty that the last word is with the messengers — and history, in the sūrah's own reading of the *āthār* (40:21), is the accumulation of illustrations.

**"And Those Who Believe": the Promise Is Not Only About Prophets**

*Rusulanā wa-lladhīna āmanū*: the believers are joined to the messengers in the same verb of aid, which is why the sūrah's long digression on the man of Pharaoh's house matters structurally. The believing man "won" without winning anything visible: he was *protected from the evil of their scheme* (40:45), and this verse now states the general rule that his case illustrates. Al-Rāzī draws from it a definition of *naṣr* that the tradition repeats: God's aid to a servant is not measured by the number of enemies killed but by the evil he was turned from, the truth he was enabled to speak, and the *ʿāqiba* that ends with him — which is why the Prophet ﷺ said, in the two Ṣaḥīḥs (al-Bukhārī 3674, Muslim 1786, from ʿAbdullāh ibn al-Zubayr at the siege of the Ditch):

> *"O God, Sender of the Book, Swift in the reckoning, defeat the confederates! O God, defeat them and shake them!"*

and why the sūrah of Muḥammad says *God will disgrace them and make their deeds fruitless* (47:25) after promising the same aid.

**The Witnesses**

*Al-ʾashhād* — the plural of *shahīd* — is defined in the sources as a set of witnesses, and al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr list all the candidates without choosing exclusively: (1) the prophets, who testify with the measure of justice (*every people will be brought, and We will produce against them a witness from among themselves*, 45:19; *how will it be when We bring from every nation a witness and bring you as a witness over these?* 16:89; and 2:141 — *and thus We made you a middle nation, that you may be witnesses over the people and the Messenger a witness over you*, and 22:78, and 5:117 where ʿĪsā says *I was a witness over them as long as I was among them*); (2) the angels, of whom the sūrah of Jāthiya says *and We brought others besides them as witnesses* in effect (10:46 — *and We bring others as witnesses*); (3) the record-keepers, *al-ʿadl*, *khālaṣa min kulli umma* (see 16:89 and 28:75 in effect); (4) the created things themselves — the skin, the earth, the limbs (41:20–21, 99:4 — *that Day it will tell its news*), and (5) the community's own dead and absent. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the tense: *yaqūmu*, "when the witnesses stand," is a legal image — witnesses in an Arab court stood to give testimony and did not sit while doing it; the Day is a *qiyām* twice over, the resurrection of bodies and the rising of testimony. And Ghāfir's own contribution to the theme has been made already, in the man who *stood* and testified before the *malāʾ*, which is the verse's quiet self-reference: the witnesses who stand on that Day include men who were the only ones to stand in the councils of this one.

**Practical Reach: How to Live Inside a Promise**

Because the promise is asseverated but not dated, the Qur'an's own companion verse is 3:144 — *Muḥammad is only a Messenger; messengers have passed before him* — and Ghāfir will place that lesson in 40:77 in this very passage: *whether We show you part of what We promise them, or We take you*. The two verses together (40:51 and 40:77) are the sūrah's answer to the two temptations of every persecuted community: despair, because nothing has visibly happened, and opportunism, because something might. The believer stands with the promise in force and the deadline unknown, which is exactly the position of 40:55's injunctions: patience, forgiveness-seeking, and praise at the two ends of the day.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:52

> **52** *The Day when their excuses will not benefit the wrongdoers, and upon them is the curse and theirs is the worst of homes.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Court Where Argument Ends**

*Yawma lā yanfaʿu al-ẓālimīna maʿdhiratuhum wa-lahumu al-laʿnatu wa-lahum sūʾu al-dār*. The verse resumes the Day's scene from 40:51 and adds three facts about it. First, excuses will be useless — not because they are weak, but because the case has moved from *investigation* to *execution*. Second, *the curse is theirs*. Third, *the worst of homes is theirs*. The three are not repetitions: they are, respectively, the failure of defense, the loss of relationship, and the loss of place. A person reduced to those three conditions is precisely what the sūrah means by *ẓulm* having run its course.

**Maʿdhirat: the Grammar of an Alibi**

*Maʿdhira*, from *ʿ-dh-r*, is the noun of the instrument or place of excusing — that by which, or in which, one is *ʿudhr* (i.e., legally not blameworthy); the same root gives *iʿtadhara*, *taʿdhīr*, and the legal term *ʿudhr qāhīr* (overwhelming compulsion). The Qur'an's use of the word is a study in the difference between a real *ʿudhr* and an *excuse*: the *maʿādhīr* are said to be useless on that Day at 5:36 (*they will wish to be ransomed with all the wealth in the earth and as much again with it; and they will hide their remorse from God*), at 18:52, at 25:22, at 30:57 (*the Day when their excuses will not benefit those who did wrong, and upon them is the curse and theirs is the worst of homes* — the near-identical twin of this verse, from the sūrah of al-Rūm), and at 40:52. What *does* benefit is named elsewhere and never here: faith, repentance before the seal (40:3), and a *ḥujja* — which is why al-Rāzī comments that the sūrah's whole vocabulary of *ḥujja*, *sulṭān*, and *bayyina* is the promise that excuses are not the currency of that court; *evidence* is, and evidence was available (40:49–50). Al-Qurṭubī adds the legal nuance that a *maʿdhira* is not the same as a *ḥujja*: the first is what the defendant offers for himself; the second is what stands whether or not he offers it — and the sūrah's damned have the second against them and only the first for them.

**The Curse: Distance as Punishment**

*Wa-lahumu al-laʿna*. *Laʿna*, from *l-ʿ-n*, is being driven away from mercy, and the Lisan and *Tahdhīb al-Lugha* record the concrete usage: a *lāʿin* is a horse that has been expelled from the herd, and the *liʿān* between spouses (24:6–9) is a mutual invoking of God's curse, which shows how deeply the word meant *removal of association*, not emotional violence. The Qur'an applies it to those who conceal what God sent down (2:159 — *upon them is the curse of God and the cursers, and of the angels and of all the people, collectively*, and 2:161 — *the curse of God upon the liars, and the angels and the people all together*), and here in Ghāfir it is attached to the *ẓālimūn* of 40:47–48 whose leaders promised *they will bear nothing of their sins* and to those whose *makr* is *ḍalāl*. Al-Ṭabarī notes that *lahum* with the *lām al-mulk* makes the curse a *property* of theirs: it clings to them the way a title attaches to an estate, which is the same grammar as 40:6's *ʿalayhim* for the word that came due. Ibn Kathīr's gloss on the phrase: *their being cursed by their Lord, driven far from His mercy; for the people of the Curse are the people of the Fire, and this is a curse in this world, since the curse of God upon them was already written and the angels and the believers curse them, and they are cursed by all creation*.

**"The Worst of Homes" — *Dār* and Its Two Uses**

The sūrah has just described Paradise as *dār al-qarār* (40:39) and here describes Hell as *sūʾu al-dār* — the *dār* is the same word, and the point of using it for both is that the punishment is not a torture-chamber but a *home*: the place where one's existence continues, furnished by one's own habits (cf. 3:151, 3:197 — *an evil lodging*; 14:29 — *the abode of arrogance*; 39:72 — *an evil abode*; 66:10 — *their abode is the Fire*). Al-Rāzī treats this as the Qur'an's answer to the objection that punishment by fire is physically excessive for temporal sins: the sin does not receive a quantity of pain, it receives an environment; the *ẓālim* chose the company that God confirms, and a *dār* is what a confirmed choice becomes when the time for changing it is over. It is also worth noticing that 40:52 closes the sūrah's account of Pharaoh's household with the same word used in 40:46 of their exposure: the "worst of homes" is *home*, and *being exposed morning and evening* is *the furniture*, which is why the sūrah's audience should not expect anything to be added at the Hour that was not already true in the grave — and the same symmetry, inverted, is what makes 40:8's "gardens of Eden" a promise rather than a reward: a *dār* fits the person who is already at home in it.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:53

> **53** *And indeed We gave Moses the guidance, and We made the Children of Israel inherit the Book,*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah Zooms Out From One Court to a Book**

*Wa-laqa ātaynā Mūsā al-hudā wa-awrathnā Banī Isrāʾīla al-kitāb*. The Moses-story (40:23–46) has ended with a drowning, and 40:53 restates the whole episode in two clauses: Moses was *given* guidance, and his people were *made to inherit* a Book. The verse's work is to shift the sūrah's audience from the fate of tyrants to the transmission of guidance — from how the story ended to what the story leaves behind — and the shift is signaled by the change of verb: *ātaynā* (We gave) and *awrathnā* (We made inherit), both perfect-tense, both with the royal plural, so that the giving and the inheritance are treated as completed acts of God rather than human achievements.

**"The Guidance," With the Article**

Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr record the reading of *al-hudā* as a title: the Torah itself, or, more precisely, *al-hudā* as the guidance that the sūrah has already named for Moses in other words: *We gave him the Book and made it a light for the Children of Israel* (40:53's twin at 17:2 — *and We gave Moses the Book and made it a guidance for the Children of Israel*); and *and We gave Moses the Book, completing [our favor] upon the one who did well, and detailing of all things, and a guidance and a mercy* (6:154). The definite article, on the reading of al-Zamakhsharī, means the specific guidance — namely, the guidance of *tawḥīd*, and the guidance of the way of deliverance — since in the sūrah's context Moses was engaged in a contest not of general piety but of who is the Lord of the heavens (40:36–37). The sūrah has itself stated the other half of the same truth at 40:33 — *whomever God misguides, he has no guide* — and at 40:35 — *whomever God guides, he is the guided one* — so the pairing here matters: God *gave* Moses the guidance as a gift, exactly as He gives guidance, and Pharaoh's *rashād* (40:29, 38) was a claim, and claims and gifts have different shelf-lives.

**ʿIṣr al-Nubuwwa: the "Burden" of Being Entrusted With the Book**

*Wa-aṭa... khaṭru al-kitābi*: the Qur'an elsewhere describes this inheritance as a weight rather than an honor — *We offered the trust to the heavens, the earth, and the mountains, but they refused to carry it and were afraid of it, and humankind carried it* (33:72) — and the tradition explains that what humankind took on was *amāna*, the accountability of revelation, which is the sense in which Ibn ʿAbbās is reported to gloss *al-amāna* here as *al-ṭāʿāt*, the acts of obedience. Al-Qurṭubī quotes at this verse the famous statement of ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib, as transmitted by al-Ḥākim and Ibn ʿAsākir: *God took a covenant from the scholars: you shall explain it to people and not hide it.* And Ibn ʿAbbās' saying, transmitted in ʿAbd al-Razzāq's *Muṣannaf* and Aḥmad's *Musnad* with a chain some judge weak but which is one of the most quoted maxims of the tradition, is the classical gloss on *inheritance of the Book*: *"knowledge is better than wealth; knowledge guards you, while you guard wealth; wealth decreases with spending, and knowledge comes back when you spend it; the possessor of wealth has one enemy and the possessor of knowledge has many admirers, and the death of a possessor of knowledge is a loss that no gap can fill."* That reason the maxim is attached to our verse is that this is the locus classicus of *al-ʿilmu mīrāth* — knowledge as inheritance — which is why the tradition adduces it for the ruling that *the scholars are the heirs of the prophets*: *"the prophets left neither dirham nor dinar; they left knowledge, and whoever takes it has taken a great share"* (Abū Dāwūd 3641, al-Tirmidhī 2682, Ibn Mājah 223, from Abū al-Dardāʾ, graded *ḥasan* by al-Tirmidhī).

**Why "Inherit" Is the Right Word for Revelation**

*Īrāth*, from *w-r-th*, is the transmission of a thing after the death of its first holder — the *mīrāth* is an estate. The choice tells the reader three things at once. (1) Guidance survives its carriers: Moses is dead and the Book remains; and this is the sūrah's answer to the very human anxiety of a follower who loses his leader — expressed in the Qur'an by ʿImrān's family (3:144) and answered here in the past tense: *awrathnā*. (2) Inheritance implies a *debt of stewardship*: an heir does not own the estate freely, he answers for it; the Children of Israel are described in the same sūrah's sequel as those who "differed about it" (42:14 in effect; cf. 2:213, 3:105, 6:65) and whose inheritance is a warning to the *umma*, not a certificate. (3) Inheritance is unearned by the individual — the child of Israel received the Book before he did anything, and this is why 40:53's *aṭānā* is grace and 40:54's *dhikr* is a task: what is given free is not free of obligation.

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Turn Toward the Prophet ﷺ**

Having stated the Mosaic inheritance, the sūrah will move in two verses to an imperative addressed to Muhammad ﷺ: *fa-ṣṭabir* (40:55). The logic of that move is the logic of Ghāfir's whole chapter: the promise (40:51) is stated, the Book (40:53) is handed on, and the man who has inherited something that survived a drowning is told to be patient. Al-Rāzī notes that *awrithnā al-kitāb* is also, in context, a rebuttal of Pharaoh's calculation at 40:34 — *God will not send a messenger after him*: Egypt could bury Joseph, but it could not bury a Book; and the sūrah's own first hearers, whose Prophet was mortal and whose enemies were numerous, were being told the same thing about the Qur'an (cf. 15:9 — *We have sent down the Reminder and We are its guardians*).

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:54

> **54** *A guidance and a reminder for the possessors of depth.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Functions of a Book, Named in the Passive**

*Hudan wa-dhikra li-ʾulī al-nuhā*. The verse takes the Book inherited in 40:53 and gives it a double purpose: *hudā* (direction, showing the road) and *dhikr* (reminder, that which returns a thing to mind). The distinction is the Qur'an's own and the commentators keep it in view: direction is for the person who has not yet seen where to go; reminder is for the person who has seen and forgotten. This is why the sūrah of Qāf opens *Qāf. By the glorious Qur'an — rather they marvel that a warner from among them has come to them; so the disbelievers say: this is an astonishing thing* (50:1–2), and why the Qur'an's most-used self-designation is *dhikr*: *We have sent down the Reminder (al-dhikr) and We are its guardian* (15:9), *and We made the Qur'an easy for reminder — is there any who will be reminded?* (54:17, 22, 32, 40), *a Reminder for all the worlds* (81:27). *Dhikr* shares its root with *dhikrā*, "the heart's act of holding something present," and with *mudhakkir*, the one who admonishes; the sūrah's own verb for the man of Pharaoh's house is *u-udhkurū mā aqūlu lakum* (40:44), which is Ghāfir's definition of what it is doing to *its* audience.

**"The Possessors of Depth": *Nuhā* and the Anatomy of Intelligence**

*ʾUlū al-nuhā* occurs only in three places in the Qur'an: 39:18, 39:21, and here, and each time with *dhikra* or in a context of attentive listening — 39:18 — *they listen to speech and follow the best of it; those are the ones God has guided, and those are the possessors of depth.* The lexicographers explain the word: *nuhā* is intellect, and it is derived from *nāʾikhat al-tamr*, softened dates, because the intellect "softens and moistens" the soul's hardness, or — the second etymology in Ibn Fāris and al-Azharī — from the *nākh*, the marrow, the interiormost of the bone, hence the mind as the marrow of a human being. On either etymology, the phrase *dhikra li-ʾulī al-nuhā* says that a reminder is only received where there is depth: the surface is impermeable to *dhikr*. This is the sūrah's own criterion restated: the *nukh* (forelock) is what a person is dragged by, and the *ʿaql* is what restrains — *each [pair of] jinn and humankind, whoever turns away, God will bind his forelock* in effect, cf. 55:41 and 96:15 — *We will seize the forelock, the lying, sinful forelock*.

The choice of *nuhā* here rather than the sūrah's more frequent *albāb* (the plural of *lubb*, the pure kernel) is treated by al-Zamakhsharī and al-Rāzī as a stylistic match: *dhikr* is a liquid, and *nuhā* is the softening that lets it be absorbed; the Book is called *dhikra* to *ulū al-nuhā* because remembrance works on minds that yield rather than bounce.

**A Reminder Also Explains Why the Story Is Long**

The sūrah has spent twenty-eight verses on a narrative that the source commentary calls "a spiritual example rather than history," and 40:54 supplies its technical justification: the Torah was *dhikr*, and *dhikr* is served by narrative detail, because memory — in oral cultures and our own — is stored in *scenes*, not in propositions. That is the principle behind 12:111's verdict on the story of Joseph, which the sūrah of Ghāfir echoes almost word for word: *there is, in their accounts, a lesson for the possessors of depth; it is not a tale that can be fabricated, but a confirmation of what is between its hands.* Al-Biqāʿī, whose *Naẓm al-Durar* reads the entire Qur'an through exactly this pairing of verses, treats 40:53–54 as the *ghāya* (purpose) clause of the whole Moses section — i.e., the sūrah tells its own story's *why*, and the why is not information but *dhikr*.

**Who, Then, Are Not Addressed**

The qualifier is exclusive in force, and the sūrah has already named the reason in 40:35 and 40:12: the sealed heart and the arrogant breast receive nothing, because a reminder presupposes a memory that has not been overwritten. Al-Ṭabarī's gloss is worth preserving for its realism: *the reminder is of benefit to the possessors of depth, because they are the ones who return to what they were reminded of; as for the others, the reminder only increases their evidence against them.* This is the same logic as 17:82 — *and We send down of the Qur'an that which is healing and mercy for the believers, and it does not increase the wrongdoers except in loss* — and it explains a difficulty in 40:49–52 that every reader feels: why do the excuses of the damned fail? Because the faculty that files a reminder was, in this world, used for *jidāl*. The practical reading the commentators draw is both sobering and useful: the *dhikr* is currently working on you, and the presence or absence of its effect is the diagnostic — *if the reminder moves you, you are among ʾulū al-nuhā; if it only gives you material for argument, read 40:35 again.*

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:55

> **55** *So be patient. The promise of God is true. And seek forgiveness for your fault, and glorify your Lord with praise at evening and at dawn.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Prescriptions for a Man Under Pressure**

*Fa-ṣṭabir wa-istaghfir li-dhanbika wa-sabbiḥ bi-ḥamdi rabbika bi-ʿashiyyin wa-ṣubḥān* — "so be patient, and seek forgiveness for your fault, and glorify the praise of your Lord at evening and at dawn." The sūrah, having shown how a prophet's people and a court's lone dissenter behaved, turns from the past tense of narrative to three imperatives addressed to the Prophet ﷺ — and, since revelation is not addressed to one man alone, to every believer who reads it in the same position. The sequence is a complete program: endurance, self-repair, and praise at fixed hours. Its ordering is psychologically exact: the hurt person is told first to stop reacting (*ṣabr*), then to clean his own side of the ledger (*istighfār*), then to resume the rhythm of worship (*tasbīḥ*) — not in the other order, and not by first securing an apology from his tormentors.

**Sabr: the Virtue That Is Not Stoicism**

*Ṣabr*, from ṣ-b-r, means *restraining*: a *ṣābir* in pre-Islamic usage is a man who holds himself in place when he could run, and the Arabs say *ṣabartu nafsī ʿalā al-amr*, "I held my self to the matter." The Qur'an's vocabulary distinguishes it from passivity in three ways visible here. (1) It is *ʿalā*, an active bearing *of* something: *be patient over what they say* (also 6:111, 20:130, 26:127, 52:48, 76:5, 46:35 — *be patient as the messengers of resolve were patient*, which the source commentary cites in its next note). (2) It is commanded in the *ṣiygha* of a *fa* of result (*fa-ṣṭabir*) — i.e., patience is *produced* by the preceding promises (40:51, 53), not by willpower alone; al-Rāzī makes this the key to the verse: whoever knows that aid is promised and that the Book is inherited can bear what the same man cannot bear without those two facts. (3) Its model is the *ʿabīd* of 40:28–45, whose patience was not silent: he spoke, and patience was what let him finish. The hadith supplies the practical definition of the *fruit* of patience — not relief but compensation: *"no one is given a better and vaster gift than patience"* (al-Bukhārī 1469, Muslim 1053, from Abū Saʿīd), and the verse of the two Ṣaḥīḥs cited by Ibn Kathīr here: *those who, when a calamity strikes them, say: to God we belong, and to Him we return; upon them are blessings from their Lord and mercy, and those are the guided ones* (2:156–157), which the sūrah pairs with *and the patience of Job* (38:44, 38:41 in effect).

**"Seek Forgiveness for Your Fault" — the Question of Prophetic *ʿIṣma***
