*Wa-istaghfir li-dhanbika*. The verse raised the sharpest doctrinal debate in the section, and both sides are preserved in the source. (1) For those who hold that prophets may commit minor faults (*ṣaghāʾir*) and that these are real but are quickly repented from — a position associated with some Muʿtazilī and Ḥanafī juristic treatments of *zalla* — the verse is direct evidence that even the best of creation is commanded to *istighfār*. (2) For the majority — *ahl al-sunna*'s position that prophets are *maʿṣūm* from lying about God and from persisting in sin, defended at length by al-Qāḍī ʿIyāḍ in *al-Shifāʾ*, al-Rāzī in *ʿAwāṣim*, al-Juwaynī in *al-Irshād*, and Ibn Ḥazm in *al-Fiṣal* — the command is either (a) in the sense of *shukr*, so that seeking forgiveness is itself an expression of humility before the magnitude of God's favor (Ibn Kathīr's report from Ibn ʿAbbās: *God commanded him with istighfār and made his forgiveness a mercy for his community* — cf. the famous gloss "his *istighfār* is for him and *tawba* for his community" transmitted from al-Ḥasan al-Baṭrī and quoted by al-Qurṭubī), or (b) addressed to the community through the address to the Prophet, since *khāṭib al-ʿāmm murādun bi-hi al-ʿāmm wa-khāṣṣu al-khāṣṣ al-ʿāmm* — and the sūrah's own use of *dhanb* for sin generally (40:11's *dhunūbanā*) makes the second reading natural. The hadith literature settles the practical side and shows the Prophet ﷺ performing exactly this verse: *"By God, I seek God's forgiveness and repent to Him more than seventy times in a day"* (al-Bukhārī 6307, from Abū Hurayra) and, in Muslim's wording, *"I used to seek God's forgiveness in a day a hundred times"* (Muslim 2702, from al-Mughīra) — reports that the commentators quote precisely because they answer the Muʿtazilī inference: the *istighfār* is not evidence of a deficiency in the one who asks, but evidence of the distance between a servant's gratitude and a Lord's favors.

The pastoral effect of placing *istighfār* between patience and praise should not be missed. A community that has been wronged is liable to develop a *victim's certainty of purity*, and this verse inserts *li-dhanbika* — for *your* fault — into the middle of the response to persecution. The tradition's summary of the same discipline is the report in Aḥmad and al-Ḥākim from Ibn Masʿūd, graded *ḥasan* by some: *"the believer sees his sins as though he were sitting under a mountain that is about to fall on him, and the hypocrite sees his sins as a fly on his nose — he waves it away and it is gone."* (al-Bukhārī 6308) Ghāfir's Prophet is taught to keep the mountain in view even while his people are being killed, and that combination — moral seriousness toward the self, patience toward the enemy — is the sūrah's answer to the moral hazard of every righteous cause.

**Praise at Evening and Dawn: the Architecture of a Day**

*Wa-sabbiḥ bi-ḥamdi rabbika bi-ʿashiyyin wa-ṣubḥān*. The *tasbīḥ* (declaring God remote from defect) is bound to *ḥamd* (affirming praise), which is the same pairing as the angels in 40:7 — *yusabbiḥūna bi-ḥamdi rabbihim* — so the sūrah has arranged a correspondence: the household of Pharaoh is exposed to the Fire *morning and evening*, while the Prophet is commanded to praise *morning and evening*, and both are living in the same rhythm of *ghuduww* and *ʿashiyy*, at the same hour, with the same frequency. Al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Qurṭubī record the practical identifications: *al-ʿashiyy* covers the time from the sun's decline to sunset, and *ṣubḥ* the time from dawn to sunrise, which is why some (Āl, and in part Q and Ṭs, hence the claim about the verse's "Madinan" character) read the phrase as a reference to the prayers whose obligation was established late — ʿaṣr and ẓuhr at one end, fajr at the other; and why others read it as the two *rakʿas* before ʿaṣr and before sunrise, the prayers the Prophet ﷺ called "dearer to me than the world and what is in it" (al-Bukhārī 1129, Muslim 725) and commanded *between the two calls* (i.e., before ʿaṣr) as in al-Bukhārī 1130; and 40:55's *bi-ʿashiyyin wa-ṣubḥān* is thus a *mubham* (unspecified) that the Sunna fills. The same idiom occurs in the angels' language in 33:42 — *O you who believe, remember God with much remembrance, and glorify Him morning and evening* — and the verse's broader function is *dhikr* at the boundaries of the day, which the hadith makes a shelter: *"Whoever says at daybreak and at dusk: I testify that God, besides whom there is no god, and that Muhammad is His servant and His Messenger — God will free him from the Fire, or will enter him into Paradise — a hundred times."* (Muslim 2692, from Anas) and, in the tradition of Abū Dharr, the Prophet ﷺ said: *"Whoever says, at daybreak, twelve times: blessed be God, Lord of the worlds — it will be a protection for him from every accursed devil"* (Aḥmad 5/152 and al-Ḥākim, in a chain whose strength the muḥaddithūn discuss). Ibn Kathīr, quoting these, notes the sūrah's own general rule: *and be patient, and your patience is only from God* (16:127), and *and remind, for reminder benefits the believers* (51:55) — the fixed hours exist because a heart that is left to its own momentum drifts, and praise at both ends of the day is the rope by which a man holds himself at the length the revelation prescribes. The *dhikr* is thus the sūrah's answer to the *khalīl* it denied the damned at 40:18: the believer's true companion at morning and evening is praise, and Pharaoh's house was left with argument.

---
## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:56

> **56** *Truly those who dispute concerning the signs of God without any authority that has come to them — there is nothing in their breasts except a self-importance they will never attain. So seek refuge in God; truly He is the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain, With the Motive Now Exposed**

*Inna lladhīna yujādilūna fī āyāti Llāhi bi-ghayri sulṭānin jāʾahum in fī ṣudūri-him illā kifirun mā hum bi-bālighīhi*. The sūrah's third use of the disputers' formula (40:4, 35, and here, with 40:69 to come) and the second with the qualifier *bi-ghayri sulṭān* (40:35). Each repetition adds a layer: 40:4 said disputation is the disbelievers' habit; 40:35 said it ends in a sealed heart; 40:56 says what is *driving* it — and the answer is not an argument at all. Al-Rāzī observes that the verse thus completes the sūrah's diagnosis of *jidāl*: act, consequence, cause. It is also the Qur'an's most direct statement of a principle the modern world has learned to call interest-based bias: people often dispute a claim not because of its evidence but because of what accepting it would do to their standing.

**Kifār: Self-Elevation as a Religion**

*Kifār*, from *k-f-r*, is literally covering — the *kāfir* is the night (which covers everything with its darkness) and the farmer (who covers the seed with soil), and the root's second sense here, given by al-Ṭabarī, Ibn ʿAbbās, Qatāda, and Mujāhid in the sources the chapter preserves, is *takaabbur*, lifting oneself up while covering the truth that would bring one down. The verse says the disputers' breasts contain *nothing but kifār*: a total emptiness, and within it one moving principle. Al-Qurṭubī transmits the two readings of *mā hum bi-bālighīhi*: (a) *the greatness they claim will never be attained* — God will not give them the supremacy they are fighting for; and (b) *the thing for the sake of which they dispute* — namely, extinguishing revelation — will never be reached; both are recorded in al-Ṭabarī's *Jāmiʿ al-Bayān*, and the second is the one that fits the verse's position between the promise of aid (40:51) and the argument from creation (40:57): their project is not only forbidden to them, it is already decided against.

There is a third nuance the classical readers preserve: *kifār* is here *in the breast*, and the sūrah has just said *He knows... what the breasts conceal* (40:19) — so the disputers' hidden interior, exposed by 40:19, is now named, and the reader is meant to notice that the one thing they could not conceal in God's court was the thing they refused to examine in their own.

**The Political Anatomy of a Courtroom**

The verse's realism is valuable for anyone who has argued in public. The three signs that a dispute has stopped being about evidence are precisely the three named in Ghāfir: no *sulṭān* (no warrant, 40:56 and 35), a motive lodged in the chest rather than in the argument (*kifār*), and a goal the disputant cannot reach but will keep chasing. Ibn Taymiyyah, in *Darʾ Taʿāruḍ* and in his *al-Jawāb al-Ṣaḥīḥ*, quotes 40:56 against *mutakallimūn* of all schools who made disputation a devotional act, and his point is exactly the sūrah's: *jidāl* pursued to *attain* standing, rather than to *submit* to evidence, is *kifār*, not *munāẓara*. Ibn al-Qayyim, in *Iʿlām al-Muwaqqiʿīn*, adds the diagnostic: the disputant described here is not defending a conclusion; he is defending a rank, which is why his arguments never change shape when the evidence changes.

**"Seek Refuge in God" — *ʿIyāḏa* as Worship, Not Superstition**

*Fa-ʿudh bi-Llāhi inna-Huwa al-Samīʿu al-Baṣīr*. The imperative is addressed to the Prophet, not to the disputers, and its content is remarkable: the cure for encountering *kifār* is not a better rebuttal but a refuge. Al-Ṭabarī explains: *seek protection from their dispute and from their plotting, and from being one of them*; and Ibn Kathīr's gloss — *God commands him to take refuge in Him from the corruption of the People of Discord and their like, and from what they are in, lest something of it cling to him* — names the real danger the verse sees: prolonged argument with bad-faith opponents does something to the arguer. The Qur'an elsewhere treats *istiʿādha* as an act of the religion itself: *and say: my Lord, I seek refuge in You from the promptings of the devils, and I seek refuge in You, my Lord, lest they be present with me* (23:97–98); *whether a prompting from Satan strikes you, seek refuge in God; He is the Hearing, the Knowing* (7:200, and 41:36 — *and if there is from Satan a prompting, seek refuge in God; He is the Hearing, the Knowing*, in a verse whose whole context is *fighting* arguments about the Qur'an). The two names that close the verse answer the two threats: *al-Samīʿ* — He hears the disputers' words and your plea, so you do not need to have the last word; *al-Baṣīr* — He sees their motive and your sincerity, so you do not need to be believed when you are misrepresented. That, al-Qurṭubī notes, is the practical difference between a *ḥisba* that is done for God and one done for reputation.

**Reading the Verse With 40:4 as a Frame**

The sūrah's first mention of the disputers told the Prophet not to be *deceived* by their movement; this, its last-but-one mention before the cosmic argument, tells him to *take refuge* from it. Between the two, the verse's placement does the work: after the promise of aid (40:51–52), a man can afford to stop arguing. This is the chapter's counsel to every believer tempted to treat a losing controversy as a test of loyalty: the issue has already been adjudicated in the court of 40:16–17, and the disputers' *kifār* will not be attained; what remains is to keep one's own breast clean, and to that the sūrah adds its final prescription of *dhikr* (40:55, 65) and *duʿāʾ* (40:60).

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:57

> **57** *The creation of the heavens and the earth is greater than the creation of people, but most people do not know.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument the Disputers Cannot Argue With**

*La-khalqu al-samāwāti wa-al-arḍi akbaru min khalqi al-nāsi wa-lākinna akthara al-nāsi lā yaʿlamūn*. The sūrah, having just dismissed disputation (40:56), now produces a proof of the resurrection that no amount of *kifār* can dispute — because it is not an appeal to prophethood but to a fact the objectors themselves concede. The structure is an *a fortiori* (in the jurists' idiom, *qiyāsa al-awlā*, and in the logicians' *dalīl al-awlā wa-l-akthā*): if the greater is admitted, the lesser cannot be denied. Al-Ṭabarī's paraphrase: *the One who created the heavens and the earth, in their vastness and their strength, is able to recreate you, and this is the greater of the two; so do not be unable to conceive the lesser.*

**The Qur'an's Own Parallel Texts**

The argument is Ghāfir's own elsewhere in the Qur'an in fuller form, and the sūrah's verse 57 must be read with them: *Have they not considered that God, who created the heavens and the earth and was not tired by them, is able to give life to the dead? Yes — He is over all things capable* (46:33; and *they say: when we are lost in the earth, will we then be in a new creation? Rather they deny meeting their Lord* (32:10)). Ibn Kathīr's note on the verse stresses *w-lam yaʾūdhu* — *and was not tired by them* — because the objection that most often accompanies the intuition "recreation is impossible" is fatigue or effort, and the sūrah of Qāf has already closed that: *We did not tire from the first creation, and they are in doubt about a new creation* (50:15). The sūrah also answers the argument from size with an image of scale: *the heavens and the earth were a joined thing, and We tore them apart* (21:30) — and the sūrah of the Roman adds the cosmological parallel that Ghāfir will use in the very next section (40:61–68): *He originates creation, then repeats it, and it is easiest for Him* (30:27).

The Makkan context supplies the urgency: the Quraysh's most repeated objection is not "who is your Lord" but *"when we are dust, will we then be in a new creation?"* (32:27; 37:16; 50:3; 56:47), and Ghāfir's own section voices the same incredulity at 40:82 — *they were more numerous, stronger, and left firmer traces* — and 40:57 is the answer. That is why the sūrah places it after 40:56 rather than before: once the motive for arguing has been exposed, an argument that does not depend on the arguer's honesty can be offered — and the verse's ending clause (*but most people do not know*) concedes that it will still be refused by those whose problem is not ignorance of the premise but unwillingness to reach its conclusion.

**"Greater" — in What Sense?**

The comparative *akbar* is read three ways in the tafsīr, and the commentators keep them together: (1) greater in *power required*, so that re-creating human beings is comparatively trivial — this is al-Ṭabarī's and Ibn Kathīr's plain sense; (2) greater in *number and magnitude*, which is the *ʿilla* of the comparison; and (3) greater in *significance as an argument*, i.e., a premise whose size makes it harder to evade. Al-Rāzī adds the observation that the *creation* of the heavens is mentioned rather than the heavens themselves, because the point is the *act* and the will behind it, not the objects: whoever doubts an act doubts a power, and the verse's target is the assumption that the same power would find re-creation difficult.

**A Cosmological Saying Worth Cautious Handling**

Later preachers frequently attach to this verse a report in which Ibn ʿAbbās compares the world to a mustard seed and the *kursiyy* to a ring in a desert, and a further material from Kaʿb al-Aḥbār and Wahb ibn Munabbih about the vastness of creation. These come mostly through *Isrāʾīliyyāt* and weak chains, and al-Dhahabī and Ibn Kathīr treat them accordingly; the sound material on the greatness of creation is the hadith in Muslim (2787) about the folding of the heavens and the earth in God's hand (already cited at 40:16), which yields the same moral: the "greater" of 40:57 is *small* relative to its Creator, and that is why the verse is a proof of ease, not of effort. The practical consequence the commentators draw is devotional rather than cosmographic: whoever is impressed by the heavens should stop being unimpressed by his own accountability.

**"Most People Do Not Know" — the Epistemology of Waste**

The clause is the sūrah's answer to the question the verse would otherwise leave open: if the argument is that simple, why does unbelief persist? Al-Zamakhsharī's gloss is characteristically blunt: *they do not know* — that is, the argument requires only an act of attention, and a person who has never directed thought at the sky or at his own beginning has no exposure to the proof at all; and al-Qurṭubī notes that the verse says *most*, not *all*, so that the sūrah's real audience — the few who do reflect (40:13's *mā yatafakkaru illā man yunīb*) — is being handed a demonstration they can actually use. The pairing of *lā taʿlamūn* is the sūrah's most repeated verdict on the Quraysh (40:57, 61, and 40:82), and it is the verse's modern reach too: the resurrection is not refuted by an argument; it is ignored by an absence of reflection, and the cure for the latter is not more evidence but the discipline of *dhikr* that this sūrah has been prescribing since 40:7.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:58

> **58** *And the blind man and the seeing man are not equal, nor those who believe and do righteous deeds, nor the evildoer. Little do you take heed!*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Equality That Must Be Proved, Not Assumed**

*Wa-mā yastawi al-aʿmā wa-al-baṣīr wa-lladhīna āmanū wa-ʿamilū al-ṣāliḥāti wa-lā al-musīʾ*. The verse is a denial of a tacit premise: that people are, morally and intellectually, interchangeable. Its logic is not that the blind man is worth less as a human being — the Qur'an elsewhere refuses precisely that inference (cf. 80:1–10, where the Prophet ﷺ was reproved for turning from the blind man ʿAbdullāh ibn Umm Maktūm, and the sūrah of ʿAbasa exists in the Qur'an to this day because of that single incident, and 6:52 and 18:28 — *and do not drive away those who call upon their Lord morning and evening seeking His face, and you are not responsible for them in anything*). It is that *insight*, not persons, is unequally distributed, and that a society which treats the two as the same will make the same mistake twice.

The structure is famously disputed by grammarians: is there an elided second element (*wa-lā al-musīʾūn* — "nor the evildoer [and the one who does good]") after the second pair, or is the second clause a coordinated appositional pair? Sibawayh's *al-Kitāb* and al-Farrāʾ's *Maʿānī al-Qurʾān* discuss the verse as an instance of the *ʿaṭf* of a clause onto a negated comparison, and the practical reading is identical: two comparisons are denied at once — the comparison of perception (blind/seeing) and the comparison of conduct (believer-doing-good/evildoer). The blindness of the first is a metaphor for the second: Ghāfir has already made the point in 40:19 and 40:35 that what the eyes fail to see is what the heart refuses to accept.

**The Metaphor Elsewhere in the Qur'an**

*Al-aʿmā wa-al-baṣīr* occurs in three other places (6:50 — *say: are the blind and the seeing equal? Then do you not reflect?*; 11:24 — *the blind and the dumb, and one who is on a clear path vs one who is in manifest error*; 35:19–20 — *not equal are the blind and the seeing, nor the darkness and the light, nor the shade and the heat, and not equal are the living and the dead; God makes whom He will hear, and you cannot make those in the graves hear*). Al-Rāzī's remark on this cluster is the sharpest: the Qur'an uses the *blind/seeing* pair to say something no amount of information can fix, because the problem is an organ and not a message; and 35:20's closing clause — *you cannot make the dead hear, nor can you make the deaf turn back when they have been given warning* — is there for exactly the reason the pair is here, since Ghāfir has just described a sealed heart (40:35) and an unattainable *kifār* (40:56): a corpse has no use for more evidence, and neither, in the relevant sense, does a man who has decided that being wrong would cost him more than dying. The verse's third pair — believers-doing-good against *musīʾ* — then adds the sūrah's most practical point: what distinguishes them is *ʿamal*, and the Qur'an never says "the believers and the disbelievers are not equal," but "the believers who *do* and the one who *does evil* are not equal," because in this sūrah a secret believer (40:28) has been shown to outrank a court of nominal worshippers.

**"Little Do You Take Heed" — the Grammar of a Diminished Capacity**

*Qalīlan mā tadhakkarūn*. The adverb *qalīlan* ("in a small amount") modifies an entire act of *tadhakkur*, and the *mā* between adverb and verb is the instrument of emphasis, a construction Sibawayh and al-Mubarrad discuss as *taʾkīd* (al-Zamakhsharī in *al-Kashshāf* explains it as making the smallness more biting: it is not that you reflect little, it is that there is almost nothing of it). *Tadhakkur*, from *dh-k-r*, is *returning* to what is already in the mind — a reminder of a known thing, and the Qur'an's favorite for *dhikr* itself (*We sent down the Reminder and We are its guardian*, 15:9; *so remind, for reminder benefits the believers*, 51:55). So the reproach is not "you lack information" but "you will not do the thing that costs nothing, namely *remembering what you already know*" — which, in Ghāfir's context, is a devastatingly fair description of people who knew Joseph's monotheism (40:34), who knew the *bayyināt* (40:49), and who knew the size of the heavens.

**Why the Verse Follows an Argument From the Heavens**

The sequence 57–58 is a single piece of pastoral reasoning: the sky is a proof open to anyone (40:57); what prevents people from using it is not distance but blindness (40:58); therefore no further proof can be added, only sight restored. That is the sūrah's implicit definition of *hidāya* — not more light but an eye — and it matches the prophetic supplication the tradition places at exactly this juncture: *"God, if there is in my heart a blind spot after it has seen, restore to me the sight of it"* — and the sound text of the same meaning is the duʿāʾ of the two Ṣaḥīḥs: *"God, I seek refuge in You from being turned back on my heels, and from the punishment of the grave"* (al-Bukhārī 1377, Muslim 590); and 40:13's condition reappears as the practical conclusion: sight is given to the one who turns. Al-Ṭabarī closes the note with the reading that best fits the sūrah's audience: the blind man and the seer are not equal *in what profits them*, so the Makkan who had *seen* — the Prophet ﷺ, the believers — were told their advantage was real, and those who mocked the advantage of guidance (40:31, 41) were told that mockery of an unequal world is itself the blindness.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:59

> **59** *Truly the Hour is coming — about it there is no doubt — but most people do not believe.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Promise Reasserted After the Argument**

*Inna al-sāʿata ātiyatun lā rayba fīhā wa-lākinna akthara al-nāsi lā yu'minūn*. The sūrah has been proving the resurrection from creation (40:57) and from the anatomy of insight (40:58); here it stops proving and simply asserts, in the form the Qur'an reserves for matters that need no more argument: an *inna*, a future participle *ātiya*, and a total negation of doubt. Al-Rāzī notes the order: *ā'tiyatun* (it is coming) is followed by *lā rayba fīhā* (no doubt about it) rather than the reverse, because the Qur'an's claim is not that belief in the Hour is *reasonable* only, but that its arrival is certain and every hesitation is an artifact of the *nafs*, not of the evidence. The same pairing of assertion plus denial of doubt occurs in the Qur'an's most emphatic places: *We created the heavens and the earth and what is between them in truth, and the Hour is surely coming; so turn away with a gracious turning* (15:85), and *that is the Day of Judgment; so whoever wills, let him take a path to his Lord* (78:39); and *He will surely gather you for the Day of Resurrection, about which there is no doubt* (3:9; cf. 4:87, 6:12, 40:15, 45:26, 46:7).

**"The Hour" — a Clock for a World That Thinks It Has None**

*Al-sāʿa*, from *s-w-ʿ* (an hour, a brief span), is the Qur'an's chosen word, and the image is that of a scheduled appointment whose time is short — a *mīqāt* (7:187 — *its knowledge comes only to its Lord; He will disclose it when its time comes*), a *waʿd* that cannot be missed (18:48 — *and they ask you to hasten it, and if not for an appointed term...*), and a thief: *does the Hour then come to you suddenly?* (43:66, 47:18). The word does a specific polemic: the Quraysh's demand was for the Hour's *date* (8:32 — *our Lord, hasten for us our share* — and 46:21's demand, *is this the warning you used to threaten us with?*). Their wish for a timetable is answered by 70:6–7 — *the disbeliever asked to hasten what is certain of it; it is the Day whose duration is fifty thousand years of what you count* — while 46:22 belongs to the other side of the scene, the admission in the Fire: *how long did you remain in the world? They will say: a day or part of a day*. The sūrah's answer, repeated across the *ḥawāmīm*, is that timing is withheld precisely in order to make the present hour the operative one — which is why 40:59 is immediately followed by an imperative to *call upon God* (40:60): a scheduled Day is a threat, and an unscheduled one is an invitation to act today.

**The Third "Most People" — the Sūrah's Sociological Refrain**

The clause *wa-lākinna akthara al-nāsi lā yu'minūn* is the third of the sūrah's three *lākinna akthara al-nāsi* (40:57 *lā yaʿlamūn*, 40:59 *lā yu'minūn*, and 40:61 *lā yashkurūn*), and the sequence of the three verbs is the sūrah's diagnosis in one word each: first they *do not know*, therefore they *do not believe*, therefore they *do not give thanks*. Al-Rāzī draws out the order as a causal chain: ignorance is epistemic, unbelief is volitional, ingratitude is practical — and the last is the condition God describes as *qalīlan min ʿibādiya al-shakūr* (34:13), a divine statement that gratitude is the rarest of all achievements, which is a comfort to the small band in Makkah and a warning to anyone who thinks he has already arrived. Al-Ṭabarī adds the note that *akthar* (most) is used rather than *kull*: the sūrah's pessimism is statistical, not absolute, and it will in the same breath promise that the *qalīl* — the few — are precisely the ones for whom the Throne-bearers pray (40:7). The hadith that the Quraysh and the world's majority are always against the minority of the sincere is the Qur'an's own: *and if you obey most of those on the earth, they will lead you astray from God's path; they follow nothing but conjecture, and they are only guessing* (6:116) — Ghāfir's *akthar* clauses are the sūrah's version of 6:116.

**"Asked to Hasten It," and the Verse That Answers**

The Hour's imminence, Ghāfir's own 40:18 (*the Day that is drawing near*), and 54:1 (*the Hour has drawn near and the moon has split*) are the framework for the prophetic report on the sign of the times: *"I and the Hour have been sent thus," and he joined his two fingers* (al-Bukhārī 6503, Muslim 2274) — and, in Muslim's other wording: *"the best of this community's generations is the one to which I was sent, then those who follow, then those who follow"* (Muslim 2533). The sūrah's purpose in asserting *ā'tiya* rather than *qad ātat* is precisely to hold both: it *is* coming, and it has already begun with the sending of the Messenger. That double tense is the reason the *dhikr*-prescriptions of 40:55 and 40:60 are the chapter's answer to the Hour, and not despair: a person who believes the appointment is real and unscheduled lives with a fixed heart in a moving world.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:60

> **60** *And your Lord says: "Call upon Me, and I will answer you. Truly those who are too proud to worship Me will enter Hell debased.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**God Quotes Himself**

*Wa-qāla rabbuku adʿūnī astajib lakum*. The sūrah has quoted God's words about Himself for fifty-nine verses; now He speaks in the first person plural of address, and the verse is one of the most frequently memorized in the Qur'an because of the symmetry of its two imperatives: *adʿūnī* (you call on Me) and *astajib* (I will answer) — the second is the *istijāba* form, which in Arabic is the reply that *meets* the request, and its imperfect tense signals that the answer is continuous, not a one-time event. The promise's structure — command then guarantee — is the same as 2:186's (*if My servants ask you about Me — I am near; I answer the call of the caller when he calls to Me; so let them respond to Me and believe in Me, that they may be guided*), which the commentators always place beside this verse, and Ghāfir's own 40:14 (*so call upon God, devoting religion to Him*) is the third piece of the triangle: call, sincerely, and be assured of an answer.

**The Verse's Own Hadith: Supplication Is Worship**

The source commentary records the reason the sūrah's second half (*too proud to worship Me*) is read as a statement about *duʿāʾ*: the Prophet ﷺ equated them:

> *"The most excellent of acts is supplication."* (cf. al-Bukhārī and Muslim on *duʿāʾ*), and the prophetic definition that binds the two: *"Supplication is the worship,"* and then the Prophet ﷺ recited: *"and your Lord says: call upon Me and I will answer you; truly those who are too proud to worship Me will enter Hell debased"* (al-Tirmidhī 2969, Abū Dāwūd 1479, Ibn Mājah 3828, from al-Nuʿmān ibn Bashīr — graded *ḥasan* by al-Tirmidhī))

The implication drawn by al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr is precise: because the sūrah calls *duʿāʾ* "worship" in the second half, the first half's *duʿāʾ* includes all of *ʿibāda*, and — as al-Tirmidhī's chapter heading states — the verse is read as *all duʿāʾ is ʿibāda*, so that asking is not a supplement to religion but its working definition. The *ʿibāda* of *duʿāʾ* is the acknowledgment of two realities at once: I do not control this, and you do. That is why 40:60 is placed immediately after 40:57–59's triple refrain of *most people*: the majority's failure to ask is not an absence of need but an excess of pride, and the sūrah names the mechanism (*istikbār ʿan ʿibādatī*) rather than the symptom.

**"I Will Answer You": a Promise With a Known Analysis**

The hadith literature resolves the obvious objection — many people call and nothing visibly changes — with the same threefold distinction the commentators quote at this verse:

> *"No one among the people supplicates to God except that God gives him what he asked, if it contains no sin and no severing of ties; or God averts from him its like of evil; or God stores up for him its like of reward."* (al-Tirmidhī 3572, from ʿAbdullāh ibn ʿAmr, graded *ḥasan*; with related wordings in Aḥmad 2/172 and al-Ḥākim)

and the complementary report that explains the felt delay: *"The supplication of any of you will be answered so long as he does not ask for something sinful or the severing of ties, and so long as he is not impatient: 'I supplicated and supplicated and saw no answer,' and then gives up."* (the two halves of the report are in al-Bukhārī 6407 and Muslim 2735 — from Abū Hurayra: *"the servant's supplication is answered so long as he does not hasten"*; and from Anas: *"...so long as he does not ask for a sin or the severing of ties"*) Ibn ʿAṭiyyah's comment on the verse — quoted in the source — preserves the Sufi formulation: *there is no believer who asks God except that God gives him, in this world or the next, and says to him: this is what you asked for in the world, and I kept it for you until today.* The upshot for the sūrah's original audience is enormous: the Makkan believers had nothing and were being denied everything, and 40:60 told them their *duʿāʾ* was not merely heard but *productive* — every unanswered call was either averted evil or stored reward, which is precisely the theology that made the boycott survivable.

**Istikbār ʿan ʿibādatī: The Sin That Refuses to Ask**

*Inna lladhīna yastakbirūna ʿan ʿibādati sayadkhulūna jahannama dākhurīn*. *Dākhurūn*, from *dh-k-r*, is "upon the back of the hand," hence thrown behind, despised, humiliated (the *dukhār* is the dung of a camel that the young licks after birth, and *idhakkir* means to be so abased that one is as worthless as that); and the word's root is *dhikr*, which gives the verse its final irony: the people who refused *dhikr* enter the Fire *dākhurīn* — with their own *dhikr* (remembrance, name, mention) erased or inverted. This is the sūrah's own definition of the *mutakabbir* of 40:27 and 40:35, and the tradition's definition of pride as *rejecting the truth and despising people* (Muslim 91) fits perfectly: asking God for something is the single act that a proud person finds hardest, because it is a public admission that he is not God. Ibn Kathīr notes that this is the sūrah's *ʿilla* (efficient cause) statement for the whole of the Pharaoh narrative: Pharaoh is not punished primarily for idolatry but for the refusal to *need* anything. The hadith cited by Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī at this verse makes the image exact —

> *"The arrogant will be resurrected on the Day of Resurrection in the form of ants, in the shape of men; humiliation will surround them from every place; they will be driven to a gathering-place in Hell, and the people will pour over them the punishment of God."* (al-Tirmidhī 2490, from Jābir; al-Ḥākim graded it *ṣaḥīḥ*, and al-Dhahabī concurred)

— and it is the Qur'an's own word for the punishment of *istikbār*: *dhul* and *ḍaʿf* (28:4 — *he treated a party of the people as weak, and he slaughtered their sons*), reversed on the Day when the abased are *trampled*, which is the sūrah's most literal possible picture of *dākhurīn*.

**The Verse as the Chapter's Hinge**

Ghāfir's remaining twenty-five verses (40:61–85) will be, in effect, an argument for why *duʿāʾ* to any other than the One of 40:61–68 is *ḍalāl*: night and day, the sun and moon, the stages of the embryo, cattle and ships, and the ruins of the arrogant. And the sūrah's structure makes 40:60 the hinge: *everything above it has been about who God is; everything below it is about what He has made, so that the reader has no remaining excuse for not calling on Him.* The practical reading is the one the Shāfiʿī and Ḥanbalī manuals draw from this verse in their chapters on *qunūt* and *dhikr baʿd al-ṣalāh*: the believer is commanded to *ask* at the end of every prayer — the prophetic practice cited at 40:14's note — because the moment of standing after worship is the moment when *istikbār* is least likely to win, and 40:60's promise is the only rational ground for a life of petition.

---
## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:61

> **61** *God is the one who made for you the night so that you may rest in it, and the day giving sight. Truly God is Possessor of Bounty to humankind, but most people do not give thanks.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Second Proof-Tier: From Argument to Atmosphere**

*Allāhu lladhī jaʿala lakumu al-layla li-taskanū fīhi wa-al-nahāra mubṣira*. The hinge from 40:60 is exact: having commanded *duʿāʾ* and threatened the proud, the sūrah now lists the benefactions that make *duʿāʾ* reasonable. It does not begin with the spectacular but with the ordinary — night and day — because Ghāfir's epistemology, set out at 40:13 and 40:57, is that the most persuasive signs are the ones we have stopped noticing. Al-Rāzī comments that God named the two most repeated of all His acts upon the world, and repeated-ness is exactly what makes a sign invisible to the ungrateful and obvious to the thankful.

**Sukūn: Rest as the Purpose, Not the By-Product**

*Taskunū*, from *s-k-n*, is the settling of a thing that was moving — the same root gives *sakīna*, the "tranquility" that descends on the believers (48:4, 48:18, 9:26, in effect) and *suknā*, a dwelling and a spouse's calming presence (30:21: *He created for you from yourselves spouses that you may find rest — taskunūna — in them*). Night is thus described in the Qur'an not as an absence of light but as a positive provision of *repose*: *and We made sleep for rest, and the night for clothing, and the day for making a living* (78:9–11); *It is He who made the night for you to cover you and sleep a rest, and made the day manifest* (25:47); *and out of His mercy He made for you night and day that you may rest in it and seek of His bounty* (28:73). Ibn Kathīr's gloss on 40:61 is physiological and moral at once: the body's repair, the mind's silence, and the suspension of *kasb* are all required, and the one who never stops working has refused the *sukūn* God legislated into the sky. That is why the devotional commentators cite at this verse the hadith of the two rakʿas at the day's opening — *"God says: O son of Adam, do not abandon for Me the two rakʿas at the beginning of the day, and I will suffice you at its end"* (Aḥmad 5/267; al-Tirmidhī 475, graded *ḥasan*) — and Ibn Kathīr notes: the day's opening is the point at which a believer consciously accepts the *mubṣira* — the light he will use for seeking — and hands back the request for *sukūn* at its close.

**Mubṣira: the Day That Makes Things Visible (and the Day That Sees)**

*mubṣir* is read actively ("making sight possible," hence enabling work, trade, and travel — IK, Ṭ) and intransitively ("the day that sees," i.e., that itself bears witness to what is done in it — the source commentary's second reading, which connects to *the day will be manifest* in 81:13's *wa-bassirat* and to *a witness and one witnessed* (85:3 — *and the witnessing and the witnessed*; al-Ṭabarī transmits the classical identification that the witnessing Day is Friday and the witnessed is the Day of Resurrection). The double reading is a rhetorical device the sūrah loves elsewhere: *That day it will report its news* (99:4), and *that Day every soul will find whatever good it did present* (3:30), and *a witness over these* (40:51's witnesses), and *on that Day We will seal their mouths and their hands will speak to Us and their feet will testify* (36:65). The *nahār* of 40:61 is thus both the condition of human activity and its record; and the record is *mubṣir* before it is seen — a fact the verse uses to make gratitude obligatory, since a gift that is simultaneously an audit is the only gift that teaches responsibility.

**"Possessor of Bounty," Again — and Ingratitude as the Real Subject**

*Inna Llāha dhu faḍlin ʿalā al-nāsi wa-lākinna akthara al-nāsi lā yashkurūn*. *Faḍl* is that which is added beyond what is owed — the *fiḍa* of surplus, the same root as *dhū al-ṭawl* in 40:3, and the sūrah has named God with both, which is why 40:61 quotes the *fāʾida* of the whole chapter: the night and day are not wages; they are *faḍl*, and are not renewed by gratitude — only *acknowledged* by it (cf. 14:7 — *if you are thankful, I will increase you, and if you are ungrateful, My punishment is severe*; and 31:12 — *whoever thanks, thanks for his own self, and whoever is ungrateful, my Lord is rich, generous*; and 27:40 — *this is of the bounty of my Lord*). Al-Rāzī observes that ingratitude is named here rather than disbelief: the worst fate of the Quraysh, in this sūrah's diagnosis, is not that they failed to *believe* the giver of the night but that they failed to *thank* Him — because ingratitude is the moral state in which unbelief is comfortable. The fourth *lākinna akthara al-nāsi* of the sūrah (following 40:57 and 40:59, with 40:64 to come) thus lands on the virtue most easily trained: a person can be taught *shukr* faster than *ʿaqīda*, and the sūrah is showing the ladder's rungs in order.

The prophetic practice attached to the two daily gifts is direct and simple: the duʿāʾ of rising, *"Praise be to God who gave us life after causing us to die, and to Him is the resurrection"* (al-Bukhārī 6312, from Ḥudhayfa) and of lying down, *"In Your name my God I die and live"* (al-Bukhārī 6324, from Ḥudhayfa and Abū Hurayra) — which are, in effect, an oral recitation of 40:61 at each transition, and a demonstration that the sūrah's cosmology was meant to be *spoken* twice a day, not only studied.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:62

> **62** *That is God, your Lord, Creator of all things; there is no god but He. How, then, can you be turned away? In the same way were those who used to reject the signs of God turned away.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Syllogism Compressed Into Two Words**

*Dhālikumu Llāhu rabbukum khāliqu kulli shay'in lā ilāha illā Huwa fa-annā tu'faqūn*. The verse builds its argument in four steps and leaves the fifth to the reader: (1) *that* — the God of the night and the day, just described; (2) is *your Lord* — related to you, not merely powerful; (3) *Creator of all things* — with no exceptions, including the idols, the bodies that carve them, and the hands that build the towers; (4) *there is no god but He*. The conclusion is then posed as a question of direction rather than of truth: *fa-annā tu'faqūn* — "then how are you turned around?" The *fa* carries the whole weight: given (1)–(4), the only possible remaining question is about the turning, not about the premises.

**Tu'faqūn: the Verb of Being Deluded Out of What You Know**

*Ifk*, from *ʾ-f-k*, is turning a thing over so that it presents its underside, and the *ifk* of 24:11–22 — the slander against ʿĀʾisha, whose very name for the crime in the Qur'an is *al-ifk*, "the overturning" — fixes the meaning: *ifk* is not error but inversion of a known truth. The passive *tu'faqūn* therefore accuses the audience of something they cannot claim to be ignorant of (al-Ṭabarī's gloss: *how are you turned from the truth, when you acknowledge that God is the Creator of all things?*). Ibn Kathīr cites 31:32 in support — *if you asked them who created the heavens and the earth and subjected the sun and the moon, they would say, "God"; how then are they turned away?* — which is the identical argument, in the identical verb (*fa-annā yu'fa* *k* *ūn*), and this sūrah's own 40:31 and 43:87 supply the same *istifhām* of astonishment. The *turning* is what the sūrah has been describing all along: Pharaoh was *ṣudda ʿani al-sabīl* (40:37), the disbelievers of the Hour are *ya'fikūn* (46:24 in effect), and God "turns their hearts and their eyes away" as a verdict (6:110). The verse thus teaches the difference between not knowing and not *staying*: in the Qur'an's psychology, the most common failure is not a lack of evidence but a turning away from evidence one already possesses — which is why it is called a *turning*, an act, and not a state.

**"Creator of All Things": The Clause That Excludes Every Intermediary**

*Khāliqu kulli shay'in* is the sūrah's master-claim and its favorite proof (cf. *your Lord is God, who created the heavens and the earth in six days* — 7:54; *Is One who creates like one who does not create?* 16:17; *or they were created without anything, or are they themselves the creators?* 52:35; *God created everything, and He is the One, the Subduer* 13:16; *do you call upon that which you carved, while God created you and what you make?* 37:95–96, which is Abraham's version of 40:62's question). The argument is what the tradition calls *burhān al-niẓām* and *burhān al-ikhlāq wa-l-tarbiya*: the same God who originates cannot share His divinity with anything He originated, and to worship a creature is not merely a mistake in rank but a confusion of categories (cf. al-Ghazālī's *al-Iqtiṣād fi al-Iʿtiqād* and Ibn Taymiyyah's *al-Radd ʿalā al-Manṭiqiyyīn*, both of which treat the *khāliq/makhlūq* distinction as the sole foundation of tawḥīd and the sole ground for rejecting *sharīk*). Al-Zamakhsharī notes the elegance: the sūrah proves *lā ilāha illā Huwa* from *khāliqu kulli shay'in*, since a partner in godhead would have to be a partner in creation, and that is self-contradictory for something created.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:63
> **63** *God is the one who made for you the earth as a place of rest and the sky as a structure, and formed you and made beautiful your forms, and provided you with the good things. That is God, your Lord; blessed be God, Lord of the worlds.*

**40:63: the Historical Mirror**

*Kadhālika yu'faku lladhīna kānū bi-āyāti Llāhi yajraḥūn* — the second clause is a *mathal* (parallel case), and the source commentary's point is that it converts a reproof into a law: the *ifk* of the Quraysh is the *ifk* of every nation that *rejected* (a verb of denial after recognition) the signs, and the sūrah's *kadhālika* is a warning that the pattern is replicable in both directions. Al-Ṭabarī reads the *yā* of the imperfect as habitual: *this is how the habit of the rejecters of the signs runs*. The verse thus performs the sūrah's own method of 40:21 and 40:82 — *travel and see* — in miniature, with the "traces" reduced to a single verb: *yu'fa*. Ibn Kathīr, at this place, quotes 6:110 in full and 40:35's sealing: *the same turning away that God performed on them, He performs on those who turn away today, and the *kayfa* of astonishment belongs to both*.

**Practical Theology: Where the Verse Reaches a Modern Reader**

The verse's target is not the person who has never thought about God; it is the person who has, and has nevertheless arranged a life in which something else functions as the final authority. That is exactly the *ifk* it describes, and its cure is not more argument but the reversal of the turning — *ināba*, the "turning back" of 40:13, which is the word the sūrah chose for the only person who reflects. So 40:62's question *fa-annā tu'faqūn* and 40:13's *wa-mā yatafakkaru illā man yunīb* are a matched pair: a turning is reversed by a turning, and the sūrah has already told the reader who does it (40:56's *fa-ʿudh bi-Llāh*, and 40:60's *udʿūnī*).

**Kudhhibū: Rejection as the Name for the Whole Career**

*Bi-mā kānū yukadhdhibūn* — the imperfect inside the *bi-* of cause makes the verse's point: they were diverted *by the very act they kept doing*, and the passive *kuḍḥibu* (they were called liars / their signs were denied) carries the sūrah's note that *takdhīb* is a *process*, not an event (cf. 40:5's *kadhdhabat... wa-l-aḥzāb*, 40:24's *kadhdhāb*, 40:70's *yukadhdhibūna bi-āyātinā*, 40:83's mockery). The source commentary's other reading — that the verse explains how God treats the deniers of *His* signs — is confirmed by the grammar of *kadhdhabū* in the sūrah's own refrain at 40:4: the *jidāl* is conducted *about* the *āyāt*, and *takdhīb* is its terminus. To be "diverted" (*yu'fakūn*) is thus the *eschatological* name for a *this-worldly* habit, which is Ghāfir's most repeated claim (40:35's *kadhdhālika yaṭbaʿu*, 40:75's *kadhālika yuḍillu*).

**The Verse's Second Half as a Law of Asymmetry**

*Kadhālika yanṭabiʿu Llāhu ʿalā kulli mutakabbirin jabbārin* — the sūrah's *mithāl* is introduced in 40:35, and 40:63's twin, *kadhālika yuḍillu Llāhu al-ẓālimīn* (40:74's neighbourhood) and *kadhālika yu'faku lladhīna kānū bi-āyāti Llāhi yujraḥūn* (40:63) — one of four such clauses in the chapter, each following an act, never precedes it. The rhetoricians' rule (al-Sakkākī, *Miftāḥ al-ʿUlūm*, on *al-tamthīl*) is that a *mathal* requires *wiḥdat al-ḥukm* — the two cases must share a single ruling — and the sūrah's use is strict about it: sealing, misguiding, diverting, and *ṣarf* are all named *after* a refusal, so that no verse can be quoted as evidence of predestination without the corresponding act being quoted with it. The source commentary's note on the *inkār* of the *āyāt* (40:63, 40:69) is the same point from the other side: the disbelievers' *takdhīb* is a *choice that gets a name*, and the name is then used as the description of their end. Al-Bayḍāwī's compressed gloss — *that is their recompense, and the recompense of the like of them is the like of it* — makes the moral lesson: the sūrah never says that God invents a punishment; it says that He *lets the crime keep doing what it was already doing*, which is also why the verse's only agent-verb is *ṣarafa* in 40:75 and *khalaṭa* in 40:85, both verbs of letting-continue.

**Degrees, in the Sūrah's Own Arithmetic**

The verse's passive is the sharpest single datum in the closing section, because it makes the *degree* of punishment correspond to the *degree* of the act: *li-kullin darajātun mimmā ʿamilū*, "for each are degrees according to what they did" (6:132; 46:19). Ghāfir's eschatology is thus not a flat binary, and its verses about the Fire are graded accordingly (40:45–46's exposure *morning and evening* before the Day, 40:48's *laʿna* added to entry, 40:71–72's *ghaya* and *ḥamīm*, 40:76's gates with allotted portions) — a ladder of consequence that mirrors the ladder of refusal, and which is the reason the sūrah pairs *lā yuḍāʿʿu ḥukmuhu bi-aḥad* (6:114 — *and the word of your Lord has been fulfilled in truth and in justice* — with 36:54's *you are only repaid what you used to do*).

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:64

> **64** *God is the one who made for you the earth as a place of rest and the sky as a structure, and formed you and made beautiful your forms, and provided you with the good things. That is God, your Lord; blessed be God, Lord of the worlds.*

**Four Benefactions in Ascending Scale, Ending on the Human Body**

*Allāhu lladhī jaʿala lakumu al-arḍa qarāran wa-al-samāʾa bināʾan wa-ṣawwarakum fa-aḥsana ṣuwarakum wa-razaqakum min al-ṭayyibāti*. The sūrah moves from the ground, to the roof, to the body, to the table — an order that is architectural and not arbitrary: dwelling, shelter, form, food. Al-Rāzī notes that the four are exactly the four necessities of a human community (the place, the weather and the heavens that govern it, the bodies that inhabit them, and the food that keeps them), and that Ghāfir compresses into one verse what other sūrahs spread across whole passages (cf. 2:22, *who made the earth a bed for you and the sky a structure and sent down water... so do not set up rivals to God while you know*).

**Qarār: a Place Where You Can Put Things Down**

*Qarār*, from *q-r-r*, is settling — the same root as *dār al-qarār* in 40:39, which is the verse's most important internal echo: the earth is called by the word the sūrah had reserved for Paradise's permanence, and the effect is an argument: if you have made of the temporary *qarār* your true home, you have made a mistake of vocabulary, and vocabulary is, in this sūrah, a moral instrument (40:39 already called the world *matāʿ qalīl*). The lexica gloss *qarār* as *mustaqarr*, a settled place, and the earth's firmness — *and We made in the earth firm mountains so that it would not shake with them* (21:31; *and He cast into it firm mountains from above it*, 31:10; *and the mountains as pegs*, 78:7) — is the physical datum Ghāfir is using, exactly as at 40:21's *āthār* and 40:82's *asbata*. The same root gives *taqarrur*, the fixing of a decision, and *muqarrar*, a confirmed thing — which is why the sūrah's earth is not scenery but a *foundation for action*.

**Bināʾ: the Sky as a Built Structure**

*Bināʾ*, from *b-n-ʾ*, is a building in the sense of what is *constructed above* you — the Qur'an's parallel words are *saqf maḥfūẓ* (a protected roof, 21:32), *bināʾun mashū* (a well-raised structure, 50:6), *al-samāʾa rafatha-hā wa-istawa ʿalā al-arḍi* (55:7), and *He raised the heavens without pillars that you see* (13:2). The image is architectural to make a point about *ownership*: a roof implies a house, a house implies a builder, and a builder implies a claim on the inhabitants. Al-Ṭabarī glosses *bināʾan* as *saqfan mahfūẓan* and cites the tradition that the sky is kept from falling on the earth only by God's holding (22:65 — *and He holds the heavens and the earth that they not fall, except by His leave*), which is the Qur'an's own version of the *suspension* motif, and which answers the *bināʾ* of Pharaoh's tower (40:36) with the one *bināʾ* no human kiln built.

**Ṣawwarakum fa-aḥsana Ṣuwarakum: Form and Beauty as Evidence**

*Ṣūra* — shape, figure, portrait — is the root of the *muṣawwir*, one of God's names, *the Fashioner* (59:24: *He is God, Creator, Fashioner; His are the most beautiful names*), and the sūrah of the Human cites the *taṣwīr* of the embryo (76:2 — *We created humankind from a mixed drop, and We made him hearing and seeing*) and 22:5 in effect, and 32:7–9 — *who made everything He created beautiful, and began the creation of humankind from clay; then made his progeny from an extract of a base fluid; then proportioned him and breathed into him of His spirit*. The conjunction *fa-aḥsana* is a *fa* of *taʿjīb* (admiration) and of consequence: He formed you *and thereby* made your forms beautiful. Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr both note the two scopes of *ṣuwarakum*: the visible human form (face, proportion, the *iʿtidāl* of features, and the fact that no two faces are identical, which the sūrah elsewhere uses as a sign of will and not of chance: *and among His signs is the creation of the heavens and the earth and the difference of your languages and your colors*, 30:22) and the interior form — the faculties of knowledge, speech, and choice (cf. 95:4 — *We created humankind in the best of stature*, and its sequel: *then We returned him to the lowest of the low, except those who believe*). The *taṣwīr* therefore becomes, in the sūrah's hands, an argument from design *and* a moral claim: beauty is a trust, and the one who has been *made beautiful* is expected to make his conduct beautiful. Al-Ṭabarī preserves an early *faqīh*'s remark that this is one of the grounds on which the traditionists warn against the *muṣawwirūn* who imitate *taṣwīr* (the famous report in Muslim 2107: *"the most severely punished of people on the Day of Resurrection will be the image-makers, until God says to them: give life to what you created"*), and while the legal details of that hadith are famously debated, its logic here is the sūrah's own: *ṣawwarakum* belongs to God alone, and that is why *lā ilāha illā Huwa* follows.

**Rizq min al-Ṭayyibāt, and the Formula of Blessing**

*Wa-razaqakum min al-ṭayyibāti* joins the chapter's other two *rizq*-verses (40:13, 40:40), and the qualifier *ṭayyib* — wholesome, pleasant, permitted — is the Qur'an's standard for lawful provision (2:168 — *O humankind, eat of what is on the earth, lawful and wholesome*; 2:172; 5:88; 7:31, 157; 23:51 — *O messengers, eat of the wholesome things and do good*). Then comes *tabāraka Llāhu rabbu al-ʿālamīn* — *blessed is God, Lord of the worlds* — a doxology the Qur'an reserves for the climaxes of its creation-narratives (23:14 — after the embryo's stages: *then We produced him as another creation; blessed be God, the best of creators*; and 25:2, 6, 7's context). *Tabāraka*, from *b-r-k*, is the root of the *baraka* that multiplies without visible addition, and the sūrah's use of it here after the *rizq* clause is the point: provision is the *baraka* — the same food that suffices a family, the rain that arrives before the plough. *Rabb al-ʿālamīn* (Lord of all the worlds, of which *ʿālam* is the plural of that which *signifies*, from *ʿ-l-m*) universalizes the address: the Benefactor of the Arabs is Lord of *every* ordered world — which, in the sūrah's own logic (40:51), means the promise of aid is cosmic and not tribal.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:65

> **65** *He is the Living; there is no god but He. So call upon Him, devoting the religion entirely to Him. Praise belongs to God, Lord of the worlds.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Name That Makes the Promise Possible**

*Huwa al-Ḥayyu lā ilāha illā Huwa*. The sūrah places the name *al-Ḥayy* at the center of the whole argument for *duʿāʾ*, and its reason is one that the commentators never omit: a dead god cannot hear. The Qur'an states the refutation of idolatry at its barest in 25:3 — *and they take, besides Him, gods who are not able to create anything, and they themselves are created, and they have no power to benefit or harm themselves, nor death nor life nor resurrection* — and the same *ḥayāh*-argument in 25:58: *and put your trust in the Living who does not die, and praise Him with His glory*. Al-Ṭabarī glosses *al-Ḥayy* as *the one who has perpetual life, which death does not touch, and whose life has no beginning and no end*, and al-Qurṭubī records that *al-Ḥayy* and *al-Qayyūm* (2:255, 3:2, 20:111, 42:23 — the latter not with *al-Ḥayy* in the verse but in effect as a pair in the sūrah of Āl ʿImrān and al-Baqarah and the *Āyat al-Kursi*) are *al-Ism al-Aʿẓam* (the Greatest Name) according to a group of the salaf, on the strength of the hadith in Abū Dāwūd (1493) and al-Tirmidhī and Ibn Ḥibbān:

> *"The Greatest Name, in which, when He is called by it, He answers, is in three sūrahs: al-Baqarah, Āl ʿImrān, and Ṭā Sīn"* (and when asked about it: *in the sūrah of Baqara, in the Verse of the Throne; in Āl ʿImrān, in "God, there is no god but He, the Living, the Self-Subsisting"; and in Ṭā Sīn, "the faces shall be humbled before the Living, the Self-Subsisting"* — the wording of the explanation, which al-Ḥākim and Ibn Ḥibbān authenticated and Ibn Kathīr cites at this verse.)

In a sūrah that has just told its audience that the most dangerous human posture is *istikbār*, the Greatest Name's functional definition — *He answers* — is not incidental: the God whose name is greatest is the One whose *ijāba* is guaranteed (40:60), which is exactly the two clauses 40:65 binds together.

**The Command Repeated, and Why Repetition Is Not Redundancy**

*Fadʿūhu mukhliṣīna lahu al-dīn* is verbatim 40:14's *fa-udʿū Llāha mukhliṣīna lahu al-dīn*, and the sūrah has made *ikhlāṣ* the frame of its central section (14 at the beginning of the argument, 65 after its proofs) so that the reader can measure what the intervening fifty verses did: the command at 40:14 was issued before the proofs; at 40:65 it is issued after them, and the *fa* in both cases means "therefore." Al-Rāzī's observation is that repetition in the Qur'an often marks a *change in the ground of obligation*: the first command asks for trust, the second for trust that now has evidence behind it. Ibn Kathīr notes that *mukhliṣīn* appears with *duʿāʾ* in the sūrah's own 40:14 and here and in 39:2–3, 11, 14 and 31:32, and that *ikhlaṣ* is the sūrah's single non-negotiable condition for the *ijāba* promised at 40:60 — since a *duʿāʾ* made to be seen is not a call to the Living but a performance for the dead.

**"Praise Belongs to God, Lord of the Worlds": the Sūrah Closes Where the Book Began**

*Al-ḥamdu li-Llāhi rabb al-ʿālamīn* is the exact wording of 1:2, and its appearance here at the end of Ghāfir's long proof-section is one of the most careful structural features in the chapter: the sūrah that began with *Ḥā Mīm* and with the names of mercy, severity, and bounty now answers its own opening with the Fātiḥa's sentence. The *ḥamd* is thanks for what 40:61–64 enumerated, and the *ḥamd* is owed to *rabb al-ʿālamīn* because the *rubūbiyya* — nurturing, sustaining, providing — is what the benefactions were: *rabb* is the one who brings a thing to completion, stage by stage, and Ghāfir's night, day, earth, sky, form, and food are *tarbiya* in the literal sense. Al-Zamakhsharī's rhetorical note on this verse is that the *al-* in *al-ḥamdu* is the article of *istighrāq*, total inclusion: all praise, of every kind, in every language, belongs to God by right, and if all praise belongs to Him, then the praise that the polytheists were distributing among idols and ancestors is being claimed back from them in the most courteous possible form — not as confiscation but as a statement of ownership. And since the verse follows *mukhliṣīna*, the two clauses complete each other: sincerity in calling, totality in praising.

**The Verse as a Prescription for Anxiety**

Read practically, 40:65 gives the believer three instruments for a life of pressure: the *Living* God (so your case is not with a memory, a tradition, or a dead cause), the *exclusive* devotion of religion (so you stop serving two masters and the exhaustion of double bookkeeping ends), and *ḥamd* (so gratitude becomes a fixed posture rather than a mood). The sūrah's own first audience, at the end of a chapter that has shown Pharaoh's palace emptied and a secret believer vindicated, was given the Fātiḥa's words as its summary — a fitting instruction, since the Fātiḥa is the prayer one *recites* and Ghāfir is the prayer one *lives*.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:66

> **66** *Say: "I am forbidden to worship those you call upon, besides God, since clear proofs have come to me from my Lord; and I am commanded to submit to the Lord of the worlds.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet's Own Verdict, After All the Evidence**

*Qul innī nuhītu an a'buda lladhīna tadʿūna min dūni Llāhi qad jāʾatni al-bayyinātu min rabbī wa-umirtu an aslima li-rabbi al-ʿālamīn*. The verse begins with a command to *say*: public declaration is the form Ghāfir's monotheism takes, since the sūrah's imperative *qul* runs through its argument at 40:66, 40:67, 40:78, and 40:80, and its content is a *forbidden*, not a preference: the Prophet's monotheism is not a taste or a policy but a prohibition he cannot cross. Al-Ṭabarī notes the deliberate humility of the phrasing: he does not say "I have decided" but "I have been forbidden and commanded" — a prophet who argues on the basis of a *command* is a man with no personal interest in winning (cf. 6:56 — *I have been forbidden to worship those you call upon besides God... and I am commanded to submit to the Lord of the worlds* and 6:66 and 6:14; 10:15, 10:104 — *say: O humankind, if you are in doubt about my religion, then I do not worship what you worship besides God, but I worship God who will take you; and I am commanded to be among the believers*, and 109:1–6's *I do not worship what you worship*), and this is precisely the argument of the man of Pharaoh's house, who also insisted that he was only reporting and obeying (40:42–44). The repetition is the sūrah's design: the Meccan courtier and the Meccan Prophet speak the same sentence because the same position — an insider being asked to stop telling the truth — has the same answer.

**Nuḥītu: the Passive of the Prohibiter**

The passive *nuhītu* is the divine passive, and its use with *an* + imperfect after the root *n-h-y* (to forbid, literally to *reach* a thing and turn it back — hence *nahy* and *manhī*, and *nāhīya*, the district, because it "reaches" and bounds) makes the verse a statement about God's legislation rather than about the Prophet's conviction; and the source commentary's note on 6:56's parallel is that the verb and the second clause (*umirtu*) bracket the sentence in a *command-and-prohibition* pair (*al-nahy wa-l-amr*), which in legal theory is the entire content of revelation (cf. 16:90 — *God commands justice and good conduct... and forbids indecency and wrongdoing*). The reason Ghāfir puts the pair here, after 40:61–65, is that the sūrah wants *ʿibāda* to be grounded in evidence and not in custom, so it says, in effect: I have proofs, and the proofs are what forbid me (cf. 6:79 — *I turn my face toward the one who created the heavens and the earth, upright, and I am not among the polytheists*).

**Bayyināt From Your Lord and Mine: a Shared Epistemology**

*Qad jāʾatni al-bayyinātu min rabbī*: the same *bayyināt* he reported at 40:28 as Moses' credential are now the Prophet's own, and the word *rabbī* is placed against their *rabb* (40:29's Pharaoh's claim *mā arā*) and against *rabb al-ʿālamīn* at the end of the verse. Ibn Kathīr notes that the *qad* + perfect makes the arrival of the proofs an accomplished fact, which is what makes his obedience a *rational* act and not a leap; and al-Rāzī emphasizes the verse's implicit syllogism: I do not worship them, *because* the clear proofs have come — so if you can produce your own *bayyina*, the prohibition is negotiable, and if you cannot, the negotiation is not about God but about appetite.

***An Aslima li-Rabbi al-ʿĀlamīn*: Submission as the Whole, Not the Name**

*Islam* here is *aslama*, from *s-l-m*, to surrender, to enter into peace and safety (the same root as *salam* and, in the *iftiʿāl* of *taslīm*, total handing-over), and the sūrah defines the religion of the prophets as *islām* at 3:19, 3:67, 3:85, 5:111, and of Abraham specifically at 2:131 — *when his Lord said to him "submit," he said "I have submitted to the Lord of the worlds."* The repetition of 37:103's *aslamā* (both father and son), 2:131, and 6:14 and 6:71 — *and I am commanded to be the first of those who submit*, and *say: We believe in God and what was sent down... and to Him we submit* — makes 40:66's last clause a claim about continuity rather than novelty, which is the Qur'an's persistent answer to the accusation of innovation (46:9 — *say: I am not a novelty among the messengers*). And the *li-* before *Rabbi* makes submission *to a Person* the whole content: not a creed about a Person, and not service *for* a Person, but *aslama lahu*, and the sūrah has just established why that is coherent: because He is *al-Ḥayy* (40:65), and only the living can be addressed by a surrender that is answered.

**Why the Verse Belongs to the Persecuted**

The last thing a Makkan believer needed was a sentence that made *worship* the site of conflict, since that was exactly where the pressure was applied: 40:66's *nuhītu* tells the pressured that the prohibition is not theirs to lift, and its *umirtu* tells them what to do instead. Ibn al-Qayyim's homiletic gloss on this pair, in *al-Fawāʾid*, is the sūrah's whole practical theology: *"God removed from His Messenger the choice in two matters — worship and surrender — so that no one could say that the religion was a matter of preference, and preference can be traded; and He left to him the choice in nothing else, so that the heart be at rest in one direction."*

---
## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:67

> **67** *He is the one who created you from dust, then from a drop, then from a clot; then He brings you out as an infant, then so that you reach your full strength, then so that you grow old — and among you is one who is taken earlier — so that you reach an appointed term, and so that you use reason.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Most Intimate Evidence**

*Huwa lladhī khalaqakum min turābin thumma min nuṭfatin thumma min ʿalaqatin thumma yukhrijukum ṭiflan thumma li-tablughū ashudda thumma li-takūnū shuyūkhan wa-minkum man yutawaffā min qablu*. After the heavens, the earth, the sky, the day, and the night, Ghāfir turns the telescope inward, because its argumentative order is deliberate: the argument from *size* can be met with "so what?", but the argument from *one's own body* cannot be deflected. The sūrah's own summary of the method is 41:53 — *We will show them Our signs on the horizons and within themselves, until it is clear to them that it is the truth* — and the two halves of that verse correspond exactly to 40:57–64 (the horizons) and 40:67 (the self).

**The Stages, and the Word for Each**

*Turāb* — dust, earth, the point is its ordinariness: the material of the most exalted animal is topsoil (cf. 15:26, 23:12, 18:37, 22:5, 35:11, 76:2, and *He began the creation of humankind from clay* 32:7). *Nuṭfa* — a drop, from *n-ṭ-f*, a trickle of liquid (the Qur'an's own use: *was it not a drop of fluid poured out?* 75:37; *from a fluid gushing forth, from between the backbone and the ribs* 86:6–7). *ʿAlaqa* — a clot, from *ʿ-l-q*, to hang clinging, and the Arabs used *ʿalaqa* for a leech because it clings and feeds: hence "the clinging thing." The Qur'an adds *muḍgha* (a chewed-like lump), then *ʿiẓām* (bones), then flesh *clothed* on the bones, then *another creation* (23:12–14; 22:5; 39:6's *fī buṭūni ummahātikum khalqan min baʿdi khalqin fī ẓulumātin thalāth* — "creation after creation, in three darknesses," which the embryologists of the tafsīr (Ibn Kathīr and al-Qurṭubī among them) gloss as the abdominal wall, the uterus, and the fetal membranes). Al-Ṭabarī reports the same sequence as a proof of *ḥikma*, not of chance: each stage is impossible without the one before, and none of them is chosen by the one who passes through them.

The hadith gives the timeline, and it is quoted by the commentators at this verse:

> *"Verily the creation of each of you is gathered in the womb of his mother for forty days as a drop (nuṭfa); then he is a clot (ʿalqa) for the same; then a lump (muḍgha) for the same; then God sends the angel and he breathes the soul into him, and he is commanded with four words: his provision, his term, his deed, and whether wretched or blessed."* (al-Bukhārī 3208; Muslim 2643, from ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd)

The four words are the sūrah's answer to the question of *qadar* in the most personal register possible — provision, term, act, and end are recorded before the person exists — and the verse's *ajal musammā* (an appointed term) is the same datum stated in the sūrah's own terms: *and no soul dies except by God's leave, a term written* (3:145), *and every community has a term; so when their term comes, they cannot delay it an hour nor advance it* (7:34, 10:49; 15:38; 23:43; 39:42; 40:67; 46:35; 55:26; 67:2? the closest is 32:11).

**Ashidda, Shuyūkh, and the Curve of a Life**

*Ashudda* is the state of strength — plural *shidad*, from *sh-d-d*, binding tight, the word for a knotted rope and for a firm place; the Qur'an uses it for the age of responsibility and full physical and mental power (46:15: *until when he reaches his full strength (ashuddahu) and reaches forty years, he says: my Lord, enable me to thank Your blessing... and to act righteously*), and 6:64. *Shuyūkh*, old age, from *sh-y-k*, is the phase of return to weakness: *God is He who created you from weakness, then after weakness made strength, then after strength made weakness and grey hair* (30:54) — a three-beat rhythm that Ghāfir's verse states as a narrative of stages and 30:54 states as a verdict. The *thumma... thumma* (then... then) chain is thus the sūrah's own argument against the Quraysh's pride of ancestry and the Pharaohites' pride of monuments: the line they are so proud of is a line of dependency, and every point on it is given, not achieved.

***Minkum Man Yutawaffā min Qablu*: the Verse That Comforts the Grieving**

*And among you is one who is taken before* — an unremarked clause in many readings, and one of the Qur'an's most compassionate: it states, in the middle of an argument, that some lives end before maturity or old age. Al-Ṭabarī, Ibn Kathīr, and al-Qur᭣ubī record the three referents: death before old age (Q, Aj), before full strength (Aj, Ṭs), or even before birth (Aj, Q) — and the third sense is the one the commentators use to speak of miscarried and stillborn children. The hadith literature supports the same pastoral reading: *"the aborted child will drag its mother into Paradise by its umbilical cord, if she was patient"* (Aḥmad 5/267 and Ibn Mājah 1608, from Umm Salamah, in a chain the muḥaddithūn dispute and which should be cited as such), together with al-Bukhārī's (1382) report that the Prophet ﷺ was asked about the children of the polytheists and said *"God knows best what they would have done"*, and the same book's chapter on the *aṭfāl al-muslimīn*. The verse's own contribution, in any case, is clear: the term that ends a life is *musammā* (named, numbered) and therefore is not an accident, and to call an early death an accident is the specific error the sūrah is refuting.

**The Purpose Clause: *Laʿallakum Taʿqilūn***

The verse ends with the reason for the whole sequence: *li-tablughū ajalan musammā wa-laʿallakum taʿqilūn*. The *ʿaql* is the band that restrains a camel (an *iqāl* is the cord of the headdress) and, figuratively, the faculty that restrains a person from what damages him — and the sūrah says the point of the embryological itinerary is not information but *restraint*: a man who has genuinely thought about how he was made does not behave as if he were self-made. That is the direct line to 40:35's sealed heart and to 40:27's *mutakabbir*: arrogance is a failure of *ʿaql* about one's own origin, and Ghāfir's argument closes by returning to the beginning, exactly as the sūrah's first argument (40:57) closed with *lā taʿlamūn* and as 40:58 closed with *qalīlan mā tadhakkarūn*.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:68

> **68** *He is the one who gives life and causes death; and when He decrees a matter, He only says to it, "Be," and it is.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Verbs, One Author**

*Huwa lladhī yuḥyī wa-yumīt fa-idhā qaḍā amran fa-innamā yaqūlu lahu kun fa-yakūn*. The verse's structure is a proof: because the same subject gives life *and* death, death is not an independent power, a rival god, or a mere cessation of being; it is one of His acts, symmetrical to the other. This is the Qur'an's answer to the two ancient errors about death: to treat it as annihilation without a Master (materialism), and to treat it as the work of an anti-divine principle (dualism). The pairing of the two verbs occurs at 2:258 (Ibrāhīm's argument with the king: *my Lord gives life and causes death*), 3:156, 7:158, 9:116, 10:56, 19:23, 22:6, 23:80, 44:8, 45:26 (*He gives life and causes death; and to Him is the return*), 50:43, 53:44, 57:2, and 85:14 — *and He is the Forgiving, the Loving (al-Ghafūr al-Wadūd)*, which is the *ḥawāmīm*' last word on the same pairing: the power that gives life and death is the power that forgives and loves, and 40:1's *dhikr* of *al-ʿAzīz al-ʿAlīm* beside *ghāfir al-dhanb* is the identical conjunction.

**Kun: the Act That Requires No Instrument**

*Innamā amru-hu idhā arāda shay'an an yaqūla lahu kun fa-yakūn* is the Qur'an's formula of divine creativity (36:82; 2:117; 3:47; 3:59; 16:40; 19:35; 36:82; 40:68), and it is placed at the end of Ghāfir's long section on creation and re-creation to do one job: to make the resurrection an *easy* case. The resurrection is not the difficult second half of a two-part project; it is another *kun*. Al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr both observe that the *fa* of *fa-yakūn* expresses consequence without interval — and the sūrah of Qamar supplies the interval's measure: *our command is only one, like a blink of the eye* (54:50), and 36:53's *"then they will be before Us"* — an instant between the two tenses. The verse's *innamā* ("only") is the sūrah's denial of *āla* and *mudda* (tools and materials): God's act does not depend on clay being available, on a mother's womb, or on the resurrection's machinery, and this is the ground of the classical Sunni doctrine of *ījād* (bringing-into-being from non-existence, without a substrate), which the *falāsifa* rejected in favor of eternal emanation.

**Secondary Causes: the Debate This Verse Anchors**

Because the verse can be read as abolishing causality, it became a locus of one of the most important arguments in Islamic thought. Al-Ashʿarī's school, with al-Juwaynī and al-Ghazālī (*al-Iqtiṣād*; *Tahāfut al-Falāsifa*, where the philosophers are charged with *tawqīf*, making God's acts dependent on conditions), held that God creates each event directly and that "causes" are His custom, not independent agents. Ibn Rushd's rebuttal (*Tahāfut al-Tahāfut*, and *al-Kashf ʿan Manāhij al-Adilla*) insisted that a fixed order of causes is itself the *sunna* of God in creation and not a limit on Him. Ibn Taymiyyah (*Darʾ Taʿāruḍ*, *al-Radd ʿalā al-Manṭiqiyyīn*, and the *Fatāwā*) took the strongest text-based position: God creates through will, wisdom, and means that are themselves created — *kun* is real, and the natural order is real, because *your Lord is the one who knows best who has strayed* (68:7's principle that wisdom governs) and the Qur'an itself attributes rain to clouds and healing to honey (16:69; 2:164) while saying *you did not kill them, but God killed them* (8:17). The verse's own context in Ghāfir favors the balance: 40:36–37 has already mocked Pharaoh's search for a *sabab* to the heavens as though God could be reached by engineering, and 40:68 answers not by denying means but by locating the Source: the *sabab* exists by *kun*, and Pharaoh's tower failed not because cause and effect do not work but because they do not add up to access to God.

**Life, Death, and the Verse's Pastoral Function in the Sūrah**

The chapter has spent forty verses on the death of nations and of individuals, and it closes this section not with a threat but with the statement that the same being who gives life gives death. That is a consolation for the bereaved (the *ajal musammā* of 40:67 is administered by the *yuḥyī wa-yumīt* of 40:68, not by fortune) and a warning to the persecutor: a man who can only shorten a life has no leverage over the One who owns both verbs. Ibn Kathīr, citing 40:68 alongside 2:258, notes that Ibrāhīm's interlocutor was struck dumb by this very pairing, because a king whose power consists of killing has just been told he does not even control that.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:69

> **69** *Have you not considered those who dispute concerning the signs of God — how they are turned away?*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Refrain, Final Form**

*A-fa-lam yaraw ilā lladhīna yujādilūna fī āyāti Llāhi annā yuṣarrafūn* — the translation's "have they not considered" renders the seeing (*yara*) with the verb of reflection (*iʿtibār*) that the verse implies. The fourth and last appearance of the disputers (40:4, 35, 56), and the verse's verb of *ʿibra* is *yura* — "have they not *seen*," which is the faculty 40:58's parable of the blind and the seeing was built to expose. Al-Ṭabarī glosses *yuṣarrafūn*: "turned away from the truth after it was clear to them," and connects it to 40:62's *tu'faqūn* and to the sūrah's law that turning is reciprocal — *so We turn their hearts and their eyes away, as they refused to believe in it the first time* (6:110). Al-Rāzī notes that the interrogative of 40:69 is deliberately not an argument but an exhortation to look at an *argument already given*: it does not add a premise; it asks why the previous ones failed, and answers with the verb *yuṣarrafūn* — divine *ṣarf* (turning away) as the fate of a people who made turning away their project.

**The Signs of 40:69: Which Ones?**

The source commentary's note is exact and important for reading the verse: the *āyāt* here follow immediately upon a long sequence of cosmological and biological signs (40:61–68), so the "signs" the sūrah's final disputers are arguing about are not primarily verses of the Qur'an but the created facts — night and day, the making of the heavens, the human body's origin. This has a consequence for how the verse is applied: *jidāl fī āyāti Llāh* includes argument about the world as revelation — evolution, cosmology, medicine, history — and Ghāfir's criterion, once again, is *sulṭān* (40:35, 56): the disputant with a warrant is a scientist or a theologian; the disputant whose only motive is that his standing not be disturbed is one of 40:69's. Ibn Taymiyyah's celebrated treatment of the *āyāt* in *al-Radd ʿalā al-Manṭiqiyyīn* and *— the operative point he repeatedly makes, and that the sūrah makes here, is that the "sign" is a thing that *signifies beyond itself*, so that to dispute it as though it were only itself is to change the subject and call it inquiry.

**Annā Yuṣarrafūn: an Astonishment, Not a Description**
