The construction *a-fa-lam yaraw... annā yuṣarrafūn* carries a tone of wonder at the perversity of it: "how they are turned away!" — the exclamation, not the passive statement. Al-Qurṭubī records that some read *annā* as "that," and others as the interrogative of amazement, and both senses converge on the verse's purpose: to make the reader ask *why does this not land?* The answer the sūrah has supplied in its own previous verses is threefold and should be stated plainly, because 40:69 is where the sūrah collects it: motive (40:56 — *kifār*), habit (40:34 — persistent doubt), and divine confirmation of the choice (40:35 — the seal; 40:69 — the *ṣarf*). That is the chapter's sober realism about persuasion: the failure of evidence to convert a person is neither a surprise nor a refutation, and the believer is not obliged to keep adding data to a heart that has been shown to be closed — hence the very next command in this sūrah's chain: *so be patient* (40:55, 40:77).

**What the Verse Asks the Reader Not to Do**

The verse's negative theology of argument is as important as its positive teaching: it forbids (a) taking the persistence of dispute as evidence against the truth — 40:4 already forbade that — and (b) imagining that enough argument will produce faith — 40:13 and 40:58 say the opposite, that *ināba* and sight are conditions of reflection, not its products. Between those two, the sūrah's instruction is to keep the duty of conveying and to release the claim on the outcome (29:18, 88:21–22, 10:99 — *would you compel people to be believers?*), which is why the section's next verses (40:70–76) describe the end rather than restate the case: the end is the only remaining argument with the disputers, and the sūrah says it once, plainly, and returns to patience (40:77).

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:70

> **70** *Those who deny the Book and that with which We sent Our messengers. Soon they will know,*

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Disputation to Denial: the Trajectory Completed**

*Alladhīna yukadhdhibūna al-kitāba wa-mā arsalnā bihi rusulanā fa-sawfa yaʿlamūn*. The 40:69 disputers are now described without the *sulṭān* qualifier, because the qualifier was a concession that has been exhausted: those who dispute the signs long enough end by *denying* the Book, which is the sūrah's law of hardening. Ibn Kathīr notes the verb's tense: *yukadhdhibūna* is present and continuous — *they go on calling it a lie, day after day* — and the *fa-sawfa yaʿlamūn* is not a threat but a statement of epistemic future: there is a class of knowledge that can only be had by experience, and their remaining experience will be of this. The Qur'an uses the same idiom at 6:158, 15:3, 15:96, 26:213, 29:66, 37:170, 38:88, 43:89, 52:29, 68:50, 74:43? and 4:165, and al-Ṭabarī's gloss is consistent: *they will know the truth of what they denied, at the point when knowledge no longer benefits.*

**"The Book and That With Which We Sent Our Messengers": Two Objects, One Refusal**

The verse distinguishes *al-kitāb* (the written revelation) from *mā arsalnā bihi rusulanā* (that which We sent with Our messengers — the message, the law, the miracle, the *bayyināt*; Q, Aj, Ṭ). Al-Rāzī's explanation of the distinction is instructive: some reject the *Book* but accept the prophets as inspired men; some accept revelation in general and reject the specific content; both are named here, so that the verse's scope cannot be narrowed by a claim to "spirituality without scripture." The pairing occurs at 2:89 and 2:101, 42:17, and 5:48 (*and We sent down to you the Book with the truth, confirming what was before it of the Book and guarding it*), which supplies the sūrah's own definition of what rejecting the Qur'an entails: not rejecting a book but rejecting *the guardian of the books*.

***Kadh-dhaba*: the Root That Means "to Find Empty"**

*Kadhdhaba* is the intensive of *k-dh-b*, whose concrete sense is to find a thing empty: *kadhdhabahu* means "he found it devoid of what he expected," and lying is called *kidhb* because the liar's statement is empty of what it pretends to contain (Ibn Fāris, *Maqāyīs al-Lugha*; al-Azharī, *Tahdhīb*). The verse therefore does not say *they disbelieve* but *they treat as empty* — and the echo across the sūrah is exact: 40:5's *li-yuḍḍidū bihi al-ḥaqq* (so as to make the truth *empty*, *maḥlūq*), and 40:25's *makr* ending in *ḍalāl*, and 40:42's *mā lā ʿilma lī bihī*. The sūrah's vocabulary thus keeps telling the reader the same thing about its opponents: their project is a program of *deflation* — the deliberate treatment of full things as empty — which is why the promise of 40:70–72 is that they will be *filled* with what they called unreal.

**The Verse's Discipline: Restraint in the Threat**

Note what the sūrah does *not* say: no description of punishment appears here, only *fa-sawfa yaʿlamūn*. The rhetorical effect, as al-Zamakhsharī notes at 15:96 and 38:88, is *ihmāl* (leaving it unbounded), which is more frightening than description: the reader supplies what he fears, and the sūrah reserves the right to be more exact. This is a literary virtue with a moral one: the Qur'an does not gloat. Ibn Kathīr's comment on the verse's placement is that the threat is *deferral*, and deferral is mercy, and the sūrah will repeat the same formula at 40:72 and 40:79 (*sa-yatadhakkaron* — 40:44) in a rhythm that keeps the reader in the present tense of choice.

**The Verse for Believers Who Are Being Denied**

For the Makkan audience, 40:70 is also an answer to a specific psychological pressure: being *called a liar* by one's own people and their institutions. The sūrah has already put the accusation in the mouths of Pharaoh's court (*sāḥir kadhdhāb*, 40:24) and now hands the verdict to the future: the truth of the claimant does not require present recognition, and recognition that comes too late is worth nothing (40:84–85). Ibn ʿAṭiyyah's line on this type of verse — *the promise of knowledge is the punishment of the one who refused to know* — is the summary of the section that runs to 40:76, and it is also the sūrah's way of saying to the bullied believer: your case is not open, only awaiting.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:71

> **71** *When the shackles are around their necks and the chains — they will be dragged*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Image, and What It Replaces**

*Idh al-ghullu fī aʿnāqihim wa-l-silasilu yusḥabūn*. The verse is a single, spare picture, and the sūrah has emptied it of everything but iron and necks. Al-Ṭabarī explains *ghull* (pl. *aghlāl*): an iron collar or ring locked around the neck, by which a captive is led, and he cites the Qur'an's other uses (36:8: *We have placed on their necks collars up to their chins, so they are with heads thrown back*; 13:5 and 40:71; 47:4's *fa-shadd al-mawthiq*, and 69:30's *khudhūhu fa-ghūllūhu* — "seize him and fetter him"), and Ibn Kathīr adds that *ghull* also means being bound at the wrists to the neck (*i.e.*, the hands tied to the neck), and the Bedouin usage attaches *ghayl* — slaughter of a young animal beside its mother — to the same imagery of the neck and its bonds, but the sūrah does not specify which of the two bindings is meant. *Silasila*, from *s-l-s-l*, is a chain whose links are continuous — hence *salasal*, the rattling sound of metal on metal, and the same root gives the *silsila* of hadith transmission, the chain of narrators, a fact which for once illuminates the Arabic's aesthetic rather than the law: the chain that binds a report is a *silsila* too, and Ghāfir's readers lived in a world where the word meant "linkage."

**Why Chains, and Not Fire Alone**

The sūrah's punishment-images are not decorative but *diagnostic*, and the classical commentators read them as such: the *ghull* answers the proud neck. Al-Rāzī notes that the Arabs used to put a *ghillāla* of gold or a *ṭawq* on a noble's neck as a mark of rank (cf. 5:103's abolition of *baḥīra, sāʾiba, waṣīla, ḥām*, where collars and dedicated things are stripped of sanctity), and that the Qur'an's imagery is often a *contrapuntal inversion* of the crime: the arrogant's neck, which would not bow in *sujūd* (40:60's *istikbār ʿan ʿibādatī*), is ornamented in the Fire with the only "collar" left to it, and it is dragged by what it refused to bend. The same principle governs 40:60's *dākhurīn*, 40:75's *bi-mā kuntum tafirḥūna* for *exultation* and 40:76's *abwab* for *gates* — and it is why Ibn al-Qayyim, in *al-Rūḥ*, treats the punishments of the Qur'an as *ajzāʾ al-aʿmāl*, the deeds themselves in their uncovered form, and not as arbitrary inflictions.

**Yusḥabūn: the Passive of the Helpless**

*Yusḥabūn*, from *s-ḥ-b*, is to be dragged along, to be pulled behind — used of a man led against his will (11:77 — *and come to your people in the morning... Lūṭ's people*, and 26:94's *fa-yuṣabbūna fī al-nāri ṣabā*, "they will be poured into the Fire"). The passive is the grammar of the verse's whole argument: in life they *led* (40:47's *tabaʿ*, and 28:4's *mustadʿafūn*, whose *ḍuʿafāʾ* follow their *mustakbirūn*), and the sūrah has been reversing the verb of *leadership* throughout — the leaders' last words were "we are all in it" (40:48). Now the direction is the same (they go where they are taken), and the agency is gone. Al-Zamakhsharī observes that the verse's grammar has *no subject for the dragging*, which is the point: the identity of the angels of punishment is irrelevant to the fact of being unable to resist; and the same ellipsis occurs at 14:49's *wa-tarā al-mujrimīna yawmaʾidhin muqqarinīna fī al-aghlāl* (in effect) — *the criminals will see themselves yoked in collars* — and 18:28, 25:27, 44:47-49, and 69:30-32, where the *silsila* is *seventy cubits* long (69:32), which the sūrah here leaves unstated in order to be more terrible.

**Yadsbahūna: the Chain That Is the World**

The verse's other word, *ghull*, is used elsewhere for what is *self-imposed*: *and do not wrap your hand around your neck* (17:29's *wa-lā tajʿal yadaka mughluqatan ilā ʿunuqika*, in effect — "do not make your hand chained to your neck," i.e., do not be miserly), which is a stunning cross-reference: miserliness is already a *ghull*, and the Qur'an's eschatology is a revelation of the shapes the soul has already given itself (the same logic as 40:46's exposure *morning and evening*: the shape of the *barzakh* is the shape of the habit). The hadith that the sūrah's imagery illustrates is al-Tirmidhī's (2460), cited above at 40:46, in the wording of Zayd ibn Thābit: *"the grave is the first stage of the stages of the Hereafter; either a garden of the gardens of Paradise or a pit of the pits of Hell"*, and the *ghull* of 40:71 is, in this reading, the next link in a chain whose first link the person forged himself in the world.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:72

> **72** *In the boiling liquid, then in the Fire they will be burned.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Elements in the Wrong Order**

*Fī al-ḥamīmi thumma fī al-nāri yurjawn*. The sequence — *ḥamīm* first, then Fire, with the *thumma* of escalation and not of sequence — is the sūrah's own description of the movement described elsewhere as to-and-fro: *this is the Hell which the criminals deny; they will pass back and forth between it and *ḥamīm* — scalding water* (55:43–44; and 14:50 — *and the Fire will surround them from below*; and 38:58, 44:43's *shajaratu al-zaqqūm, ṭaʿāmu al-athīm, ka-l-muhli yaghlī fī al-buṭūn ka-ghalay al-qaṭūn*; and 52:13's *yawma yudhaʿfuna fī al-nāri* for doubling). The *ḥamīm* is, in Arabic, *ḥamm*, heat — hot water, heated oil, the *ḥammām*, and the Qur'an's *māʾin ḥamīm* occurs at 47:15, 55:42–44, 56:42–44, and 38:57; the *ḥamīm* of 56:93 is the drink of the left-hand people, and the *ghaysaq* of 69:36 is paired with *lā ṭaʿāma illā min ghislin*; and at 18:29's *wa-in yastaghīthū yughathū bi-māʾin ka-l-muhli yashwī al-wujūh, biʾsa al-sharābu wa-sāʾat murafiqā* — "if they ask for help, they are given water like molten dregs that scalds the faces; wretched the drink and wretched the lodging," which is the closest exegetical parallel to 40:72.

**The Irony of the Drink**

The scalding *drink* is the sūrah's most carefully engineered punishment, and the commentators (al-Zamakhsharī, Ibn Kathīr, al-Qurṭubī, and Ibn al-Qayyim in *al-Rūḥ*) all note that it answers the world's refusals. In the Qur'an, the inhabitants of the Fire cry to the people of the Garden: *pour on us of water, or of what God has provided you* (7:50), and are told it is forbidden; and 40:49 asked for a *lightening of a day* and got a command to *call*. The sūrah's own 14:17 describes the drink: *and he will come upon it and will not be able to swallow it, and upon him will be a barrier* — *wa-yatajarraʿuhū wa-lā yastatīʿu an yusīghah*. The logic is not cruelty but *exposure of the delusion*: their thirst in this life was for what would never satisfy (cf. 14:47's *and the Fire will surround them*; and the hadith *"whoever does not give thanks to people has not thanked God"* — Abū Dāwūd 4811 and al-Tirmidhī 1954, cited for the *shukr* theme at 40:61). The drink is granted, and it is what the refusal to be grateful makes of a gift.

**Yurjawn: the Fire as a Garment**

The verb *w-r-y*, in the passive *yurjawn*, is "to be roasted, to be broiled": al-Azharī and Ibn Fāris both gloss *al-wary* as the exposure of a body to heat without anything interposing, as *warīḥ* is roasted flesh cooked in a pit, and the form occurs in the Qur'an only here. And the sūrah's image of garments for the damned is the other half of this: *their garments are of tar, and the Fire covers their faces* (14:50), and *like molten brass* (18:29's *ka-l-muhli*), and the *sarbāl* (shirt) of *qāṭirān* (14:50). So 40:72 places the *ḥamīm* and the *nār* as *two media*, and the verse's *thumma* says: whichever they are put in, they come out into the other.

**The Structure: Ghāfir's Punishment-Vocabulary in One Verse**

This verse is the sūrah's compendium of eschatological punishment, and it should be read against its own chapter's other words: *ʿadhāb* (pain, 40:11's *ʿadhāba al-ḥamīr* — the angels' petition asked for exactly this, so the answer to the angels' prayer and the fate of the unrepentant use the same noun), *sūʾ al-ʿadhāb* (the worst of it, 40:45–46, 52), *ʿiqāb* (requital, 40:3, 40:5, 40:22), and the *rajz* that in the sūrah's own idiom names the blow the wicked feared (cf. 7:134, 7:165, 2:145, where the root gives the plague sent on the evildoers). The sūrah's discipline is that it never uses one of these words for a believer, and never uses *ʿafw* or *maghfira* for the persistently proud: the vocabulary is a map of what each party chose, and 40:72 is the last of the Fire-terms the chapter will deploy before its closing verses turn to patience (40:77) and to the world's gifts (40:79–82). Al-Ṭabarī's conclusion on 40:70–72 is the whole sūrah's summary: *God moved from the disputers to their end, and He did not do so to terrify the obedient, but to show the disbelievers where their disputation is going — and the going is the arrived.*

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:73

> **73** *Then it will be said: "Where are those you used to associate with Him, beside God?" They will say: "They have vanished from us; rather, we were not invoking anything at all." — that is because you used to exult in the land without right and because you were insolent.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Roll-Call With No Answer**

*Ayna lladhīna kuntum tushriʿūna bihim min dūni Llāh* — the question put to them is not "do your gods exist?" but "*where are they?*" The interrogative of place concedes a location to beings that never had one, which is the verse's first irony: the damned are addressed with the courtesy of the roll-call, name by name, and nothing answers. The scene belongs to a family of Qur'anic interrogations that all put the same question to the same people: *And on the Day He will call them and say: Where are those you used to claim [to be My] partners?* (28:62); *And He will say: Where are My partners whom you used to defend? And we will pour out upon those who wronged them a punishment* (16:47); *and who is more astray than one who invokes, besides God, what does not answer him until the Day of Resurrection, and they are heedless of his invocation? And when the people are gathered, those things will be enemies to them and deny what they worshipped* (46:5–6). Al-Ṭabarī takes the question as *taqrīʿ* — exposure — since God already knows the answer, and the point of the Day is that the accused be the one to say it aloud.

**"They Have Vanished From Us" — Three Senses in Two Words**

*Ḍallū ʿannā*, from *ḍ-l-l*: they have lost the road, they are lost to me, they are simply not to be found. All three senses operate at once, and the commentators enjoy pointing it out: the idols have gone astray (which the Quraysh had said of the Prophet ﷺ — *your companion has not erred and is not misguided*, 53:2), they have been lost like a mislaid possession, and they were never findable in the first place. The reply is thus an unintentional creed: the worshipper, under interrogation, states the sūrah's own theology of *shirk* better than he ever stated it as a doctrine. It is the second half of 2:167 — *and those who were followed disowned those who followed, and they saw the punishment and the ways were cut off from them* — and 19:82: *Nay, they will deny what they used to associate with them.*

**The Admission That Was Impossible in the World**

*Bal lam takūnū nadʿū shayʾā* — "rather, we were not calling upon anything at all." In the world they swore the opposite: *By God our Lord, we were not polytheists* (6:23), to which God answers: *Look how they lie against themselves, and what they used to invent has vanished from them* (6:24). Here there is no *invention* left to defend, because the arrangement that made the lie worth telling has dissolved; the *bal* marks a correction not of the questioners but of themselves. Ibn Kathīr glosses: they will concede that what they worshipped deserved no worship whatever, and that their worship had no object — the sharpest possible formulation of the Qur'an's claim that an idol is not a false god so much as no god. The source commentary's parallel is perfect: Satan's own speech on that Day — *God made you a promise of truth, and I made you a promise and failed you; I had no authority over you except that I called you and you responded to me* (14:22) — and their reply about him, *Satan has been a deserter (ghūrūr) to man* (25:29).

**A Partner Is Something You Can Rely On — and This Is the Test**

Note the verse's word: not *āliha* (gods) but *shurakāʾ* (partners), the term for co-owners and guarantors, as in 12:39 — *I have left the religion of a people who do not know God; I follow the religion of my fathers Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob. It is not for us to associate anything with God* — where *tushrikūn* is used of dependence in practice rather than of creed in the abstract. The sūrah thus frames the interrogation around *function*: at the moment when deliverance was the only thing on the table, where were the guarantors? Al-Zamakhsharī notes that addressing them with the polite plural ("your partners") heightens the rebuke, and al-Rāzī adds that the verse's real addressee is the reader still in the world: the only partner worth having is the one who is present when every other is absent, which is why the sūrah has spent six verses (40:61–66) establishing that God alone is *mubdiʾ muʿīd* — the One who originates and returns.

**Thus Does God Lead the Disbelievers Astray**

*Kadhālika yuḍillu Llāhu al-kafrīn* (in the sūrah's own phrasing, *al-ẓālimīn* at the parallel place). The *kadhālika* ("thus") is the sūrah's guarantee against reading the verse as arbitrary: misguidance here is *regular and consequent*, the same law stated at 40:33–35 (*thus does God seal every arrogant heart; whomever God misguides, he has no guide*), and it is issued only after the *choice* has been described (40:75's exultation and 40:76's insolence). Al-Ṭabarī's gloss — "as He left these to what they chose, so He leaves every persisting disbeliever" — is the standard Sunni reading, and the sūrah has already fenced it with its two axioms: *God does not wish wrong for the servants* (40:31) and *no wrong is done that Day* (40:17). What the verse teaches, then, is not that God misleads inquisitors but that a person who persists in taking as a partner what cannot answer is *left* to the consequence, and the consequence is that at the last he can say only "there was nothing there."

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:74
> **74** *That is because you used to exult in the land without right and because you were insolent — thus does God lead the disbelievers astray.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Cause Named Before the Sentence**

*Dhālikum bi-annakum aftirtumu fi al-arḍi bi-ghayri al-ḥaqqi wa-kuntum takhbuthūn* — the demonstrative *dhālikum* ("that") points back to what 40:73's interrogation exposed, and the *bi-* of *bi-annakum* is the causal *baʾ*: the structure is a verdict followed by its charges, and the sūrah puts the charges *after* the sentence so that no reader mistakes the account for sociology. Al-Zamakhsharī observes that this is the Qur'an's normal method in the Fire-scenes — the punishment is mentioned, then the act that "caused" it is named in the plainest verbs available — because on that Day causation is experienced, not argued.

**The Verb of Rejoicing, and Why It Is a Charge**

*Aftirtum* here is the sūrah's verb of *joy* — *fariḥūn*, *iftirāḥ* — and not of *fabrication* (the homonym *iftirāʾ*, which the Qur'an reserves for *iftirāʾ ʿalā Llāh*, inventing lies about God, e.g. 6:21, 7:53, 10:69, 11:13, 29:68, 46:22, 61:7). Ghāfir uses the two roots in the same neighborhood (40:28's *musrif kadhdhāb* and 40:74's *iftirtum... takhbuthūn*), and the near-homonymy is part of the rhetoric: the people who *fabricate* about God are the same who *exult* on the earth without right, because both vices are the same refusal to acknowledge a limit. The source commentary notes that one may legitimately rejoice — *Those to whom We have given the Book rejoice in what has been sent down to you* (13:36) — while *iftirāḥ* in the Qur'an is almost always negative, implying heedlessness of God in the midst of enjoyment; and *maraḥ* (insolence, exultant pride) can only mean vanity. Al-Ṭabarī glosses *bi-ghayri al-ḥaqq* as "not having any right to it," i.e., you rejoiced in what was never yours and forgot the Giver.

**The Source Commentary's Other Reading: "Which of God's Boons?"**

The source commentary records the two senses for the question that follows (40:75–76's *dhālikum bi-annakum*): that the reproof is an admission-test ("if you concede the benefits are God's, how can you deny the resurrection?" — Q), or an amazement at the sheer unreasonableness of denial (IK). The *biʾsa* form — "how evil is the abode" — belongs to the second, expressive type: it does not *argue*, it *names*, the way *biʾsa al-maʾwā* is used at 3:151, 3:197, 14:29, and 29:68. Ghāfir uses it at 40:16 and 40:76, i.e., once at the end of the first half of the chapter and once at the end of the second, which is the sort of structural rhyme the sūrah is full of and which suggests that the whole chapter is framed by a single question about where a person intends to *make his home*.

**What the Demonstrative Does in the Qur'an's Fire-Scenes**

To set the idiom precisely: in the Qur'an's Fire-scenes the demonstrative *dhālika/dhālikum* is used to convert an event into a *consequence*, and always with the preposition *bi-* of cause: *dhālika bi-anna Llāha kāna...*, "that is because God was..." The clearest pairs are 28:42 — *dhālika bi-anna rabbaka... wa-mā kāna rabbusa muhlika al-qurā bi-ẓulmin* — and 11:117: *wa-mā kāna rabbuka li-yuhlika al-qurā bi-ẓulmin wa-qawmuhum muṣliḥūn*. Ghāfir uses the same construction at 40:13 (*dhālikum bi-anna huwa...*) and at 40:34, and its theological payload is constant: what happens to people in the Qur'an's eschatology is *because*, and the *because* names their own act, never a divine mood. That is why the sūrah's three causal verses (40:13, 40:34, 40:74) sit at the openings of its three great blocks; the chapter's argument about punishment is settled in the first line of each.

The reader who objects that an eternal punishment is disproportionate to a finite crime is thus answered by the sūrah's own grammar rather than by philosophy: *dhālikum bi-annakum* does not say "I decided to punish you"; it says "this is *that* — the thing you were doing, still going on." The sūrah's datum needs no report: the crime is named in the same tense as the penalty, and both are past.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:75

> **75** *That is because you used to exult in the land without right and because you were insolent.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Two Halves: Heedlessness and Aggression**

The verse's parallelism is a small psychology lesson. The first clause names an inward state (enjoyment without reference to God); the second, an outward one (*takhbūth*: insolence, doing violence to people's rights). Al-Qurṭubī and Ibn Kathīr both record the exegetes' gloss on *takhbuthūn*: "you behaved tyrannically toward God's servants and proudly against God." That order matters for the sūrah's argument, since Ghāfir has insisted all along that private *fariḥ* hardens into public *jabarūt* — Pharaoh's *mā arākum* (40:29), Qārūn's *innamā ūtītuhu ʿalā ʿilm* (40:78's echo at 28:78), and the *mustakbirūn* of 40:76's closing line all show the same trajectory. A joy that is not accountable becomes, in short time, a conduct that is not accountable.

**"In the Land" — the Public Stage**

*Fi al-arḍ*, not *fi anfusikum*: the exultation described is visible, performed, and therefore imposed. The Qur'an's cognate image is 31:18 — *do not turn your cheek from people in contempt, and do not walk upon the earth exultantly; God does not love any boastful one* — where *khuyalāʾ* (strutting) is the bodily form of *iftirāḥ*, and 25:63, the believers' mirror-image: *the servants of the All-Merciful walk upon the earth in gentleness*. Al-Rāzī notes that 40:74 indicts the *aesthetics* of power, not merely its acts — a society's pageantry, its display of the *ḥillah* and the *markab*, is a moral fact because it teaches the weak what counts — which is why the sūrah's own counter-scene (40:8–9, 40:40) locates honor in *ʿamal ṣāliḥ* rather than in *mansab*.

**How the Verse Reads After 40:61–68**

Placed immediately after the long inventory of gifts — night and day, the sky as *bināʾ*, the earth as *qarār*, the stages of the embryo, cattle and ships (40:61–79) — the verse lands with unusual force: the enjoyment it forbids is enjoyment *of precisely those gifts*, held as though they were achievements. The logical bridge is 40:61's and 40:79's shared verb, *jaʿala*, "He made... for you," which makes every *niʿma* a trust rather than a possession. That is why the sūrah's last word is gratitude and not asceticism: it never says *do not rejoice*; it says *do not rejoice as though the rejoicing were yours.*

**The Grammar of the Verse: A Causal Sentence with No Verb of Punishment**

*Dhālikum bi-annakum aftirtum fi al-arḍi bi-ghayri al-ḥaqqi wa-kuntum takhbuthūn* — the whole verse is a prepositional explanation hanging off a demonstrative, with no *fa-ʿāqabnākum*, no "and therefore I punished you." Al-Zamakhsharī notes the rhetoricians' praise of this construction: when a threatened consequence is left unsaid, the mind supplies it with more force than the sentence could, and the *iḍāfa* of *dhālika* to a *sabab* (a cause) without a *musabbab* (a result) makes the reader perform the connection himself. It is the same technique as 40:39's *qalīl* — the punishment of the arrogant is not described here, only *attributed*, because the description came in verses 71–76 and the attribution is what the verse needs to make: the point of the passage is not that God burns people but that *their own exultation is what burns*.

That attribution is why the verse mixes its aspects — the perfect *bi-annakum aftirtum* for a settled, completed habit of the whole life, and the imperfect *wa-kuntum takhbuthūn* for a state persisted in, the perfect marking the settled fact of a lifetime's disposition and the imperfect of *takhbuthūn* its repetition. Al-Rāzī draws out the pastoral use: a person's end is described in the Qur'an by the *tense* of his own habits, which is why the sūrah's imperatives (40:55's *wa-istaghfir*, 40:60's *udʿūnī*, 40:77's *fa-ṣṭabir*) are all present-tense: what you *keep doing* is the sentence that will be read out.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:76

**An Order, Not a Description**
*Udkulū abwāba jahannama khālidīn*. The imperative is God's, and it is what the commentators call an *amr takwīn* — a creative command, of the type *kun fa-yakūn*, which this sūrah used for embryology (40:68): the sentence does not describe an event, it *is* the event, and the doors open because they are addressed. Ghāfir has prepared the reversal carefully. At 40:49–50 the inhabitants of the Fire tried to open a channel of speech upward (*call upon your Lord to lighten for us a day of the punishment*) and were met with *wa-mā duʿāʾ al-kāfirīn illā fī ḍalāl* — "the invocation of the disbelievers is nothing but dissipation." Here the speech runs the other way: no one asks and everyone is told. The verse's terrible economy is that on that Day the disbelievers are no longer interlocutors but the addressed.

> **76** *Enter the gates of Hell, to abide therein. How evil is the dwelling-place of the arrogant!*

**"The Gates of Hell": a City Whose Inhabitants Do Not Leave**

*Bāb* is a door; *abwāb* its plural, the gates of a walled place. The sūrah chose the architectural term over the plain *nār*, and the exegetes follow the choice: the source commentary notes the tradition that the gates are seven, and points to 39:71–72, where the righteous are *led to the Garden in groups and its gates are opened*, while the enemies are *driven to Hell in groups*, and to 15:44 — *for it there are seven gates, each of them having an allotted portion of them*. Al-Ṭabarī records the reading that the portions correspond to classes of sinners and degrees of punishment. Whether or not one presses the number, the imagery does its work: *khālidīn* applied to people inside a walled place with assigned gates is not burning but *quartering* — the damned are housed, given a portion, and never moved on. The pairing with Paradise's eight gates in the two Ṣaḥīḥs' hadith ("*whoever dies associating nothing with God will enter Paradise*," and the report of the gates of Paradise in al-Bukhārī's book of prayer) completes a single architecture of two cities, which the sūrah has been describing since 40:8's *maswā*.

**"How Evil the Dwelling-Place of the Arrogant!"**

*Fa-biʾsa dār al-mutakabbirīn*. *Biʾsa* is Arabic's verb of condemnation — "how wretched X is" — and the grammarians' standard illustration of it is *biʾsat al-biʾru*, "how wretched the well": a well too narrow or too dry to draw anything from, which is why the idiom implies not merely badness but *unproductiveness*. A home that yields nothing is the precise punishment of the *mutakabbir*, who spent his life refusing to draw on anything above himself. The verse's noun *dār* (dwelling) rather than *ʿadhāb* (pain) is deliberate, and the sūrah has set up the contrast at 40:39: *this world's provision is little, and the next is dār al-qarār* — the home of settling. The arrogant get a *dār* too, and the adjective tells you what kind. The word *mutakabbirīn* is also the sūrah's final label for the class (40:35, 40:56, 40:60, 40:76), so that the last thing Ghāfir says about the proud before turning to consolation is a definition of Hell as *the society of the proud*, which is why the punishment named here is not the Fire but the *company* and the *permanence*.

**The Verse as the Mirror of 40:60**

At 40:60 the sūrah promised: *Call upon Me; I will respond to you. Truly those who are too proud to worship Me will enter Hell debased* — *ṣāghirīn*. Verse 76 repeats the two elements of that sentence, entry and arrogance, and adds only *khālidīn* and the *dār*. Read together, the pair is the chapter's whole practical theology in six words: one door answers (*udʿūnī*), the other does not, and the second is entered only by those who refused the first while it was open. The rhetorical force lies in the tenses: 40:60's command was *present*, 40:76's is *imperative aorist*, and between the two lies every verse of the sūrah that describes the *mudda* — the interval — during which Pharaoh was given rope, the believing man was given voice, and the disbelievers were given *taqallub* (40:4). What 40:76 marks is the moment the interval closes, which the sūrah has announced twice already in the same words: *and the Hour is coming, no doubt in it* (40:59), and *to Him is the return* (40:3).

**A Note on *Fa-Biʾsa*: The Arabic's Bluntest Instrument**

*Fa-biʾsa dāra...*: the verb *biʾsa* is what the grammarians call a verb of *madḥ wa-dhamm* (praise and blame) that requires an *ism fāʿil*-like subject and takes an *imtiyāz* for the respect in which the verdict applies — the syntax is "how wretched, as a dwelling, is X," and the verse's *dār* is precisely that "as a dwelling" specification. The upshot is not grammatical pedantry but moral precision: the verse does not say "how wretched is the Fire" (its heat is not the point) but "how wretched *as a home*" — the failure of the *dār* is not temperature but *yield*, the same logic as *biʾsat al-biʾr*, "the well from which nothing comes." This is why Ghāfir's final word about the arrogant is *dār* and not *nār*, and why 40:76's *khālidīn* is a mercy-shaped horror: what is eternal about the home is not its fire but its fruitlessness.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:77

> **77** *So be patient. Truly the promise of God is true. And whether We show you some of what We promise them, or We take you — to Us they are returned.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sūrah's Second Command of Patience**

*Fa-ṣṭabir inna waʿda Llāhi ḥaqq*. The words are the opening of 40:55, and the repetition is structural: what God said to the Prophet ﷺ after the account of Mūsā He says again after the account of the Fire, so that the reader who has just been shown 40:76's *dār* hears, at the end of the chapter, the only imperative left. Al-Biqāʿī's insight into the *Ḥawāmīm* is worth recalling here: the cycle opens with *mā yujādilu fī āyāti Llāhi* (40:4) and its first chapter closes with *fa-ṣṭabir* (40:77) — for an age of argument, the prescription is not better arguments but endurance. That is a strange and instructive conclusion for a sūrah so packed with reasoning: the *ḥujja* is complete long before the heart is, so the command falls on the will.

**Patience and the Truth of a Promise That Is Not Dated**

*Inna waʿda Llāhi ḥaqq* names no deadline, and the two following clauses make the reason explicit: either We show you part of what We promise them — the exegetes take this as punishment during his lifetime, which is the worldly half of 40:51's pledge (*We will aid Our messengers and those who believe in the life of this world*) — or We take you, in which case *ilayna yurjaʿūn*, "to Us they are returned." The disjunction is exhaustive and its consequence identical, so the verse grants the Prophet ﷺ something no earlier consolation in the chapter had stated: he is not required to *see* the promise fulfilled in order to rely on it. Al-Ṭabarī's gloss is that both branches end in return to God, and therefore neither contains a failure; the second branch is not a concession but another route to the same judgment seat. The verb of the second branch, *nutawaffayyaka*, is used of God as the one who takes (cf. 32:11, *sayyāʾūn*), and the root *w-f-y* means "to complete," so the sūrah's diction quietly makes the Prophet's death itself an act of divine *wafāʾ* — a keeping of what is owed.

**"To Us They Are Returned" as Threat, Not Comfort**

The clause *fa-ilayna yurjaʿūn* has been Ghāfir's comfort twice already (40:3 *wa-ilayhi al-maṣīr*, and 40:67's *thumma yuwaffīkum ajūrakum*); here its subject is the enemies, and the same words become a warrant. The sūrah's eschatology depends on this single mechanism: if everything returns to one court, then no verdict of this world is final and no impunity in it is real — the inference the chapter drew explicitly at 40:16–17 (*the King is God; that Day no wrong is done; God is swift in reckoning*). Al-Rāzī notes that the phrase is placed at the end of the threats rather than the beginning so that patience is grounded in the *return* and not in the *score* — the believer is not told how much will be paid to whom, but only that the accounting is certain.

**The Measure of the Patience Commanded**

The standard is 46:35 — *be patient as the possessor of resolve among the messengers was patient* — and the sūrah's own roll-call supplies the content: Noah's long mockery (11:38), Abraham's fire and exile, Mūsā's double flight and Pharaoh's court, Joseph's prison, ʿĪsā's rejection. To these the Sunna adds the two definitions the chapter's vocabulary fits exactly: *"the strong believer is better and more beloved to God than the weak believer, while there is good in both"* (Muslim 2664) — strength being the *ṣabr* of 40:77 rather than the *quwwa* of 40:56's *ashadd min hādhā* — and *"patience is light"* (Muslim 223, in the hadith of purification), meaning that patience does not remove the darkness but lets a man walk in it. And the reward Ghāfir has already priced: *the patient are given their reward without measure* (39:10), the same phrase the Qur'an uses for the people of the Garden — *their reward is with their Lord, gardens of ʿAdn beneath which rivers flow, abiding therein forever* (9:72) and *and they will be given to drink... a cup whose mixture is camphor* in the sūrah of 76's description of *ʿadn* — which is to say that the only quality whose pay escapes reckoning is the one this verse commands.

**The Chapter's Three Commands**

Step back and the sūrah's imperatives form a program: *wa-istaghfir li-dhanbika* (40:55, echoing 40:3) — *iʿlam annamā ujīru... fa-aʿbud Llāh mukhliṣan lahu al-dīn* (40:65–66) — *fa-ṣṭabir* (40:55, 40:77). Fault, direction, time. The first two can be executed in a moment and the third cannot be finished at all, which is why the chapter closes on it: after the *ḥawāmīm*'s long demonstration that God is *al-ʿAzīz al-ʿAlīm*, *ghāfir al-dhanb*, *shadīd al-ʿiqāb*, the human being is given the single virtue that matches a life, and is told that its payment will not be counted.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:78

> **78** *And indeed We sent messengers before you — among them some We have recounted to you and some We have not recounted to you. It is not for a messenger to bring a sign except by God’s leave; so when God’s command comes, judgment is made with truth, and there the makers of false claims are lost.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse That Bounds the Reader's Curiosity**

*Wa-laqa arsalnā rusulan min qablika minhum man qaṣaṣnā ʿalayka wa-minhum man lam naqṣuṣ ʿalayka*. The clause consoles (you are not the first to be denied, as 40:5 said) and, in the same breath, disciplines the appetite for information: the Qur'an names its own limits. 4:164 is the twin — *and messengers We have recounted to you before, and messengers We have not recounted to you* — and the source commentary's note is the right one: the Book mentions a small number of prophets by name while Islamic tradition holds the company to be far larger, on the strength of the widely circulated report of 315 messengers and 124,000 prophets, whose chains the muḥaddithūn discuss and from which no doctrine should be built. Al-Suyūṭī treats these numbers in *al-Taḥadduth bi-Niʿmat Allāh* and Mālik's reported refusal to enumerate what God did not enumerate is the classical etiquette; the sūrah's own rule is simply: recount what is recounted, and stop.

**The Wisdom in the Unnamed**

Why hide some of the company? The verse's second clause supplies the reason by analogy: *wa-mā kāna li-rasūlin an ya'tiya bi-āyatin illā bi-idhni Llāh*. If a messenger cannot produce signs at demand, then neither his mission nor its record is available for verification on the skeptic's schedule; both run by *idhn*, leave. Al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr both take 40:78's middle clause as a rebuttal of the Makkans' bargaining, the form of which the Qur'an preserves elsewhere: *you will not believe in us until we are given the like of what God's messengers were given* (6:124), and 17:90–93's list of demanded springs, palaces, and swallowed earth. The source commentary adds the decisive historical datum: had every demand been met it would have changed nothing, as the splitting of the moon showed (54:1–2 — *the Hour has drawn near and the moon has split, and they see a sign and turn away and say: perpetual magic*) — and the Qur'an states the law: *and if We had sent down to them the angels, and the dead spoke to them, and We gathered everything before them, they would still not believe unless God wills* (6:111).

**The Dignity of the Office, Protected**

*Wa-mā kāna li-rasūlin* — "it was not for a messenger," i.e., not congruous with his rank — is not a demotion but a safeguard: a sign produced under compulsion from a doubter ceases to be a *āya* (a mark recognized from a distance, 40:4's *āyāt*) and becomes a trick, and the office of the messenger would become a service industry. The same logic governs the she-camel of Ṣāliḥ (7:73, 91:13, 54:27) and the requested table of the Māʾida, which was granted with the warning *God said: I will send it down to you, and whoever disbelieves after that among you — I will punish him with a punishment I do not inflict on any creature* (5:115). Al-Qurṭubī's note here is exactly on target: the limitation exists so that belief remains an act of the will; had signs been manufactured until faith was compulsory, *law shāʾa rabbuka la-āmana man fī al-arḍi kulluhum* (10:99) would have been executed, and the sūrah's own vocabulary — *ṣabr*, *waʿd*, *rujūʿ*, *shukr* — would have nothing to describe.

**"Judgment Is Made With Truth": the Day's Single Procedure**

*Fa-idhā jāʾa amru Llāhi quḍiya baynahum bi-l-ḥaqqi*. The passive of *qaḍā* with *bayna* is the Qur'an's technical phrase for adjudication (cf. 22:68–69 — *then God will judge between you regarding that in which you were disputing*; 10:93; 39:31), and 40:78's *amr* is the same event as 40:59's *inna al-sāʿata ātiyatun* and 40:77's *ilayna yurjaʿūn*: three consecutive verses, each naming the Day by a different one of its aspects — return, promise, command — so that the reader cannot localize the Hour into any single picture. The prepositional phrase *bi-l-ḥaqq* closes the argument of 40:20 (*wa-lā yuʾkhatu illā al-ḥaqq* — "nothing is taken but by right"): the same *ḥaqq* that made every gift and every demand legitimate in this life is the sole basis of the sentence in the next.

**"There the Mubṭilūn Are Lost": the Root Consumes Its User**

*Wa-khasira hunālika al-mubṭilūn*. The verse's final word picks up 40:5's description of the disputers' method — *jādilū bihi li-yubṭilū al-ḥaqq*, "they disputed with falsehood in order to nullify the truth" — and gives them their proper name: *mubṭil*, the one whose trade is *ibṭāl*, rendering things void. The verb of their end is *khusūr*, bankruptcy, a commercial term for capital spent with nothing to show (the sūrah's own economics: 40:39's *matāʿ qalīl* versus *dār al-qarār*, and the Qur'an's image of belief as *a trade that will never fail* (35:31)). The Qur'an's general maxim stands behind the line: *and say: the truth has come and falsehood has gone; falsehood was ever going* (17:81), and *what they used to invent has vanished from them* (6:24). So *khasira... al-mubṭilūn* is the chapter's most compressed irony: the man who spent his life emptying truth of weight arrives to find himself the empty thing — and *hunālika*, "there," names the one place where nothing can be faked.

**Why the Verse Comes Before the List of Gifts**

Ghāfir ends not with threats but with inventory: cattle, ships, stillness, provision, the *āyāt* of the cosmos (40:79–82). Its placement of 40:78 immediately before that inventory is the point: *you will not be given a sign on demand — and yet look at what is already in front of you.* The chapter's final word about evidence is thus not that it is scarce but that it is being ignored while a special proof is requested, which is precisely the disease 40:4 named and 40:82 will name again (*but most people do not know*).

**The Chapter's Last Word About Evidence, Spoken Just Before Its Inventory**
Verse 77 commanded patience; verse 78 grounds it. *Wa-laqa arsalnā rusulan min qablika* — the appeal to precedent recurs at 40:5 and 40:34 — but here it comes with a new qualification: *minhum man qaṣaṣnā ʿalayka wa-minhum man lam naqṣuṣ ʿalayka*, some of them We have recited to you and some We have not. The Prophet ﷺ is told, in the same breath in which he is told he is not alone, that he will not be told everything about those who went before. Al-Ṭabarī takes the clause as a rebuke of the demand for exhaustive verification: the record of revelation is selective by divine choice, and to ask "tell us about all of them, show us all the signs" is to mistake the character of the Book, which is *dhikr* (40:54, 40:65) and not an archive.
**Twenty-Six Names and a Closed Register**
The Qur'an names its prophets a small number of times, and the source commentary notes the figure of twenty-six named prophets while Islamic tradition holds the total to be far larger — the widely circulated report of 315 messengers among 124,000 prophets, whose chain the muḥaddithūn discuss without consensus. Al-Suyūṭī collects the varying enumerations in *al-Taḥadduth bi-Niʿmat Allāh*, and Malik's reported refusal to count what God did not count is the etiquette the chapter itself teaches. The reason for the reserve lies in the second half of the verse: if a messenger cannot bring a sign except by God's leave, then neither can the historian bring the complete file of the unseen. What God recites is what guides; what He withholds is not a gap in the guidance but a limit on curiosity — *you have not been given of knowledge except a little* (17:85), and *say: if the sea were ink for the words of my Lord, the sea would be exhausted before the words of my Lord were exhausted* (18:109).
**Why Signs Are Not Produced on Demand**
*Wa-mā kāna li-rasūlin an ya'tiya bi-āyātin illā bi-idhni Llāh* — the identical clause of 13:38, cited by the source commentary here. The construction *mā kāna li-* denotes unfitness rather than inability: it is not that the messenger lacks power but that the office does not work that way. Al-Rāzī and Ibn Kathīr both read the clause as the answer to the Makkans' bargaining (17:90–93; 6:8, 6:37; 74:52), and the source commentary adds the historical proof: the splitting of the moon was a sign given and rejected (54:1–2). The clause therefore protects the reader as much as the prophet: a faith that had to be certified by spectacle would not be the trust the sūrah asks for. The table of the Māʾida shows the cost of granting such a demand (5:112–115), and Noah's people show its futility (11:27–28).
**"Judgment Is Made With Truth" — the Closure of All Argument**
*Fa-idhā jā'a amru Llāhi quḍiya baynahum bi-l-ḥaqqi*. The sūrah's long sequence of *jidāl* (40:4, 35, 56, 69) ends in a single passive verb of adjudication: argument is not refuted, it is terminated by the arrival of the *amr*. Al-Zamakhsharī notes the force of *hunalika*, "there": the place where disputes are settled is the place where they can no longer be conducted. And the phrase *bi-l-haqq* is the same standard named at 40:20 (*la yu'khathu illa al-haqq*): the rule that governed every claim and every gift now governs the verdict, so the Day is not a new act of power but the last act of consistency.
**"There the Mubtilun Are Lost" — the Bankruptcy of the Nullifier**
*Wa-khāsira hunālika al-mubṭilūn*. The noun picks up 40:5's description of the disputers' method — they disputed with falsehood *in order to nullify* the truth — and hands them the name of their own instrument: the *mubtil*, the man whose trade is *ibṭāl*, voiding. The verb of his end is *khusūr*, a commercial term for capital spent with nothing to show, which fits a chapter that has been pricing things throughout: 40:39's *matāʿ qalīl* against *dār al-qarār*, 40:56's strength "all of it in loss," and 40:85's identical formula. The rhyme between *al-mubtilun* here and *al-kafirun* at the chapter's end is Ghafir's final equation: the falsifier and the disbeliever are one person, seen before and after the evidence runs out. Al-Qurṭubī quotes against them the Qur'an's own maxim — *and say: the truth has come, and falsehood has gone; falsehood was ever going* (17:81).
**Why This Verse Comes Just Here**
The chapter's closing movement turns to gifts — cattle, ships, stillness, provision, the shown *āyāt* (40:79–82). Placing 40:78 immediately before them makes the argument explicit: you will not be handed a sign on demand, and yet look at what is already in front of you. The sūrah's last word about evidence is therefore not that proof is scarce but that it is being ignored while a special proof is requested — the disease named at 40:4, diagnosed again at 40:83 (*they exulted in what they had of knowledge*), and answered in the five verses that follow.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:79

> **79** *God it is Who made for you the cattle, that some of them you may ride,*

**The Chapter Turns from Fire to Fleece**

*Allāhu lladhī jaʿala lakumu al-anʿāma li-tarkabū minha wa-minha ta'kulūn*. After six verses of eschatology, the sūrah changes register without changing argument: the next five verses (40:79–83) are an inventory of blessing, and their placement is the point. Ghāfir's case for *tawḥīd* has been built from cosmology (40:61–64), biography (40:67–68), and history (40:82–85); here it is built from *household* — the animals at the door, the ships at the shore, the night that makes the animal still. The logic is: if you cannot deny that these are *made for you* (the verse's *jaʿala lakum*), you have already conceded a Maker who is *for* you, and a Maker who is *for* you is not one you may address as one among several. Al-Rāzī notes that the Qur'an's *dālil al-inʿām* — the argument from benefaction — is the weakest for the proud to resist, because it requires no theory, only a memory of what you own and did not build.

**Al-Anʿām: the Grazing Blessing**

*Anʿām* is the collective for domesticated grazers — camels, cattle, sheep, goats — from *n-ʿ-m*, a root whose basic sense the lexicographers give as softness and ease, hence *niʿma* (blessing), *tanuʿumu* (to live at ease), *al-munʿam bihi* (that which is blessed with). Ibn Fāris treats *niʿma* and *anʿām* as from one family: the animal is called *anʿām* because it is the visible *nemah*, the growing increase, and the blessing is called by the same root because ease is what it buys. The sūrah that names this chapter *Ghāfir* thus ends by naming the *niʿma*, and 40:81's question — *fa-ayya āyāti Llāhi tunkirūn* — is a *shukr* question as much as a creed question. The Qur'an's fullest treatment of the same list is 36:71–73 (*Do they not see that We created for them, of what Our hands have made, cattle, so that they own them? And We made them submissive to them, so some of them they ride and some of them they eat...*), to which our verses are the *Ḥawāmīm* counterpart; and 16:5–8 adds the beauty and the warmth that Ghāfir omits in favor of riding and eating, because its subject is *strength*, not ornament.

**"That Some of Them You May Ride" — the Verb of Submission**

*Tarkabū* — you mount, you embark — is used of riding animals in 43:12 (*and who creates the pairs all of them, and makes for you of the ships and of the cattle what you ride*) and in 8:60 (*you have not the strength to mount*), and 40:79's phrase *li-tarkabū minha* means "for you to ride *from among them*," the partitive marking that no single kind does everything (camels in the sand, donkeys and mules in 16:8 and 36:73 for the "what you do not know"). What the sūrah leaves unstated but 36:72 supplies — *wa-dhallalnāhā lahum fa-lam yakūnū lahā muqrinīn* — "and We subdued them to them, and they would not have subdued them" — is the theological center: the animal's obedience to a child and a stranger is a *created* docility, and the sūrah's *jaʿala* is the verb of that creation. The modern reader's equivalent is the fact that a horse lets itself be steered by its mouth; the classical reader was offered the same datum and told to see a *mashīʾa* behind it.

**"And From Them You Eat" — Provision, Not Right**

*Wa-minha ta'kulūn*, with the shift from the subjunctive of purpose (*li-tarkabū*) to the indicative of fact (*ta'kulūn*), which the rhetoricians take as signaling that eating is not a *goal* one sets but a plain condition of life already in force: you do not ride in order to ride, but you already eat. The Qur'an's dietary framing is stable here (cf. 6:142–144's list of the eight pairs, which forbids *waṣīla* and *baḥīra* and returns the animals to God's ownership, and 36:73's *lahum fīhā manāfiʿu wa-mashārib*). Ghāfir's one-word contribution is *lakum*, "for you": the animal is *made for you*, and by the same token you are *made for* the next clause — *fa-ṣṭabir* (40:77). Provision and command come from one hand, which is why the sūrah puts the list of gifts immediately after the command to patience: the patience it asks for is not ingratitude's.

**Cattle and Ships, the Sūrah's Paired Proof**

As in 23:21–22 and 43:12–13, the cattle are named with ships (40:80), so that the two media of travel — dry land and sea — are covered by gifts the audience cannot dispute, since Quraysh's wealth *was* the camel and the Mediterranean-Indian trade the ship. Al-Ṭabarī's note is that the pairing is evidential rather than poetic: whoever denies resurrection is being shown two things he already uses daily and could not produce if he tried.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:80

> **80** *and for you therein are benefits, and so that you may reach through them any need that is in your breasts. And upon them and upon ships are you carried.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Benefits": the Word for What Works Whether You Thank or Not**

*Wa-lakum fīhā manāfiʿu*. *Manfaʿa* is from *n-f-ʿ*, and its *maṣdar* is the *nafaʿ* of a tree whose roots drink: what benefits *whether or not* anyone notices — used absolutely of the utility of animals, plants, and rain and the same noun recurs in the sūrah's own twin, 36:72's *lahum fīhā manāfiʿu wa-mashāribu*, and in 6:142's *manāfiʿ* and 16:5–6's *wa-lakum fīhā jamālun wa-manāfiʿ* — "for you in them is adornment and benefits." Al-Ṭabarī explains the verse's *manāfiʿ* as what the Arabs took from the beasts: wool, hair, felt, skins, milk, butter, dung as fuel and for the ground, the carrying of water, the ploughing, the hides for tents; and he notes that the verse lists these *without praising them*, because its business is not to flatter the gift but to name the Giver. The rhetorical gain of the noun, as Ibn ʿĀshūr? the later rhetoricians point out, is its *generality*: a *manfaʿa* is unglamorous and unavoidable, and the Qur'an's choice to endow the unglamorous with evidential weight is one of the sūrah's quiet inversions of value (cf. 40:39's *matāʿ qalīl*, and 96:1–5's teaching by the pen).

**"The Need That Is in Your Breasts" — Revelation of the Interior**

*Wa-tablughū fī ṣudūrikum ḥājatan tummūnhā*. The *ḥāja* is a need, and its seat is placed in the *ṣadr*, the breast, in Arabic's own physiology of desire — cf. 40:19's *khāʾinat al-aʿyun wa-mā tukhnī al-ṣudūr*, "the betrayal of the eyes and what the breasts conceal," The verse's claim is thus a double one: God made the animal to carry *what you did not dare to hope for*, and He knows the hope before it is hoped, which is exactly the pair the sūrah established at 40:19 and 40:60 (*wa-qāla rabbukumu udʿūnī*). Al-Zamakhsharī takes *tumma* ("attain") as the verb of reaching a limit after travel — *ammata al-masāfa*, the distance covered — and reads the image as the pack-animal carrying the man to the edge of his wish. Al-Rāzī, with the plainest sense, glosses: the camel and the ship do not merely relieve the body, they make possible projects that would otherwise be *concealed as impossible in the chest* — a marriage across a distance, a market, a flight to safety, a pilgrimage. That the verse names *ḥāja*, not *ghinā*, is deliberate: it says that God answers *needs*, not greed, and the sūrah has already priced both (40:39).

**"Upon Them and Upon Ships You Are Carried" — One Verb for Two Elements**

*Wa-ʿalayhā wa-ʿalā al-fulki tuḥmalūn*. The verb *ḥamala* is the carrying of a load, used both of a beast and of a ship (cf. 6:143–144, 23:22, 29:15, 31:32, 42:32, 52:31, 54:13, 69:11, 81:4, 82:12) and, on the other side, of burden — *wa-ʿalayhā yuḥmalū al-awzār* is not the Qur'an's wording; the contrast is inside the sūrah: 40:72's *yusḥabūn*, being dragged, versus *tuḥmalūn* here, being carried — the same passive voice, one borne to a need, the other dragged to a *ḥamīm*. That contrast, in the sūrah's own words, is 40:80's real contribution: a *ḥamla* is only ever a *direction*. *Fulk* is a ship — a collective noun that takes singular or plural agreement, which is why the Qur'an can say *wa-l-fulki allatī tajrī fī al-baḥri bi-mā yanfaʿu al-nās* (2:164), *wa-l-fulki tajrī fī al-baḥri bi-niʿmati Llāhi* (31:31; 35:12; 42:32; 45:12), and *until, when they are in the ship and it carries them with a fair wind and the people therein rejoice, a wind of punishment strikes it* (10:22) — and 29:15's *fa-najjaynāhu wa-man maʿahu fī al-fulki wa-jaʿalnāhā āyatan li-l-ʿālamīn*, Noah's ship as the archetype of every saved voyage: the sūrah thus names, in a single word, both a vehicle and a *sign*, and its hearers knew the Red Sea and the Indian trade as both.

**Why the Breast, the Beasts, and the Ships Come Together**

The three elements of 40:80 — hidden need, domesticated animal, ship — make one syllogism: God knows what you want (40:19), He has made things to get it (40:79–80), and He alone is the one to be asked (40:60). Set against 40:49's failed upward call from the Fire — *ask your Lord to lighten for us a day of the punishment* — the pair is the chapter's final practical teaching: the *duʿāʾ* that is answered is the one made while *fulk* and *anʿām* are still carrying you.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:81

> **81** *And He shows you His signs — which of God's signs, then, do you deny?*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Can Be Answered Only by Not Answering**

*Wa-yurīkum āyātihi fa-ayya āyāti Llāhi tunkirūn*. The verse's interrogative is not seeking information; it is a *taqrīʿ*, a pinning-down, and the sūrah has already shown how such a question works (40:13's *fa-kadhālika yaṭbaʿu* being its mirror image, and cf. 55:13). Al-Ṭabarī and Ibn Kathīr record the two senses: (i) if you admit these benefits are from God, how can you deny His power to resurrect (Q, and the source commentary's first reading); and (ii) an expression of amazement and reproof, as if to say that nothing but stubbornness could make a man deny what he sees with his own eyes (IK). Both work in the sūrah's context: 40:81 follows 40:79–80's gifts and precedes 40:82–85's history, so the question straddles the two halves of evidence — the present and the archived.

**The Refrain's Quiet Relative: Sūrat al-Raḥmān**

*Fa-ayya āyāti rabbikumā tukadhdhibāni* — "which of your Lord's boons do you both deny?" — occurs thirty-one times in the sūrah of al-Raḥmān, and the source commentary notes the parallel. The family resemblance is instructive, because the two sūrahs ask the same question with different verbs: al-Raḥmān accuses of *takdhīb* (calling something a lie), Ghāfir of *inkār* (not recognizing). To *deny* a sign is, in this sūrah's vocabulary, the *opposite* of *iqrār* (acknowledgment), and the Qur'an's most moving uses of *ʿarafa* are for the moment when denial is impossible: *fa-law tarā idh al-mujrimūna nākisū ru'ūsihim ʿinda rabbihim rabbana abṣarnā wa-samiʿnā inna al-ʿadaba ḥaqq* — "they would say: our Lord, we have seen and heard" (32:12), and 6:23's *wallāhi rabbana ma kunnā mushrikīn*, so that the verb chosen here, *tunkirūn*, already concedes the sūrah's point: the sign is knowable, and *unkar* is a refusal of knowledge rather than an absence of it.

**"He Shows You" — Revelation as an Ongoing Present**

The verb is present-tense *yurīkum*, and the sūrah's whole sequence of *āyāt* (40:61–82) is likewise in the imperfect: *yuwaṭṭiʾukum*, *yusawwirakum*, *yurīkum*. That is not stylistic. Al-Biqāʿī and al-Rāzī both note that the Qur'an's habit in sign-passages is the imperfect, because the *āya* is not a past event appealed to but a present event appealed from — and the aorist is what makes 40:81's question live: He is *showing you*, now, in the animals you milk and the sea you cross, so the denial in question is not a 7th-century creed but this reader's present inattention. Ibn al-Qayyim's epistemology fits here: in *Madārij al-Sālikīn* he treats the *āyāt* as *maẓāhir al-tawḥīd*, places where God's oneness appears, so that *tunkirūn* is not an intellectual error but a failure of sight, which is why Ghāfir's argument for *iqrār* is built on the verb *raʾā* (40:69's *a-fa-lam yaraw*, 40:82's *fa-lam yaraw*, 40:84's *lammā raʾaw ba'sanā*).

**The Verse as the Sūrah's Own Method Summarized**

Put 40:81 beside 40:34's sentence about the man who argued *bilā sulṭānin ātāhu* — "without any authority that came to him" — and the chapter's theory of evidence appears complete: the *sulṭān* (authority) is exactly these *āyāt* that God shows, and *kufr* is not the absence of authority but its rejection, which is why 40:81's question ends the section of *gifts* and opens the section of *ruins* (40:82): the same *āyāt* are on display in what God gives and in what He destroys, and the verse's *tunkirūn* applies to both.

**The Refrain, and Why the Dual**

*Fa-ayya āyāti rabbikumā tukadhdhibāni* — thirty-one times in al-Raḥmān, and the source commentary's point about the *dual* is that the sūrah's addressees are humans and jinn, so the refrain's *ʿadāb* is a *contract*: both are addressed, both are asked, and both are given the chance to answer. Ghāfir's single *fa-ayya āyāti Llāhi tunkirūn* differs in the verb and the tense — the Qur'an's other refrain-verse asks about *takdhīb* in the *past and habitual*, this one about *inkār* in the *present* — and the shift is the whole of the sūrah's argument about evidence: to *call a thing a lie* is an act you do to a report, to *fail to recognize* is a state you fall into while something is being shown (40:81's *yurīkum*). The verse's implication is therefore severe and simple: the more *maḥḍa* the showing, the less excuses the un-recognizing leaves.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:82

> **82** *Have they not journeyed upon the earth and seen how the end of those before them was? They were more numerous than them, mightier than them, and firmer in the traces upon the earth; and what they earned did not avail them.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fourth Travel, and the Same Report**

*Afalam yakūnū yaraw kam ahlaknā min qablihim min qarnin mashaw fī masākinihim*. Ghāfir has sent its readers on this journey once already (40:21 — *afalam yasīrū fī al-arḍi fa-yankurū na-lladhīna min qablihim...*), and the refrain recurs at 12:109, 30:9, 30:42, 35:44, and 47:10, and each time the destination is the same: the *āthār*, traces, which are the Arabs' own library. The Makkan caravan routes to Syria passed the dwellings of ʿĀd and Thamūd, and 40:82's *mashaw fī masākinihim* ("they walked in their dwellings") is a claim about the landscape the audience *knew*: the verse does not ask them to believe a report, only to draw the inference they already had the means to draw. Al-Ṭabarī glosses: "have they not travelled and *looked*, so that the seeing of the destroyed should teach them?" — and the point of the repetition is that the same people who refuse the argument from cattle cannot claim to have never heard the argument from stones.

**Three Comparatives That Dismantle Deterrence Theory**

The sūrah's earlier uses of the same root — 40:82's *āthara*, 40:56's *quwwa* that is "all in loss," and 40:35's *ṣarrafa* of the sealed heart — build one lesson, and the same tricolon appears elsewhere in the Qur'an: *And We made them stronger in body than they are, and We had given them hearing and sight and hearts; but their hearing and their sight and their hearts availed them nothing at all, since they used to reject the signs of God* (46:26) — and 89:6–13's account of Iram *dhāt al-ʿimād*, whose like was not created in the lands, and of Pharaoh *dhī al-awtād*, with its verdict: *fa-ṣabba ʿalayhim rabbuka saṭa ʿadhābin — your Lord poured over them a lash of punishment*. The comparative is thus Ghāfir's answer to the argument from development: capacity is not immunity, and permanence of footprint is not permanence of tenure (cf. 40:39's *qalīl* and 57:20's *ladīb*).

**"And What They Earned Did Not Avail Them"**

*Fa-lā yanfaʿuhum mā kānū yaksibūn*. The verse's verdict uses the sūrah's own moral physics: *kasb* (acquisition, 40:17, 40:40) is what a person actually owns on the Day, and everything not *kasb* — the inherited wall, the bought army, the built trace — is *lā yanfaʿ*. Al-Ṭabarī's double reading (source commentary's note on 40:82) is worth preserving: the clause can be a statement about the ancients ("what they earned did not avail them") or a rhetorical question about the Quraysh ("what will what they earn avail them?"), and both senses are Ghāfir's, since the sūrah's method all along has been to read past destruction as present address (40:13's *dhālika bi-annahu...*, 40:22's *fa-astamtiʿtum bi-l-matāʿi...*). The source commentary's parallels for the verb of avail are 15:84 and 39:50 — *and what they used to earn did not avail them* — the latter in the sūrah of al-Zumar, whose own *kulū ya ʿibādiya lladhīna asrafū ʿalā anfusihim* is a note of hope that Ghāfir has already sounded in 40:3, 40:9, 40:53, and 40:55.

**The Eye, Not the Argument**

The verse's verbs are *yaraw* and *mashaw* — seeing and walking — not *tafakkara* or *qālū*. This is Ghāfir's epistemology stated plainly (40:81's *yurīkum* is the divine half of the same idea): signs are for eyes that travel, and the sūrah repeatedly indicts not a failure of intellect but of *attention* (40:4, 40:5, 40:6, 40:69, 40:70). Al-Qurṭubī draws the practical rule that the commentators keep repeating here: the traveler through ruined lands should travel as a *muʿtabir*, one taking *ʿibra* — and that is why the Prophet ﷺ forbade entering the dwellings of the punished *unless weeping* (a note the exegetes attach to 40:82's context, from the entry at al-Ḥijr).

**What the Verse Does to the Reader**

Read after 40:76's *biʾsa dār al-mutakabbirīn*, 40:82 is not a prophecy but a photograph of what a sūrah is: the chapter's whole movement from *anʿām* to *āthār* says that a civilization's wealth and its works are, from the standpoint of the One who "shows His signs," the *same class of datum* — gifts and graves. The verse's application is therefore a single question, and it is the one Ghāfir has been asking since 40:4: which of the two columns of evidence is a man more likely to ignore?

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:83

> **83** *So when their messengers came to them with the clear proofs, they exulted in what they had of knowledge; and what they used to mock surrounded them.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Repetition With One Word Changed**

The verse's Arabic is *fa-lammā jāʾat hum rusuluhum bi-l-ḥaqqi fariḥū bimā ʿindahum minā al-ʿilmi wa-ḥāqa bihim mā kānū bihi yastahziʾūn*, and it repeats 40:24's and 40:25's *lammā jāʾat hum* structure, so that the *repetition* produces the sūrah's verdict: the same event — the messenger arriving with *al-ḥaqq* — described once in the court of Pharaoh and once in the archive of the nations, has one outcome each time, and the outcome is fixed not by the quality of the evidence but by the disposition of the audience. What 40:83 adds, and 40:24–25 did not, is the psychology of that disposition: *fariḥū bimā ʿindahum minā al-ʿilmi*.

**The Sin of Being Pleased With One's Own Knowledge**

*Fariḥūn* is 40:75's word, and its reappearance is the sūrah's own commentary: the exultation in the land and the exultation in one's knowledge are *the same vice at two levels* — the arrogant *ʿilm* is what replaces the arrogant *māl*. Ibn Kathīr, on the verse, gives both readings the source commentary records: (i) they rejoiced in what they possessed of worldly cunning and considered it enough, saying they would not be punished or raised (Q), and (ii) they exulted in the *little* they knew, "the outward aspect of the life of this world, and of the Hereafter they are heedless" (30:7; cf. 29:20, 30:7). The verse's *ʿinda* ("what was *with* them") is the qualifier that stings: not knowledge as God's gift but knowledge as a possession, a *milk* that licenses dismissal. The same diagnosis is made of Qārūn (28:78's *innamā ūtītuhu ʿalā ʿilmin ʿindī* — "I was given it because of knowledge *with me*"), and Ghāfir's *ʿind* echoes that phrase exactly. Al-Rāzī adds that the *fariḥ* of the knowledgeable is the most durable form of pride, because it can be practiced in acts of devotion and in argument and still look like seriousness — which is why 40:4's *jidāl* and 40:83's *fariḥ* are the chapter's paired symptoms.

**"What They Used to Mock Beset Them"**

*Ḥāqa*, from *ḥ-w-q*, is to encircle and to become unavoidable, and it is the root behind a claim that has become due — the verb is used of a threat or decree that comes true, as in 20:97's *wa-qad ḥaqqa ʿalaynā qawlunā* and 46:21's *wa-ḥaqqa al-ʿiqābu bi-mā kānū yaksibūn* — so the usage is: the verb occurs in the Qur'an in the idiom *wa-ḥāqa bihim mā istahzaʾū bihi*, "and what they used to mock encircled them" (6:10, 11:8, 16:34, 21:41, 39:48, 45:33, 46:26). The image is of a ring closing: mockery is the attitude of a man who stands *outside* an event and jokes about it, and the verb names the moment he is inside it. The source commentary's parallel, 10:44 — *God does not wrong people in the least, but people wrong themselves* — supplies the sūrah's theodicy, and 40:83's *mā kānū bihi yastahziʾūn* makes the punishment the completion of a habit rather than an external penalty, exactly as Ibn al-Qayyim argues in *al-Rūḥ*: the sin is the wound and the punishment is the wound's own pain, seen without an anaesthetic.

**One Word That Turns the Verse Toward the Believer**

The source commentary records al-Ṭabarī's second reading: the *fariḥ* can refer to the *messengers'* people rejoicing in the knowledge God gave them, in which case the verse's structure is a contrast — some exult in *what they have*, and the punishment befalls them; the true *fariḥ* is the believer's: *say: by the grace of God and by His mercy — in that let them rejoice* (10:58), and *they rejoice in the bounty from God and His grace* (3:170). The double reading is not indecision; it is the sūrah's last piece of advice about this chapter's central emotion. There is a joy that is evidence of *having* and a joy that is evidence of *being given*, and 40:83 leaves the reader to decide which of the two his pleasure in his own knowledge is — the same question the sūrah asked of Pharaoh's tower and of Qārūn's wealth.

**The Verse's Place in the Chapter's Architecture**

40:83 is the chapter's last narrative verse, and it closes the loop opened at 40:4: the disputers (4), the plotters (5), the seized (6), and here the mocked-around (83). Between them lies everything Ghāfir had to say — forgiveness (40:3, 9, 53), prayer (40:14, 60), patience (40:55, 77), gifts (40:61–64, 79–80) — and the sūrah's final pair (40:84–85) will say the one thing left: that the *ʿilm* they were so pleased with will, on the Day, become sight (40:84's *lammā raʾaw ba'sanā*), and that sight, unearned, avails nothing (40:85).

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:84

> **84** *So when they saw Our might, they said: We believe in God alone, and we disavow what we used to associate with Him.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**From Pleasure in Knowledge to the Knowledge of Sight**

*Fa-lammā raʾaw baʾsanā qālū āmannā bi-Llāhi waḥdahu wa-kafarnā bimā kunnā nushriku bihi*. The verse's pivot is the verb *raʾaw*: the people of 40:83 were pleased with *ʿilm* they *had*, and here they receive the only *ʿilm* that converts — vision. Ghāfir has prepared this contrast all chapter long (40:19's eyes, 40:20's *basīr*, 40:58's blind and seeing, 40:61's *mubṣir*, 40:69's *yaraw*, 40:82's *yaraw*), and 40:84 is the payoff: knowledge that has not become sight is, in this sūrah's scale, not a partial possession but a total one, because its object is a person and only presence meets a person. Al-Ṭabarī's gloss on *baʾs* is "Our punishment," and the commentators note the verse's diction: not *ʿadhāb* but *baʾs*, the word for *might* as it is *felt* — severity experienced, not described (cf. 40:22's *mā yubarrīna*, and 40:85's *bi-baʾsinā*).

**A Perfect Creed, Uttered Too Late**

Note the quality of the sentence. *Āmannā bi-Llāhi waḥdahu wa-kafarnā bimā kunnā nushriku bihi* — the *tawḥīd* it states is exactly what 40:14 commanded (*fa-udʿū Llāh mukhliṣīna lahu al-dīn wa-law kariha al-kāfirūn*), and the *kufr* it renounces is exactly what 40:73's interrogation established they had never really had. As a proposition it is flawless. The verse's horror is that a flawless proposition is not the thing required: what was required was that the *tawḥīd* be said *before the seeing* — in the mode of the unseen (*bi-l-ghayb*, 6:3, 2:3, 35:18, 36:11, 67:12) — because faith in the Qur'an's sense is a *trust*, and trust with full visibility is not trust. Al-Rāzī makes this the verse's core: the demand was never for an estimate of God's power but for the *orientation of the will* while alternatives were still open.

**The Qur'an's Law of the Closed Window**

*Fa-lam yakun yanfaʿuhum īmānuhum lammā raʾaw baʾsanā* (40:85) is the same rule as 4:18 — *repentance is not for those who do evil until, when death confronts one of them, he says "now I repent," nor for those who die disbelieving* — and 6:158 — *the day some of the signs of your Lord comes, a soul's faith does not benefit it, if it did not believe before or earned in its faith any good* — and the sūrah of Yūnus' *thumma lam yakun yaḥtāja illā an qālū āmannā bi-llāhi waḥdahu wa-kafarnā bimā kunnā bihi mushrikīn*? keep: *so would we be hastened with evil... ? Rather, We let them enjoy... they did not have the patience for it*? Set aside the disputed wording and keep the firm pair: 10:21's description of despair after respite, and 22:11's *fa-in kanzaba... wa-yabna* no. The sound classical statement of the doctrine is in al-Ṭabarī: *the sign is not to be believed once it has forced belief*, because compulsion abolishes the very thing that was being asked for; and he cites 6:158 in support. The point can be stated without further citation: the window for faith is the interval of the unseen, and Ghāfir's whole chapter — 40:3's *wa-man yaʿṣi* ... — has been an argument for spending the interval.

**"We Disavow What We Used to Associate" — the Return of 40:73**

*Kafarnā bimā kunnā nushriku bihi* answers 40:73's *ayna lladhīna kuntum tushriʿūna bihim* almost word for word, and the sūrah's arrangement lets the reader measure the difference: then, in the interrogation, they said *ḍallū ʿannā*, "they vanished from us," an excuse cast as an observation; here, in sight of *baʾs*, they say *kafarnā*, "we disbelieved in them" — the grammar of responsibility. The two verses are the same confession at two degrees of honesty, and it is Ghāfir's final psychology of impenitence: a man will describe his idols' absence long before he admits his own choice.

**What the Verse Does Not Say**

It is important that the verse records no reproach, no dialogue, no negotiation — unlike 40:47–50, where the weak argue with the strong and the keepers of Hell refuse. At the sight of *baʾs* the sūrah stops letting anyone speak at length: the single *qālū* and its two verbs (believed, disavowed) are all the room given, because the time of words is over. That silence is the verse's rhetorical force, and 40:85 will supply the single sentence that ends the chapter's last discussion: *sunnatallāhī allatī qad khalat fī ʿibādihi wa-khāsira hunālika al-kāfirūn* — a law, and a verdict, with nothing said in between.

---

## Sūrah Ghāfir 40:85

> **85** *But their believing did not avail them when they saw Our might — God's practice that has gone through among His servants. And the disbelievers lost there.*

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Single Clause of Law, Inserted Between Two Clauses of Loss**

*Fa-lam yakun yanfaʿuhum īmānuhum lammā raʾaw baʾsanā sunnatallāhī allatī qad khalat fī ʿibādihi wa-khāsira hunālika al-kāfirūn*. Grammarians and rhetoricians alike note that *sunnat Allāh* is in *naṣb* as a *maṣdar mu'akkid li-mā qablahā* — an accusative of emphasis confirming the preceding sentence — the reading the source commentary gives, and the effect is that the verse does not add new information but *names the genus*: "that was God's practice that has passed among His servants." The second sense the classical exegetes allow ("like God's practice") points to 3:137 and 35:43, and both are present: the loss is *an instance* of a law, and the law is *known from its instances*.

**Sunnat Allāh: the Word That Kills Exceptionalism**

A *sanna* is a path trodden smooth, and *sunnat Allāh* in the Qur'an is, as the source commentary's parallels show (3:137? keep 33:62, 35:43, 48:23), always the same claim: there is no exception, no *tabdīl*, no private arrangement with anyone's status, talent, or repentance-at-the-last-minute. Al-Rāzī's comment is that the verse's function is to destroy the one comfort the arrogant always keep — "the rule applies to others, my case is special" (cf. 2:88's *qulūbunā ghulfun* and 25:32's *law shāʾ Allāhu la-ātanā bi-mithli hādhā qurʾānan*). Ghāfir has dramatized exactly that belief through its whole cast: Noah's people, ʿĀd, Thamūd, the *quarn* after *quarn*, and Pharaoh, who asked for one door and got the river. The *khalat* ("has passed through, is settled") is the tense of experience: this is not a decree issued today but the oldest fact about the human case.

**The Verse's Theological Hardness, and Why It Is Not Cruelty**

Critics have asked why a faith that would be saving if held yesterday is worthless today, and the sūrah's own answer is built into its vocabulary. Ghāfir never says belief is *arbitrarily* refused; it says the *function* of belief is to reorient a will while alternatives are live, and 40:85 describes a statement that has no will in it at all: the "believers" of the moment of *baʾs* believe exactly as a drowning man believes in buoyancy. Al-Ṭabarī's gloss on *fa-lam yanfaʿhum* is: "it did not benefit them, because *īmān* is only of benefit when it is done in the interval during which God is being obeyed and God's command is awaited, not when God's command has come." That formulation is the sūrah's whole moral economy restated: 40:77's *inna waʿda Llāhi ḥaqq* and 40:85's *sunnat Allāh* are one proposition seen from the two ends of the interval, and the interval is what the chapter's imperatives (40:60, 40:66, 40:77) are for.

**"And the Disbelievers Lost There" — the Chapter's Last Word About Us**

*Wa-khāsira hunālika al-kāfirūn* rhymes with 40:78's *wa-khāsira hunālika al-mubṭilūn*, and the two-verse echo is the sūrah's final teaching: the *mubṭil* is the man who nullified truth with false argument (40:5, 40:34, 40:56, 40:69, 40:73), and the *kāfir* is that same man after the argument has run out — and *hunālika*, "there," keeps both clauses in the same place. The word *khusūr* is Ghāfir's answer to the chapter's opening *inḍalāf*: the only real loss is *loss*, and the sūrah's last sound is not the Fire but a bankruptcy settlement.

**The Rhetorical Ring: *Khaṭāʾ*, *Khusūr*, and the Root That Ends the Chapter**

The chapter's ring is worth stating plainly: it opens with a *dhikr al-kitāb* whose letters are a claim that nothing in it is accidental (40:1–2: *Ḥāʾ. Mīm. Revelation of the Book... from God, the All-Mighty, the All-Knowing*), and it closes with a *khāsira* whose verb is about nothing else than what a person actually *acquired*. The two are the same assertion at opposite ends: everything in the Book is deliberate, and nothing in the soul is uncounted. That is Ghāfir's whole lesson, and the sūrah's final word — *al-kāfirūn*, the ones who *covered* what they were shown (40:81's *tunkirūn*, 40:83's *kafarnā*) — names the loss as the *cover*: the only real ruin in this chapter is having been shown the *āyāt* and having preferred the *matāʿ*.

**How the Chapter Ends a Reader**

Ghāfir's last verses do not console by describing Paradise — 40:8's *jannāt ʿadn* was said fifty verses earlier and left un-embellished — because the sūrah's pastoral design is to return the reader to the *interval*: to *istighfār* (40:55), to *duʿāʾ* (40:60), to *ikhlāṣ* (40:65–66), to *ṣabr* (40:77), and to *shukr* for the animals and the ships (40:79–81). Read as a whole, the chapter's shape is a single sentence: *He made for you* (40:79) and *He shows you* (40:81); *call upon Me* (40:60) and *be patient* (40:77); *no wrong is done that Day* (40:17) and *God's practice has passed among His servants* (40:85). Everything else in the sūrah — the *ḥawāmīm*' long trial of Pharaoh, the believing man's five speeches, the angels around the Throne — is elaboration of those six clauses, and the chapter ends, as its own 40:3 said, with the journey's end in view: *wa-ilayhi al-maṣīr*.

---
